OF THIS BOOK

WRITTEN AT THE FRONT OF THIS BOOK IN THE THIRD SUMMER OF THE SIEGE, WHEN THE BOOK WAS ALREADY HALF FULL · OF THE HEARTH, AND OF THIS BOOK.

By Seren Two-Inks, scribe of this Book, and the third of the hearth of Halyna.

It is the third summer of the siege, and the short dark of the year lies upon the mountain; about me the hall is asleep, and the fire of the Long Hearth has sunk to a bed of coals, ember at the heart and ash at the rim, and up on the wall the banner lifts and falls in a wind I cannot hear. This Book is half full. One of its tellers lies already under the east cairns, and others whose names are written in it lie beside him; and tonight I have turned it back to its first leaf, which I left bare when I began, for a mason does not cut the lintel ere the posts are raised.

This is the Book of the Riven Stone, wherein are set down the tales that are laid at the Long Hearth of Rivenkeep, in the hall behind the inner gate, under the wall where the torn cloak flies. They are laid in the hours after an assault, when the guns are cooling and the mortar has not yet set, and there is nothing left to do with the hands but hold them to the fire.

We did not make the custom; the first builders of the Keep made it, in the age ere ever a haven stood upon the shore, and when their stone was set and their tools were cleaned, they sat at this hearth, and each night one of them laid a tale. In time they went down to the sea to raise the havens, and the Keep was left, and their children’s children forgot the custom as they forgot the Keep; and we found it again at the bottom of a chest in the deepest vault, as you will read. Now we sit where they sat. Our wall is rising again, and so is this Book.

Beside the hearth, in a niche of the hearth-wall, lies the old capstone of the inner gate. It fell in the forgotten years, when no one was left upon the ridge to hear it fall, and it lay in the gateway with its vow to the earth, while the grass grew over it and the snows of uncounted winters came and went, till Halyna, he and she, set their four hands to it at dusk on the day of the banner and turned it over, for it is a stone that takes two. Halvard’s fathers cut that stone, and Rhyna’s forebears laid it; and on its underside, where no weather has come at it since it was set, the vow of the first builders keeps its edge.

Whoever holds that stone tells. When the tale is done, the teller sets it back in its niche, and we do not say then that the tale was told, but that it was laid: for a tale that is only told is gone by morning, and a tale that is laid is part of the wall.

Wherever I write the name of a bonded pair, I set it under one thin stroke, as a lintel lies across two posts; for it is one name, and it is the name of two. Halyna are Halvard Stone-Warden and Rhyna of the Inner Gate, the two of them, the Bonded of the Keep; and the eldest pair, Idrenna, who taught them their craft, sit yet on the bench by the door, and nod when a tale is laid true. Orvenna are our wall-masters, who have never once been heard to raise their voices on the wall; and the youngest pair are Enrella, Enno and Della, who were sealed in our first summer, and the one of them cannot begin a jest but the other ends it.

When a tale of ours is laid, the hearth answers it with one voice and two words: We remember. The answer comes out of the first words of the Title, which the Captain wrote in coal from this hearth; and of all that is said at this fire, it is the one thing that is never said another way. Old Ebba of Fenholm said it first, on the first night, when none of us knew what should be said; and most nights she begins it still, a breath before us all, and in behind her we come, as the tide comes in behind its first wave.

Some of these tales are not ours but the wood’s: they come out of the wreck-wood that the tide brings in under our wall, and out of the dark stone that lies in the chest I carried up the mountain, and out of the hearts of the grey hulls we have broken; and the folk who grew that wood, behind the grey, we call the Mystaeri, the mist-folk, which is our word for them, and not theirs. The wood is not read, for there are no words in it; it is known whole, as a memory is known, the beginning and the middle and the end of it at once, and the telling is the hard part: words can be turned, and a knowing cannot, and so the wood’s tales are told in the fewest words that will hold them. When a telling of the wood is done, the hearth is silent, not knowing how such a tale should be answered; and that silence I have written down, each time it fell.

I do not know the wood myself, for I am not of the Aetherbonded, whom the guild names more simply the Bonded, but of those it calls unbonded; and when I came up the mountain alone, Halyna, he and she, took me into their hearth as its third. When they know the wood, I hold the lamp. Once I laid my own palm upon the grain, and let go, for what came into me was too great for me to hold; and so mine is the hand that writes, and not the hand that knows. Every scribe of the guild before me was one of a bonded pair; why this Book is written by one who is not, Halyna tell in its own place, to the children.

Each night, when a tale of ours has been laid and the hall has gone to its blankets, I sit on alone with the ink and set the tale in our own tongue, in measure, as a mason sets a course: so many stones to the line, and every joint broken above the joint below it, that the line may stand under the weight of a voice. The words are the teller’s, and I change not one of them; the measure is mine. Each word I say aloud three times ere I write it, to hear whether it will hold. Most nights the coals have gone to ash, and the hall is breathing in its sleep about me, and the last word is still in my mouth.

I love our tongue.

Up the mountain I carried two things, our tongue and a chest; and only the chest was heavy.

Halyna both would have the wood’s tales stand in this Book beside our own. “A tale told from one side of a wall,” one of them said, and the other finished, “is half a tale.” So wherever one thing was seen from both sides, I have set the two tellings facing, ours upon the left leaf and the wood’s upon the right; and where the wood’s leaf could not yet be told, I have left it bare, with only the grain showing, that whoever opens this Book may see, in that grain, the shape of what we did not yet understand, and wait.

When Halyna tell a tale of the wood, his voice and hers take it by turns, a paragraph to each, for they know the tales as they know each other, and neither waits upon the other’s word. I have used two inks, one for each of them, and they would not have me mark which is whose. You cannot tell. A line they speak between them, the one beginning it and the other ending it, I have set down in both the inks.

The Captain does not tell. He sits at the end of the bench with his hood up, and hears his own deeds told, and says nothing, unless it be to say that it was not like that. On the wall he is the Commander, for so the Rite named him; at this hearth, by his own wish, he is the Captain still; and when the dead are named, he puts his hood back.

Where a carving was broken, or a knowing could not be told, I have said so, and I have not mended it with guesses; and where the tales do not agree, I have kept both.

This Book is ink. Halyna mean it to be cut into the granite of this hall, course upon course, when there is peace enough to cut it; and till that day it is kept in the chest I carried, among the mute stones.

WRITTEN AT THE FRONT OF THIS BOOK IN THE THIRD SUMMER OF THE SIEGE, WHEN THE BOOK WAS ALREADY HALF FULL · ABOUT THE HEARTH, AND ABOUT THIS BOOK.

By Seren Two-Inks, who writes this Book, and who is the third member of the household of Halyna.

It is the third summer of the siege. The nights are short now. Everyone in the hall is asleep. The fire in the Long Hearth has burned down to coals: red in the middle, ash at the edges. Up on the wall, the banner rises and falls in a wind I can’t hear. This Book is half full. One of its tellers is already buried under the east cairns, and others named in it are buried next to him. Tonight I have turned back to the first page. I left it blank when I started. A mason doesn’t cut the top stone of a doorway before the posts are up.

This is the Book of the Riven Stone. It holds the tales that are told at the Long Hearth of Rivenkeep. The hearth is in the hall behind the inner gate, under the wall where the torn cloak flies. We tell the tales in the hours after an attack. The guns are cooling. The mortar hasn’t set yet. There’s nothing left to do with your hands except hold them out to the fire.

We didn’t invent this custom. The first builders of the Keep did, long before there were any havens on the coast. When they had set their stones and cleaned their tools, they sat at this hearth, and every night one of them told a tale. Later they went down to the sea to build the havens. The Keep was left empty. Their grandchildren forgot the custom, just as they forgot the Keep. We found it again at the bottom of a chest in the deepest vault. You’ll read about that. Now we sit where they sat. Our wall is going up again, and so is this Book.

Next to the hearth, in a hollow in the wall, sits the old capstone of the inner gate. A capstone is the top stone that holds a gateway together. This one fell during the forgotten years, when nobody lived on the ridge to hear it fall. It lay in the gateway, face down, with its vow pressed into the dirt. Grass grew over it. Snow came and went for more winters than anyone can count. Then, at dusk on the day of the banner, Halyna put their four hands on it and turned it over. It takes two people to turn it. Halvard’s ancestors cut that stone. Rhyna’s ancestors set it in place. On the underside, where the weather never reached it, the first builders’ vow is still sharp.

Whoever holds that stone tells the tale. When the tale is over, the teller puts the stone back in its hollow. Then we don’t say the tale was told. We say it was laid, the way a stone is laid in a wall. A tale that is only told is gone by morning. A tale that is laid becomes part of the wall.

Wherever I write the name of a bonded pair, I draw one thin line over it, like the stone that lies across the top of two doorposts. That’s because it is one name, but it belongs to two people. Halyna are Halvard Stone-Warden and Rhyna of the Inner Gate. They are a married couple, the Bonded of the Keep. The oldest pair, Idrenna, who taught them their craft, still sit on the bench by the door. They nod when a tale is told truly. Orvenna are our wall-masters. No one has ever heard either of them shout on the wall. The youngest pair are Enrella, Enno and Della. They were sealed in our first summer here. One of them can’t start a joke without the other one finishing it.

When one of our tales is laid, everyone at the hearth answers together with two words: We remember. The words come from the start of the Title, which the Captain wrote in coal from this very hearth. Of everything said at this fire, it’s the only thing that is always said exactly the same way. Old Ebba of Fenholm said it first, on the first night, when none of us knew what to say. Most nights she still starts it, a moment before the rest of us. Then we all come in behind her, the way the tide comes in behind its first wave.

Some of these tales aren’t ours. They belong to the wood. They come from wreck-wood that the tide washes in below our wall. They come from the dark stone in the chest I carried up the mountain. They come from the hearts of the grey ships we have broken. We call the people who grew that wood, out behind the grey, the Mystaeri. It means “the mist-folk.” That is our word for them, not theirs. You can’t read the wood. There are no words in it. You know it all at once, the way you know a memory: beginning, middle and end together. The hard part is telling it. Words can be twisted. A knowing can’t. So the wood’s tales are told in as few words as possible. When a tale from the wood is finished, the hearth stays silent. Nobody knows how to answer a tale like that. Every time that silence fell, I wrote it down.

I can’t know the wood myself. I’m not one of the Aetherbonded, whom the guild just calls the Bonded. I’m one of the people the guild calls unbonded. When I came up the mountain alone, Halyna took me into their household as its third member. When they know the wood, I hold the lamp. Once I put my own hand on the grain. I let go fast. What came into me was too big for me to hold. So my hand is the one that writes, not the one that knows. Every writer the guild ever had before me was half of a bonded pair. Halyna explain why this Book is written by someone who isn’t, in its own place, in a tale for the children.

Every night, after a tale has been laid and everyone has gone to bed, I stay up alone with the ink. I write the tale in our own language, in a steady beat, the way a mason lays a row of stones. So many stones to each row, and every joint set over the middle of a stone below it, so the row can carry the weight of a voice. The words are the teller’s. I don’t change a single one. The beat is mine. I say every word out loud three times before I write it, to hear if it will hold. Most nights the coals have turned to ash, the hall is breathing in its sleep around me, and the last word is still in my mouth.

I love our language.

I carried two things up the mountain: our language and a chest. Only the chest was heavy.

Halyna both wanted the wood’s tales in this Book next to our own. “A story told from one side of a wall,” one of them said, and the other finished, “is half a story.” So wherever the same thing was seen from both sides, I have put the two versions facing each other. Ours is on the left page. The wood’s is on the right page. Where the wood’s page couldn’t be told yet, I have left it blank, with only the wood grain showing. That way, whoever opens this Book can see in the grain the shape of something we didn’t understand yet, and wait for it.

When Halyna tell a tale from the wood, they take turns, one paragraph each. They know the tales the way they know each other, and neither of them waits for the other to speak. I have used two inks, one for each of them. They asked me not to mark which ink is whose. You can’t tell. When they say one line between them, one starting it and the other finishing, I have written it in both inks.

The Captain doesn’t tell tales. He sits at the end of the bench with his hood up. He listens to people tell about the things he did. He says nothing, except sometimes to say that it wasn’t like that. On the wall he is the Commander, because that’s the title the Rite gave him. At this hearth, because he wants it that way, he is still the Captain. When the names of the dead are said, he pushes his hood back.

Where a carving was broken, or a knowing couldn’t be told, I have said so. I haven’t filled the gaps with guesses. Where the tales disagree, I have kept both.

This Book is written in ink. Halyna plan to have it carved into the granite walls of this hall, row after row, when there’s enough peace to do it. Until then it is kept in the chest I carried, with the stones that can’t be read.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

THE INVOCATION

Spoken at the Long Hearth before the first tale of a night, and set here before the first tale of the Book.

IN MEMORY OF OUR HOME, OUR FAMILIES, OUR GOD, OUR FREEDOMS, OUR PEACE.

Sit, if there be room, and stand if there be none; for the guns are cooling on the wall, and the mortar will not set ere dark.

Here is the hearth, and here the stone that fell from the inner gate; and whoever holds it tells.

We tell these tales, for once we forgot: our fathers forgot a keep, and it crumbled on its ridge; and their fathers forgot a wrong, and it came back to us under grey sails. So we tell all of it, the building and the breaking, the wrong we suffered and the wrong we wrought.

Some of these tales are ours, and they are cut square, as the stones of the wall are cut; and some are the wood’s, and those are hard to tell, nor will they be hurried; and where the two do not agree, we have kept both.

Hold the stone. Lay the tale. When it is laid, the hearth will answer.

And the hearth said: We remember.

Said at the Long Hearth before the first tale of the night, and placed here before the first tale of the Book.

IN MEMORY OF OUR HOME, OUR FAMILIES, OUR GOD, OUR FREEDOMS, OUR PEACE.

Sit down if there’s room. Stand if there isn’t. The guns are cooling on the wall, and the mortar won’t set before dark.

This is the hearth. This is the stone that fell from the inner gate. Whoever holds it tells the tale.

We tell these tales because we forgot once. Our fathers forgot a keep, and it fell apart on its ridge. Their fathers forgot a wrong they had done, and it came back to us under grey sails. So we tell all of it. The building and the breaking. The wrongs done to us, and the wrongs we did.

Some of these tales are ours. They are cut square, like the stones of the wall. Some belong to the wood. Those are hard to tell, and they can’t be rushed. Where the two disagree, we have kept both.

Hold the stone. Lay the tale. When it is laid, the hearth will answer.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

BOOK ONE · THE BOOK OF THE WALL

At Rivenkeep, on the day of the banner and in the month after it, a broken people finds a ruin, lifts a torn cloak, raises its builders, anoints its Captain, and takes the Keep’s name.

I.1 · The Torn CloakStone leaf

LAID ON THE FIFTEENTH NIGHT AFTER THE BANNER, THE NIGHT AFTER THE RITE, THE FIRST NIGHT THIS HEARTH ASKED FOR A TALE · OF THE DAY OF THE BANNER, AT DUSK, AND THE LONG RETREAT BEHIND IT.

Laid by Kael the Counter, oath-keeper of the True Men, at the first asking of this hearth. He would not let me change a word of it, and I have not. When he had set the stone back in its niche, and the True Men had sung, no one in the hall knew what should be said, till old Ebba of Fenholm said it, out of the first words of the Title; and the hearth took it from her: We remember.

“Give me the stone. No, I will stand.”

Fourteen nights I have sat at this fire, and a soldier stands to give his count.

I swear on the lintel of the western stair, where the old wardens cut their names, that I will tell this as I counted it, for I have no other way of telling. Counting is my trade, and not singing; and what I did not count I will not tell you.

Hear how the cloak was torn on the day of the banner, and who answered it; for I stood on the gate that dusk, and I counted.

On the day of the banner, as the light began to go, the column came in under the outer gate, and I counted it as it came: old men, and women with bundles, and children asleep upon their feet, and the soldiers of six broken companies who no longer knew their officers; two thousand and some hundreds. From the ashes of Glasspire we had walked a month, through the passes, to come to a castle; and we came to a heap of stones. The outer gate hung from one hinge, and the ward behind it was grass to the knee; and where the songs had put a great hall, there was a roof fallen in, and snow upon the hearth.

Some shook. Some wept. Some fell and would not rise.

Then the crying began, and it was not one cry but many. Some cried that we should send to the grey sails and beg for terms, though no man living had ever understood one word the grey sails said; and some cried for the high passes, and whatever lay beyond the snow; and some cried that we should go down into the cellars of the Keep, and bar the doors, and wait, as if the sea would forget us. I had heard men break on the long retreat; but never till that dusk had I heard a whole people break. It is a sound like ice going out on a river.

Now I will tell you what the Captain was, and first what he was not. He was no general, and the generals were slain; he was no councillor, and the councillors were forgotten; he was no lord, and the lords were scattered; and he was no Stonewright, and the Stonewrights sat in the grass with their chisels in their laps, and despaired. But he was the captain of a small company, and we were his company.

Before ever the cloak was torn, in the garrisons and on the roads, the Captain had found us one by one, twelve of us in all, and he bound us to him by nothing but trust. We were soldiers who kept our oaths, who built what we promised, who fought only when the cause was just. That is the whole of a True Man. I say it so that you will know it is not much. Nor is it little.

The Captain did not want to lead. I swear to that. All through the long retreat, from the burning of the havens to the passes, he waited for some man whose place it was to lead to come forward and take the leading from him; and none came, for the leaders were dead.

Do not mistake me: it was not that the greater men were gone, and the Captain the best of what was left. There was no greater man. I do not think his equal ever lived.

It was dusk on the day of the banner, and the last of the light was going off the snow in the ward, and down off the riven ridge came the wind, with the whole of the winter in its mouth. The True Men looked to their Captain. The Captain looked at the wall.

Then he climbed it. As he came through the hall he had taken a coal from the dead hearth, from this hearth, where the snow lay then and where you sit now; and why he took it, no man of us knew. He went up the broken face of the inner wall alone, by the stones that stood proud of the mortar, and we stood at the foot and let him go, for he had not bidden us follow. I have counted those stones since: thirty-one there are, and four of them move.

At the top, where the wall yet stood whole, the Captain took off his cloak, a plain soldier’s cloak, crimson once and gone to the colour of old brick; and on that mountain, in that wind, it was all he had against the cold. None in the ward spoke; among the women a child coughed, and his mother hushed him, and after that there was nothing in the ward but the wind.

As a man at a poor table, in the dead of the year, holds the last loaf of his house in his two hands, and breaks it, and gives the one half to the hungry at his door, and lays the other by against the morning, which will come whether there be bread or none: so the Captain held his cloak. He tore it down the back.

The one half he spread on the capstones, and knelt upon it with the coal, and wrote, slowly, as a man writes who has not written much; and when it was written, he bound that half to a pike out of the rubble. The other half, the half with nothing written on it, he put back on his shoulders.

Up after him I went, the first of us, and the first to read it; and not once had his hand shaken. I swear to that. Then I turned to the ward below, to the two thousand and some hundreds of us, and read it out, and my voice was not steady, and I will not pretend it was:

IN MEMORY OF OUR HOME, OUR FAMILIES, OUR GOD, OUR FREEDOMS, OUR PEACE.

The Captain set the pike in a crack of the wall and gave it to the wind, and the wind took it. That was the last unbroken wall of the Shorelands, and that was the banner above it.

Now hear who answered, and in what order, for I counted. First answered the True Men, each of them calling his name up the wall to the Captain where he stood beside the pike; and I will name them as they called, for a count is not a count until it is named.

Stannard of the ridge, the quarryman, who will trust no stone till he has knocked on it with his knuckle. Corlen of the harbour, the eldest of us, who will not fire until the sea has finished. Tamm of the three spans, who carries in his pack a stone from each span.

Marl the fen-born, who carries his grandmother’s stone, and would carry his grandmother too, if she would let him. Aske of the canopy, who will not pass a felled tree but he lays his hand on the bark. Garvel of the smithies, who listens to a gun as a mother listens to a cough.

Harl of the burning shore, who would burn the sea, if the sea would take fire. Ulden of the galleries, who sleeps with his ear to the ground. Hesk of the mere, who lit his mother’s lamp on the north wall the first night, and no night since has it gone dark.

Lanner of the crags, who smells a wind ere it comes. Pellow of the spire, the youngest of us, who carried his city’s glass up the mountain in straw, and ground it into our far-glasses. And Kael the Counter, oath-keeper of the True Men; that is me, of no haven, for the Captain found me on a road.

Twelve. I counted us twice, and it did not take long.

After them answered the soldiers of the six companies, who had walked the long retreat beside us; and they are our troops now, a troop to each True Man, and they trust us as we trust the Captain. Then the people of the column, who had carried their children on their backs through the passes: one voice, and a dozen, and then hundreds, till I could not count them, and I stopped trying. It is the only time I ever stopped.

By no command did any of us answer, for the Captain gave none; he had only written down what we had left, and it was enough.

The cloak the Captain wrote on was crimson once, ere the road wore it to the colour of brick; and for that the pennant on this keep is crimson, and on every keep we raise.

Look to the end of the bench, you children at the front: the Captain of the Torn Cloak is sitting there now, with his hood up and his hands upon his knees, where the fire has burned down to its coals.

On his back is the other half.

He will tell you it was not like that. It was like that.

This I lay as it was laid for me.

Then the True Men sang, as they sing it still:

The gate was broken, the lords were scattered, the grey sails gathered along the shore; one man climbed where the stones lay loosened and tore the cloak his shoulders wore. He wrote five things a man might die for, and none of us has needed more.

And the hearth said: We remember.

On the leaf that faces it there is nothing but the grain of the wood, and one line beneath:

LAID ON THE FIFTEENTH NIGHT AFTER THE BANNER, THE NIGHT AFTER THE RITE, THE FIRST NIGHT THIS HEARTH ASKED FOR A TALE · ABOUT THE DAY OF THE BANNER, AT DUSK, AND THE LONG RETREAT BEFORE IT.

Told by Kael the Counter, the oath-keeper of the True Men, the first time this hearth asked for a tale. He wouldn’t let me change a word of it, and I haven’t. When he had put the stone back, and the True Men had sung, nobody in the hall knew what to say. Then old Ebba of Fenholm said it, using the first words of the Title, and everyone at the hearth said it after her: We remember.

“Give me the stone. No. I’ll stand.”

I’ve sat at this fire for fourteen nights. A soldier stands up to give his count.

I swear on the top stone of the western stair, where the old wardens cut their names, that I’ll tell this the way I counted it. I don’t know any other way to tell things. Counting is my job. Singing isn’t. If I didn’t count it, I won’t tell you about it.

This is how the cloak was torn on the day of the banner, and who answered it. I stood on the gate that evening, and I counted.

On the day of the banner, as the light started to fade, the column of people came in under the outer gate. I counted them as they came. Old men. Women carrying bundles. Children so tired they were asleep on their feet. Soldiers from six broken companies who didn’t know their own officers anymore. Two thousand and a few hundred. We had walked for a month from the ashes of Glasspire, through the mountain passes, to reach a castle. What we reached was a pile of stones. The outer gate hung from one hinge. The yard behind it was knee-deep in grass. The old songs said there was a great hall here. There was a fallen roof, and snow on the hearth.

Some people shook. Some cried. Some fell down and wouldn’t get up.

Then the shouting started. It wasn’t one voice. It was many. Some shouted that we should send word to the grey sails and beg for peace, even though no one alive had ever understood a word the grey sails said. Some shouted that we should keep going over the high passes, to whatever was past the snow. Some shouted that we should go down into the Keep’s cellars, lock the doors and wait, as if the sea might forget about us. I had heard men break on the long retreat. But I had never heard a whole people break until that evening. It sounds like river ice cracking in spring.

Now I’ll tell you what the Captain was. First, what he wasn’t. He wasn’t a general. The generals were dead. He wasn’t a councillor. The councillors were forgotten. He wasn’t a lord. The lords were scattered. And he wasn’t a Stonewright. The Stonewrights were sitting in the grass with their chisels in their laps, without hope. He was the captain of a small company. We were his company.

Long before the cloak was torn, in army forts and on the roads, the Captain had found us one at a time. There were twelve of us. He held us to him with nothing but trust. We were soldiers who kept our promises, who built what we said we’d build, and who fought only for a just cause. That’s all a True Man is. I tell you so you’ll know it isn’t much. It isn’t nothing, either.

The Captain didn’t want to lead. I swear to that. All through the long retreat, from the burning of the havens to the mountain passes, he kept waiting for someone whose job it was to lead to step up and take over. Nobody did. The leaders were dead.

Don’t get me wrong. It wasn’t that the great men were gone and the Captain was the best of what was left. There was no greater man. I don’t think anyone like him ever lived.

It was dusk on the day of the banner. The last light was leaving the snow in the yard. The wind came down off the split ridge, cold with the whole winter in it. The True Men looked at their Captain. The Captain looked at the wall.

Then he climbed it. On his way through the hall, he had picked up a coal from the dead hearth. This hearth. Snow was lying on it then, where you are sitting now. None of us knew why he took it. He climbed the broken inner wall alone, using the stones that stuck out from the mortar. We stood at the bottom and let him go, because he hadn’t told us to follow. I’ve counted those stones since. There are thirty-one, and four of them wobble.

At the top, where the wall was still whole, the Captain took off his cloak. It was a plain soldier’s cloak. It had been red once, but it had faded to the colour of old brick. On that mountain, in that wind, it was all he had to keep out the cold. Nobody in the yard said a word. A child coughed somewhere among the women, and his mother hushed him. After that there was only the wind.

Picture a poor man at his table in the middle of winter. He holds his last loaf of bread in both hands. He breaks it, gives half to the hungry stranger at his door, and saves the other half for morning, because morning will come whether there’s bread or not. That’s how the Captain held his cloak. Then he tore it down the back.

He spread one half on the top stones of the wall. He knelt on it with the coal and wrote, slowly, like a man who hasn’t written much. When he was done, he tied that half to a pike he’d pulled out of the rubble. The other half, the one with nothing written on it, he put back on his shoulders.

I climbed up after him. I was the first of us up, and the first to read it. His hand hadn’t shaken once. I swear to that. Then I turned to the yard below, to the two thousand and a few hundred of us, and I read it out loud. My voice wasn’t steady, and I won’t pretend it was:

IN MEMORY OF OUR HOME, OUR FAMILIES, OUR GOD, OUR FREEDOMS, OUR PEACE.

The Captain jammed the pike into a crack in the wall and let the wind have it, and the wind took it. That was the last unbroken wall in the Shorelands. And that was the banner flying over it.

Now here is who answered, and in what order, because I counted. The True Men answered first. Each one shouted his name up the wall to the Captain, who was standing next to the pike. I’ll name them in the order they called, because a count isn’t a count until it has names.

Stannard of the ridge, the quarryman. He won’t trust a stone until he’s knocked on it with his knuckle. Corlen of the harbour, the oldest of us. He won’t fire until the sea has finished moving. Tamm of the three spans. He carries a stone from each of his city’s three bridges in his pack.

Marl the fen-born. He carries his grandmother’s stone, and he’d carry his grandmother too, if she’d let him. Aske of the canopy. He can’t walk past a cut-down tree without putting his hand on the bark. Garvel of the smithies. He listens to a gun the way a mother listens to her child’s cough.

Harl of the burning shore. He would set the sea on fire if it would burn. Ulden of the galleries. He sleeps with his ear to the ground. Hesk of the mere. He lit his mother’s lamp on the north wall that first night, and it hasn’t gone dark a single night since.

Lanner of the crags. He can smell a wind before it comes. Pellow of the spire, the youngest of us. He carried his city’s glass up the mountain packed in straw, and ground it into our spyglasses. And Kael the Counter, oath-keeper of the True Men. That’s me. I’m from no haven. The Captain found me on a road.

Twelve. I counted us twice. It didn’t take long.

Next to answer were the soldiers of the six companies, who had walked the long retreat beside us. They’re our troops now, one troop for each True Man, and they trust us the way we trust the Captain. Then came the people of the column, who had carried their children on their backs through the passes. One voice. Then a dozen. Then hundreds. I couldn’t count them anymore, so I stopped trying. It’s the only time I ever stopped.

Nobody told any of us to answer. The Captain gave no order. All he did was write down what we had left. It was enough.

The cloak the Captain wrote on was red once, before the road wore it down to the colour of brick. That’s why the pennant on this keep is red, and on every keep we build.

Look at the end of the bench, you children at the front. The Captain of the Torn Cloak is sitting there right now, with his hood up and his hands on his knees, where the fire has burned down to coals.

On his back is the other half.

He’ll tell you it wasn’t like that. It was like that.

I have laid this tale the way it was laid for me.

Then the True Men sang, as they still sing it today:

The gate was broken. The lords had run. Grey sails were gathering all along the shore. One man climbed up the loose stones and tore the cloak he was wearing. He wrote down five things worth dying for, and none of us has ever needed more.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

The page facing it shows nothing but wood grain, and one line underneath:

Being set down anew in its own hands.

I.2 · The Cry of the BondedStone leaf

LAID ON THE NIGHT AFTER THE FIRST ASSAULT THE RIVENMEN HELD, ABOUT THE TWENTY-FIFTH NIGHT · OF THE DAY OF THE BANNER: THE COURTYARD, THE TERMS, AND THE FIRST LAYING.

Laid by Seren Two-Inks, apprentice to Halyna, he and she, and the third of their hearth; and it was the second tale that this hearth ever heard, and the first that ever I laid. I was a child of the guild on the day I tell of, twelve winters old, and sat in the courtyard with the rest.

Tonight, when I was afraid to speak, Halyna took my two hands, he the one and she the other, and laid them on the stone in its niche; and one of them said, and the other finished:

“Hold the stone.”

“Hold it, and it will hold you.”

So I hold it.

Hear now how the guild of the Stonewrights, on the day of the banner, rose up out of the grass, and what it promised ere that night was done; and hold it, for it is ours.

It was dusk on the day of the banner, and the last of the light lay thin as ash upon the snow, and the guild sat in the old ward where it had gone to grass, some hundreds of us, masters and prentices, with our chisels in our laps and the bonded pairs a little apart; and I sat among them, for I was of the guild, though I had no craft yet but the carrying of water. All the way over the high passes I had walked beside a boy of the guild, and at night slept at his side. I came into that courtyard alone.

A little after me, under the outer gate that hung from its one hinge, two children of the guild came in with their hands joined, a boy and a girl of fifteen, Enno and Della; and of all the pairs among the children, none came up the mountain whole save those two. In the grass they sat down together, and did not let go.

In the twenty months before that dusk, ten havens had burned behind us, the one after the other, and we watched every one of them die, and from each we walked away with our chisels in our packs, and not one of us lifted a hand, for our creed forbade it: Build, do not destroy. Protect, do not attack. No Stonewright ever bore a weapon; mortar and chisel were our only arms, and they had not been enough.

The faith of twelve generations was on us still, but it sat on us like a wet cloak; and I think that every elder in that courtyard was turning over the same hard thing, and would not say it aloud: that walls raised for peace must sometimes be held with blood, and if that were so, whose blood, and whose hands?

Then, in the last of the light, the Captain of the Torn Cloak came down from the wall. I had seen the cloak go up, and heard the soldiers shout, and knew not what it was to us. Into the middle of the courtyard he walked, a man not tall, with the coal-black yet on his fingers and the half of a cloak upon his shoulders, and looked at the elders, and past them at the broken gate, and stood; and he asked one question, and it is the only speech of his that anyone at this hearth can repeat:

“Your walls are falling. Will you build, or will you watch and die?”

No one answered him. I remember the wind in the grass.

Then Halyna stood up, he and she together. I did not know the two of them then, save as two elders of the guild who sat apart and spoke to no one, for they had no need, and who had knelt in the gateway at dusk, as the column came in, to turn over a fallen stone: Rhyna of the Inner Gate, of the line that laid it, and Halvard Stone-Warden, of the line that cut it out of the mountain; and they were husband and wife, and had been one since the cradle.

Rhyna and Halvard looked up at the torn cloak on its pike, where the wind was working at it, and bowed to it in the same breath, neither waiting upon the other; and each of them lifted a chisel, his hand and hers, in the one motion. Their two chisels took the last of the light.

The cry came out of them both, and it was one cry in two throats; and hers came first for no reason but that a cry must begin somewhere. She began it:

“The mortar cries out from the ground. Rise, Stonewrights! Lay stone upon stone. Stand every wall to the very end.”

And he ended it, with his chisel beside hers:

“Rise! Rise! Rise!”

There was no silence.

As when the masons of a haven, at the raising of a new arch, have set its last stone in the crown, and strike the wooden frame out from under it, and every stone that leaned upon the wood must lean now upon its neighbour, and the whole arch takes up its own weight and stands: so the guild stood, and I with it, though I do not remember standing. We took up the cry, and the soldiers on the wall took it from us, and the column from the soldiers, till the walls of the Keep seemed to tremble. Old stone does not tremble. Under my feet I felt it tremble.

That night, in the ruin of this hall, with the snow swept back from the hearth where you sit now, and a fire lit upon it for the first time in more years than any of us knew, Idrenna, the eldest pair, Idlan and Denna, made the terms with the Captain; and I was there, for I carried in the water. Old they were, older than any pair of the guild, and they spoke slowly, as the old speak; and no one hurried them.

The Stonewrights would not take up arms, nor did the Captain ask it; but they would build between the volleys, and mend a breach while the guns still spoke, with the speed and precision that only Aetherbonded pairs could achieve. Idrenna gave it to him in two halves; one of them said, and the other finished:

“Not the sword, but the stone.”

“Not the killing, but the keeping.”

The Captain bowed his head; and it has been the guild’s word ever since.

Ere that fire burned down, Orvenna, the wall-masters, Ormund and Penna, shared out the siege day between the stone and the guns, and neither of them raised a voice, for they never do; and they split the day in three, as a quarryman splits a block along its own seam, and named each third: the laying, and the hauling, and the holding.

“Every gun you haul is a course we do not lay,” Ormund said to the Captain, and Penna finished, “so spend a gun as a careful man spends silver.”

Then the masons sang, as they sing it still:

Lay the stone while the sea lies bare, haul the gun while the guns are cold, hold the wall when the grey sails come, and the wall will hold.

And wherever you see a Stonewright on the wall in the holding, look again. You will see two.

Late that night, by torches that hissed in the falling frost, in the gap of the inner gate, we laid the first course of the war, with every joint broken over the joint below; and for such work the guild has an old song, which the masons of the havens sang over every gate that ever they raised, and Orvenna asked for the clearest voices to lead it. Enno and Della stood up, the two who had come in hand in hand, and took the first voice between them, the one to the comma and the other the rest.

Then Enno and Della sang, as they sing it still:

Stone upon stone, and the stone keeps faith; hand over hand, and the hand holds true; two at the gate when the grey sails gather, two at the gate, and the gate stands through.

When the torches had burned down to their stubs, Enno and Della lay asleep against the new course, the two of them, with their hands yet joined, as they had come up the mountain; and I lay down a little way off, by the water-pails, and slept. By morning the mortar had set.

This I lay as it was laid for me.

And the hearth said: We remember.

LAID THE NIGHT AFTER THE FIRST ATTACK THE RIVENMEN HELD OFF, AROUND THE TWENTY-FIFTH NIGHT · ABOUT THE DAY OF THE BANNER: THE COURTYARD, THE AGREEMENT, AND THE FIRST ROW OF STONE.

Told by Seren Two-Inks, apprentice to Halyna and the third member of their household. It was the second tale this hearth ever heard, and the first I ever laid. On the day I’m describing, I was a child of the guild, twelve years old, sitting in the courtyard with everyone else.

Tonight, when I was too scared to speak, Halyna took my two hands, he one and she the other, and put them on the stone in its hollow. One of them said, and the other finished:

“Hold the stone.”

“Hold it, and it will hold you.”

So I’m holding it.

This is how the guild of the Stonewrights got up out of the grass on the day of the banner, and what it promised before that night was over. Hold on to it. It belongs to us.

It was dusk on the day of the banner. The last light lay thin as ash on the snow. The guild sat in the old yard, which had gone to grass. There were a few hundred of us, masters and apprentices, with our chisels in our laps. The bonded pairs sat a little apart. I sat with them, because I belonged to the guild, even though the only job I had was carrying water. All the way over the high passes I had walked next to a boy of the guild, and slept next to him at night. I came into that courtyard alone.

A little while after me, two children of the guild came in under the outer gate, holding hands. They were a boy and a girl, both fifteen: Enno and Della. Of all the pairs among the children, they were the only ones who came up the mountain together and alive. They sat down in the grass side by side, and didn’t let go.

In the twenty months before that evening, ten havens had burned behind us, one after another. We watched every one of them die. We walked away from each one with our chisels in our packs. Not one of us raised a hand to fight, because our beliefs forbade it: Build, don’t destroy. Protect, don’t attack. No Stonewright had ever carried a weapon. Mortar and chisels were our only tools, and they hadn’t been enough.

We still held the faith of twelve generations. But it hung on us like a wet coat. I think every elder in that courtyard was thinking the same hard thought and wouldn’t say it out loud. Walls built for peace sometimes have to be held with blood. And if that’s true, whose blood? And whose hands?

Then, in the last of the light, the Captain of the Torn Cloak came down from the wall. I had seen the cloak go up and heard the soldiers cheer, but I didn’t know what it meant for us. He walked into the middle of the courtyard. He wasn’t a tall man. There was still coal-black on his fingers, and half a cloak on his shoulders. He looked at the elders. He looked past them at the broken gate. He stood there. Then he asked one question. It’s the only speech of his anyone at this hearth can repeat:

“Your walls are falling. Will you build, or will you watch and die?”

Nobody answered. I remember the wind in the grass.

Then Halyna stood up, he and she together. I didn’t know them then. They were just two elders of the guild who sat by themselves and didn’t talk to anyone, because they didn’t need to. At dusk, while the column was coming in, they had knelt in the gateway to turn over a fallen stone. She was Rhyna of the Inner Gate, from the family that set that stone. He was Halvard Stone-Warden, from the family that cut it out of the mountain. They were husband and wife. They had been one since they were babies.

Rhyna and Halvard looked up at the torn cloak on its pike, with the wind tugging at it. They bowed to it at the same moment, neither one waiting for the other. Each of them raised a chisel, his hand and hers, in one movement. Their two chisels caught the last light.

The cry came out of both of them. It was one cry from two throats. Hers came first, only because a cry has to start somewhere. She began it:

“The mortar cries out from the ground. Rise, Stonewrights! Lay stone upon stone. Stand every wall to the very end.”

And he finished it, with his chisel next to hers:

“Rise! Rise! Rise!”

There was no silence after it.

Think of masons building a new arch. They set the last stone at the very top. Then they knock out the wooden frame that was holding it up. Every stone that was leaning on the wood now has to lean on the stone beside it. And the whole arch takes its own weight, and stands. That’s how the guild stood up. I stood with them, though I don’t remember doing it. We took up the cry. The soldiers on the wall took it from us. The column took it from the soldiers. It got so loud the walls of the Keep seemed to shake. Old stone doesn’t shake. I felt it shake under my feet.

That night, in the ruined hall, we swept the snow off the hearth where you’re sitting now and lit a fire on it, the first one in more years than anyone knew. There, Idrenna, the oldest pair, Idlan and Denna, made an agreement with the Captain. I was there, because I carried in the water. They were old, older than any other pair in the guild. They spoke slowly, the way old people do, and nobody rushed them.

The Stonewrights would not pick up weapons, and the Captain didn’t ask them to. But they would build between the volleys, and fix a broken wall while the guns were still firing, with the speed and precision that only Aetherbonded pairs could achieve. Idrenna gave him the deal in two halves. One of them said, and the other finished:

“Not the sword, but the stone.”

“Not the killing, but the keeping.”

The Captain bowed his head. Those have been the guild’s words ever since.

Before that fire burned out, Orvenna, the wall-masters, Ormund and Penna, split up the siege day between the stonework and the guns. Neither of them raised their voice. They never do. They split the day into three parts, the way a quarryman splits a block along its natural crack, and gave each part a name: the laying, the hauling, and the holding.

“Every gun you haul up is a row of stone we don’t lay,” Ormund told the Captain. Penna finished, “So spend each gun the way a careful man spends money.”

Then the masons sang, as they still sing it today:

Lay the stone while the sea is low. Haul the guns while the guns are cold. Hold the wall when the grey sails come, and the wall will hold.

Whenever you see a Stonewright on the wall during the holding, look again. You’ll see two.

Late that night, by torches that hissed in the falling frost, in the gap of the inner gate, we laid the first row of stone of the war. Every joint sat over the middle of a stone below it. The guild has an old song for that kind of work. The masons of the havens sang it over every gate they ever built. Orvenna asked for the clearest voices to lead it. Enno and Della stood up, the two who had come in holding hands. They sang the lead between them, one up to the comma and the other the rest.

Then Enno and Della sang, as they still sing it today:

Stone on stone, and the stone stays true. Hand over hand, and the hand holds on. Two at the gate when the grey sails gather. Two at the gate, and the gate stands strong.

When the torches had burned down to stubs, Enno and Della fell asleep leaning against the new stones, the two of them, still holding hands, the way they’d come up the mountain. I lay down a little way off, by the water buckets, and slept. By morning the mortar had set.

I have laid this tale the way it was laid for me.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

I.3 · The Captain’s Long BreathStone leaf

LAID IN THE SECOND MONTH OF THE FIRST WINTER, IN HALE’S FIRST WEEKS ON THE WALL · OF THE FIRST ASSAULT, ABOUT THE TWENTY-FOURTH DAY AFTER THE BANNER.

Laid by Hale the runner, who carries along the wall in the holding what is seen, and nothing else; and he was seventeen, and in his first weeks on the wall. It is the tale of a saying of this hearth, and of the morning on which it began; and Kael the Counter mends it at the end, as he mends most things.

“Give me the stone, then. I am not a teller. I run.”

There is a saying at this hearth that you will hear on the wall ere you have stood a month upon it, and I think it began with me, though Voss says it is older; and hear now of the morning whereon it was born, for I was at the Captain’s shoulder.

A day has four-and-twenty hours in it, be it siege or peace; and in the laying they go slowly, for stone is heavy, and in the hauling slower yet, for guns are heavier; but in the holding, when the grey sails come in, an hour goes by in a heartbeat, and in four-and-twenty heartbeats a whole day.

In the first week that we were here, we runners set up our stone in the ward, a fallen block by the inner gate, as every haven of the coast once had its runners’ stone; and one after another we laid our hands upon it, and swore the old oath of the runners of the havens:

“What I saw, I carry. I carry nothing else.”

Wick swore it last, for he is the youngest of us and the quickest, and he can run the wall-walk end to end, in the time it takes to say the Title twice. That is Wick, on the floor at the front; he came to this hearth at a run, as he comes everywhere, and there is nothing in the world he wants so much as to carry a word to the Captain.

On the day of the first assault, at the grey of the morning, ere a sail had come, I stood by the guns of Kael the Counter, oath-keeper of the True Men, and watched the Captain of the Torn Cloak give his mind to Kael, as he gives it to each of his True Men: where to watch, and what shot to lay, and when to hold. When the Captain had gone on along the wall, Kael set his hand upon my shoulder and gave me the law of it, once: that no word along the wall may change that mind, save the Captain’s own voice at the battery’s side; and that a man can stand in only one place. So what I see, I carry to wherever the Captain stands, and to no man on the way. Where the wall is broken, I carry that to the masons, and a plank with marks upon it, to Halyna. We runners are his eyes, where the smoke lies between him and the water.

Then the True Men drilled their troops upon the wall-walk in the three calls, the True Man giving out each line, and his troop answering it.

Then the troops sang, as they sing it still:

Shift your ground, and watch new water; change your shot for the sea, the shore, the sky; hold, or loose, when the Captain is by.

When the drill was done, Jory of Eldhythe, a soldier of the east wall, leaned upon the parapet and began to whistle a tune that the old ship-masters hate; and Merrick, eldest of the ship-masters, who was coiling rope at the stair-foot below, lifted his head and told him to stop. Jory laughed down at the old man:

“It’s only a rhyme.”

Merrick did not laugh.

Ere the morning was half gone, the sails had come out of the grey faster than I could count them, and the guns spoke, and the smoke came down over the water and the wall; and that was my first holding, and when it was done I could not have told you whether an hour had gone by, or a morning.

In those first weeks there was no battery on the wall save one, for the guns we had dragged up the mountain were few yet, and all of them stood at Kael’s place on the wall. Late in that holding, when the rest were spent, Kael’s lookout put his eye to the far-glass, one that Pellow of the spire ground for us, out of the glass of his city; and in it the lookout saw a hull that had hung back in the grey, where none had hung before, and was coming in now, late and alone.

At the far end of the wall, above the gate, stood the Captain; and between him and that hull lay the smoke. Kael gave me three words to carry:

“Hull hangs back.”

I ran it.

Under the smoke I went, with my head down, past the masons at the breaches and the soldiers on the ropes, and spoke to no man of it, nor stopped, till I came up into the clear at the far end, above the gate.

The Captain was standing still.

That is what I remember first. Everything on that wall was moving but him. One hand he had on the parapet, and his face was to the sea, though the smoke lay on the water; and he did not look round at me, yet he heard me to the last word.

Then the Captain walked, at his own pace, along the wall-walk toward Kael’s guns, and I went at his shoulder, for no man had sent me back; and as he walked, the hour began to stretch.

As a cart of quarry-stone, coming off the flat road to the foot of a long hill, does not stop, but slows, and the ox leans into the yoke, and the wheels go round slower and slower, till the boy who walks at the tailboard can count every spoke as it comes up out of the mud: so the hour slowed around the Captain. I have no better way to say it.

All that way along the wall I walked at the Captain’s shoulder, and I saw the water sheet off the late hull’s bow, and I counted the ripples going down its grey sail, as you count the rings on a cut log, one ring and then the next, and each, I think, a minute, while the gulls hung, and the smoke stood, and on he walked.

I had time to be afraid. Then I had time to be ashamed of it. Then I had time to stop.

At the battery the Captain stopped, at Kael’s shoulder, and Kael was counting under his breath. Then the Captain gave the call, with his own voice:

“Hold.”

The battery held, and the late hull came on through the smoke, and on, till I could see the wet upon its timbers; and then the Captain gave the other call:

“Loose.”

The hour let go of us slowly, as a held breath goes out, till the world was at its own pace again, and the guns went; and out in the smoke the hull broke up in the one stretch of that water where it could hurt no one, where the Captain had meant it to break.

I do not know what the Captain thought. I know what he did.

That evening, when the guns were cooling, I walked the tide-line under the wall, as we runners do after every holding, and saw marks cut upon a plank of the wreck-wood in the shallows; and I carried them up past the fires to Halyna, the two of them, in three words:

“Marks on the wood.”

I ran it.

That night I came back up onto the wall in the dark, and found my way by the lamp that Hesk of the mere keeps burning, his mother’s lamp, upon the north wall; and so I knew the north was awake.

So now the saying goes along the wall from man to man: “For the Captain, an hour lasts as long as a day.” They say it as though it were a gift the Stonewrights laid on him at the Rite, with the oils. I said it so myself, my first week upon the wall, and thought I knew what it meant: that the Captain was quicker than other men, and the world held still for him; and I said as much at this fire. I know better now, for Kael the Counter, oath-keeper of the True Men, mended it for me across this fire.

I carry it to you as he said it, as I would carry a word to the Captain, and nothing else:

“It is not the world that slows, lad. It is the Captain who will not hurry. The moment he chose was the moment that came.”

This I lay as it was laid for me.

And the hearth said: We remember.

LAID IN THE SECOND MONTH OF THE FIRST WINTER, IN HALE’S FIRST WEEKS ON THE WALL · ABOUT THE FIRST ATTACK, AROUND THE TWENTY-FOURTH DAY AFTER THE BANNER.

Told by Hale the runner. During the holding, he carries what he sees along the wall, and nothing else. He was seventeen, in his first weeks on the wall. This is the tale of a saying at this hearth, and of the morning it started. Kael the Counter fixes it at the end, the way he fixes most things.

“Give me the stone, then. I’m not a storyteller. I run.”

There’s a saying at this hearth you’ll hear on the wall before you’ve been up there a month. I think it started with me, though Voss says it’s older. This is the morning it was born. I was standing right at the Captain’s shoulder.

A day has twenty-four hours, siege or no siege. During the laying, the hours go slowly, because stone is heavy. During the hauling, they go even slower, because guns are heavier. But during the holding, when the grey sails come in, an hour goes by in a heartbeat. Twenty-four heartbeats, and a whole day is gone.

In our first week here, we runners set up our own stone in the yard. It was a fallen block by the inner gate, like the runners’ stone every haven on the coast used to have. One after another we put our hands on it and swore the old oath of the havens’ runners:

“What I saw, I carry. I carry nothing else.”

Wick swore it last. He’s the youngest of us and the fastest. He can run the whole length of the wall in the time it takes to say the Title twice. That’s Wick, sitting on the floor at the front. He came to this hearth running, the way he goes everywhere. There’s nothing in the world he wants more than to carry a message to the Captain.

On the day of the first attack, at the grey of dawn, before any sails had come, I stood by the guns of Kael the Counter, oath-keeper of the True Men. I watched the Captain of the Torn Cloak tell Kael what he had in mind, the way he does with each of his True Men: where to watch, what shot to load, and when to hold fire. When the Captain had moved on down the wall, Kael put his hand on my shoulder and explained the rule to me, once. No message along the wall can change the Captain’s plan. Only the Captain’s own voice can, standing right next to the guns. And a man can only stand in one place. So whatever I see, I carry to wherever the Captain is standing, and I tell nobody on the way. If the wall is broken, I carry that to the masons. If I find a plank with marks on it, I carry it to Halyna. We runners are the Captain’s eyes when the smoke is between him and the water.

Then the True Men drilled their troops on the walkway in the three calls. The True Man shouted each line, and his troop shouted it back.

Then the troops sang, as they still sing it today:

Move your spot, and watch new water. Change your shot for the sea, the shore, the sky. Hold, or fire, when the Captain is by.

When the drill was over, Jory of Eldhythe, a soldier from the east wall, leaned on the wall and started whistling a tune the old ship-masters hate. Merrick, the oldest of the ship-masters, was coiling rope at the bottom of the stairs. He looked up and told Jory to stop. Jory laughed down at the old man:

“It’s just a rhyme.”

Merrick didn’t laugh.

Before the morning was half over, the sails came out of the grey faster than I could count them. The guns fired. The smoke came down over the water and the wall. That was my first holding. When it was over, I couldn’t have told you if an hour had gone by, or a whole morning.

In those first weeks there was only one battery on the wall. We hadn’t dragged many guns up the mountain yet, and all of them were at Kael’s spot. Late in that holding, when the other ships were finished, Kael’s lookout looked through his spyglass. Pellow of the spire had ground it for us out of his city’s glass. Through it, the lookout saw a ship that had stayed back in the grey, where none had stayed back before. Now it was coming in, late and alone.

The Captain was standing at the far end of the wall, above the gate. The smoke lay between him and that ship. Kael gave me three words to carry:

“Hull hangs back.”

I ran it.

I went under the smoke with my head down. I ran past the masons at the broken spots and the soldiers on the ropes. I didn’t tell anyone, and I didn’t stop, until I came out into clear air at the far end, above the gate.

The Captain was standing still.

That’s the first thing I remember. Everything on that wall was moving except him. He had one hand on the wall, and he was facing the sea, even though the smoke covered the water. He didn’t turn to look at me. But he heard every word.

Then the Captain walked, at his own pace, along the walkway toward Kael’s guns. I stayed at his shoulder, because nobody had sent me back. As he walked, the hour started to stretch.

Think of a cart full of quarry stone coming off a flat road to the bottom of a long hill. It doesn’t stop. It slows down. The ox leans into the yoke. The wheels turn slower and slower, until the boy walking behind the cart can count every spoke as it comes up out of the mud. That’s how the hour slowed down around the Captain. I don’t know a better way to say it.

All the way along the wall I walked next to him. I watched the water pour off the front of the late ship. I counted the ripples running down its grey sail, the way you count the rings on a cut log, one ring and then the next. Each one felt like a minute. The gulls hung in the air. The smoke stood still. And he kept walking.

I had time to be scared. Then I had time to be ashamed of being scared. Then I had time to stop being scared.

At the battery, the Captain stopped next to Kael. Kael was counting under his breath. Then the Captain gave the call, in his own voice:

“Hold.”

The guns held. The late ship kept coming through the smoke, closer and closer, until I could see the water shining on its timbers. Then the Captain gave the other call:

“Loose.”

The hour let go of us slowly, like a held breath going out, until the world was back to its normal speed. The guns fired. Out in the smoke the ship broke apart, right in the one stretch of water where it couldn’t hurt anyone. That was exactly where the Captain had wanted it to break.

I don’t know what the Captain was thinking. I know what he did.

That evening, while the guns were cooling, I walked along the waterline below the wall, the way we runners do after every holding. I saw marks cut into a plank of wreck-wood in the shallows. I carried it up past the fires to Halyna, the two of them, with three words:

“Marks on the wood.”

I ran it.

That night I climbed back up onto the wall in the dark. I found my way by the lamp Hesk of the mere keeps burning on the north wall, his mother’s lamp. That’s how I knew the north was awake.

So now this saying gets passed along the wall from man to man: “For the Captain, an hour lasts as long as a day.” They say it as if it were a gift the Stonewrights gave him at the Rite, along with the oils. I said it that way myself in my first week on the wall. I thought I knew what it meant: that the Captain was faster than other men, and the world held still for him. I said that out loud at this fire. I know better now. Kael the Counter, oath-keeper of the True Men, set me straight from across this fire.

I’ll give it to you the way he said it, the way I’d carry a message to the Captain, and nothing else:

“It’s not the world that slows down, lad. It’s the Captain who won’t hurry. The moment he picked was the moment that came.”

I have laid this tale the way it was laid for me.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

I.4 · The Rite of the CornerstoneStone leaf

LAID IN THE FIRST SPRING OF THE SIEGE · OF THE FOURTEEN NIGHTS AFTER THE BANNER.

Laid by Seren Two-Inks, who sets down this Book, and who is the third at the hearth of Halyna, he and she. I was a child of the guild in the Fourteen Nights, and carried water in the ward. The slates of the chest are cut in the Hal, the hand of the first builders, which no one living can sound; Halvard and Rhyna read it for its meaning, as Idrenna, the eldest pair, had taught them, and the words of the slates in this tale are that meaning, set in our own tongue.

“Hold the stone, Seren. I tell myself so every time.”

Hear now how the Rite of the Cornerstone was found again, in the deep of the ridge, and what had to be made ere it could be done, and what was done with it in the sight of all the Keep.

It was the third night after the cry, and the fires were down to their coals, and the guild lay asleep in the ruined hall under broken beams, with the stars between them; and in the dark Rhyna of the Inner Gate rose from her blanket, and in that same breath Halvard Stone-Warden rose from his, and took up the lamp. I lay nigh the head of the stair, and I woke when the light moved; and I did not call out, but lay and watched it go down from me into the dark, dwindling at every turning, as a lamp dwindles that is let down into a well.

Beneath the hall there are vaults, and beneath those vaults are others, cut down into the living ridge ere ever a haven was raised upon the coast; and the deepest of them no Stonewright had entered in the memory of the guild. Down through them went Rhyna and Halvard, she before, and he behind her with the lamp, stair after stair and turning after turning, and down into the cold of the riven stone itself, where the walls sweat in the lamplight and the air has not stirred since the founding; till at last the stair went no more, and they stood upon the lip of a shaft, where the first builders had let their stone down on ropes.

When Rhyna and Halvard lay in their one cradle, sixty years and more ere the banner, her mother sang over them a rhyme of her line, which runs back to Aldwena, the first builders, the two who laid the capstone of the inner gate; and every mother of that line sang it over every cradle after, and in all those generations no one asked what it meant.

Long after, one of them sang it for me, and the other finished it:

Under the hall and under the floor, under the stair that goes no more, down where the rope let the first stone go, the first two keep what the first two know.

Now, upon the lip of that shaft, under the stair that went no more, Rhyna knew where to look, and Halvard went down; she paid out the rope, and neither said a word, and the light sank from her into the deep, and she stood on in the dark with the rope running through her hands. What she knew and what he found were one thought, as their thoughts are.

What lay at the foot of the shaft I did not see, and I have it only as the two of them told it.

At the foot of it, in that hour, Halvard stood a long while with the lamp held low, and the rope hung slack beside him, and nothing moved in all that deep but the flame; and before him, against the rock, there stood a chest of black stone, and the dust of the forgotten years lay upon its lid a finger deep.

As when a man comes home from a long war, to the house of his fathers, and sees it open to the sky and its hearth choked with ash, and stoops to sweep the hearthstone with his sleeve, and finds beneath the ash the mark of the hands that first laid it: so Halvard stooped, and swept the lid of the chest with his sleeve, and knew it.

It was the mark of Aldwena.

Halvard laid his palm flat upon it, and held it there; and high above him, on the lip of the shaft, in the dark, Rhyna’s hands were still upon the rope.

It was the same mark that lies beneath the stone I hold now in its niche, with both my hands; and they knew whose chest it was ere ever they opened it.

Hear now what lay in the chest, and in what order, for I have laid it back since: uppermost, the Rite of the Cornerstone, cut small upon thin leaves of slate; beneath it, the slates of building for battle; beneath those, the annal of the founders, in the words of two; along the floor of the chest, a staff; and in the last corner three stone flasks, stoppered, which Halvard lifted one after another and shook beside the lamp, and in not one of them did anything move. That was all that Rhyna and Halvard found.

On the fourth morning, in the thin light under the broken beams, Halvard and Rhyna sat at the fire with the slates upon their knees, and read them through, slowly, a line and a breath and a line; and I, who was carrying water past them, set my bucket down near enough to hear, and forgot it. The first slate names itself in its first line, as a mason’s mark names the hand.

“This is the Rite of the Cornerstone,” one of them said, and the other finished, “by which a keeper is set over a great work, to raise it and to keep it, till it stand.”

Lower upon that same slate stands the law of the staff, which neither of them had heard in all their years: a staff is cut for its work, and does not leave it, and when the work is done, the staff is laid down in it; and so the founders had laid this one down, as a woman sets her house in order ere a long road, when they went down from the ridge to raise Eldhythe, the Eldest Haven.

But it was the slates beneath that troubled Halvard and Rhyna, and I saw it in their two faces ere I understood a word of it: for on those slates the walls were sloped back to turn a shot, and the gates were set crooked, that no ram might run at them straight, and the courses were so laid that a breach would fall outward into the ditch, and not inward upon the men who held the wall. The first builders of the Keep had known war. Against what, the slates do not say.

Twelve generations long, in all the havens, the guild taught that walls are raised for peace and for weather, and believed it, and was proud. The first fathers of the guild had known better, and we had forgotten them.

In the days that were left of the Fourteen Nights, Halyna, he and she, had to make with their own hands what no Stonewright living had ever made; for the slate names three oils for the Rite, and where each of them is to be got, and the three flasks in the corner of the chest were dry.

First came the cedar. The slate sends for it, to the stand of old cedars at the head of the cleft, the same stand from which the first staff was cut in the First Days, when the ridge was new to the hand of man; and Stannard of the ridge, the quarryman, led Rhyna and Halvard up to it through the snow.

Halvard and Rhyna would not fell a living tree, and they did not; so Stannard went among the fallen trunks that lay under the snow, and knocked upon each with his knuckle, as he knocks upon a stone before he trusts it, and set his ear to the wood, and listened.

“I know which of these will hold,” he said.

Then night after night, while the snow came down through the broken beams and hissed upon the coals, Rhyna and Halvard rendered the heartwood at the hearth, slowly, as the slate bids; and the smell of it went into our hair and into our blankets, and into the very stones of this hall, which breathe it out yet upon a wet night.

The second oil is granite-bloom: a pale crust that grows only upon worked stone left untended for many lives of men, and masons call it bloom, for it flowers where no one comes; and at the Keep it had flowered upon everything, and thickest of all upon the upper face of the old capstone, which had lain to the sky in the gateway through all the forgotten years.

So we children of the guild were set to scrape it into bowls, with the edges of old chisels, day after day in the cold, bent along the forgotten stones as gleaners bend along a stubble-field; and it was the first work given me that was not water, and a pinch at a time the bowls filled, and a pinch at a time Rhyna and Halvard steeped it in the cedar oil. The bloom came away grudgingly, and the old edges were blunt, and the cold of the stones went up into our hands.

My fingers were raw with it.

The third oil the mountain could not give. Idrenna, the eldest pair, Idlan and Denna, had carried one flask of myrrh-gum the whole length of the road out of the havens, to anoint the dead of the guild; and on the pass the ground would not take the dead, nor was there time, and the flask came up the mountain unopened.

Rhyna warmed the gum into the cedar oil, as the slate directs, while Halvard held the bowl for her; and its savour went through the whole hall, and the talk in the hall fell away before it, as talk falls away in a street where a bier is passing. With the myrrh of the road’s dead the Captain would be anointed for the burden of command.

I knew for whom it had been carried.

The flask of Idrenna stood empty all that night upon the hearthstone, and none of us would touch it.

Then came the fourteenth day, a day of thin sun, the first that had shone on us since the banner; and by its light, in the ward, before all who had come up the mountain, the Rite of the Cornerstone was done, under the shadow of the Title where it flew above the gate. The new capstone lay before the gate upon its timbers, pale and square and not yet raised: Stannard had quarried it from the same face as the old, and Orvenna, the wall-masters, Ormund and Penna, had dressed its faces.

Beside it sat Idrenna, the two of them, with the three flasks in their laps, for the slate says that the eldest pair must anoint; and the Captain stood where the slate said the overseer of the work must stand, with his hand upon the new stone and his face to the people. He did not like it. He stood there.

Above the gate the torn cloak lifted once upon the wind, and fell; and in all that ward, among two thousand and some hundreds, no one moved, and I heard the snow-water running in the old gutters.

Then Idrenna rose, slowly, the one leaning upon the other, and took up the flasks one after another, each in their four old hands, and tipped each over the bowed head of the Captain; and their hands, which shook that winter at everything else, did not shake upon the flasks. One of them named each oil, and the other gave its meaning:

“Cedar, for endurance. Granite-bloom, for strength. Myrrh, for the burden of command.”

The smell of the cedar went out over the ward, and the oil ran down into the Captain’s collar, nor did he wipe it; and we children at the front held up our raw hands to one another, and smiled, for the first time since the passes.

Then Idrenna took up the staff of the guild: a rod of polished wood capped with the finest polished ashlar, borne by every overseer of a great Stonewright work since the founding; and this was the first of them. The two of them held it out in the thin sun, and the ashlar of its cap took the light, and the Captain looked upon it a long while ere he put out his hands.

Four hands laid it in two.

By it he was named Commander of Rivenkeep; and the ward took up the name, and he stood in the noise of it, still, with the staff held in his two hands.

That night, when the people had gone to their fires, Rhyna and Halvard turned the new stone over upon its timbers, and knelt beside it in the gateway, and I held the lamp for them; and they cut their own mark into its underside, and their vow beside it, as the pair who raise that gate: the one set the chisel, and the other struck.

I saw their mark by my own lamp, and it is not mine to draw.

Then beside the vow, for the first time under any capstone since the founding, Rhyna and Halvard cut words that are not a vow. That afternoon they had climbed to the wall-top, where the banner flies above the gate, and copied them from the cloak upon its pike, stroke for stroke; and I held the lamp closer as the chisel went, and read them:

IN MEMORY OF OUR HOME, OUR FAMILIES, OUR GOD, OUR FREEDOMS, OUR PEACE.

It was not the hand of Rhyna, nor the hand of Halvard, but the hand of the Captain, large and slow, as a man writes who has not written much; and the stone-dust lay upon my feet, and I did not move them.

Neither of them had mended one stroke.

Before the lamp was out, the True Men took the ropes, and Rhyna and Halvard set the stone into the gate together, mark down, for that gate is of Rhyna’s line; and the True Men carried the old stone into the hall, and set it in the niche of the hearth-wall, where yet it lies.

That same night, ere he slept, the Captain leaned the staff in the corner of the gatehouse, for a staff does not leave its work, and his work was the wall and the war; and I think he would not have carried it further had the slate allowed it. Nor did he wear the oils past that day, but led as he had always led, from the wall, beside his True Men, with mortar upon his hands, and his half of a soldier’s cloak upon his back.

No one has moved the staff. If you doubt the tale, go and look at the dust on it.

Late that night, when the stone was set and the tools were cleaned, Halvard and Rhyna sent me down the shaft alone with a fresh lamp, to lay the slates back in the chest in their order; for the masons had set a ladder in it ere then, and I was light, and I was the third of their hearth.

Down I went, rung after rung, into the cold, with the lamp swinging at my wrist; and at the foot of the shaft I laid the slates back as I had seen them lie, and left the long place of the staff bare along the floor, and lifted the flasks, empty now of their oils, to set them back in their corner. The lamp smoked, and above me the shaft was black to its lip.

There was one leaf more.

It had lain beneath the flasks, flat to the floor of the chest and as grey as the floor, and the letters upon it were large and careful, as a child’s letters are, and as mine are yet; for a child had cut it. I carried it up inside my shirt, and the cold of it lay against my skin the whole length of the ladder.

At the fire, ere morning, Rhyna and Halvard read it for its meaning, and it was the leaf of the youngest apprentice of the First Days, and it held the custom of the hearth of the founders; and one of them said, and the other finished:

“When the stone is set and the tools are cleaned, we sit at the hearth, and one of us lays a tale.”

“As a course is laid upon a wall, one course each night, so the wall and the telling rise together.”
