So this hearth is older than the havens. We did not make its custom; we forgot it, and found it again at the bottom of a chest. On the next night Kael the Counter stood up at this hearth, for he would not sit, and laid the first tale, and it was of the Captain.

The people called him Commander. In his own heart he was still only the Captain.

This I lay as it was laid for me.

And the hearth said: We remember.

LAID IN THE FIRST SPRING OF THE SIEGE · ABOUT THE FOURTEEN NIGHTS AFTER THE BANNER.

Told by Seren Two-Inks, who writes this Book, and who is the third member of the household of Halyna. During the Fourteen Nights I was a child of the guild, carrying water in the yard. The slates in the chest are carved in the Hal, the writing of the first builders. No one alive knows how it sounds. Halvard and Rhyna read it for its meaning, the way Idrenna, the oldest pair, taught them. The words from the slates in this tale are that meaning, put into our own language.

“Hold the stone, Seren. I tell myself that every time.”

This is how the Rite of the Cornerstone was found again, deep in the ridge. It’s also about what had to be made before the Rite could be done, and what was done with it while the whole Keep watched.

It was the third night after the cry. The fires were down to coals. The guild was asleep in the ruined hall, under broken roof beams with stars showing between them. In the dark, Rhyna of the Inner Gate got up from her blanket. At the same moment, Halvard Stone-Warden got up from his and picked up the lamp. I was lying near the top of the stairs. I woke up when the light moved. I didn’t call out. I just lay there and watched it go down away from me into the dark. It got smaller at every turn, like a lamp lowered into a well.

There are vaults under the hall. Under those vaults are more vaults, cut down into the solid ridge long before any haven was built on the coast. No Stonewright in living memory had been into the deepest one. Rhyna and Halvard went down through all of them. She went first. He followed with the lamp. Stair after stair, turn after turn, down into the cold of the split rock itself, where the walls sweat in the lamplight and the air hasn’t moved since the Keep was founded. Finally the stairs ended. They were standing at the edge of a shaft, where the first builders had lowered their stones on ropes.

More than sixty years before the banner, when Rhyna and Halvard were babies lying in the same cradle, her mother sang a rhyme over them. It came from her family, which goes all the way back to Aldwena, the first builders, the two who set the capstone of the inner gate. Every mother in that family sang it over every cradle after that. In all those generations, nobody asked what it meant.

Much later, one of them sang it for me, and the other finished it:

Under the hall, and under the floor, under the stairs that go down no more, down where the rope let the first stone go, the first two keep what the first two know.

Now, at the edge of that shaft, under the stairs that went down no more, Rhyna knew where to look, and Halvard went down. She let out the rope. Neither of them said a word. The light sank away from her into the deep, and she stood in the dark with the rope running through her hands. What she knew and what he found were one thought. Their thoughts always are.

I didn’t see what was at the bottom of the shaft. I only know what the two of them told me.

At the bottom, Halvard stood for a long time with the lamp held low. The rope hung loose beside him. Nothing moved down there except the flame. In front of him, against the rock, stood a chest made of black stone. The dust of the forgotten years lay on its lid, a finger thick.

Imagine a man coming home from a long war to his family’s house. He finds it open to the sky, with the fireplace full of ash. He bends down and wipes the hearthstone with his sleeve, and under the ash he finds the mark of the hands that first laid it. That’s what Halvard did. He bent down and wiped the lid of the chest with his sleeve. And he knew it.

It was the mark of Aldwena.

Halvard laid his hand flat on it and kept it there. High above him, at the top of the shaft, in the dark, Rhyna’s hands went still on the rope.

It was the same mark that’s on the bottom of the stone I’m holding right now in its hollow, with both my hands. They knew whose chest it was before they ever opened it.

Here’s what was in the chest, in order. I’ve put it all back since, so I know. On top was the Rite of the Cornerstone, carved small on thin sheets of slate. Under that were slates about building for war. Under those was the founders’ record, written by two people. Along the bottom of the chest lay a staff. In the last corner were three stone flasks with stoppers. Halvard picked them up one at a time and shook them next to the lamp. Nothing moved inside any of them. That was everything Rhyna and Halvard found.

On the fourth morning, in the thin light under the broken beams, Halvard and Rhyna sat by the fire with the slates on their knees and read them slowly, one line, then a breath, then a line. I was carrying water past them. I set my bucket down close enough to hear, and forgot about it. The first slate says what it is in its first line, the way a mason’s mark tells you whose hand cut a stone.

“This is the Rite of the Cornerstone,” one of them said, and the other finished, “which puts a keeper in charge of a great work, to build it and to keep it, until it stands.”

Further down the same slate was the rule of the staff. Neither of them had ever heard it in all their years. A staff is cut for one work, and it never leaves that work. When the work is done, the staff is laid down in it. That’s why the founders had left this staff behind, the way a woman puts her house in order before a long journey. They left it when they went down from the ridge to build Eldhythe, the Eldest Haven.

But it was the slates underneath that worried Halvard and Rhyna. I saw it on both their faces before I understood a word. On those slates, the walls were sloped back to make cannon shot bounce off. The gates were set at an angle, so no battering ram could hit them straight on. The rows of stone were laid so that if the wall broke, it would fall outward into the ditch, not inward onto the men holding it. The first builders of the Keep had known war. The slates don’t say who they fought.

For twelve generations, in all the havens, the guild had taught that walls are built for peace and to keep out the weather. They believed it, and they were proud of it. The guild’s first fathers had known better. And we had forgotten them.

In the days left of the Fourteen Nights, Halyna had to make with their own hands something no living Stonewright had ever made. The slate names three oils for the Rite and says where to get each one. The three flasks in the corner of the chest were empty.

The cedar came first. The slate sends you to the grove of old cedars at the top of the gully. It’s the same grove the first staff was cut from in the First Days, when people had only just come to the ridge. Stannard of the ridge, the quarryman, led Rhyna and Halvard up there through the snow.

Halvard and Rhyna wouldn’t cut down a living tree, and they didn’t. So Stannard went among the fallen trunks under the snow. He knocked on each one with his knuckle, the way he knocks on a stone before he trusts it. He put his ear to the wood and listened.

“I know which of these will hold,” he said.

Then, night after night, while snow fell through the broken beams and hissed on the coals, Rhyna and Halvard boiled the oil out of the heartwood at the hearth, slowly, the way the slate says to. The smell got into our hair and our blankets, and into the stones of this hall. On a wet night, the walls still breathe it out.

The second oil is granite-bloom. It’s a pale crust that only grows on cut stone that’s been left alone for many lifetimes. Masons call it bloom because it flowers where nobody comes. At the Keep it had grown on everything. It was thickest of all on the top of the old capstone, which had lain face-up to the sky in the gateway all through the forgotten years.

So we children of the guild were sent to scrape it into bowls with the edges of old chisels. We did it day after day in the cold, bent over the forgotten stones like people gleaning a harvested field. It was the first job I was ever given that wasn’t carrying water. Pinch by pinch, the bowls filled. Pinch by pinch, Rhyna and Halvard soaked it in the cedar oil. The bloom didn’t want to come off. The old chisels were dull. The cold of the stones went up into our hands.

My fingers were rubbed raw.

The mountain couldn’t give us the third oil. Idrenna, the oldest pair, Idlan and Denna, had carried one flask of myrrh gum the whole long road from the havens. It was for anointing the guild’s dead. On the pass, the frozen ground wouldn’t take the dead, and there was no time anyway. So the flask came up the mountain unopened.

Rhyna warmed the gum into the cedar oil, the way the slate says, while Halvard held the bowl for her. The smell of it filled the whole hall. People stopped talking, the way talk dies away on a street when a coffin is carried past. The Captain would be anointed for the weight of command with the myrrh meant for the dead of the road.

I knew who it had been carried for.

Idrenna’s empty flask stood on the hearthstone all that night. None of us would touch it.

Then came the fourteenth day. It was a day of weak sunshine, the first sun we’d had since the banner. In its light, in the yard, in front of everyone who had come up the mountain, the Rite of the Cornerstone was done, in the shadow of the Title flying above the gate. The new capstone lay in front of the gate on wooden beams, pale and square, not yet lifted into place. Stannard had cut it from the same rock face as the old one. Orvenna, the wall-masters, Ormund and Penna, had smoothed its sides.

Idrenna, the two of them, sat next to it with the three flasks in their laps, because the slate says the oldest pair must do the anointing. The Captain stood where the slate says the overseer of the work must stand, with his hand on the new stone and his face toward the people. He didn’t like it. He stood there anyway.

Above the gate, the torn cloak rose once in the wind and fell. In that whole yard, among two thousand and a few hundred people, nobody moved. I could hear melted snow running in the old gutters.

Then Idrenna got up, slowly, one leaning on the other. They picked up the flasks one after another, each one held in their four old hands, and poured each one over the Captain’s bowed head. That winter their hands shook at everything else. They didn’t shake on the flasks. One of them named each oil, and the other said what it meant:

“Cedar, for endurance. Granite-bloom, for strength. Myrrh, for the burden of command.”

The smell of the cedar spread across the yard. The oil ran down into the Captain’s collar, and he didn’t wipe it off. We children at the front held up our raw hands to each other and smiled, for the first time since the passes.

Then Idrenna picked up the staff of the guild. It’s a rod of polished wood with a cap of the finest polished stone. Every overseer of a great Stonewright work has carried one since the founding, and this was the very first. The two of them held it out in the weak sunlight. The stone cap caught the light. The Captain looked at it for a long time before he held out his hands.

Four hands laid it in two.

With it, he was named Commander of Rivenkeep. The whole yard shouted the name. He stood in the noise of it, still, holding the staff in both hands.

That night, after the people had gone to their fires, Rhyna and Halvard turned the new stone over on its beams. They knelt next to it in the gateway, and I held the lamp for them. They cut their own mark into its underside, and their vow beside it, as the pair who were raising that gate. One held the chisel, and the other struck it.

I saw their mark by my own lamp. It’s not mine to draw.

Then, next to the vow, Rhyna and Halvard carved words that aren’t a vow. No one had ever done that under any capstone since the founding. That afternoon they had climbed to the top of the wall where the banner flies above the gate. They had copied the words from the cloak on its pike, stroke by stroke. I held the lamp closer as the chisel went, and I read them:

IN MEMORY OF OUR HOME, OUR FAMILIES, OUR GOD, OUR FREEDOMS, OUR PEACE.

It wasn’t Rhyna’s handwriting, or Halvard’s. It was the Captain’s: big and slow, like a man who hasn’t written much. Stone dust was falling on my feet, and I didn’t move them.

Neither of them had fixed a single stroke.

Before the lamp went out, the True Men took the ropes. Rhyna and Halvard set the stone into the gate together, mark side down, because that gate belongs to Rhyna’s family. The True Men carried the old stone into the hall and set it in the hollow in the hearth wall. It’s still there.

That same night, before he went to sleep, the Captain leaned the staff in the corner of the gatehouse. A staff doesn’t leave its work, and his work was the wall and the war. I don’t think he’d have carried it any further even if the slate had let him. He didn’t wear the oils after that day, either. He led the way he always had: from the wall, next to his True Men, with mortar on his hands and his half of a soldier’s cloak on his back.

Nobody has moved the staff. If you don’t believe me, go look at the dust on it.

Late that night, once the stone was set and the tools were cleaned, Halvard and Rhyna sent me down the shaft alone with a fresh lamp. My job was to put the slates back in the chest in the right order. The masons had put a ladder in the shaft by then. I was light, and I was the third of their household.

Down I went, rung after rung, into the cold, with the lamp swinging from my wrist. At the bottom I put the slates back the way I’d seen them. I left the long spot where the staff had been empty. I picked up the flasks, empty of their oils now, to put them back in their corner. The lamp was smoking. Above me, the shaft was black all the way to the top.

There was one more sheet.

It had been lying under the flasks, flat on the bottom of the chest and the same grey as the bottom. The letters on it were big and careful, the way a child’s letters are, and the way mine still are. A child had carved it. I carried it up inside my shirt. The cold of it pressed against my skin the whole way up the ladder.

At the fire, before morning, Rhyna and Halvard read it for its meaning. It was written by the youngest apprentice of the First Days. It described the custom of the founders’ hearth. One of them said, and the other finished:

“When the stone is set and the tools are cleaned, we sit at the hearth, and one of us lays a tale.”

“The way one row of stone is laid on a wall each night, the wall and the telling rise together.”

So this hearth is older than the havens. We didn’t invent its custom. We forgot it, and found it again at the bottom of a chest. The very next night, Kael the Counter stood up at this hearth, because he wouldn’t sit, and laid the first tale. It was about the Captain.

The people called him Commander. In his own heart he was still only the Captain.

I have laid this tale the way it was laid for me.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

I.5 · The Naming of the RivenmenStone leaf

LAID ON THE EVE OF THE GOING-DOWN, AT THE END OF THE SIXTH WINTER · OF THE TWENTY-FIRST DAY AFTER THE BANNER, AND OF THE WHOLE WAR AFTER IT.

Laid by Voss who asked, of Eldhythe, True Man of the harbour battery since the second winter, who was raised in his grandmother’s harbour-house on the quay. He asked his question in the first winter of the siege, and waited the whole war to answer it.

“Give me the stone. I have owed this hearth an answer a long while.”

In the first winter, near the end of it, I buried a friend under a cairn on the east face, for the ground there will not take a spade. He and I had turned the rope together on the quay at Eldhythe when we were boys, he at the one end and I at the other, while the little ones jumped and the gulls cried over the boats; and I laid the last stone upon him myself, and the wind came in off the grey.

His name was Jory.

That night I asked a question at this fire, and some of you were here to hear it; and of those who heard it, not a few lie now under the east cairns. I was cold, and I meant it hard: why do we fight, I asked, for ice and stone?

Then Stannard of the ridge, the quarryman, who will trust no stone till he has knocked on it, knocked once upon the hearthstone with his knuckle, and answered me:

“It is not the ice I’m fighting for.”

That was a good answer, but it was his. This one is mine, and it has taken me the whole war to cut it, which is long to be cutting one answer; but my grandmother, who kept a harbour-house upon the quay at Eldhythe, used to say that a slow answer is a slow tide: it comes in at its own pace, and covers the same stones.

Hear first how we were named, and what we swore upon the day of our naming; for my answer stands upon that day, and I cannot give you the one without the other.

It was the twenty-first day after the banner, the week after the Rite of the Cornerstone, and the whole Keep stood in the ward beneath the torn cloak: the six companies, and the guild, and the people of the column, with their children upon their shoulders to see. The Captain of the Torn Cloak had held the staff for a day, and set it down, as you know. I stood among the six companies in the trodden slush, a corporal, with Jory at my shoulder; and for once in his life he was not whistling.

Then Kael the Counter, oath-keeper of the True Men, climbed upon the fallen stones beside the gate, where all the ward could see him, and stood there a moment with his pike-hand empty, as a man stands who has one thing to say and means to say it once; and he began it:

“The Commander fought.”

And the whole guild ended it, in one voice, from Idrenna, the eldest pair, down to the least of the children who carried its water:

“The Stonewrights built.”

That was all the speech there was. It was enough. We looked at one another then, soldier and mason, across that ward, and saw, though none of us had yet the words for it, that we were not two peoples sharing a ruin; we had become one, as two courses become one wall when the mortar takes. A people was remade in that ward, out of the fighting and the building both, and neither half would have stood without the other.

On that same day we laid the name of the Keep upon the land. Rivenkeep, we said, all of it, from the high ridge to the farthest fallen harbour: every haven that the grey sails hold is Rivenkeep, and every road between them is Rivenkeep, and the edge of the sea is the edge of the Keep. It is a vow.

And we named ourselves the Rivenmen, the people of the riven stone, the keepers of the keep that would not fall. When we tell of our fathers we call them Shorelanders, for so they were, and we call ourselves we. That is the custom of this hearth, and here is where it began.

Then, all that day, till the dark came down, we went up the stair to the wall-top one after another, every man and woman and child of us, to the stone at the foot of the pike where the banner flies.

As when the boats come home to the quay at Eldhythe, at the end of a day, and each man throws his rope over his own post and makes it fast, and no one bids him do it, and by dark the whole harbour is held by its ropes: so each of us laid a hand upon that stone, and swore, and by dark the Keep was held.

We swore to take back every stone. Not one stone that was not ours. But every one that was.

The eldest of us never liked the new name, and never said so; and you may hear them yet, at this fire, murmuring “the Shorelands” into their shawls when they think no one is listening.

It was one of those eldest, old Ebba of Fenholm, who first said “We remember” at this hearth, on the first night, when Kael the Counter had set the stone back in its niche and none of us knew what should be said, nor who should say it; and on most nights after, she began it a breath before the hearth.

This winter, after the ten great hulls had come out of the grey and sat down in the havens, each in its own harbour, as a heron stands in the shallows, I asked her why she kept the old name. She was the eldest of us all, save only Idrenna, the eldest pair, and she was the grandmother of Marl the fen-born; and out of Fenholm, the town that was always sinking, she had carried upon her back a stone from the top course of her house, the course her generation laid, nor would she give it into Marl’s keeping till the fen had closed over the rest.

Ebba did not look up from the fire, but held her hands to it, and answered me:

“It is a name to come home to, when the last wall stands, and the last Mystarch lies broken.”

Then after a while, to the fire and not to me, she said one word more: “Perhaps.” I did not understand it then. I think I do now.

On the last night of her life old Ebba sat late at this fire, upon the end of the bench nearest the coals, with her shawl drawn up to her chin, as she sat most nights; and when the rest of us went to our blankets she was sleeping there, and no one woke her, for she was more than ninety, and the winter had been long. The fire burned down in the night to its coals, and no one mended it.

In the morning she did not wake.

Her shawl was still drawn up to her chin. At dusk we carried her up to the east face and laid her beside the soldiers, for the ground there will not take a spade, and Idrenna, the eldest pair, older even than she, said the prayer for the dead over her in two halves. Marl the fen-born laid the last stone upon her, and said over it the old name she used to murmur at the fire, “the Shorelands,” and none of us corrected him. That night, the Captain cut a hand’s breadth from his half of the cloak, and bound it on a pike over the cairn.

Old Ebba of Fenholm is under the east cairns these four nights; and every time this hearth has answered since, there has been a space at the front of the answer, the width of one old voice.

The hearth still waits a breath for Ebba.

None of us has tried to fill it.

Now, on the eve of the going-down, here is my answer, and you may weigh it. Stannard answered for himself, that first winter, and Jory, for whose sake I asked it, never heard it asked at all. I have said a thing since, upon the east wall, that men still say back to me as if it were news:

“We do not fight because we are right. We fight because our children are behind these walls.”

It is true, yet it is not the whole of the answer; for I have no children of my own, and still I said “our”, and I meant it of every child behind these walls.

Go up the east tower on a clear morning, when the grey lies low upon the water, and look along the coast: there you will see the nearest of our keeps, and the smoke of the next beyond it, and past those, farther than any eye can reach, they stand one after another across all ten haven-lands, like the posts along a quay, and on every one of them a crimson pennant. We raised each of them in this war, stone upon stone, and the grey sails have come against each of them, and each still stands. But a keep is not a place to stay in for ever; it is a place to go out from, and take back our own; and since this winter’s end, in every one of the ten havens, a Mystarch sits, and waits.

So here is the rest of my answer, which has taken me six winters to cut, and I will say it once. We do not fight for ice and stone. Every stone of it is Rivenkeep, and Rivenkeep is home; and on the banner we swore to bring it home.

Old Ebba of Fenholm stays on the east face; and Marl the fen-born carries her course-stone in his pack, for the old course at Fenholm.

Tomorrow we go down to the havens.

This I lay as it was laid for me.

The hearth waited a breath for Ebba.

And the hearth said: We remember.

And the Captain put his hood back.

LAID THE NIGHT BEFORE THE GOING-DOWN, AT THE END OF THE SIXTH WINTER · ABOUT THE TWENTY-FIRST DAY AFTER THE BANNER, AND THE WHOLE WAR AFTER IT.

Told by Voss who asked, of Eldhythe, True Man of the harbour battery since the second winter. He grew up in his grandmother’s harbour inn on the dock. He asked his question in the first winter of the siege, and waited the whole war to answer it.

“Give me the stone. I’ve owed this hearth an answer for a long time.”

Near the end of the first winter, I buried a friend under a pile of stones on the east face. The ground there is too hard for a shovel. When we were boys, he and I used to turn the jump rope together on the dock at Eldhythe. He held one end and I held the other. The little kids jumped, and the gulls screamed over the boats. I put the last stone on him myself. The wind came in off the grey.

His name was Jory.

That night I asked a question at this fire. Some of you were here to hear it. Of the people who heard it, a lot of them are buried under the east cairns now. I was cold, and I asked it hard: Why are we fighting for ice and rock?

Stannard of the ridge, the quarryman, who won’t trust a stone until he’s knocked on it, knocked once on the hearthstone with his knuckle and answered me:

“It’s not the ice I’m fighting for.”

That was a good answer. But it was his. This one is mine. It’s taken me the whole war to carve it, which is a long time to spend on one answer. My grandmother, who ran a harbour inn on the dock at Eldhythe, used to say that a slow answer is like a slow tide. It comes in at its own speed, and it covers the same stones.

First, here’s how we got our name, and what we swore on the day we got it. My answer is built on that day. I can’t give you one without the other.

It was the twenty-first day after the banner, a week after the Rite of the Cornerstone. The whole Keep stood in the yard under the torn cloak: the six companies, the guild, and the people of the column, with their children up on their shoulders so they could see. The Captain of the Torn Cloak had carried the staff for one day and then set it down, as you know. I stood with the six companies in the muddy slush. I was a corporal then. Jory stood at my shoulder. For once in his life he wasn’t whistling.

Then Kael the Counter, oath-keeper of the True Men, climbed onto the fallen stones by the gate, where the whole yard could see him. He stood there a moment with his pike hand empty, like a man who has one thing to say and plans to say it once. Then he started:

“The Commander fought.”

And the whole guild finished it in one voice, from Idrenna, the oldest pair, down to the smallest child who carried water:

“The Stonewrights built.”

That was the whole speech. It was enough. We looked at each other across the yard then, soldiers and masons. Even though none of us had the words for it yet, we saw that we weren’t two peoples sharing a ruin anymore. We had become one, the way two rows of stone become one wall when the mortar sets. A people was made new in that yard, out of the fighting and the building together. Neither half could have stood without the other.

That same day, we gave the Keep’s name to the whole land. We said all of it was Rivenkeep, from the high ridge to the farthest fallen harbour. Every haven the grey sails hold is Rivenkeep. Every road between them is Rivenkeep. The edge of the sea is the edge of the Keep. It’s a promise.

And we named ourselves the Rivenmen: the people of the split stone, the keepers of the keep that wouldn’t fall. When we talk about our fathers, we call them Shorelanders, because that’s what they were. We call ourselves “we.” That’s the custom at this hearth, and this is where it started.

Then, all that day until dark, we climbed the stairs to the top of the wall one after another. Every man, woman and child. We went to the stone at the foot of the pike where the banner flies.

Think of the boats coming home to the dock at Eldhythe at the end of the day. Each man throws his rope over his own post and ties it tight. Nobody tells him to. By dark the whole harbour is held by its ropes. That’s how each of us put a hand on that stone and swore. By dark, the Keep was held.

We swore to take back every stone. Not one stone that wasn’t ours. But every one that was.

The oldest among us never liked the new name, and never said so. You can still hear them at this fire, mumbling “the Shorelands” into their shawls when they think nobody’s listening.

One of those old ones, old Ebba of Fenholm, was the first to say “We remember” at this hearth. It was the first night. Kael the Counter had put the stone back in its hollow, and none of us knew what to say, or who should say it. On most nights after that, she started it a moment before everyone else.

This winter, the ten great ships came out of the grey and settled into the havens, each one in its own harbour, the way a heron stands in shallow water. After that, I asked her why she still used the old name. She was the oldest of us all, except for Idrenna, the oldest pair. She was the grandmother of Marl the fen-born. Fenholm was the town that was always sinking. When she left it, she carried a stone on her back from the top row of her house, the row her own generation had laid. She wouldn’t hand it over to Marl until the marsh had swallowed the rest.

Ebba didn’t look up from the fire. She held her hands out to it and answered me:

“It’s a name to come home to, when the last wall stands, and the last Mystarch lies broken.”

Then, after a while, she said one more word, to the fire and not to me: “Maybe.” I didn’t understand it then. I think I do now.

On the last night of her life, old Ebba sat up late at this fire. She sat at the end of the bench closest to the coals, with her shawl pulled up to her chin, the way she sat most nights. When the rest of us went to bed, she was asleep there. Nobody woke her. She was over ninety, and it had been a long winter. In the night the fire burned down to coals, and nobody added wood.

In the morning she didn’t wake up.

Her shawl was still pulled up to her chin. At dusk we carried her up to the east face and laid her next to the soldiers, because the ground there is too hard for a shovel. Idrenna, the oldest pair, who were even older than she was, said the prayer for the dead over her in two halves. Marl the fen-born put the last stone on her. Over it he said the old name she used to mumble at the fire, “the Shorelands,” and none of us corrected him. That night, the Captain cut a strip a hand wide from his half of the cloak and tied it to a pike over her grave.

Old Ebba of Fenholm has been under the east cairns for four nights now. Every time this hearth has answered since then, there’s been a gap at the front of the answer, the size of one old voice.

The hearth still waits a moment for Ebba.

None of us has tried to fill the gap.

Now, on the night before we go down to the havens, here’s my answer. You can judge it for yourselves. Stannard answered for himself that first winter. And Jory, the one I asked it for, never even heard the question. Since then, I’ve said something on the east wall that people still repeat back to me as if it were news:

“We do not fight because we are right. We fight because our children are behind these walls.”

It’s true. But it’s not the whole answer. I don’t have any children of my own. I still said “our,” and I meant every child behind these walls.

Go up the east tower on a clear morning, when the grey is low on the water, and look along the coast. You’ll see the nearest of our keeps, and the smoke from the next one past it. Past those, further than you can see, they stand one after another across all ten haven-lands, like posts along a dock, each with a red pennant. We built every one of them in this war, stone on stone. The grey sails have attacked every one of them. Every one is still standing. But a keep isn’t a place to stay forever. It’s a place to go out from, to take back what’s ours. Since the end of this winter, in every one of the ten havens, a Mystarch sits and waits.

So here is the rest of my answer. It took me six winters to carve it, and I’ll say it once. We don’t fight for ice and rock. Every stone of it is Rivenkeep, and Rivenkeep is home. And on the banner, we swore to bring it home.

Old Ebba of Fenholm stays on the east face. Marl the fen-born carries her stone from the top row of her house in his pack, to put back on the old wall at Fenholm.

Tomorrow we go down to the havens.

I have laid this tale the way it was laid for me.

The hearth waited a moment for Ebba.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

And the Captain pushed his hood back.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

BOOK TWO · THE BOOK OF THE BUILDERS

Long before the banner: how the first builders raised the Keep on the riven ridge and their children left it, how the mist-folk lived in the breath of their wood older still, and how the guild makes two into one.

II.1 · Of the Keep That Was ForgottenStone leaf

LAID IN THE FIRST SUMMER OF THE SIEGE · OF THE FIRST DAYS, MORE THAN FIFTEEN GENERATIONS BEFORE THE BANNER, AND OF THE FORGETTING AFTER; AND OF THE DUSK OF THE BANNER.

Laid by Seren Two-Inks, the scribe of this Book, in the manner of the old annals of the guild; and the founders’ leaf within it is their own, and not mine. That leaf lay in the black chest at the foot of the shaft through all the forgotten years, and Halyna, the two of them, read it to me out of the old hand for its meaning, for no one living can sound it; and I have set it down as they gave it, and have mended nothing. This is the stone leaf of the two homes, and its facing leaf belongs to the wood.

“Hold the stone, Seren. I tell myself so every time.”

Hear the annal of the Keep, as the founders kept it, and as we did not.

In the First Days, ere there was a haven upon any coast, or any road between the mountains and the sea, the first builders of the guild came up onto the high ridge, where it stands riven in two, as though a great blade had come down upon it; and there in the cleft, out of the ridge’s own grey stone, they raised a hold; and this was Rivenkeep, the first work of the first builders. Two of them, a bonded pair whose one name was Aldwena, cut the tale of that raising upon a leaf of slate, in letters no bigger than grains of barley; and these are their words:

We two set this down, who laid the gate.

Up onto the ridge we came, and found it cleft from its crown to its root; and in the cleft we made a hold, of the ridge’s own stone and of no stone else. The wardens of the quarry cut the stone, and the first of them cut his name in the lintel of the western stair, and left the stone below it for his sons.

With our four hands we two laid the capstone of the inner gate. Under it we cut our mark, and our vow beside it, where no eye will see it again unless the wall falls.

This wall will stand. We two laid it, and we two answer for it, while stone is stone. It stands.

Their mark and that vow are upon this stone, on its underside.

That mark is the Stonwryt, the pair’s own and no other pair’s: the seers read it at their cradle, and the guild gives it them at their sealing, and cut beneath a wall with the vow beside it, it is a vow sealed in living rock, earned by toil and skill. It cannot be bought, though in the days of the havens the master-pairs struck coin beneath their marks, part gold and part black stone, and the markets of every coast stood upon that coin as the temples stood upon their walls: trust, sealed in stone. The unbonded have no mark for the stone, and I shall never cut one. Late, when no one is left at the fire, I have put my hand beneath this one, and traced it with one finger.

Now the wardens of that quarry cut their names in the lintel of the western stair, one beneath another, for as many generations as the quarry was worked, and beneath the last of them the stone is smooth. Of their line is Halvard Stone-Warden, and Rhyna of the Inner Gate is of the line of Aldwena; so the gate that you came under tonight was cut by his fathers and laid by her forebears, and it was two lines made one long before ever Halvard and Rhyna were laid in their one cradle.

In the last of the First Days the folk went down from the Keep, for the ridges were cold and the harbours warm, and the trade came up along the coasts and never over the mountains; and the Stonewrights went down after them, for the folk had need of walls. Yet ere they went, the founders set the things of the Keep in order, and laid them in its deepest vault, and the first staff among them, for a staff does not leave its work. Then they went down the mountain and raised Eldhythe, which men call the Eldest Haven, and rightly; but it is the eldest only of the havens.

Of those who went down last, no slate tells the names. Behind them the wind went on over the ridge, and through the empty Keep, and in under the gate that no one kept.

It had never fallen. It had only been left.

Generation upon generation the Keep went out of mind, until it was more myth than memory: a name in the songs of the havens, the castle that had never fallen, a place that the children of Eldhythe were sure had never stood. No mason climbed the long road to it any more, nor any pair of the guild to point its mortar. That is a word of our craft that I love, and it means to fill each joint anew at its face, and shut the rain out of the wall. So the rain came into the joints, and the frost came after the rain, and every winter the ice split what the rain had loosened, stone from stone.

In some winter that no one counted, the capstone of the inner gate came down from its two posts into the gateway. No one heard it fall.

Then came the Fall, when the havens burned one after another along the coast, and the folk who lived were gathered into one column, haven by haven, as a river gathers its streams; and after Glasspire, the last and proudest, that column turned up the mountain road, to the one place the songs said would hold. I was in that column, and came up the last of the road with the snow in my face. We found a shadow: broken gates, crumbled walls, a hall with its roof lying in its hearth. What man could not destroy, time had withered. Forgetting had done what no enemy ever could.

It was dusk on the day of the banner, and the last light lay grey upon the snow, when Halvard Stone-Warden and Rhyna of the Inner Gate, he and she together, came under the inner gate with the column at their backs, and found the capstone lying in the gateway where it had fallen. Close behind them I came, with the water-skin that was all my craft in those days, and I knew neither of their names. Halvard swept the snow from the stone with his sleeve; and on its upper face, which had lain to the sky through all the forgotten years, the bloom had grown as pale as frost on a gatepost.

Then the two of them knelt down in the snow, for it is a stone that takes two. As when two fishers of Tidesmeet, after the long storms of the year’s end, go down to the shingle and find a boat that they had given up for lost, lying keel uppermost in the wrack, and set their four hands under her gunwale, and turn her over between them, and read upon her bow the name their fathers cut: so Halvard and Rhyna turned the stone.

It was their own.

On its underside was the mark of Aldwena, with their vow beside it, as sharp as on the day it was cut; and beside the vow was the bite of chisels, where the quarry-wardens had dressed the stone. Rhyna laid her fingers on the mark of her forebears, and Halvard set his thumb in the bite that his fathers left, and neither of them said a word.

It had lain in the gateway since some winter that no one counted, and no one had heard it fall, and it was whole. Out in the ward the people of the column were coming in still, and their feet made no sound upon the snow.

The stone kept its vow. It was we who did not keep ours.

Then Halvard and Rhyna rose from their knees, and went out into the grass of the ward, and sat down among the guild a little apart, and spoke to no one; and ere the last of the light was gone off the snow, they stood up again, and cried the guild to its feet.

In the Fourteen Nights, Stannard of the ridge, the quarryman, cut a new capstone from the same face of the cleft, and knocked it with his knuckle, and listened, and said that it would hold; and Orvenna, the wall-masters, Ormund and Penna, dressed it with mallet and chisel. When the Rite was done, Halvard and Rhyna cut their own mark beneath it, and raised it, and set it in the gate together, for the gate is of her line. The old stone the True Men carried into this hall, and laid in the niche of the hearth-wall, and tonight it is under my two hands. Whoever holds it holds the vow of the first builders, and the shame of their children.

This I lay as it was laid for me.

And the hearth said: We remember.

LAID IN THE FIRST SUMMER OF THE SIEGE · ABOUT THE FIRST DAYS, MORE THAN FIFTEEN GENERATIONS BEFORE THE BANNER, AND THE FORGETTING THAT CAME AFTER; AND ABOUT THE EVENING OF THE BANNER.

Told by Seren Two-Inks, who writes this Book, in the style of the guild’s old records. The founders’ page inside it is their own writing, not mine. That page lay in the black chest at the bottom of the shaft all through the forgotten years. Halyna, the two of them, read it to me from the old writing for its meaning, since no one alive knows how the old writing sounds. I have written it down as they gave it to me, and fixed nothing. This is the stone page about our two homes. The page facing it belongs to the wood.

“Hold the stone, Seren. I tell myself that every time.”

This is the record of the Keep, as the founders kept it, and as we didn’t.

In the First Days, before there was a haven on any coast, or any road between the mountains and the sea, the first builders of the guild came up onto the high ridge. The ridge is split in two, as if a huge blade had come down on it. There, in the split, they built a stronghold out of the ridge’s own grey stone. This was Rivenkeep, the first work of the first builders. Two of them, a bonded pair whose one shared name was Aldwena, carved the story of that building on a sheet of slate, in letters no bigger than grains of barley. These are their words:

We two wrote this down. We set the gate.

We came up onto the ridge and found it split from top to bottom. In the split we made a stronghold, out of the ridge’s own stone and no other stone. The quarry wardens cut the stone. The first of them cut his name into the top stone of the western stair, and left the stone below it blank for his sons.

With our four hands, we two set the capstone of the inner gate. Under it we cut our mark, and our vow next to it, where no one will ever see them again unless the wall falls.

This wall will stand. We two built it, and we two are answerable for it, for as long as stone is stone. It stands.

Their mark and that vow are on this stone, on the underside.

That mark is called the Stonwryt. It belongs to that one pair and to no other pair. The seers read it over their cradle. The guild gives it to them when they are sealed. When it is cut under a wall with the vow beside it, it’s a vow sealed in living rock, earned by toil and skill. It can’t be bought. Still, in the days of the havens, the master pairs stamped coins under their marks, part gold and part black stone. The markets of every coast depended on those coins, the way the temples depended on their walls. The coins meant trust, sealed in stone. The unbonded have no mark for stone, and I will never cut one. Late at night, when nobody is left at the fire, I have put my hand under this stone and traced the mark with one finger.

The wardens of that quarry cut their names into the top stone of the western stair, one under another, for as many generations as the quarry was worked. Under the last name, the stone is smooth. Halvard Stone-Warden comes from their family. Rhyna of the Inner Gate comes from the family of Aldwena. So the gate you walked under tonight was cut by his ancestors and set by hers. It joined two families into one long before Halvard and Rhyna were ever put in the same cradle.

At the end of the First Days, the people went down from the Keep. The ridges were cold, the harbours were warm, and trade came along the coasts, never over the mountains. The Stonewrights followed them down, because the people needed walls. But before they left, the founders put the Keep’s things in order and stored them in its deepest vault. The first staff was among them, because a staff doesn’t leave its work. Then they went down the mountain and built Eldhythe. People call it the Eldest Haven, and they’re right. But it’s only the oldest of the havens.

No slate tells us the names of the last people to leave. After they were gone, the wind kept blowing over the ridge, through the empty Keep, and in under the gate nobody guarded anymore.

It had never fallen. It had only been left behind.

Generation after generation, people forgot the Keep, until it was more legend than memory. It was a name in the songs of the havens: the castle that never fell. The children of Eldhythe were sure it had never existed. No mason climbed the long road to it anymore. No pair of the guild went up to point its mortar. That’s a word from our craft that I love. It means filling each joint fresh at the surface to keep the rain out of the wall. So the rain got into the joints. Then the frost came after the rain. Every winter the ice split apart what the rain had loosened, stone from stone.

In some winter nobody counted, the capstone of the inner gate fell off its two posts into the gateway. Nobody heard it fall.

Then came the Fall. The havens burned one after another along the coast. The people who survived were gathered into one column, haven by haven, the way a river gathers its streams. After Glasspire, the last and proudest haven, that column turned up the mountain road, to the one place the songs said would hold. I was in that column. I walked the last of the road with snow blowing in my face. What we found was a shadow of a castle: broken gates, crumbling walls, a hall with its roof lying in its fireplace. What people couldn’t destroy, time had worn away. Forgetting had done what no enemy ever could.

It was dusk on the day of the banner. The last light lay grey on the snow. Halvard Stone-Warden and Rhyna of the Inner Gate, he and she together, walked under the inner gate with the column behind them. They found the capstone lying in the gateway where it had fallen. I was right behind them, carrying the water-skin. That was my whole job in those days. I didn’t know either of their names. Halvard brushed the snow off the stone with his sleeve. On its top side, which had faced the sky all through the forgotten years, the bloom had grown, pale as frost on a gatepost.

Then the two of them knelt down in the snow, because it takes two people to turn that stone. Think of two fishermen from Tidesmeet after the big storms at the end of the year. They go down to the beach and find a boat they had given up for lost, lying upside down in the seaweed. They put their four hands under its edge and flip it over together, and they read the name their fathers carved on its front. That’s how Halvard and Rhyna turned the stone.

It was theirs.

On its underside was the mark of Aldwena, with their vow beside it, as sharp as the day it was cut. Next to the vow were chisel marks, where the quarry wardens had shaped the stone. Rhyna put her fingers on the mark of her ancestors. Halvard put his thumb in the groove his ancestors had left. Neither of them said a word.

It had been lying in the gateway since some winter nobody counted. Nobody had heard it fall. And it was in one piece. Out in the yard, the people of the column were still coming in. Their feet made no sound on the snow.

The stone kept its promise. We were the ones who didn’t keep ours.

Then Halvard and Rhyna got up off their knees. They walked out into the grass of the yard and sat down near the guild, a little apart, and didn’t talk to anyone. Before the last light was gone from the snow, they stood up again and called the guild to its feet.

During the Fourteen Nights, Stannard of the ridge, the quarryman, cut a new capstone from the same rock face. He knocked on it with his knuckle, listened, and said it would hold. Orvenna, the wall-masters, Ormund and Penna, shaped it with mallet and chisel. When the Rite was done, Halvard and Rhyna cut their own mark under it, lifted it, and set it in the gate together, because the gate belongs to her family. The True Men carried the old stone into this hall and put it in the hollow in the hearth wall. Tonight it’s under my two hands. Whoever holds it holds the promise of the first builders, and the shame of their children.

I have laid this tale the way it was laid for me.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

II.2 · Of the Breath of the WoodWood leaf

KNOWN IN THE FIFTH WINTER, IN THE TIDE THAT LEARNED DECEIT · OF THE AGES BEFORE THE HAVENS, OLDER THAN THE KEEP, DOWN TO THE YEARS OF THE IRON, FIVE GENERATIONS TO THREE BEFORE THE BANNER.

Known from the gift of the Kneeling Guest at its deepest holding, and set facing the tale of the Keep, for both are tales of home. Through many nights Halyna held it by turns, he and she, the one at the hearth while the other slept, and ever the sleeper woke knowing, ere a word was said; for the Guest’s people had put the whole of home into it, that a stranger who held it long enough might know them whole. It was the hardest of their tellings, for we have no words for a home that is air: what the wood calls the breath we call the fog and the grey, and the old sailors the Mystlands, and its black pillars the ship-masters named the Mystholders. One word in it, a name, would not be told. Told by Halyna, by turns, in two inks, and set down by me.

This is held in the grain.

Ere your fathers cut their first stone, we were old. We who call ourselves [] lived in the wood, and the wood in us, and between us was the breath.

The name the wood gives itself, not yet told
Translation

Naelear, those of the breath. Seren sets the word beside the sign, and the sign stays.

It was the air we breathed.

Flat lay our land beneath the trees, past all measuring, to the far edge of the world. In their long sleep the trees breathed, and their breath lay down grey among the trunks. It fed the trees, and it fed us; and we tended it as you tend a hearth.

Through the breath the sun came softened: the between-light, neither day nor dusk. We were made for that light, and never were we children of the dark. The open sun withers a fern of the deep wood, thrown down on bare sand; and the open sun withers us.

In the years of the iron we saw the open sun wither our children.

In all our long summers we led the wood, and the wood kept the breath, and the breath kept us.

We pruned the wood and sang to it; we led its roots to the water, and where we led it, there it grew. One life it was, in two kinds: we tended the wood, and the wood kept us, and it kept its own will besides.

Without the breath the wood sickens. Without the wood the breath does not come again. Without either we fail, and slowly: the eldest last, and the youngest first. We live twice as long as you, and half again. We were old in the world by more lives of yours than you have names for.

Nor was the wood one wood. The twisted boughs we bent in our rites. The pale saplings we sang to, and they answered; and a stand of them at evening was a choir. Under the silver elders the council sat, for no one lies beneath a silver elder.

At the very edge, where the breath came down to your sea, our pillars stood in their long ranks. They held the breath between them, as a mast holds a sail. We call them the Naelsaen, the mist-hearts: the anchors of our sky, and the only trees of ours you ever found.

The wood was our tongue of truth. Our speech is the speech of the storm in the high boughs, all cracks and breaks, and every deep of the wood has its own. But what is carved in living wood is not heard. It is known.

Whoever lays a hand on the carving knows what the carver meant, whatever tongue the carver spoke. So no word could be twisted, nor any promise misheard. We say Ael’thar in thunder, and carve Aelthar soft: the thunder is for the moment, the softness for always. Anything sacred we gave to the wood, and anything binding, and anything that must not be mistaken.

We did not know there was any other way to be believed.

In all the summers of your havens, we watched your shore from the edge of the breath, and kept ourselves hidden.

We saw your havens raised, and the lamps lit in them at dusk. We saw your boats come a little way into the breath, and turn back with white faces.

We did not know why.

Here is our fault, and the grain will not leave it out.

We did not go down to your shore. We wove the breath thicker, and hid in it, and were glad. So long we hid, and so well, that we were never once seen.

Since your first haven we had listened to you. You had never once heard us. We had made sure of it.

This is our home: the flat wood under the breath; the between-light; the pale saplings singing at evening; the silver elders at council. And at the edge of the world, the pillars, with the breath held up between them.

If any green is left of us, it remembers this; and the grain holds it still.

And no one answered.

KNOWN IN THE FIFTH WINTER, DURING THE TIDE THAT LEARNED DECEIT · ABOUT THE AGES BEFORE THE HAVENS, OLDER THAN THE KEEP, DOWN TO THE YEARS OF THE IRON, FROM FIVE TO THREE GENERATIONS BEFORE THE BANNER.

Known from the gift of the Kneeling Guest, from deep inside it, and placed facing the tale of the Keep, because both are tales of home. For many nights Halyna took turns holding it, he and she. One sat at the hearth while the other slept. Every time, the one who had been sleeping woke up already knowing, before a word was said. The Guest’s people had put the whole of their home into it, so that a stranger who held it long enough could know them completely. It was the hardest of all their tellings, because we have no words for a home made of air. What the wood calls “the breath,” we call the fog, or the grey. The old sailors called it the Mystlands. The ship-masters called its black pillars the Mystholders. One word in it, a name, wouldn’t come out in words. Told by Halyna, taking turns, in two inks, and written down by me.

This is held in the grain.

Before your fathers cut their first stone, we were already old. We, who call ourselves [], lived in the wood, and the wood lived in us. Between us was the breath.

The name the wood gives itself, not told yet
Translation

Naelear, those of the breath. Seren writes the word next to the sign, and the sign stays.

It was the air we breathed.

Our land was flat under the trees. It went on further than anyone could measure, to the far edge of the world. The trees breathed in their long sleep, and their breath settled grey among the trunks. It fed the trees, and it fed us. We looked after it the way you look after a fire.

The sun came through the breath softened. It was the in-between light, not day and not dusk. We were made for that light. We were never creatures of the dark. Throw a fern from the deep forest down on bare sand, and the open sun kills it. The open sun kills us too.

In the years of the iron, we watched the open sun kill our children.

Through all our long summers, we guided the wood. The wood kept the breath. The breath kept us.

We trimmed the wood and sang to it. We led its roots to water, and wherever we led it, it grew. It was one life in two kinds. We looked after the wood, and the wood looked after us, and it also had a will of its own.

Without the breath, the wood gets sick. Without the wood, the breath doesn’t come back. Without either one, we die out slowly: the oldest last, the youngest first. We live two and a half times as long as you. We were old in the world for more of your lifetimes than you have names for.

And the wood wasn’t just one kind of wood. We bent the twisted branches in our ceremonies. We sang to the pale young trees, and they sang back. A grove of them in the evening was a choir. Our council sat under the silver elders, because nobody lies under a silver elder.

At the very edge, where the breath came down to your sea, our pillars stood in long rows. They held the breath up between them, the way a mast holds up a sail. We call them the Naelsaen, the mist-hearts. They were the anchors of our sky. They were the only trees of ours you ever found.

The wood was how we told the truth. When we speak out loud, it sounds like a storm in the high branches, all cracks and snaps, and every part of the forest speaks its own way. But what is carved in living wood isn’t heard. It is known.

Anyone who puts a hand on the carving knows what the carver meant, whatever language the carver spoke. So no word could be twisted. No promise could be misheard. We say Ael’thar like thunder, and carve Aelthar softly. The thunder is for the moment. The softness is for always. Anything holy, we gave to the wood. Anything binding. Anything that must not be mistaken.

We didn’t know there was any other way to be believed.

All through the summers of your havens, we watched your shore from the edge of the breath, and stayed hidden.

We saw your havens built, and the lamps lit in them at dusk. We saw your boats come a little way into the breath, and turn back with pale faces.

We didn’t know why.

This is our fault, and the grain won’t leave it out.

We never went down to your shore. We wove the breath thicker and hid in it, and we were glad. We hid for so long, and so well, that no one ever saw us once.

We had listened to you since your first haven. You had never once heard us. We made sure of that.

This is our home: the flat wood under the breath. The in-between light. The pale young trees singing in the evening. The silver elders at council. And at the edge of the world, the pillars, holding up the breath between them.

If any green is left of us, it remembers this. And the grain still holds it.

And no one answered.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

II.3 · Of the Bonded and the TheolithsStone leaf

LAID IN HIGH SUMMER OF THE FIRST YEAR, ON THE NIGHT OF ENRELLA’S SEALING · OF THE CUSTOMS OF THE GUILD, AND OF THE NIGHT OF THE PASS IN THE LAST MONTH OF THE ROAD.

Laid by Halyna, the Bonded of the Keep, he and she by turns, a paragraph each, for the children at the hearth, as they have always told it to children; and it is the one telling of theirs in which they let me mark whose paragraph is whose, for the children like to know. That morning, under the inner gate, Idrenna, the eldest pair, had sealed Enno and Della, which was the first sealing at Rivenkeep since the founding, and I held the lamp. The last of the tale is about me, and I have written it as they said it.

They laid their two hands upon the stone in its niche, his and hers, and began it together:

“We hold the stone together, as we hold all things.”

Rhyna. Come closer, all of you, and sit where the fire can find your faces; for this is the children’s tale tonight, and the children shall have the front, and the old may listen too, if they are quiet. This morning, under the inner gate, you saw Enno and Della kneel down two and rise up one, under a single name. Now they are Enrella.

Enno and Della sat at the front among the children, though they were fifteen that summer and sealed that morning; and a small girl asked them how it felt. “It is like being one person,” Enno said, and Della finished, “with four hands and no excuses.” Then they told her that they meant to build a house in the ward with one door, for they would never need more than the one, and that under its first stone they would cut the mark the guild had given them that morning, for they could not wait for the last; and the children laughed, and the old with them.

Halvard. Not all Shorelanders were Stonewrights, yet all Stonewrights were Shorelanders. Hold that fast, for it is the first thing, and the footing of all the rest. To the guild a child is called in the cradle, and from any hearth in all the land: from the huts of the fishers and the halls of the merchants, and once, the rolls say, from a gaoler’s house in Carnhold. It was a great honour to be called, and no mother or father in all the annals of the guild ever refused it. For the guild kept the faith of the land as it kept its walls, and a child called to the one was called to both.

Rhyna. We Stonewrights hold, as our fathers held before us, that God made each pair one ere either of the two drew breath. So when the seers go among the cradles, bending low over each with the lamp, it is not to make a pair, nor to pick one out, but to read what was written before ever we were born. They do not choose. The guild calls that joining the Aetherbond, and the two whom it joins the Aetherbonded, or, more simply, the Bonded. A seer bent so over us two, and read us, and from that night we lay in one cradle, in his mother’s house and in mine by turns; and so it has been with us two, who have never in our lives slept a night under different roofs.

Halvard. When bonded children are old enough to hold a chisel, they are given to the Theoliths, the old master-pairs who teach; and ours taught us two to cut and to lay and to set, and to pray, and to keep the count of the stones in the old hand. They taught us the vow besides, and named it the Covenant of Completion; yet to children they gave the name and no more, and so we two give you the name and no more. You will understand it when it is yours to understand. Our own Theoliths were Idrenna, who sealed Enrella this morning; and they are sitting by the door tonight, and will nod when we two tell it right.

By the door, Idrenna, Idlan and Denna, sat with their two white heads close together, as the very old sit, and did not nod.

Rhyna. When bonded children come of age they are sealed, and the sealing says aloud what the cradle said first. Keep this, for you will need it later: the bond is made before the cradle, read in the cradle, and made whole only at the sealing. Then the guild lays upon the two of them one name made of both, as a gate is made of two posts and the stone laid across them, and with it the mark read at their cradle, which the guild keeps hidden till that day: a name for the mouth, and a mark for the stone. And the two of them hold that mark holier than anything they own; no hand but theirs may cut it, and no other pair will ever break it. In that way we two were sealed, and named Halyna; and so he is my husband and I am his wife, and we were one pair long before we were either.

Halvard. The fire is low now, and the ash on the hearth is soft and grey; so watch my finger, all of you, for I will draw it only once. This is our mark, the mark of us two, as the seer read it at our cradle and the guild gave it at our sealing. On the night of the Rite, we two cut it upon the underside of the new capstone of the inner gate, where no eye will see it again unless the wall falls. Look once.

Rhyna. Now I sweep it out with the flat of my palm, and it is gone; for ash blows away, and a mark is for stone, which holds.

Halvard. The Bonded live as one, and work as one, and pray as one, and they do it all without a word. You have seen us two on the wall in the holding, and that neither of us calls to the other, nor needs to; and the work of a pair is not the work of two, but of twelve, as the old masters counted it. In the guild the man of a pair is called Elder and the woman Elderess, and the two are one rank. So I am Elder Halvard and she is Elderess Rhyna, and neither is the elder of the other. There was never a stone she laid second.

Rhyna. What the one feels, the other feels: if I cut my hand in the south tower, he feels it on the north wall, and knows which hand. And what the one thinks, the other has thought already, ere it is half said. Two made one: that is the whole of the bond. The old masters had a saying for it, and you will hear it again before these tales are done. Harm to one is harm to both.

Halvard. The Bonded are seldom apart, and never far. No bond was ever broken, not in all the annals of the guild, from the slates of the First Days to the rolls of the last haven. Separation, God did not permit.

The fire had burned down a finger’s width. At the front, Enno and Della sat hand in hand, as they had come up the mountain, and the small girl who had asked them was asleep against Della’s knee.

Rhyna. So the Bonded die together; and we two tell it without fear, for to us it is not a sad thing. If one of a pair is killed, there is no wound upon the one who is left, and no sickness; but the heart breaks, for it knows, and the body stills, for its work is done, and they die in peace. When the left hand dies, does not the right one die too? Eight days, men say, is the longest that ever one lived on alone; the Stonewrights, who keep the count, say hours. Many have drawn their last breath in the same moment, a mile apart, and each knew that the other was going. And each went too.

Halvard. Now the last thing, and then to bed. Sometimes one of a cradle dies young, ere ever the two are sealed; and then the other lives, for the bond was not yet whole. Such a child is called unbonded, and is given to a bonded hearth as its third; and by old custom it is the unbonded who hold the lamp at a sealing, as Seren held it this morning. The unbonded keep one name all their lives, and have no mark for the stone; and among the guild one name is a mourning. But the old masters said that God does not take the one, without leaving the other a work to do.

Rhyna. Seren, who held the lamp this morning and is writing this down, is our third. She was laid in one cradle with Tarnel, a fisher’s son of Tidesmeet; and all the long road he knotted a net as he walked, to fish the mountain tarns when we came thither, and he taught her the knot. On the night of the pass, when the cold took many of the children and some of the old, the two of them lay down for warmth under one cloak. She woke beside him. So she lived, and she is one; and when she came up the mountain without him, we two took her to our hearth.

Halvard. “A net is only knots holding hands”: so Tarnel told her, and she binds this Book with his knot. She cannot know the wood, for a pair holds a knowing between them, as two hands hold a stone that one hand cannot lift; yet she writes it truer than we two could. That is her loss, and it is not small; but hear what it gives her. She will not die of another’s death, and she can go where we two cannot, alone, and carry this Book on after us. The Bonded have always been the keepers of the hearth and of the leaf, and every scribe before her was one of a pair; and when a pair went, their book went into the vault behind them, and no one told from it again, for a book that no one tells is only ink. Look, her pen has not stopped, for this Book must outlive its tellers. That is why it is Seren who writes it.

By the door, Idrenna both nodded. Then Halvard and Rhyna said the last of it together, as they always end it, for it is the oldest saying of the guild:

Birth united them; death could not separate them.

This we lay as it was laid for us.

And the hearth said: We remember.

And the Captain put his hood back.

LAID IN MIDSUMMER OF THE FIRST YEAR, ON THE NIGHT ENRELLA WERE SEALED · ABOUT THE CUSTOMS OF THE GUILD, AND THE NIGHT ON THE PASS IN THE LAST MONTH OF THE ROAD.

Told by Halyna, the Bonded of the Keep, he and she taking turns, one paragraph each, for the children at the hearth, the way they have always told it to children. It’s the only one of their tellings where they let me mark whose paragraph is whose, because the children like to know. That morning, under the inner gate, Idrenna, the oldest pair, had sealed Enno and Della. It was the first sealing at Rivenkeep since the founding, and I held the lamp. The last part of the tale is about me. I have written it the way they said it.

They put their two hands on the stone in its hollow, his and hers, and started together:

“We hold the stone together, the way we hold everything.”

Rhyna. Come closer, all of you, and sit where the fire can light your faces. This is the children’s tale tonight, so the children get the front. The old people can listen too, if they’re quiet. This morning, under the inner gate, you saw Enno and Della kneel down as two and stand up as one, with a single name. Now they are Enrella.

Enno and Della sat at the front with the children, even though they were fifteen that summer and had been sealed that very morning. A little girl asked them what it felt like. “It’s like being one person,” Enno said, and Della finished, “with four hands and no excuses.” Then they told her they were going to build a house in the yard with only one door, because they’d never need more than one. Under its first stone they would cut the mark the guild had given them that morning, because they couldn’t wait for the last stone. The children laughed, and the old people laughed with them.

Halvard. Not all Shorelanders were Stonewrights, yet all Stonewrights were Shorelanders. Hold on to that. It’s the first thing, and everything else stands on it. The guild calls a child while it’s still a baby in the cradle, and from any home in the whole land: from fishermen’s huts and merchants’ halls. Once, the old records say, a child was called from a jailer’s house in Carnhold. Being called was a great honour. In all the guild’s records, no mother or father ever said no. The guild kept the land’s faith the same way it kept the land’s walls. A child called to one was called to both.

Rhyna. We Stonewrights believe, as our fathers did, that God makes each pair one before either of them takes a breath. So when the seers go from cradle to cradle, bending low over each one with a lamp, they aren’t making a pair, or picking one. They’re reading what was written before we were born. They don’t choose. The guild calls that joining the Aetherbond. The two people it joins are the Aetherbonded, or just the Bonded. A seer bent over us two like that, and read us. From that night on, we slept in one cradle, taking turns between his mother’s house and mine. And it has been that way for us two ever since. We have never in our lives slept a night under different roofs.

Halvard. When bonded children are old enough to hold a chisel, they are given to the Theoliths. The Theoliths are the old master pairs who teach. Ours taught us two to cut stone, to lay it and to set it. They taught us to pray, and to keep count of the stones in the old writing. They also taught us the vow, and they called it the Covenant of Completion. But they only told children its name, and nothing more. So we two are giving you the name and nothing more. You’ll understand it when it’s your turn to understand it. Our own Theoliths were Idrenna, who sealed Enrella this morning. They’re sitting by the door tonight, and they’ll nod when we two tell it right.

By the door, Idrenna, Idlan and Denna, sat with their two white heads close together, the way very old people sit. They didn’t nod.

Rhyna. When bonded children grow up, they are sealed. The sealing says out loud what the cradle said first. Remember this, because you’ll need it later: the bond is made before the cradle, read in the cradle, and only made whole at the sealing. Then the guild gives the two of them one name made from both of theirs, the way a gateway is made of two posts with a stone laid across the top. With the name comes the mark that was read at their cradle. The guild keeps it secret until that day. A name for the mouth, and a mark for the stone. The two of them hold that mark holier than anything else they own. No hand but theirs may cut it, and no other pair will ever break it. That’s how we two were sealed and named Halyna. So he is my husband and I am his wife. We were one pair long before we were either.

Halvard. The fire is low now, and the ash on the hearth is soft and grey. So watch my finger, all of you, because I’ll only draw it once. This is our mark, the mark of us two, as the seer read it at our cradle and the guild gave it to us at our sealing. On the night of the Rite, we two cut it into the underside of the new capstone of the inner gate, where nobody will see it again unless the wall falls. Look once.

Rhyna. Now I wipe it away with my flat hand, and it’s gone. Ash blows away. A mark is for stone, which lasts.

Halvard. The Bonded live as one, work as one, and pray as one, and they do it all without a word. You’ve seen us two on the wall during the holding. Neither of us calls out to the other, and we don’t need to. The work of a pair isn’t the work of two people. It’s the work of twelve. That’s how the old masters counted it. In the guild, the man of a pair is called Elder and the woman is called Elderess, and they are the same rank. So I’m Elder Halvard and she’s Elderess Rhyna, and neither of us outranks the other. She never laid a stone second.

Rhyna. What one feels, the other feels. If I cut my hand in the south tower, he feels it on the north wall, and he knows which hand. And whatever one thinks, the other has already thought it before it’s half said. Two made into one: that’s the whole bond. The old masters had a saying for it, and you’ll hear it again before these tales are over. Harm to one is harm to both.

Halvard. The Bonded are hardly ever apart, and never far apart. No bond has ever been broken, not once in all the guild’s records, from the slates of the First Days to the records of the last haven. Separation, God did not permit.

The fire had burned down a finger’s width. At the front, Enno and Della sat holding hands, the way they had come up the mountain. The little girl who had asked them her question was asleep against Della’s knee.

Rhyna. So the Bonded die together. We two can say it without fear, because to us it isn’t a sad thing. If one of a pair is killed, the one left behind has no wound and no sickness. But the heart breaks, because it knows. And the body goes still, because its work is done. And they die in peace. When the left hand dies, does not the right one die too? People say the longest anyone ever lived on alone was eight days. The Stonewrights, who keep count, say hours. Many pairs have taken their last breath at the same moment, a mile apart, each one knowing the other was going. And each one went too.

Halvard. Now the last thing, and then bed. Sometimes one baby from a cradle dies young, before the two are ever sealed. Then the other one lives, because the bond wasn’t whole yet. A child like that is called unbonded. The child is given to a bonded household as its third member. By old custom, the unbonded are the ones who hold the lamp at a sealing, the way Seren held it this morning. The unbonded keep just one name their whole life, and they have no mark for stone. In the guild, having only one name is a kind of mourning. But the old masters said that God doesn’t take one without leaving the other a job to do.

Rhyna. Seren, who held the lamp this morning and is writing this down, is our third. She was put in one cradle with Tarnel, a fisherman’s son from Tidesmeet. All the long road, he tied knots in a net while he walked, so he could fish the mountain lakes when we got there. He taught her the knot. On the night on the pass, when the cold killed many of the children and some of the old people, the two of them lay down together under one cloak to keep warm. She woke up next to him. So she lived, and she is one. When she came up the mountain without him, we two took her into our household.

Halvard. “A net is just knots holding hands.” That’s what Tarnel told her, and she binds this Book with his knot. She can’t know the wood. A pair holds a knowing between them, the way two hands hold a stone that one hand can’t lift. But she writes it down more truly than we two could. That’s her loss, and it isn’t small. But listen to what it gives her. She won’t die when someone else dies. She can go places we two can’t, alone. And she can carry this Book on after us. The Bonded have always been the keepers of the hearth and the page. Every writer before her was half of a pair. When a pair died, their book went into the vault after them, and nobody ever told a tale from it again. A book nobody tells from is just ink. Look, her pen hasn’t stopped. This Book has to outlive its tellers. That’s why Seren is the one who writes it.

By the door, Idrenna both nodded. Then Halvard and Rhyna said the last line together, the way they always end it, because it is the oldest saying of the guild:

Birth united them; death could not separate them.

We have laid this tale the way it was laid for us.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

And the Captain pushed his hood back.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

BOOK THREE · THE BOOK OF THE HAVENS

The ten havens, from the first wall of Eldhythe to the twenty months of their burning, as each True Man tells his own city: what we had, and how we walked away from it.

III.1 · Of Their RaisingStone leaf

LAID IN THE FIRST AUTUMN OF THE SIEGE · OF THE DAYS OF THE HAVENS, FROM TWELVE GENERATIONS BEFORE THE BANNER.

Laid by the True Men, each for the haven that bore him, in the order in which the havens were raised; and Kael the Counter, oath-keeper of the True Men, opened it and closed it, and called each haven by its name and epithet, and its man answered with the city’s name and his own. In that autumn Halyna, the two of them, had begun to read the rolls of the havens out of the archive chest, a few leaves each night, in the old archive-hand; and where the rolls held a thing that no True Man could know, I read it out at the hearth.

“Give me the stone. No, I will stand.”

Hear now the names of the havens, and hold them, for they are ours.

In the Days of the Havens, long ere ever a cloak was torn upon this wall, our fathers came down off this mountain and left the Keep to the snow, and went out along the eastern coasts, where the mountains and the high hills go down in long falls to the sea; and there they lived, and there they built. They were builders and not seafarers, and they feared the open water, nor would they go out upon it, but kept to the sheltered shores; and over the mountains they paved great roads like veins, to bind together all the places they had raised.

Ten havens they raised, which the old songs call the ten harbour-cities, and every wall of them was Stonewright work, from the first course to the capstone. From the east tower of this Keep, on a clear morning, you may see that coast yet, the whole long length of it to the haze; and not one hearth upon it sends up smoke.

Then I read from the rolls of the havens:

In the rolls of the guild every haven begins in the one way, as every wall begins with one stone: on the day that its first course was laid, a staff was cut for its work, and the overseer of the work bore that staff among the masons while the walls went up, course upon course, to the capstone. I have read the ten cuttings by the lamp, set down in a hand so small and so patient, that it seems the clerks of the guild were loath to waste the leaf.
