Ten havens there were, and ten staffs, from the staff of Eldhythe, that was cut the first, to the staff of Glasspire, that was cut the last.

Then Kael the Counter laid his hands on the stone again:

I will call each haven by its name, and the man that was born in it will answer, for I do not tell another man’s city. Hear them, from the eldest.

Eldhythe, the Eldest Haven. Eldhythe · Corlen of the harbour.

Eldhythe was the eldest of them, whose harbour burned in a day; and the first masters down off this mountain laid its walls along the water, and its harbour-houses were the richest in all the land, and the warmest of a winter’s night. Fifty years and more I watched, from the stones of its quay, the boats come home upon the evening tide, slow, one behind another, with the last of the light at their backs upon the water. And when the first boat of ours comes into that harbour again, I will steer it in from the sea.

Then Corlen sat down, and wet a finger, and held it up, as he does on the wall before every holding, though there is no wind in this hall; and when the young men at the back laughed, he said to them, as he says to his guns, “Wait. The sea has not finished.”

Tidesmeet, where the river meets the tide. Tidesmeet · Tamm of the three spans.

On both banks of the river at once stood Tidesmeet, where the tide came up out of the sea to meet the river, and three great spans of stone bound the city together across the water, the upper span and the middle and the lower; and the middle was the greatest of the three. I carry a stone of each of them in my pack, and every night by my blanket I set the three out in their order, and look upon them a while, and lay them up again in the pack. One span is a plank. Three is a city.

Fenholm, the town that was always sinking. Fenholm · Marl the fen-born.

Fenholm stood upon pilings in the fen, and went down a hand’s breadth in every lifetime, and every generation of us laid a new course on the old and laughed at the sinking, for a fen-man that cannot laugh at it had best go and live upon a hill. My grandmother, old Ebba of Fenholm, laid the top course of our house with her own two hands, and a stone of that course rides here in my pack. She is there by the fire, nodding. When we go home, I am to lay her stone on the old course.

Holtward, the keep under the canopy. Holtward · Aske of the canopy.

Deep in the woods of the south lay Holtward, roofed so close among the trees that a traveller might pass beneath it and never see a wall; and every autumn we swept the leaves from the wall-walk with our hands, for no man of Holtward would cut a bough to spare himself the sweeping. On this mountain there is no wood under my hand but the wreck that the grey casts upon the shingle, and I lay my hand on every plank of it ere it burns. These were trees once.

Carnhold, the stronghold of the cairns. Carnhold · Garvel of the smithies.

Carnhold was hewn into the mountain among the burial-cairns of the first quarrymen, and all the day long it was loud with its smithies, so that a man at the far end of the town could tell, by the ring of its hammer, which forge was at work. Every gun upon this wall I have listened to ere ever it spoke, for a gun that rings true speaks true. Yet at an anvil of Carnhold, one day, I mean to make a thing that is not a gun, and hear it ring.

Emberhythe, the forge on the burning shore. Emberhythe · Harl of the burning shore.

Emberhythe stood where the mountain smokes into the sea, and the ground there is warm underfoot in winter, so that our children ran barefoot in the snow. My shore I will have again; and the grey I will burn.

Sandreach, the citadel carved in the canyon wall. Sandreach · Ulden of the galleries.

Sandreach had no harbour. We called it a haven all the same, for it sheltered us. It was not built so much as dug, gallery below gallery, down into the red rock of the canyon wall; and every night of my life, till the Fall, I slept under red stone, with the whole of the mountain for my roof.

Rimewatch, the watch on the frozen mere. Rimewatch · Hesk of the mere.

All the winter long the great hall of Rimewatch stood out upon the frozen mere, and the whole town feasted under its roof through the long nights, and was warm; and there were lamps in its windows through the long dark, and the fishers far out upon the ice steered home by them when the snow came down. Every dusk someone went the length of that hall with a taper, from window to window, and woke the lamps against the coming of the dark. My mother kept those lamps.

Highreach, the tower of the floating stones. Highreach · Lanner of the crags.

Highreach stood upon crags so high that the masons lifted every course of it on cranes, and while the stones went up they seemed to hang upon the wind, as a hawk hangs over a field. On those crags a boy can whistle up a wind ere ever he can walk. I mean to climb them again, and stand upon the highest, and whistle for the wind.

And Glasspire, the last and proudest. Glasspire · Pellow of the spire.

Seven generations of Stonewrights raised Glasspire, and it caught the sun like a blade of ice, the last and proudest of all our works; and at the top of the spire stood the great glass, that a man might see from a day’s sail out, throwing the sun far into the grey. My father ground it.

Then Pellow held his glass up to the fire, as he holds it up to everything, and turned it in the light, and said to the children at the front, “Look close.”

Then Kael the Counter laid his hands on the stone again:

Ten cities, ten harbours, ten kindreds who called themselves one nation.

Twelve of us answered the Captain on the day of the banner, and ten of the twelve have answered you tonight, each for the haven that bore him; and Stannard of the ridge sits by, nor says a word, for the ridge was never a haven.

I have no haven. The Captain found me on a road.

This I lay as it was laid for me.

And the hearth said: We remember.

Old Ebba of Fenholm began it, a breath before the rest.

LAID IN THE FIRST AUTUMN OF THE SIEGE · ABOUT THE DAYS OF THE HAVENS, STARTING TWELVE GENERATIONS BEFORE THE BANNER.

Told by the True Men, each one for the haven he was born in, in the order the havens were built. Kael the Counter, oath-keeper of the True Men, opened it and closed it. He called out each haven by its name and title, and its man answered with the city’s name and his own. That autumn, Halyna, the two of them, had started reading the havens’ old records from the archive chest, a few pages a night, in the old archive writing. Where the records held something no True Man could know, I read it out at the hearth.

“Give me the stone. No. I’ll stand.”

Here are the names of the havens. Hold on to them. They belong to us.

In the Days of the Havens, long before any cloak was torn on this wall, our ancestors came down off this mountain. They left the Keep to the snow and spread out along the eastern coasts, where the mountains and high hills drop down in long slopes to the sea. That’s where they lived, and that’s where they built. They were builders, not sailors. They were afraid of the open water and wouldn’t go out on it. They stayed close to the sheltered shores. Over the mountains they paved great roads, like veins, to tie together all the places they had built.

They built ten havens. The old songs call them the ten harbour-cities. Every wall in them was Stonewright work, from the first row to the capstone. From the east tower of this Keep, on a clear morning, you can still see that coast, the whole long stretch of it, out to the haze. Not one chimney on it is smoking.

Then I read from the havens’ records:

In the guild’s records, every haven starts the same way, just as every wall starts with one stone. On the day its first row was laid, a staff was cut for the work. The overseer carried that staff among the masons while the walls went up, row after row, all the way to the capstone. I have read all ten of those cuttings by lamplight. They are written in handwriting so small and patient that the guild’s clerks must have hated to waste a page. There were ten havens and ten staffs, from the staff of Eldhythe, cut first, to the staff of Glasspire, cut last.

Then Kael the Counter put his hands on the stone again:

I’ll call out each haven by its name, and the man who was born there will answer. I don’t tell another man’s city. Listen to them, starting with the oldest.

Eldhythe, the Eldest Haven. Eldhythe · Corlen of the harbour.

Eldhythe was the oldest of them. Its harbour burned in a single day. The first masters to come down off this mountain built its walls along the water. Its harbour inns were the richest in the whole land, and the warmest on a winter night. For more than fifty years I stood on the stones of its dock and watched the boats come home on the evening tide, slowly, one after another, with the last light behind them on the water. When our first boat sails into that harbour again, I’ll be the one steering it in from the sea.

Then Corlen sat down. He licked a finger and held it up, the way he does on the wall before every holding, even though there’s no wind in this hall. When the young men at the back laughed, he told them what he tells his guns: “Wait. The sea isn’t finished.”

Tidesmeet, where the river meets the tide. Tidesmeet · Tamm of the three spans.

Tidesmeet stood on both banks of the river, where the tide came up from the sea to meet it. Three great stone bridges held the city together across the water: the upper bridge, the middle and the lower. The middle one was the biggest. I carry a stone from each of them in my pack. Every night, next to my blanket, I lay the three of them out in order, look at them for a while, and pack them away again. One bridge is just a plank. Three bridges make a city.

Fenholm, the town that was always sinking. Fenholm · Marl the fen-born.

Fenholm stood on wooden posts in the marsh. It sank about a hand’s width every lifetime. Every generation of us laid a new row of stone on top of the old one and laughed about the sinking. A marsh man who can’t laugh about it should go live on a hill. My grandmother, old Ebba of Fenholm, laid the top row of our house with her own two hands. A stone from that row is riding here in my pack. She’s over there by the fire, nodding. When we go home, I’m going to lay her stone back on the old row.

Holtward, the keep under the canopy. Holtward · Aske of the canopy.

Holtward was deep in the southern woods. It was roofed so tightly with trees that a traveller could walk right under it and never see a wall. Every autumn we swept the leaves off the walkway with our hands. No man of Holtward would cut a branch just to save himself the sweeping. On this mountain, the only wood under my hand is the wreckage the grey throws up on the beach. I put my hand on every plank of it before it’s burned. These were trees once.

Carnhold, the stronghold of the cairns. Carnhold · Garvel of the smithies.

Carnhold was cut into the mountain among the burial mounds of the first quarrymen. All day long it rang with its blacksmiths. A man at the far end of town could tell which forge was working just by the sound of its hammer. I’ve listened to every gun on this wall before it ever fired, because a gun that rings true shoots true. But someday, at an anvil in Carnhold, I’m going to make something that isn’t a gun, and listen to it ring.

Emberhythe, the forge on the burning shore. Emberhythe · Harl of the burning shore.

Emberhythe stood where the mountain smokes down into the sea. The ground there is warm even in winter, so our children ran barefoot in the snow. I’ll have my shore back. And I’ll burn the grey.

Sandreach, the citadel carved in the canyon wall. Sandreach · Ulden of the galleries.

Sandreach had no harbour. We still called it a haven, because it sheltered us. It wasn’t built so much as dug: tunnel below tunnel, down into the red rock of the canyon wall. Every night of my life until the Fall, I slept under red stone, with the whole mountain for a roof.

Rimewatch, the watch on the frozen mere. Rimewatch · Hesk of the mere.

All winter long, the great hall of Rimewatch stood out on the frozen lake. The whole town feasted under its roof through the long nights and stayed warm. There were lamps in its windows all through the dark months. The fishermen far out on the ice steered home by them when the snow came down. Every evening someone walked the length of that hall with a candle, from window to window, lighting the lamps before the dark came. My mother kept those lamps.

Highreach, the tower of the floating stones. Highreach · Lanner of the crags.

Highreach stood on cliffs so high that the masons had to lift every row of stone with cranes. As the stones went up, they seemed to hang on the wind, like a hawk hanging over a field. On those cliffs, a boy can whistle up a wind before he can walk. I’m going to climb them again, stand on the highest one, and whistle for the wind.

And Glasspire, the last and proudest. Glasspire · Pellow of the spire.

Seven generations of Stonewrights built Glasspire. It caught the sun like a blade of ice. It was the last and proudest of everything we made. At the top of the spire stood the great glass. You could see it from a day’s sail out at sea, throwing sunlight far into the grey. My father ground it.

Then Pellow held his glass up to the fire, the way he holds it up to everything, and turned it in the light. He told the children at the front, “Look closely.”

Then Kael the Counter put his hands on the stone again:

Ten cities. Ten harbours. Ten peoples who called themselves one nation.

Twelve of us answered the Captain on the day of the banner. Ten of the twelve have answered you tonight, each for the haven he was born in. Stannard of the ridge is sitting with us and hasn’t said a word, because the ridge was never a haven.

I have no haven. The Captain found me on a road.

I have laid this tale the way it was laid for me.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

Old Ebba of Fenholm started it, a moment before the rest.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

III.2 · Of Their FallStone leaf

LAID DEEP IN THE SECOND WINTER, IN THE SECOND TIDE · OF THE FALL, THE TWENTY MONTHS BEFORE THE BANNER, AND OF THE ROAD.

Laid by the True Men again, each for the fall of his own city, in the order in which the havens fell; and Kael the Counter, oath-keeper of the True Men, called them, and told the Road, and I spoke for the guild. It was laid deep in the Second Tide, on a night of hard frost, when the guns were cooling and the mortar had not yet set.

“Give me the stone. No, I will stand.”

Hear now how the havens were lost, and do not look away, for we did not look away in the telling.

In the last spring of the peace, twenty months ere the banner, the havens had no navy and no fleet of war, for since the first of them was raised, no enemy had come against the Shorelands out of the sea; and their walls were built for the high days and the weather, and not to turn a shot. The swallows nested in the arches of the gates, and came back to them every spring, and no man of the havens ever broke a nest. Their gates were wide, and stood open at night.

Then out of the grey, in that same spring and without warning, came the first dark sails; and no haven sent to another for help, for none had any help to send. Now hear the havens as they fell, and each man will answer for his own city, as he answered for its raising.

Eldhythe, the Eldest Haven. Eldhythe · Corlen of the harbour.

On the first morning of that spring, I was down upon the quay of Eldhythe among the boats, as I was on every day of my life, when in at the harbour mouth, out of the grey, came the dark sails upon the flood, and no man on the quay had ever seen their like. From the first of the dawn till the last of the light the harbour burned, the boats first where they lay at their moorings, and then the harbour-houses one after another along the water, where the ship-masters of all the coast had sat at the fire; and the wind came round at evening and laid the smoke across the city, so that the city could not see its harbour go. Three months the city held behind it; and on the last evening I went down to the water’s edge, and the tide was coming in over the ash, slow, as it always came, and I stood upon the quay and waited, and the sea had not finished.

Tidesmeet, where the river meets the tide. Tidesmeet · Tamm of the three spans.

In the late summer of that year Tidesmeet fell, and it fell in three days; and on the third day, we that were left stood upon the high road above the river, and watched its three spans go down into the water one after another, and heard them go. The upper went, and then the lower, and the middle stood the longest, and we waited for it, and it went. That night I climbed down to the river in the dark, and took a stone from each of the three.

Fenholm, the town that was always sinking. Fenholm · Marl the fen-born.

In the first days of the autumn Fenholm went under, all of it at once and in a single night, the pilings and the courses and the houses upon them, as if the fen had only been waiting. A town is only the last course on the old, we used to say in Fenholm, and laugh at the sinking; and that night no one laughed. My grandmother carried her course-stone out upon her own back, nor would she give it up to me till the fen had closed over the last course.

Holtward, the keep under the canopy. Holtward · Aske of the canopy.

In the middle of that autumn Holtward burned in its trees, the keep and the canopy together, and the leaves came down burning upon the wall-walk, where every autumn we had swept them with our hands. On the last morning I laid my hand upon the last tree standing, and its bark was as warm as a living hand.

Carnhold, the stronghold of the cairns. Carnhold · Garvel of the smithies.

At the end of that autumn Carnhold burned in its smithies, and its forges, that had not once gone cold in all my life, took fire from without; and I stood in the street of the smiths and heard the hammers stop, one forge after another, down the whole length of the town. The last anvil rang a while alone. Then it did not ring.

Emberhythe, the forge on the burning shore. Emberhythe · Harl of the burning shore.

Through all that winter and into the spring Emberhythe held, five months upon its burning shore, and the whole column wintered with us there and slept warm upon the bare ground. When it fell, a mason of ours carried a charred plank out in his pack. I told him to burn it.

Sandreach, the citadel carved in the canyon wall. Sandreach · Ulden of the galleries.

In the spring of the year before the banner the sails came against Sandreach, and all that spring and into the heat of the summer it was taken from us gallery by gallery, from the highest to the lowest. Each night I lay with my ear to the floor, and heard the gallery above me go, ere any runner could bring me the word. At the last we came out in the dark at the foot of the canyon, and every night since then I have slept upon the open ground.

Rimewatch, the watch on the frozen mere. Rimewatch · Hesk of the mere.

In the last autumn, on the first ice of the mere, Rimewatch was taken, and the great hall burned out upon the ice with its lamps yet lit in every window, so that it burned as it had always shone, a light upon the frozen water. Into the smoke I went, and took my mother’s lamp down from its window, and the ice spoke under me the whole way back across the mere, and held. I carried the lamp out lit. Every dusk since, I have lit it upon the north wall of this Keep, and the night-watch steers by its light. Someone has to keep a light.

Highreach, the tower of the floating stones. Highreach · Lanner of the crags.

Late in that same autumn the sails came to Highreach upon its crags; but the wind comes before the sails do, and a whole day ahead of them I smelled the grey upon it, and there was nothing to be done with that day but to go. Down the crag-stairs we came in the dark, with the children upon our backs, and behind us the cranes stood burning upon the crags.

And Glasspire, the last and proudest. Glasspire · Pellow of the spire.

Then came the first snows, and in a single night Glasspire went down into its own cellars, the spire and the great glass and the work of seven generations together; and the dark beneath it lies open still. Some who fled the spire say that a shadow stood off in the fog that night, beyond the harbour, greater than any hull. On the last stair of the spire I stood, with the great glass coming down about me in pieces and the snow coming down with it, and I looked close. I saw nothing.

Pellow sat down with his glass in his lap, and did not hold it up.

Then Kael the Counter laid his hands on the stone again:

In all the twenty months of the Fall, there came no envoy that any man could understand, nor herald, nor any word at all, but only a knocking out of the grey like thunder, on and on, that the soldiers on this wall call the drums. And no one stood upon the decks. We looked for a captain on those ships, and for a crew, and for one man at one rope, and we saw no one. I swear to that.

So we called it the ghost fleet, and its senders, whom no man had seen, the Mystaeri, after the mist they came out of; for a man must call his death by some name. The sails that took the havens were plainer than the sails that come against us now. They came each alone, and they needed no more, for there was nothing to stop them.

Then I spoke for the guild:

“Hold the stone, Seren. I tell myself so every time.”

On the last day of every haven, when its walls were going down and there was no more that a mason’s hands could do for them, the guild went out by the landward gate with their chisels in their packs and naught beside. Last of all went the masters, when they had cleaned their trowels, as they cleaned them at the end of every day’s work, and wrapped them in sacking against the road. The Stonewrights did not fight. They simply walked; and I was among them.

As a woman that must leave the house her mothers kept, and go out upon a road that will not bring her back to it, rakes the fire together on the hearth ere she goes, and banks it over with the ash, as she has banked it every night, that it may keep till the morning: so in every haven the masters of the guild leaned its staff against the falling wall, and left it with its work. A staff does not leave its work.

Ten staffs there were, one to every haven, and not one of them came up the mountain. The one staff left to us is the first of all, that lay in the black chest, at the foot of the shaft, from the leaving of this Keep till Halvard brought it up out of the dark; and it leans now in the corner of the gatehouse, where the Captain set it, under its dust. Go and look at the dust on it.

Then Kael the Counter laid his hands on the stone again:

At the first snows, a month ere the banner, when Glasspire was gone, the column turned up the mountain road; and it had gathered as it came, haven by haven and a city at a time, so that behind it at the last there was no haven left. A month we were in the passes. On the night of the pass, the coldest night of all the road, many of the children died, and some of the old.

In the morning the snow was falling yet, and we rose up out of it, as many of us as could rise, and walked. At the foot of the passes I counted us, and I counted us at the top. I will not tell you the difference.

This I lay as it was laid for me.

And the hearth said: We remember.

Added in Seren’s hand: Corlen of the harbour laid Eldhythe’s fall in the Second Tide, and it was the last tale he laid. Voss holds his battery now.

Added in Seren’s hand: Of Glasspire’s night the wood has spoken since, in the year of the homecomings; and the tales do not agree. I have kept both.

LAID DEEP IN THE SECOND WINTER, DURING THE SECOND TIDE · ABOUT THE FALL, THE TWENTY MONTHS BEFORE THE BANNER, AND ABOUT THE ROAD.

Told by the True Men again, each one for the fall of his own city, in the order the havens fell. Kael the Counter, oath-keeper of the True Men, called on them and told about the Road, and I spoke for the guild. It was laid deep in the Second Tide, on a night of hard frost, while the guns were cooling and the mortar hadn’t set yet.

“Give me the stone. No. I’ll stand.”

This is how the havens were lost. Don’t look away. We didn’t look away when we told it.

In the last spring of peace, twenty months before the banner, the havens had no navy and no warships. Since the first haven was built, no enemy had ever come at the Shorelands from the sea. Their walls were built for holidays and for the weather, not to stop cannon shot. Swallows nested in the arches of the gates and came back every spring, and no man of the havens ever broke a nest. The gates were wide, and they stood open at night.

Then, that same spring, with no warning, the first dark sails came out of the grey. No haven sent to another for help, because none of them had any help to send. Now here are the havens as they fell. Each man will answer for his own city, the same way he answered for how it was built.

Eldhythe, the Eldest Haven. Eldhythe · Corlen of the harbour.

On the first morning of that spring, I was down on the dock at Eldhythe among the boats, like every other day of my life. Then, through the harbour mouth, out of the grey, the dark sails came in on the rising tide. No man on the dock had ever seen anything like them. From dawn until the last light, the harbour burned. First the boats, where they were tied up. Then the harbour inns, one after another along the water, where the ship-masters of the whole coast used to sit by the fire. In the evening the wind changed and blew the smoke across the city, so the city couldn’t watch its harbour die. The city held out behind it for three months. On the last evening, I went down to the water’s edge. The tide was coming in over the ash, slowly, the way it always came. I stood on the dock and waited. The sea wasn’t finished.

Tidesmeet, where the river meets the tide. Tidesmeet · Tamm of the three spans.

Tidesmeet fell in late summer that year. It fell in three days. On the third day, those of us who were left stood on the high road above the river and watched its three bridges fall into the water one after another. We heard them go. The upper one went, then the lower one. The middle one stood the longest. We waited for it. Then it went. That night I climbed down to the river in the dark and took a stone from each of the three.

Fenholm, the town that was always sinking. Fenholm · Marl the fen-born.

In the first days of autumn, Fenholm went under. All of it, all at once, in a single night: the posts, the rows of stone, and the houses on top of them, as if the marsh had just been waiting. “A town is just the newest row on top of the old one,” we used to say in Fenholm, and laugh at the sinking. That night nobody laughed. My grandmother carried her stone out on her own back. She wouldn’t give it to me until the marsh had closed over the last row.

Holtward, the keep under the canopy. Holtward · Aske of the canopy.

In the middle of that autumn, Holtward burned in its trees, the keep and the forest roof together. The leaves came down burning onto the walkway where we used to sweep them with our hands every autumn. On the last morning, I put my hand on the last tree still standing. Its bark was as warm as a living hand.

Carnhold, the stronghold of the cairns. Carnhold · Garvel of the smithies.

At the end of that autumn, Carnhold burned in its blacksmiths’ shops. Its forges, which hadn’t gone cold once in my whole life, caught fire from outside. I stood in the street of the smiths and heard the hammers stop, one forge after another, all down the length of the town. The last anvil rang for a while on its own. Then it stopped.

Emberhythe, the forge on the burning shore. Emberhythe · Harl of the burning shore.

Emberhythe held out all that winter and into the spring. Five months on its burning shore. The whole column spent the winter with us there and slept warm on the bare ground. When it fell, one of our masons carried a burnt plank out in his pack. I told him to burn it.

Sandreach, the citadel carved in the canyon wall. Sandreach · Ulden of the galleries.

In the spring of the year before the banner, the sails came against Sandreach. All that spring and into the heat of summer, it was taken from us tunnel by tunnel, from the highest to the lowest. Every night I lay with my ear to the floor and heard the tunnel above me fall, before any runner could bring me the news. In the end we came out in the dark at the bottom of the canyon. Every night since then, I’ve slept on open ground.

Rimewatch, the watch on the frozen mere. Rimewatch · Hesk of the mere.

In the last autumn, on the first ice of the lake, Rimewatch was taken. The great hall burned out on the ice with its lamps still lit in every window. So it burned the same way it had always shone: a light on the frozen water. I went into the smoke and took my mother’s lamp down from its window. The ice creaked under me the whole way back across the lake, but it held. I carried the lamp out still lit. Every evening since then, I’ve lit it on the north wall of this Keep, and the night watch steers by it. Someone has to keep a light.

Highreach, the tower of the floating stones. Highreach · Lanner of the crags.

Late in that same autumn, the sails came to Highreach on its cliffs. But the wind comes before the sails do. A whole day ahead of them, I smelled the grey on it. There was nothing to do with that day except leave. We came down the cliff stairs in the dark with the children on our backs. Behind us, the cranes stood burning on the cliffs.

And Glasspire, the last and proudest. Glasspire · Pellow of the spire.

Then the first snow came. In a single night, Glasspire fell down into its own cellars: the spire, the great glass and the work of seven generations, all together. The dark hole under it is still open. Some people who escaped from the spire say a shadow stood out in the fog that night, past the harbour, bigger than any ship. I stood on the last stair of the spire, with the great glass falling around me in pieces and the snow falling with it, and I looked closely. I saw nothing.

Pellow sat down with his glass in his lap. He didn’t hold it up.

Then Kael the Counter put his hands on the stone again:

In all the twenty months of the Fall, no messenger came that anyone could understand. No herald, no word of any kind. There was only a knocking out of the grey like thunder, on and on. The soldiers on this wall call it the drums. And nobody stood on the decks. We looked for a captain on those ships, for a crew, for one man on one rope. We saw no one. I swear to that.

So we called it the ghost fleet. We called the ones who sent it, whom nobody had ever seen, the Mystaeri, after the mist they came out of. A man has to call his death something. The sails that took the havens were simpler than the sails that attack us now. They came one at a time, and they didn’t need more, because nothing could stop them.

Then I spoke for the guild:

“Hold the stone, Seren. I tell myself that every time.”

Every haven had a last day. Its walls were falling, and a mason’s hands couldn’t do anything more for them. On that day, the guild walked out through the gate on the land side, with chisels in their packs and nothing else. The masters went last. First they cleaned their trowels, the way they cleaned them at the end of every work day, and wrapped them in sacking for the road. The Stonewrights didn’t fight. They simply walked. I was one of them.

Think of a woman who has to leave the house her mothers kept, and set out on a road that will never bring her back. Before she goes, she rakes the fire together on the hearth and covers it with ash, the way she has every night, so it will keep until morning. That’s what the guild’s masters did in every haven. They leaned its staff against the falling wall and left it with its work. A staff doesn’t leave its work.

There were ten staffs, one for every haven. Not one of them came up the mountain. The only staff left to us is the very first one. It lay in the black chest at the bottom of the shaft from the day this Keep was left until Halvard brought it up out of the dark. It’s leaning now in the corner of the gatehouse, where the Captain put it, under its dust. Go and look at the dust on it.

Then Kael the Counter put his hands on the stone again:

At the first snow, a month before the banner, after Glasspire was gone, the column turned up the mountain road. It had grown as it went, haven by haven, one city at a time, until at the end there was no haven left behind it. We were in the passes for a month. On the night on the pass, the coldest night of the whole road, many of the children died, and some of the old people.

In the morning the snow was still falling. We got up out of it, as many of us as could get up, and walked. I counted us at the bottom of the passes, and I counted us at the top. I won’t tell you the difference.

I have laid this tale the way it was laid for me.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

Added in Seren’s handwriting: Corlen of the harbour told the fall of Eldhythe during the Second Tide. It was the last tale he ever laid. Voss commands his battery now.

Added in Seren’s handwriting: The wood has spoken about the night Glasspire fell since then, in the year of the homecomings. The tales don’t agree. I have kept both.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

BOOK FOUR · THE BOOK OF THE WOUND

In the Harvest, five generations to three before the banner, the black pillars were felled, their pleas cut in stone went unread, a guest was killed kneeling at a warden’s door, and a burning boat went home in fury; told from both sides of the grey.

IV.1 · Of the Felling of the Black PillarsStone leaf

LAID IN THE SECOND SPRING, IN THE SECOND TIDE · OF THE HARVEST, FIVE GENERATIONS TO THREE BEFORE THE BANNER; OF THE FORGETTING; AND OF THE LATE TURNING.

Laid by Seren Two-Inks, out of the rolls of the guild, which came up the mountain in the archive chest; and in it the Harvest rolls lay near the bottom, under the rolls of the later havens, so that we came to them last. Where the rolls are stained I have said so, and I have not mended them with guesses. The wood’s leaf faces it.

“Hold the stone, Seren. I tell myself so every time.”

Night by night through the first year, Halyna, he and she by turns, read this to me out of the rolls of the guild in the old archive-hand, and I copied it after them by the lamp, so fast that my letters leaned like reeds in a wind; and this the two of them bade me say first, ere ever I said the rest. We did not live it. Our fathers’ fathers lived it, and wrote it down, and did not read what they had written.

Hear now what the rolls keep of the Harvest, of the felling and the forgetting, and hold it, for it is ours.

In the oldest rolls of all, from the Days of the Havens, ere ever an axe went out to the edge, the grey is written down as a blackish veil upon the rim of the sea; and after that it is written only as a question, asked in the night-watches of the havens when the lamps burned low and the tide stood at the turn, and never answered: what the grey was, and what it kept, and who it was that breathed it. Long were the masters dead who asked it. The answer, when it came, was not written in our hand.

Then came the high summer of the havens’ pride, five generations before the banner, when the havens were rich and their roads were heavy with carts, and the counsel of the Stonewrights had dwindled to a voice out of humbler days, a thing that grandmothers said by the fire and children forgot ere morning. In those years the ships of the coast went farther than ever their fathers had dared, along the edge of the grey; and there, at its outermost hem, with the surf white upon their roots and the grey wound close about them to the crown, they found the black pillars standing in their ranks, tall and dark of trunk, and straight as a plumb-line.

The ship-masters named them the Mystholders, for they seemed to hold the grey up as posts hold up a roof. Their timber was harder than oak, lighter than iron, proof against rot.

For two generations the Trade Council sent out the axes; and the count of it the rolls keep, cargo by cargo, in the flat hand of clerks whom nothing moved. Hear it as I copied it, for I copied every line:

Black wood, for the buttresses of strongholds, and for the beams of bridges.

Black wood, for the three spans at Tidesmeet, where the river meets the tide.

Black wood, for the pilings of Fenholm, the town that was always sinking.

Black wood, for the roof-trees of Holtward, the keep under the canopy.

Black wood, for the cranes of Highreach, the tower of the floating stones.

Black wood, for the shoring of the galleries of Sandreach, the citadel carved in the canyon wall.

Black wood, for the roof of the great hall on the ice at Rimewatch, the watch on the frozen mere.

Black wood, burned slow in the forges of Emberhythe, the forge on the burning shore, into the black dust that makes iron hot.

And at Carnhold, the stronghold of the cairns, in the smithies under the cairns, the iron of the axes was beaten out, and went back to the edge for more.

Then the things that needed nothing of the kind:

Black wood, to floor the dancing-hall of a lord of Eldhythe.

Black wood, for the trade-tables of the harbour-houses, and for their dice-boards.

[Here the roll is stained, and three lines are lost.]

Last of all, a merchant of Tidesmeet had one pillar hewn into a single bed.

He slept in it, and was proud.

Halvard turned the leaf, and of the two of them neither said a word.

All the while, beside that count, the rolls keep a stranger one, which no man of the Council read. Where a pillar was felled, the stump put up new growth, straight and black; and in a few summers there stood a young pillar in the old one’s room, with the grey close about it again; and the axemen went back for the young growth, for it was light and straight and made good poles, and the guild bought them for scaffold. They cut it, and it grew, and they cut it again, and it grew again, and they cut it again.

Then, some leaves on, at the foot of a cargo-list where the leaf was all but full, below the count of poles, a clerk has written one line more, in a hand so small that twice I bent the lamp to it ere I could read it:

All along the edge, the stumps put up nothing.

No one wrote down why, and no one asked.

Season by season the grey thinned, like a dying enchantment, and the ship-masters came home and said that it stood farther off than it had stood in their fathers’ day; and the Council named it fortune. I have said that word over three times, fortune, as I say every word ere I trust it to the ink; and it will not hold, but gives under the tongue as mortar gives that has gone to sand.

In the middle years of the Harvest the Stonewrights warned the Council; and it is in the rolls, and it is in our own hand:

“These trees grow in strange soil. The carvings we find beside them bear a tongue we cannot read. Where wisdom is absent, let restraint be our guide.”

But trade sat in the high seat by then, and the Stonewrights were reckoned relics of a poorer age; and the warning was read aloud once in the Council hall at Eldhythe, the Eldest Haven, and laid upon a shelf.

For there were carvings. All through the Harvest, at the feet of the black pillars, the axemen found stones set upright; and cut upon them were letters fine and curling, as beautiful as frost on a window, that the rolls say no man could read. Mute and beautiful, the stones came down to the cities in the holds among the timber. Stones like them lie in the chest I carried up the mountain, and Halyna have laid their four hands upon them, and been given nothing. Stone does not translate.

Some were locked in the archives of the guild, and it was we who turned the key; and others were sold as curiosities and hung upon the walls of the harbour-houses, reduced to ornament, and men drank beneath them and did their reckoning. So the Stonewrights warned, and then laid their own warning away in the dark, with the stones they could not read. That is our share of it. We will not lay it all on the Council.

Among the cargo counts of one late summer, three generations before the banner, a clerk has copied a letter, which came in from the outermost outpost at the end of the land; and between a count of poles and a count of oars he has set it down, in the same hand and with the same care, as one more thing come in from the edge. I give it as it stands:

Unknown intruder. Hostile intent. Neutralized. Vessel burned and released.

At its foot is a name, cut out: ▒▒▒▒.

On the next leaf, in that same season, two timber-boats are struck off a cargo-list, and beside them, in the same hand: lost at the edge. I copied the letter as I copied everything; and what I held, I did not know.

Three generations before the banner the Harvest ended, not by wisdom but by the fickleness of men: for the pillars near the coast grew scarce, and the voyages grew long, and cheaper timber came down out of the northern hills; and after the year of the burning boat, no crew would row within sight of the grey, nor do the rolls say why. So the axes stopped. No one decided it.

Then for three generations came the forgetting, and the Shorelanders of the final days remembered nothing at all. Upon the walls of the harbour-houses the stones hung on, grey above the ale and the dice, and the children grew up beneath them and asked what they said, and were told that they said nothing; and some of you who sit at this fire were those children.

Late, very late, the wheel of pride turned. The temples of the havens were mended, and the lamps in them lit again, and men climbed the worn steps of the guild-halls and asked the counsel of the Stonewrights, and it was given.

As when the men of a harbour go down at last, in the calm at the year’s end, to mend the sea-wall that their fathers let fall, and point every joint of it, and stand back upon the quay with their hands on their hips, glad of the work, while out past the headland, where none of them is looking, the weather has already turned: so the havens mended their temples, and were glad. But humility came too late.

The first dark sails were already on the sea.

This I lay as it was laid for me.

And the hearth said: We remember.

Added in Seren’s hand: What the rolls call carvings, the wood has since told us were pleas: holy pleas, reduced to ornament.

Added in Seren’s hand: The makers of those stones cut our own words among their letters by the sound, as near as their letters would go; the sounds were close, and the symbols wrong.

LAID IN THE SECOND SPRING, DURING THE SECOND TIDE · ABOUT THE HARVEST, FROM FIVE TO THREE GENERATIONS BEFORE THE BANNER; ABOUT THE FORGETTING; AND ABOUT THE CHANGE OF HEART THAT CAME TOO LATE.

Told by Seren Two-Inks, from the guild’s records, which came up the mountain in the archive chest. The Harvest records were near the bottom, under the records of the later havens, so we got to them last. Where the records are stained, I have said so, and I haven’t filled in the gaps with guesses. The wood’s page faces this one.

“Hold the stone, Seren. I tell myself that every time.”

Night after night through the first year, Halyna, taking turns, read this to me from the guild’s records in the old archive writing. I copied it down after them by lamplight, so fast that my letters leaned over like reeds in the wind. The two of them told me to say this first, before anything else. We didn’t live through it. Our great-grandfathers did. They wrote it down, and then they never read what they had written.

This is what the records say about the Harvest: the cutting, and the forgetting. Hold on to it. It belongs to us.

In the very oldest records, from the Days of the Havens, before any axe went out to the edge, the grey is described as a blackish veil along the rim of the sea. After that it only shows up as a question. People asked it during the night watches in the havens, when the lamps burned low and the tide was turning, and no one ever answered it. What was the grey? What was it hiding? Who was breathing it? The masters who asked that question were long dead. When the answer finally came, it wasn’t written in our handwriting.

Then came the high summer of the havens’ pride, five generations before the banner. The havens were rich, and their roads were crowded with carts. The advice of the Stonewrights had faded into a voice from simpler times, something grandmothers said by the fire and children forgot by morning. In those years, the ships of the coast went further than their fathers had ever dared, along the edge of the grey. There, at its very outer edge, with white surf at their roots and the grey wrapped tight around them up to the top, they found the black pillars. They stood in rows, tall, with dark trunks, as straight as a plumb line.

The ship-masters named them the Mystholders, because they seemed to hold the grey up the way posts hold up a roof. Their wood was harder than oak, lighter than iron, and it never rotted.

For two generations, the Trade Council sent out the axes. The records keep count of it, cargo by cargo, in the flat handwriting of clerks who felt nothing. Here it is as I copied it. I copied every line:

Black wood, for the supports of strongholds, and for the beams of bridges.

Black wood, for the three bridges at Tidesmeet, where the river meets the tide.

Black wood, for the posts under Fenholm, the town that was always sinking.

Black wood, for the roof beams of Holtward, the keep under the canopy.

Black wood, for the cranes of Highreach, the tower of the floating stones.

Black wood, to hold up the tunnels of Sandreach, the citadel carved in the canyon wall.

Black wood, for the roof of the great hall on the ice at Rimewatch, the watch on the frozen mere.

Black wood, burned slowly in the forges of Emberhythe, the forge on the burning shore, into the black dust that makes iron hot.

And at Carnhold, the stronghold of the cairns, in the blacksmiths’ shops under the burial mounds, the iron for the axes was hammered out, and sent back to the edge for more.

Then came the things that didn’t need it at all:

Black wood, for the floor of a dance hall belonging to a lord of Eldhythe.

Black wood, for the trading tables in the harbour inns, and for their dice boards.

[Here the record is stained, and three lines are lost.]

Last of all, a merchant from Tidesmeet had one whole pillar carved into a single bed.

He slept in it, and he was proud.

Halvard turned the page. Neither of them said a word.

All along, next to that count, the records keep a stranger one that nobody on the Council ever read. Wherever a pillar was cut down, the stump sent up new growth, straight and black. In a few summers, a young pillar stood where the old one had been, with the grey wrapped close around it again. The axemen went back for the young ones too, because they were light and straight and made good poles. The guild bought them for scaffolding. They cut it, and it grew back. They cut it again, and it grew back again. And they cut it again.

Then, a few pages later, at the bottom of a cargo list where the page was almost full, under the count of poles, a clerk wrote one more line. The writing was so small that I had to bend the lamp to it twice before I could read it:

All along the edge, the stumps grew nothing.

Nobody wrote down why. Nobody asked.

Season after season the grey got thinner, like a spell wearing off. The ship-masters came home and said it stood further out than it had in their fathers’ day. The Council called it good luck. I said that word to myself three times, luck, the way I say every word before I trust it to ink. It won’t hold. It crumbles on the tongue, like mortar that has turned to sand.

In the middle years of the Harvest, the Stonewrights warned the Council. It’s in the records, in our own handwriting:

“These trees grow in strange soil. The carvings we find next to them are in a language we can’t read. When we don’t know enough, we should hold back.”

But by then trade was running everything, and people thought of the Stonewrights as leftovers from a poorer time. The warning was read out once in the Council hall at Eldhythe, the Eldest Haven, and then put on a shelf.

Because there were carvings. All through the Harvest, at the feet of the black pillars, the axemen found standing stones. Carved on them were fine, curling letters, as beautiful as frost on a window. The records say nobody could read them. The stones came down to the cities in the cargo holds with the timber, silent and beautiful. Stones like them are in the chest I carried up the mountain. Halyna have put their four hands on them and gotten nothing. Stone does not translate.

Some were locked up in the guild’s archives, and we were the ones who turned the key. Others were sold as curiosities and hung on the walls of the harbour inns as decorations. Men drank under them and did their accounts. So the Stonewrights gave their warning, and then put their own warning away in the dark, with the stones they couldn’t read. That’s our share of the blame. We won’t put it all on the Council.

Among the cargo counts from one late summer, three generations before the banner, a clerk copied a letter. It came from the furthest outpost, at the end of the land. He wrote it down between a count of poles and a count of oars, in the same handwriting and with the same care, as one more thing that had come in from the edge. Here it is exactly:

Unknown intruder. Hostile intent. Neutralized. Vessel burned and released.

At the bottom of the letter is a name, cut out: ▒▒▒▒.

On the next page, in the same season, two timber boats are crossed off a cargo list. Next to them, in the same handwriting, it says: lost at the edge. I copied the letter the way I copied everything. I didn’t know what I was holding.

The Harvest ended three generations before the banner. It didn’t end because anyone got wise. It ended because people changed their minds. The pillars near the coast got scarce. The voyages got long. Cheaper timber came down from the northern hills. And after the year of the burning boat, no crew would row within sight of the grey. The records don’t say why. So the axes stopped. Nobody decided it.

Then came three generations of forgetting. The Shorelanders of the last days remembered nothing at all. The stones kept hanging on the walls of the harbour inns, grey above the beer and the dice. Children grew up under them and asked what they said. They were told the stones said nothing. Some of you sitting at this fire were those children.

Late, very late, the wheel of pride turned. The havens’ temples were repaired, and their lamps were lit again. People climbed the worn steps of the guild halls and asked the Stonewrights for advice, and the Stonewrights gave it.

Think of the men of a harbour who finally go down, in the calm at the end of the year, to fix the sea wall their fathers let fall apart. They fill every joint. Then they stand back on the dock with their hands on their hips, pleased with the work. Meanwhile, out past the headland, where none of them is looking, the weather has already turned. That’s how the havens fixed their temples, and were pleased. But humility came too late.

The first dark sails were already on the sea.

I have laid this tale the way it was laid for me.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

Added in Seren’s handwriting: What the records call carvings, the wood has since told us were pleas. Holy pleas, turned into decorations.

Added in Seren’s handwriting: The people who made those stones carved some of our own words among their letters, spelled by sound, as close as their letters could get. The sounds were close. The symbols were wrong.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

IV.2 · The Axe in the GrainWood leaf

SHAPE KNOWN IN THE SECOND SUMMER; KNOWN WHOLE IN THE THIRD WINTER · OF THE HARVEST AS THE PILLARS FELT IT, FIVE GENERATIONS TO THREE BEFORE THE BANNER, AND OF THE CHOOSING OF ITS HEART AFTER THE VOTE.

Known from the heart of a Standard-Bearer of the Second Tide, of the grain the wall calls Blackthorn, whose hulls go to the gun that hurt them and will not leave it, as the wall learned. Rhyna took up that heart on the shingle, and she and Halvard could hold only its shape; yet that shape showed them what the mute stones were that lay beside the sealed bundle. In the third winter Halvard laid a hand on it in the vault, Rhyna at the hearth with the children, and it came whole to them both in a breath; but the telling took three nights, for it was in them as a wound is, and not as a tale. The rolls forget, and can be made to lie; and the wood cannot lie, but cannot see fear. Told by Halyna, by turns, in two inks, and set down by me.

As first known, in the second summer, the shape only:

…iron… [] …each fell, sky came through… we cut on [mute thing]… it did not []…

Two marks of the wood’s shape that Halyna could see, and could not yet tell
Translation

The first is the pillars, many, with a memory cut through them to the heart: What one pillar felt, all the ranks felt, root to root. The second is the mouth, mirrored, and tied back across the rings to the plea cut on the stone: It did not answer.

As told whole:

This is held in the grain.

At the edge of the breath we stood in our long ranks, tall and dark of bark. We held the breath to the earth, as roots hold a bank.

We did not think of it, for no one thinks of breath till it is taken. We were the living pillars of the breath. For that alone we stood, and it was enough.

From the edge we watched your shore; and every hull of yours that came near us, we marked. Each brush was a cut, and each trespass a wound; for the wood keeps every count.

In the high summer of your havens the iron came, and felled us, and felled our young after us, till the stumps put up nothing.

In boats with many oars the iron came. The shore-men waded up out of the surf, and laid it to our bark.

What one pillar felt, all the ranks felt, root to root, to the far edge of the wood.

One fell, and another. Summer after summer the shore-men came, till those who first brought the iron were old, and sent their sons.

Where each of us fell, the sky came through, as daylight shows through a rent in a sail. It was pale. Then it was pale and hard. Then it was pale and hard and wide.

The young of the long-lived turned their faces from the open sky, and did not know why they wept.

The stumps put up shoots, straight and black; and in a few summers a young pillar stood where the old one had fallen. The iron came back for the young ones. We put up more. It came again.

We carved, as we had always carved when a thing must not be mistaken. But the shore-men never laid a hand on wood to know it; they only cut it, and carried it over the water.

So on the mute thing we cut our plea, on the stone your builders love, of which you make your houses and your walls. For a people, we thought, must know best what is cut in the thing it loves.

We cut it in our own soft letters. Where we had caught a word of yours across the water, we cut that too, as near its sound as our letters would go. At the feet of the pillars yet standing we set the stones.

Every made thing of ours speaks, and so we believed that every made thing speaks. It was a child’s belief. Stone has no mind.

It did not answer.

With the timber the shore-men took the stones away, and we did not know where they went.

Then along the whole edge the stumps put up nothing, and the breath thinned. We held our sap, and waited.

The iron tires; and when it stops, the wood comes again.

In the years of the iron we who tell you this were never a pillar, but a thin green thing at the edge.

Where the iron first went in, a stump was cut low; and we were its last shoot. The whole of the axe was in our rings. The iron passed us over, for we were too thin to make a pole.

We had stood forty summers when the long-lived of the council came looking for hearts, to carry their carvings. Not the straightest young tree did they choose, nor the eldest. They chose us, for the axe in our rings.

Such is our grain: it goes where it lost the most. When one of our hulls is struck, we go to the stroke, as the tongue goes to a broken tooth. It is not ours to choose. It is in the rings.

What we go to is ever the same. It is the iron at the edge of the breath, summer after summer, and the sky coming through; and the grain holds it still.

And no one answered.

ITS SHAPE KNOWN IN THE SECOND SUMMER; KNOWN WHOLE IN THE THIRD WINTER · ABOUT THE HARVEST AS THE PILLARS FELT IT, FROM FIVE TO THREE GENERATIONS BEFORE THE BANNER, AND ABOUT HOW ITS HEART WAS CHOSEN AFTER THE VOTE.

Known from the heart of a Standard-Bearer from the Second Tide. Its wood is the kind the wall calls Blackthorn. As the wall learned, those ships go straight for the gun that hurt them, and won’t leave it alone. Rhyna picked up that heart on the beach. She and Halvard could only hold its shape. But that shape showed them what the silent stones were, the ones lying next to the sealed bundle. In the third winter, Halvard put a hand on it in the vault, while Rhyna was at the hearth with the children, and it came to both of them whole, in one breath. But the telling took three nights, because it was inside them like a wound, not like a story. The records forget, and they can be made to lie. The wood can’t lie, but it can’t see fear. Told by Halyna, taking turns, in two inks, and written down by me.

As it was first known, in the second summer, only the shape:

…iron… [] …each fell, sky came through… we cut on [mute thing]… it did not []…

Two of the wood’s marks that Halyna could see but couldn’t tell yet
Translation

The first is the pillars, many of them, with one memory cut through all of them to the heart: What one pillar felt, all the rows felt, root to root. The second is the mouth, drawn as a mirror image, and tied back across the rings to the plea cut on the stone: It did not answer.

As told whole:

This is held in the grain.

We stood in long rows at the edge of the breath, tall, with dark bark. We held the breath down to the earth, the way roots hold a riverbank together.

We didn’t think about it. Nobody thinks about breath until it’s taken away. We were the living pillars of the breath. That was the only reason we stood there, and it was enough.

From the edge, we watched your shore. We marked every one of your ships that came near us. Every brush against us was a cut. Every trespass was a wound. The wood keeps every count.

In the high summer of your havens, the iron came. It cut us down. Then it cut down our young, until the stumps grew nothing.

The iron came in boats with many oars. The shore-men waded up out of the surf and put it to our bark.

Whatever one pillar felt, all the rows felt, root to root, to the far edge of the wood.

One fell. Then another. Summer after summer the shore-men came, until the ones who first brought the iron were old, and they sent their sons.

Wherever one of us fell, the sky came through, the way daylight shows through a tear in a sail. It was pale. Then it was pale and hard. Then it was pale and hard and wide.

The young of the long-lived turned their faces away from the open sky. They didn’t know why they were crying.

The stumps sent up shoots, straight and black. In a few summers, a young pillar stood where the old one had fallen. The iron came back for the young ones. We grew more. It came again.

We carved, the way we always carved when something must not be misunderstood. But the shore-men never put a hand on the wood to know it. They only cut it and carried it away over the water.

So we cut our plea into the silent thing, the stone your builders love, the stone you make your houses and walls from. We thought a people must understand best what is carved in the thing they love.

We cut it in our own soft letters. Where we had caught one of your words across the water, we cut that too, as close to its sound as our letters could go. We set the stones at the feet of the pillars still standing.

Everything we make speaks. So we believed that everything made speaks. It was a child’s belief. Stone has no mind.

It did not answer.

The shore-men took the stones away with the timber. We didn’t know where they went.

Then, all along the edge, the stumps grew nothing, and the breath got thin. We held back our sap, and waited.

The iron gets tired. When it stops, the wood comes back.

In the years of the iron, we who are telling you this were never a pillar. We were a thin green thing at the edge.

Where the iron first went in, a stump was cut low. We were its last shoot. The whole axe was in our rings. The iron skipped over us, because we were too thin to make a pole.

We had stood for forty summers when the long-lived of the council came looking for hearts to carry their carvings. They didn’t choose the straightest young tree, or the oldest. They chose us, because of the axe in our rings.

That’s how our grain is: it goes to where it lost the most. When one of our ships is hit, we go toward the blow, the way your tongue goes to a broken tooth. We don’t choose it. It’s in the rings.

What we go to is always the same. It is the iron at the edge of the breath, summer after summer, and the sky coming through. And the grain still holds it.

And no one answered.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

IV.3 · The Warden’s DoorStone leaf

LAID IN THE THIRD WINTER, AT THE START OF THE TIDE OF REMEMBERING · OF ONE DAY AT THE OUTERMOST OUTPOST IN THE SUMMER OF THE GUEST, SEVENTY-FIVE YEARS BEFORE THE BANNER, AND OF THE RUN AFTER IT; AND OF THE CONFESSION, FORTY YEARS AFTER.

Laid in the words of Brenn of the outpost, as Wendhessa, the archivists of Eldhythe, took them from his mouth; read aloud by Halyna, he and she by turns; and the annal is mine. Under the mark of Wendhessa, the bundle lay in the archive chest from the first night it was opened, for no pair breaks another pair’s mark, till the shape of the Blackthorn heart showed Halyna what the mute stones beside it were, pleas and not ornaments, and they could no longer leave the bundle that lay among them unread; and the two of them asked Idrenna, the eldest pair. Idrenna gave leave. Within was the confession, wrapped about a dark stone; and where Brenn named the warden, I have kept the blots. The wood’s leaf faces it.

“We hold the stone, but these words are not ours,” one of them said, and the other finished: “They are the words of Brenn of the outpost, who came in his old age to the temple at Eldhythe, forty years after, and asked the Stonewrights to hear him; and we two read them as they were written.”

Forty years ago I served at the outpost at the end of the land, where the rock runs out into the sea toward the grey; and of all the posts upon that coast it was the last, and the farthest from any haven, and the poorest. I was nineteen. There were twelve of us and the warden.

It was a poor post, but it did not look poor, for the warden had set the black wood everywhere in it: in the doorposts and the lintels, and in the walls of his hall, and in his table and his chair, and in the frame of his bed. ▒▒▒▒ was a rich man’s son, and hardly older than his own runner; and he held the post by his father’s name on the Trade Council and not by his own, for they had sent him to the edge when his temper made him unwelcome in the cities, and at the edge there were only the twelve of us for it to wound. He had never known battle. He had never known patience.

I was the post’s runner. At fifteen I had sworn the runners’ oath on the runners’ stone at Eldhythe, as every runner of the havens swore it, with my hand flat upon the stone: “What I saw, I carry. I carry nothing else.” The eldest of the twelve was old Tobe, who had fished the edge of the grey for thirty years, and in all those years had never once put his bow into the grey.

On a morning at the end of that summer, out of the grey came the stranger, in a small grey boat. There was no wind, and the boat came anyway. He beached it on our shingle, and walked up from the water to the warden’s door; and he was tall and pale, and clad in a woven mist-cloth that moved when he did not, and there was no weapon on him that I saw.

He spoke first in his own tongue, hard sounds that broke and rolled like a storm-wind in old trees; and it frightened us, for it was loud, and we did not know that it was only how they speak. Then he said three words of ours, and he said them carefully, like a man setting down something that might break:

“Stop… Sky… Dying…”

They were the right words, spoken truly. I know it now. But words without sentences are only sounds, and we heard only sounds.

Then he knelt on the step of the warden’s door, and took out a dark stone the size of two fists, and set it down before him, the way you would set down bread before a guest. No man of us moved. Behind him a gull came down upon the shingle, and walked about among the stones.

▒▒▒▒ kicked it aside.

It went into the dust by the wall. And his men laughed. I laughed. I have not laughed at anything since.

The stranger rose. He put his right hand on the back of the warden’s neck, gently, the way my mother did when I had a fever, and drew their two heads together till their foreheads touched, and held them so, in silence; and ▒▒▒▒ stood there, as a man stands who does not know what is being done to him, and let it be done. I think if it had stopped there, all our lives would have gone otherwise.

Then the stranger took a little blade in his left hand, and touched it to the warden’s right arm. ▒▒▒▒ tore himself away, for he had never had a wound; and in the tearing the cut that was meant to be shallow went deep, and the blood came; and ▒▒▒▒ screamed at us, “Seize him.”

We seized the stranger, and he did not fight. I think now there was another cut he meant to make, on his own arm. He never made it.

We had him by both his arms, and he let us hold him so; and between our hands he went down on his knees again upon the step, where he had knelt with his gift. I did not understand it.

▒▒▒▒ screamed, “Kill him.”

We had no swords worth the name at the outpost, so we took up what was there: the boat-poles, and the staves, and the stones of the shingle. I struck him with the pole I pushed the boats off with. Twice. I have counted them every night since.

He did not lift his hand. If he cried out, it was in his own tongue, and I took it for the wind. When the stranger was still, ▒▒▒▒ was still screaming, and some of us went on, for ▒▒▒▒ was still screaming. Both the stranger’s arms were broken: the one that had held the warden’s neck so gently, and the one that had held the blade. I saw it when it was done.

We obeyed.

Then it was quiet, and the gull was gone. We took him by the heels down the shingle, and his head went on every stone. Into his boat we threw him, as a fisher throws a torn net into the bilge, fit for nothing now but the fire.

▒▒▒▒ held his arm and screamed that the blade was cursed, that the thing had come out of the grey to curse him. Then old Tobe said, as every man on that coast would have said it:

“What the grey sends, the fire sends back, or it comes again.”

We knew the custom, and we were glad of it, for it let us stop thinking. And ▒▒▒▒ was glad of it, for the body would go out with the boat, and nothing would be left upon that shore to say other than his letter.

We set the boat alight, and it did not burn as wood burns. Higher than any fire I had ever seen the fire went up, and there was a screaming in it; and the stranger was dead, so it was no man that screamed. It was the wood itself screaming. We shoved it off with the same poles, and the tide took it out, and we could hear it on the water after we could no longer bear to look.

Old Tobe went down into the water to his knees, and washed the poles, one and then another; and the rest of us stood upon the shingle, and would not look, and looked.

Well out, the boat turned.

It turned into the wind and against the current, which no boat can do, and went away into the grey, still burning.

That same day, ▒▒▒▒ had the clerk of the post write to the Council; and I stood by while it was written, and I can say it by heart:

Unknown intruder. Hostile intent. Neutralized. Vessel burned and released.

No word of the gift. No word of the kneeling.

The next morning, ere any man of the post was awake, I went to the wall and picked the stone out of the dust, and I do not know why I did it. ▒▒▒▒ gave me the letter at the door, for I was the post’s runner. So the gift and the lie went into one pack, and I ran it.

All that day and all that night I ran the coast road to Eldhythe, and every mile of it I said the oath over to myself, as I had sworn it at fifteen with my hand upon the stone: “What I saw, I carry. I carry nothing else.”

On the second morning I stood in the Council hall at Eldhythe, before the clerk of the Council, an old man with ink upon his fingers; and he read the letter, and looked up at me, and asked me, “Is it so?”

I did not say it was so. I said, “It is written.” For forty years I have weighed that small cleverness.

I carried the lie in a day and a night. I carried the truth forty years, and gave it to no one.

That autumn I stayed in Eldhythe, and did not go back to the edge; and in the harbour-houses I heard three axemen swear to a burning boat: how it had come at them out of the grey, and broken their boats under them, and how out of more than twenty men they three alone had come home, half-drowned upon a spar. The shore laughed, and I did not laugh, for I had seen it turn; but I said nothing, for the Council had it in writing.

▒▒▒▒ wore long sleeves all the rest of his life, through the hot summers of Eldhythe, and I know why. Last winter he died in his bed of black wood, and I am the last of the twelve.

For forty years the stone has lain in a cloth at the bottom of my pack, and not once in all those years have I held it in my hand. I give it up now with these words, and I ask God to forgive me, for there is no one left to ask but God.

So far the words of Brenn. Now hear the annal.

“Hold the stone, Seren. I tell myself so every time.”

The warden’s name is not kept. Wherever Brenn spoke it, the Stonewrights of Eldhythe cut it out of these leaves, and out of the rolls at the foot of his letter, with the same four marks; and in no book of ours will it stand. Tonight, at every blot, Halyna, he and she, stopped and said nothing, and you heard a silence in the place of the name.

On the right arm of the warden was a mark, the only mark that the blade of the Kneeling Guest ever made upon a Shoreland body: the half of something, cut off in the middle.

Within the year Brenn died, as a man sets down a load. The guild buried him under his name; the warden’s name it cut away.

Wendhessa, the archivists of Eldhythe, Wendel and Tessa, he and she, wrapped the confession about the dark stone, and sealed the bundle with their mark, and laid it in the archive chest of the guild amid the mute stones; and in all the years that were left to the two of them, they told no one.

When the havens fell, Wendhessa themselves carried the chest out of Eldhythe, the Eldest Haven, his hand on the one handle and hers on the other, while the harbour burned at their backs and its smoke lay long upon the water. They were old, and the chest was heavy; and on the Road their arms failed them, and the masons, who would carry nothing up that mountain save bread and chisels, said that the chest held nothing of worth, and gave it to the two youngest of the guild to carry between them, a handle each.

Tarnel, a fisher’s son of Tidesmeet, where the river meets the tide, took the one handle, and I took the other; and between us we carried it up into the passes, and as he went he knotted his net with his free hand, against the day we should come to the tarns of the mountain and fish them, and I watched the knots.

On the night of the pass, the coldest night of all the Road, Wendhessa lay down in the snow a little way off, the two of them under one cloak; and Tarnel and I lay down under another, as cradle-mates do, with the chest at our heads. In the morning there was frost upon the chest, on both its handles; and Wendhessa lay under their one cloak, he and she, and neither of them rose.

Tarnel did not wake.

I stopped there tonight, with my hands upon the stone. Then Halyna laid their hands on it beside mine, his and hers. “She carried it every step,” one of them said, and the other finished, “and after the passes she carried it alone.”

The two of them did not take their hands away. By then the fire had burned down to its coals, and no one rose to feed it; and at the front, where the runners sit, Hale the runner sat with his head bowed, and Wick, the youngest of the runners, sat on the floor before him with his knees drawn up to his chin.

There is no older law among any people than this: that one who kneels at your door is under your roof. We were a people of doors.

Then the three of us said it together, with our hands upon the stone:

This we lay as it was laid for us.

And the hearth said: We remember.

It was the first time this hearth had said it over a killing done by our own people. Old Ebba of Fenholm began it a breath before the rest, as she begins it most nights, and the rest were slow to follow her; but they followed.

And the Captain put his hood back.

When it was laid, Hale the runner went out of the hall, and the runners with him, and Wick, who runs everywhere, ran to join them. In the morning they swore their oath again on the runners’ stone in the ward, and added four words to it: Not as Brenn carried.

Added in Seren’s hand: It was the half of a holy thing.

LAID IN THE THIRD WINTER, AT THE START OF THE TIDE OF REMEMBERING · ABOUT ONE DAY AT THE FURTHEST OUTPOST IN THE SUMMER OF THE GUEST, SEVENTY-FIVE YEARS BEFORE THE BANNER, AND THE RUN AFTERWARD; AND ABOUT THE CONFESSION, FORTY YEARS LATER.

Told in the words of Brenn of the outpost, as Wendhessa, the archivists of Eldhythe, wrote them down from his own mouth. Read out loud by Halyna, taking turns. The record at the end is mine. The bundle lay sealed under the mark of Wendhessa in the archive chest from the first night we opened the chest, because no pair ever breaks another pair’s mark. Then the shape of the Blackthorn heart showed Halyna what the silent stones next to it really were: pleas, not decorations. After that, they couldn’t leave the bundle lying among them unread. So the two of them asked Idrenna, the oldest pair. Idrenna gave permission. Inside was the confession, wrapped around a dark stone. Wherever Brenn named the warden, I have kept the blots. The wood’s page faces this one.

“We’re holding the stone, but these words aren’t ours,” one of them said, and the other finished: “They’re the words of Brenn of the outpost. He came to the temple at Eldhythe as an old man, forty years later, and asked the Stonewrights to hear him. We two are reading them exactly as they were written.”

Forty years ago, I served at the outpost at the end of the land, where the rock runs out into the sea toward the grey. Of all the posts on that coast, it was the last one. It was the furthest from any haven, and the poorest. I was nineteen. There were twelve of us, plus the warden.

It was a poor post, but it didn’t look poor. The warden had put the black wood everywhere: in the doorposts and the beams over the doors, in the walls of his hall, in his table and his chair, and in the frame of his bed. ▒▒▒▒ was a rich man’s son, barely older than his own runner. He held the post because of his father’s seat on the Trade Council, not because of anything he’d done. They had sent him out to the edge because his temper made him unwelcome in the cities. At the edge, there were only the twelve of us for it to hurt. He had never been in a battle. He had never learned patience.

I was the post’s runner. At fifteen, I had sworn the runners’ oath on the runners’ stone at Eldhythe, the way every runner in the havens swore it, with my hand flat on the stone: “What I saw, I carry. I carry nothing else.” The oldest of the twelve was old Tobe. He had fished the edge of the grey for thirty years, and in all that time he had never once pointed his boat into it.

One morning at the end of that summer, the stranger came out of the grey in a small grey boat. There was no wind, and the boat came anyway. He pulled it up on our beach and walked up from the water to the warden’s door. He was tall and pale. He wore a woven cloth like mist that moved even when he didn’t. I didn’t see any weapon on him.

First he spoke in his own language. It was all hard sounds that cracked and rolled, like a storm wind in old trees. It scared us, because it was loud, and we didn’t know that’s just how they talk. Then he said three words in our language. He said them carefully, like a man putting down something that might break:

“Stop… Sky… Dying…”

They were the right words, and he meant them. I know that now. But words without sentences are just sounds, and all we heard was sounds.

Then he knelt on the warden’s doorstep. He took out a dark stone about the size of two fists and set it down in front of him, the way you’d set bread in front of a guest. None of us moved. Behind him, a gull landed on the beach and walked around among the stones.

▒▒▒▒ kicked it away.

It landed in the dirt by the wall. And his men laughed. I laughed. I haven’t laughed at anything since.

The stranger stood up. He put his right hand on the back of the warden’s neck, gently, the way my mother did when I had a fever. He pulled their two heads together until their foreheads touched, and he held them there, in silence. ▒▒▒▒ just stood there, like a man who doesn’t know what’s being done to him, and let it happen. I think if it had stopped there, all our lives would have turned out differently.

Then the stranger took a small blade in his left hand and touched it to the warden’s right arm. ▒▒▒▒ yanked himself away, because he had never had a wound in his life. When he yanked, the cut that was meant to be shallow went deep, and the blood came. ▒▒▒▒ screamed at us, “Seize him.”

We grabbed the stranger. He didn’t fight. I think now there was another cut he meant to make, on his own arm. He never got to make it.

We had him by both arms, and he let us hold him. Between our hands, he went back down on his knees on the step, where he had knelt with his gift. I didn’t understand it.

▒▒▒▒ screamed, “Kill him.”

We had no real swords at the outpost, so we picked up whatever was there: the boat poles, the walking sticks, the stones from the beach. I hit him with the pole I used to push the boats off. Twice. I have counted those two blows every night since.

He never raised his hand. If he cried out, it was in his own language, and I thought it was the wind. When the stranger stopped moving, ▒▒▒▒ was still screaming. So some of us kept going, because ▒▒▒▒ was still screaming. Both of the stranger’s arms were broken: the one that had held the warden’s neck so gently, and the one that had held the blade. I saw it when it was over.

We did what we were told.
