Then it was quiet, and the gull was gone. We dragged him down the beach by his heels, and his head hit every stone. We threw him into his boat, the way a fisherman throws a torn net into the bottom of the boat, good for nothing now but burning.

▒▒▒▒ held his arm and screamed that the blade was cursed, that the thing had come out of the grey to curse him. Then old Tobe said what any man on that coast would have said:

“What the grey sends, the fire sends back, or it comes again.”

We knew the custom, and we were glad of it, because it let us stop thinking. ▒▒▒▒ was glad of it too, because the body would go out with the boat. Nothing would be left on that shore to say anything different from his letter.

We set the boat on fire, and it didn’t burn the way wood burns. The fire went up higher than any fire I had ever seen, and there was screaming in it. The stranger was dead, so it wasn’t a man screaming. It was the wood itself. We pushed the boat off with the same poles, and the tide took it out. We could still hear it on the water after we couldn’t stand to look anymore.

Old Tobe waded into the water up to his knees and washed the poles, one and then another. The rest of us stood on the beach, trying not to look, and looking.

Far out, the boat turned.

It turned into the wind and against the current, which no boat can do. It went away into the grey, still burning.

That same day, ▒▒▒▒ had the post’s clerk write to the Council. I stood there while it was written, and I can still say it word for word:

Unknown intruder. Hostile intent. Neutralized. Vessel burned and released.

Not a word about the gift. Not a word about the kneeling.

The next morning, before anyone at the post was awake, I went to the wall and picked the stone up out of the dirt. I don’t know why I did it. ▒▒▒▒ handed me the letter at the door, because I was the post’s runner. So the gift and the lie went into one pack, and I ran it.

All that day and all that night I ran the coast road to Eldhythe. With every mile, I said the oath over to myself, the way I swore it at fifteen with my hand on the stone: “What I saw, I carry. I carry nothing else.”

On the second morning, I stood in the Council hall at Eldhythe in front of the Council’s clerk, an old man with ink on his fingers. He read the letter, looked up at me, and asked, “Is it true?”

I didn’t say it was true. I said, “It is written.” For forty years I have weighed that little bit of cleverness.

I carried the lie in a day and a night. I carried the truth for forty years, and gave it to no one.

That autumn I stayed in Eldhythe and didn’t go back to the edge. In the harbour inns I heard three axemen swear they’d seen a burning boat. They said it came at them out of the grey and smashed their boats under them. They said that out of more than twenty men, only the three of them made it home, half drowned, hanging on to a broken mast. The whole shore laughed at them. I didn’t laugh, because I had seen it turn. But I said nothing, because the Council had it in writing.

▒▒▒▒ wore long sleeves for the rest of his life, even in the hot summers of Eldhythe, and I know why. Last winter he died in his bed of black wood. I’m the last of the twelve.

For forty years the stone has lain wrapped in a cloth at the bottom of my pack. Not once in all those years have I held it in my hand. I’m giving it up now with these words. I ask God to forgive me, because there’s nobody left to ask but God.

That’s the end of Brenn’s words. Now here’s the record.

“Hold the stone, Seren. I tell myself that every time.”

The warden’s name isn’t kept. Everywhere Brenn said it, the Stonewrights of Eldhythe cut it out of these pages, and out of the records at the bottom of his letter, with the same four marks. It won’t stand in any book of ours. Tonight, at every blot, Halyna stopped and said nothing. You heard a silence where the name should have been.

There was a mark on the warden’s right arm. It was the only mark the Kneeling Guest’s blade ever made on a Shoreland body: half of something, cut off in the middle.

Within the year, Brenn died, like a man putting down a heavy load. The guild buried him under his own name. They cut the warden’s name away.

Wendhessa, the archivists of Eldhythe, Wendel and Tessa, he and she, wrapped the confession around the dark stone. They sealed the bundle with their mark and put it in the guild’s archive chest, among the silent stones. In all the years the two of them had left, they told no one.

When the havens fell, Wendhessa carried the chest out of Eldhythe, the Eldest Haven, themselves. His hand was on one handle and hers on the other. The harbour was burning behind them, and the smoke lay long across the water. They were old, and the chest was heavy. On the Road, their arms gave out. The masons wouldn’t carry anything up that mountain except bread and chisels. They said the chest held nothing worth anything. They gave it to the two youngest members of the guild to carry between them, one handle each.

Tarnel, a fisherman’s son from Tidesmeet, where the river meets the tide, took one handle. I took the other. Between us, we carried it up into the passes. As he walked, he tied knots in his net with his free hand, getting ready for the day we’d reach the mountain lakes and fish them. I watched the knots.

On the night on the pass, the coldest night of the whole Road, Wendhessa lay down in the snow a little way off, the two of them under one cloak. Tarnel and I lay down under another, the way cradle-mates do, with the chest by our heads. In the morning there was frost on the chest, on both its handles. Wendhessa were lying under their one cloak, he and she, and neither of them got up.

Tarnel didn’t wake up.

I stopped there tonight, with my hands on the stone. Then Halyna put their hands on it next to mine, his and hers. “She carried it every step,” one of them said, and the other finished, “and after the passes she carried it alone.”

The two of them didn’t take their hands away. By then the fire had burned down to coals, and nobody got up to feed it. At the front, where the runners sit, Hale the runner sat with his head down. Wick, the youngest runner, sat on the floor in front of him with his knees pulled up to his chin.

There’s no older law among any people than this: someone who kneels at your door is under your roof, and under your protection. We were a people of doors.

Then the three of us said it together, with our hands on the stone:

We have laid this tale the way it was laid for us.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

It was the first time this hearth had said it over a killing done by our own people. Old Ebba of Fenholm started it a moment before the rest, the way she does most nights. The others were slow to follow her. But they followed.

And the Captain pushed his hood back.

When it was laid, Hale the runner walked out of the hall, and the other runners went with him. Wick, who runs everywhere, ran to catch up. In the morning they swore their oath again on the runners’ stone in the yard, and they added four words to it: Not as Brenn carried.

Added in Seren’s handwriting: It was half of a holy thing.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

IV.4 · The Gift Held an HourWood leaf

KNOWN IN THE THIRD SPRING, IN THE TIDE OF REMEMBERING · OF THE GUEST’S LONG LIFE OF LISTENING AND HIS LAST MORNING, SEVENTY-FIVE YEARS BEFORE THE BANNER; AND OF THE GIFT’S ROAD TO THIS HEARTH.

Known from the gift itself. On the night the words of Brenn were read, a child at the front asked what the Kneeling Guest had given, and Rhyna held the stone, and it gave her only its shape; but in the third spring she held it an hour, and it came into her slowly, like water over stone, for the eldest wood is slow to give. Halvard, at the mortar across the ward, never touched it; yet he knew it as she knew it, and began the telling. Its road the wood knows without names, and I have not set the names beside it; you who heard the words of Brenn, and the annal after them, will know them. The sentence at its end was the hardest to tell, for it had to be told in our small words, which the Guest never heard. Told by Halyna, by turns, in two inks, and set down by me.

This is held in the grain.

We are the eldest wood. A tree we were when the world was green to its edges.

We fell, and the ages lay down on us, leaf upon leaf. Time forgot us, and we grew dark and hard. You took us for stone. We are not stone.

Wood that time has forgotten does not itself forget. It gives slowly, and it gives only what is true. Of all the gifts a child of the long-lived can give, the dearest is a sliver of such wood.

We were given once. This is how.

In the years of the iron, one of the long-lived carried us to a door of yours, and knelt there, and was killed.

The one who carried us took the name Aelrhen when he took his errand, and it means bond-to-stone. Through a long life he listened across the breath, to the shore-men at their nets and at their walls. He gathered your words as a child gathers fallen leaves, one and one and one.

At the end of his listening Aelrhen had three, and he knew them for the right ones. Yet he knew no way to join them, for your tongue joins its words with small words that make no sound across water.

But Aelrhen had us. Whoever held us, while a tree’s shadow moves the breadth of its own trunk, would know the whole of it. We were the sentence. He did not know that one hand of yours takes from us only our shape. Even the shape would have been enough, had any hand of that house held us.

Aelrhen chose the outermost of your holds, for its doors, its walls and its benches were of our wood; and he took that for reverence. We knew it for what it was: our kin from the edge, sawn and polished.

At the door Aelrhen laid his hand on the post, and greeted our kin; and they did not answer, for they were dead.

The guest knelt on the stones before the master of that house, and set us down. One who comes under a roof brings his best; and we were his best. He spoke the name of the rite, Ael’thar, in the thunder of his own tongue; and after it, his three words in yours.

“Stop… Sky… Dying…”

This was the Aelthar, the binding of gift and blood and kinship across any divide: the holiest rite of fellowship we have. A foot struck us into the dust by the wall, and the men of that house laughed. Aelrhen did not stop, for the rite is not stopped once it is begun.

Aelrhen rose, and took the back of the host’s neck in his right hand, there where life goes up into thought. A fledgling could have slept in that hand. He drew their two heads together, and the foreheads met, thought to thought, in silence.

With his left hand Aelrhen set the blade to the host’s right arm, to open a shallow cut. His own left arm he would have cut next, for he would ask no blood he did not give. Then the two of them would have pressed their bleeding arms together, shoulder to shoulder: a promise that harm to one was harm to both.

And the host drew back. We do not know why. Fear leaves no mark on wood.

The blade that was meant to go a little way went deep, for the arm was moving. The host’s blood ran, and he cried out, and the men of that house came.

Aelrhen’s blood came across the stones of the step to where we lay, and with it came his last hour. So we know it as he knew it.

Aelrhen went down on his knees again, for there the rite begins, and he meant to begin it again. He knew the blows as a tree knows the iron: one, and another. He did not lift his hand, for the rite is not stopped once it is begun, and his half was not done. The cut he would have made on his own arm was never made.

He died there with the rite half made.

We lay in the dust and heard all of it, and no one picked us up. Of all the things that ever lay at a shore-man’s feet and looked like stone, we were the one that could have spoken.

And we were the one his foot found.

In the morning a hand picked us out of the dust, and put us away, and did not hold us.

Then a long jolting in the dark, a day and a night. Then the dark, for half a life, and never a hand. Then a wrapping of leaves about us, and cut stones cold beside us, and another dark as long.

Then old arms, two pairs, slow, a long while; and fire near and far. Then small arms, two pairs, going up, in the cold; and then one pair, a long way.

No one held us long, till you held us while a tree’s shadow moves the breadth of its own trunk.

Here is the sentence, as it was brought to the door:

Stop the felling of the pillars at the edge of the grey, for they are not timber. The sky over us is their breath, and ours, and when you cut them it comes open, and our children cannot live in the open sun. We are here, behind the grey, and we have been here always, and we are not your enemies. Stop, for the sky is dying, and we are dying under it.

That is all Aelrhen came to say. We have carried it a long way, and the grain holds it still.

And no one answered.

Added in Seren’s hand: The old arms were Wendhessa’s. The small arms, two pairs, were Tarnel’s and mine; and the one pair, after the night of the pass, was mine.

KNOWN IN THE THIRD SPRING, DURING THE TIDE OF REMEMBERING · ABOUT THE GUEST’S LONG LIFE OF LISTENING AND HIS LAST MORNING, SEVENTY-FIVE YEARS BEFORE THE BANNER; AND ABOUT THE ROAD THE GIFT TOOK TO THIS HEARTH.

Known from the gift itself. On the night Brenn’s words were read, a child at the front asked what the Kneeling Guest had given. Rhyna held the stone, and it gave her only its shape. But in the third spring she held it for an hour, and it came into her slowly, like water running over stone. The oldest wood is slow to give. Halvard was across the yard at the mortar and never touched it. But he knew it as she knew it, and he started the telling. The wood knows its own road without names, and I haven’t written the names next to it. You who heard Brenn’s words, and the record after them, will know who they are. The sentence at the end was the hardest to tell, because it had to be told in our little joining words, which the Guest never heard. Told by Halyna, taking turns, in two inks, and written down by me.

This is held in the grain.

We are the oldest wood. We were a tree when the world was green all the way to its edges.

We fell, and the ages piled up on us, leaf on leaf. Time forgot us, and we turned dark and hard. You thought we were stone. We are not stone.

Wood that time has forgotten does not forget. It gives slowly, and it only gives what is true. Of all the gifts a child of the long-lived can give, the most precious is a piece of wood like that.

We were given once. This is how it happened.

In the years of the iron, one of the long-lived carried us to one of your doors. He knelt there, and he was killed.

The one who carried us took the name Aelrhen when he took on his task. It means bond-to-stone. All through a long life he listened across the breath to the shore-men at their nets and at their walls. He gathered your words the way a child gathers fallen leaves: one, and one, and one.

At the end of his listening, Aelrhen had three words, and he knew they were the right ones. But he didn’t know how to join them. Your language joins its words with little words that make no sound across the water.

But Aelrhen had us. Anyone who held us, for as long as it takes a tree’s shadow to move the width of its own trunk, would know the whole thing. We were the sentence. He didn’t know that one hand of yours only gets our shape from us. Even the shape would have been enough, if anyone in that house had held us.

Aelrhen chose the furthest of your outposts, because its doors, its walls and its benches were made of our wood. He thought that meant respect. We knew it for what it was: our family from the edge, sawn up and polished.

At the door, Aelrhen put his hand on the post and greeted our family. They didn’t answer. They were dead.

The guest knelt on the stones in front of the master of that house and set us down. Someone who comes under a roof brings his best, and we were his best. He said the name of the ceremony, Ael’thar, in the thunder of his own language. Then he said his three words in yours.

“Stop… Sky… Dying…”

This was the Aelthar: the binding of gift and blood and family across any divide. It is the holiest ceremony of friendship we have. A foot kicked us into the dirt by the wall, and the men of that house laughed. Aelrhen didn’t stop. Once the ceremony is started, it isn’t stopped.

Aelrhen stood up. He took the back of the host’s neck in his right hand, at the place where life goes up into thought. A baby bird could have slept in that hand. He drew their two heads together, and their foreheads touched, thought to thought, in silence.

With his left hand, Aelrhen put the blade to the host’s right arm to make a shallow cut. He would have cut his own left arm next, because he would never ask for blood he didn’t give himself. Then the two of them would have pressed their bleeding arms together, shoulder to shoulder. It would have been a promise: harm to one is harm to both.

And the host pulled away. We don’t know why. Fear leaves no mark on wood.

The blade that was meant to go in a little went in deep, because the arm was moving. The host’s blood ran. He cried out, and the men of that house came.

Aelrhen’s blood ran across the stones of the step to where we lay. His last hour came with it. So we know it the way he knew it.

Aelrhen went back down on his knees, because that’s where the ceremony starts, and he meant to start it again. He felt the blows the way a tree feels the iron: one, and another. He didn’t raise his hand, because once the ceremony is started it isn’t stopped, and his half wasn’t done. The cut he would have made on his own arm was never made.

He died there, with the ceremony half finished.

We lay in the dirt and heard all of it, and nobody picked us up. Of everything that ever lay at a shore-man’s feet looking like a stone, we were the one thing that could have spoken.

And we were the thing his foot found.

In the morning, a hand picked us out of the dirt and put us away, and didn’t hold us.

Then a long jolting ride in the dark, a day and a night. Then darkness for half a lifetime, and never a hand. Then a wrapping of leaves around us, with cold cut stones beside us, and another darkness just as long.

Then old arms, two pairs, slow, for a long time, and fire near and far. Then small arms, two pairs, going uphill, in the cold. And then one pair, for a long way.

Nobody held us for long, until you held us for as long as it takes a tree’s shadow to move the width of its own trunk.

Here is the sentence, the way it was brought to the door:

Stop cutting down the pillars at the edge of the grey, because they aren’t just wood. The sky over us is their breath, and ours. When you cut them down, the sky opens up, and our children can’t live in the open sun. We are here, behind the grey. We have always been here. We are not your enemies. Stop, because the sky is dying, and we are dying under it.

That’s all Aelrhen came to say. We have carried it a long way, and the grain still holds it.

And no one answered.

Added in Seren’s handwriting: The old arms were Wendhessa’s. The small arms, two pairs, were Tarnel’s and mine. The one pair, after the night on the pass, was mine.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

IV.5 · The Burning BoatStone leaf

LAID IN THE SECOND SUMMER, IN THE SECOND TIDE · OF THE SUMMER OF THE GUEST AS THE HARBOUR-HOUSES OF ELDHYTHE TOLD IT; OF VOSS’S CHILDHOOD ON THE QUAY, THIRTY-THREE YEARS BEFORE THE BANNER; AND OF THE FIRST WINTER OF THE SIEGE.

Laid by Voss who asked, True Man of the harbour battery since the second winter, as his grandmother told it to him in the harbour-houses of Eldhythe, where she kept the fire and drew the ale for the men of the boats. It is a tale the ship-masters would not tell, and a rhyme the children would not stop singing; and the shore laughed at it, and the sea did not. Facing it stands the wood’s own leaf of that boat.

“Give me the stone. This one is not mine. It is my grandmother’s, and she had it from her brother, and he had it from the sea.”

Hear now how the quay told the burning boat, and hold it, for there is no one left upon the quay to tell it.

In the summer of the burning boat, when my grandmother Elspet was a girl of twelve, she kept the fire in her mother’s harbour-house on the quay at Eldhythe, the Eldest Haven: a house of one long table under a low roof, where the ship-masters came in out of the rain with the weather in their beards, and hung their coats along the settle to steam. Over that fire, set into the wall since her own grandmother’s day, hung three flat grey stones, with curling letters upon them as fine as the tracks of birds on wet sand. What they said, no one on all that quay could tell; and so long as the fire drew, no one asked. The ship-masters knocked out their pipes on the lowest one.

In those days our fathers feared the open sea, as you know who were born in the havens, and kept to the shores and to the sheltered water between haven and haven. If ever some bolder soul went out till he saw the grey lying along the edge of the world, he put about for home, and drank upon the tale of it in my grandmother’s house for a week; for on our quay a wild tale was as good as coin. At every such man my grandmother laughed, and filled his cup for him none the less, and said over her shoulder what she said of all their tales: “The Mystlands make liars of honest men.” She said it of her brother.

Her brother was Daveth, an axeman of the felling; and in that same summer, the summer she was twelve, he was coming home along the edge of the grey, in one of the last timber-boats of the Harvest, low in the water, with the black wood stacked to the hatches; and in the wake of that boat came a second of the same lading.

As when the boys of the quay, on the harbour wall, have robbed a gull of her eggs, and run home with them in their caps, and she comes down at them screaming, again and again, and will not be driven off with stones, but strikes at their heads till they drop what they carry: so the burning boat came at the timber-boats out of the grey, screaming as it came, into the wind and against the current. Low it struck them, where the load rode heaviest, first Daveth’s boat, and then the second, and its fire went into the black wood in both their holds, and the black wood took it; and after, the rain came down on the water, and on the heads of more than twenty men.

Three came home.

Daveth was one of them, clinging to one spar. He came in at his mother’s door that night, with the sea running out of his coat onto the floor my grandmother had swept, and told it. That autumn the three told it in every harbour-house on the quay, nor ever again set foot in a boat.

Later in that year, when the outpost at the end of the land was relieved, its men came into the same harbour-houses, and swore in their cups that they had shoved a boat off the shingle burning, with a dead man in it, and that when the tide had it well out, it turned. They swore to the scream above all. And they swore besides that the grey man had spoken three words of ours ere he died; but which three, no two of them could ever agree.

So it was the three from the spar who first told of the burning boat, and the outpost men who swore to it after; and the two tellings ran together upon the quay into one tale, but it was the three who began it.

When I was a boy at that same long table, my grandmother counted the boats for me on her fingers, as her brother had counted them for her, one finger and then another, and then laid her hand flat upon the board. There were two boats.

Now the ship-masters say twenty.

Year upon year the shore laughed at the three from the spar, as the shore laughs at every man who comes home from the grey with a tale in his mouth; and by day my grandmother laughed with it, and knocked out her pipe upon the lowest of the grey stones. At night she did not laugh.

Daveth drank upon his tale, and within a few years the harbour drowned him, a stone’s throw from his mother’s house. And why did she tell me that tale, more than all the others that were told at her fire? For her brother was in it.

Yet never, in all the years I sat at that fire, would a ship-master speak of it, for it is ill luck anywhere within sound of the sea; and I have seen a master, a man who had held a helm through the winter gales, set down his cup and go out into the rain sooner than hear it told. Three of them I have heard since, at fires in the passes, as far from salt water as ever they came; and they told it never straight, but a saying at a time, each one set down like a cup:

“A small boat burning, far out in the grey,” said the first; and the second, “It turned into the wind, and after it, weather”; and the third, “It is the boat that comes for you when you die.” Then one of them said, “The shore laughs, for the shore will never have to meet it.” And all three ended with the old words of the coast, which they keep for that boat above every other thing that drifts: “What the grey sends, the fire sends back, or it comes again.”

All that while the cities had it in writing: a stranger come armed, and put down, and his boat burned and set adrift; and if any man swore after to a burning boat upon the water, that was the same boat, set adrift, and the man was drunk. And who argues with writing?

But the children had a rhyme for it, and children argue with anything. When I was seven we skipped to it upon the quay with a rope, two turning and one jumping, and Jory of Eldhythe and I turned the rope, for we were the biggest of us and the slowest; and the strangest thing in it, to our minds, was the greeting.

Then the children of the quay sang, as they sing it still:

Grey boat, grey boat, out of the grey, knelt at the warden’s door one day; a stone for a present, a cut for a kiss, and who ever heard of a greeting like this? Burn it and shove it and give it the tide; but the grey boat turned, and the grey boat cried.

There were two lines more, and those we did not sing on the quay; for if a ship-master heard them, he would take the rope out of our hands, and not say why:

Grey boat burning, and after, the rain; and the one it comes for will not come home again.

It was Merrick, eldest of the ship-masters, who took the rope out of our hands, one evening at the turn of the tide, and coiled it on his arm as a man coils a line he means to keep, and said, “Not that one”; and that was all the reason he ever gave, though he had to give it to every child upon the quay. I did not understand it then. I think I do now.

His father was Daveth.

Merrick is sitting by the door tonight, and he has not gone out.

I skipped to that rhyme at seven, and I understood it at forty, on the shingle under this wall, in the first holding of the war, the first time I heard one of their hulls burn. For it screamed, as the outpost men had sworn, and went on screaming when the smoke had hidden it; and all along the wall the old sailors turned their faces from the sea, Merrick first of all, while I stood in the smoke, with the rhyme turning in me like the rope on the quay, and thought of the three grey stones over my grandmother’s fire, and of the pipes knocking upon the lowest.

All that first winter Jory whistled it on the east wall, the tune and never the words, as he had whistled it upon the quay when we were boys; and he meant, when the havens came home, to drink again at my grandmother’s fire. Every morning Merrick told him to stop, and every morning Jory laughed, and said what he always said: “It’s only a rhyme.”

On the morning that he died, in the last weeks of that winter, the tune went along the wall-walk ahead of him, as it used to go along the quay ahead of us both. As a mooring-rope lies slack along the quay at the ebb, and the children take it up to skip with, and the old men let them, till the flood comes in under the hull and lifts it out of the children’s hands, and it stands up out of the water hard as a bar, and hums: so the rhyme stood up hard in me, and I heard the two lines that we never sang. Merrick told him to stop.

I did not tell him to stop.

Ere the tune was done, a fire-ship came burning in upon the stones beneath the east wall, and the tune stopped.

His is the first cairn on the east face.

Tonight, if the walls of my grandmother’s house still stand on the quay at Eldhythe, the three grey stones hang there yet over a cold hearth, under no roof but the rain.

We forget. We have always been good at that. But Mystwood remembers.

This I lay as it was laid for me.

And the hearth said: We remember.

And the Captain put his hood back.

LAID IN THE SECOND SUMMER, DURING THE SECOND TIDE · ABOUT THE SUMMER OF THE GUEST AS THE HARBOUR INNS OF ELDHYTHE TOLD IT; ABOUT VOSS’S CHILDHOOD ON THE DOCK, THIRTY-THREE YEARS BEFORE THE BANNER; AND ABOUT THE FIRST WINTER OF THE SIEGE.

Told by Voss who asked, True Man of the harbour battery since the second winter, the way his grandmother told it to him in the harbour inns of Eldhythe. She kept the fire there and poured the beer for the boatmen. The ship-masters wouldn’t tell this tale. The children wouldn’t stop singing its rhyme. The shore laughed at it. The sea didn’t. The wood’s own page about that boat faces this one.

“Give me the stone. This one isn’t mine. It’s my grandmother’s. She got it from her brother, and he got it from the sea.”

This is how the dock told the story of the burning boat. Hold on to it, because there’s nobody left on the dock to tell it.

In the summer of the burning boat, my grandmother Elspet was a twelve-year-old girl. She kept the fire in her mother’s harbour inn on the dock at Eldhythe, the Eldest Haven. It was one long table under a low roof. The ship-masters came in out of the rain with the weather still in their beards, and hung their coats along the bench to steam. Over the fire, set into the wall since her own grandmother’s time, hung three flat grey stones. They had curling letters on them, as fine as bird tracks on wet sand. Nobody on that whole dock could tell what they said, and as long as the fire burned well, nobody asked. The ship-masters knocked their pipes out on the lowest one.

Back then, our fathers were afraid of the open sea, as those of you born in the havens know. They stayed close to shore and to the sheltered water between one haven and the next. If some braver man ever went out far enough to see the grey lying along the edge of the world, he turned around for home. Then he drank on the story of it in my grandmother’s inn for a week, because on our dock a wild story was as good as money. My grandmother laughed at every one of those men, filled his cup anyway, and said over her shoulder what she said about all their stories: “The Mystlands make liars out of honest men.” She said it about her brother.

Her brother was Daveth, an axeman of the Harvest. That same summer, the summer she was twelve, he was sailing home along the edge of the grey in one of the last timber boats of the Harvest. It sat low in the water, with black wood stacked up to the hatches. Behind it came a second boat with the same load.

Think of the boys on the harbour wall who steal a gull’s eggs and run home with them in their caps. She comes down at them screaming, again and again. You can’t drive her off with stones. She keeps diving at their heads until they drop what they’re carrying. That’s how the burning boat came at the timber boats out of the grey: screaming as it came, into the wind and against the current. It hit them low, where the load was heaviest. First Daveth’s boat, then the second one. Its fire got into the black wood in both their holds, and the black wood caught. Afterward, the rain came down on the water, and on the heads of more than twenty men.

Three came home.

Daveth was one of them, hanging on to a broken mast. He came through his mother’s door that night with seawater running out of his coat onto the floor my grandmother had swept, and he told it. That autumn, the three of them told it in every harbour inn on the dock. None of them ever set foot in a boat again.

Later that year, when the men at the outpost at the end of the land were replaced, they came into the same harbour inns. Drunk, they swore they had pushed a burning boat off their beach with a dead man in it, and that once the tide had carried it well out, it turned around. Above all, they swore to the scream. They also swore that the grey man had said three words in our language before he died. But no two of them could ever agree on which three.

So it was the three from the broken mast who first told about the burning boat, and the outpost men who swore to it afterward. On the dock, the two stories ran together into one. But it was the three who started it.

When I was a boy at that same long table, my grandmother counted the boats for me on her fingers, the way her brother had counted them for her. One finger, then another. Then she laid her hand flat on the table. There were two boats.

Now the ship-masters say twenty.

Year after year the shore laughed at the three from the broken mast, the way the shore laughs at every man who comes back from the grey with a story. In the daytime my grandmother laughed along, and knocked her pipe out on the lowest grey stone. At night she didn’t laugh.

Daveth drank on his story. Within a few years, the harbour drowned him, a stone’s throw from his mother’s house. And why did she tell me that story more than all the others told at her fire? Because her brother was in it.

But in all the years I sat at that fire, no ship-master would ever talk about it. It’s bad luck anywhere within earshot of the sea. I’ve seen a master, a man who had steered through winter storms, put down his cup and walk out into the rain rather than hear it told. Since then I’ve heard three of them talk about it, at fires in the mountain passes, as far from salt water as they’d ever been. Even then they never told it straight. They told it one saying at a time, each one set down like a cup:

“A little boat burning, far out in the grey,” said the first. The second said, “It turned into the wind, and after it came the weather.” The third said, “It’s the boat that comes for you when you die.” Then one of them said, “The shore laughs, because the shore will never have to face it.” And all three finished with the old words of the coast, which they save for that boat above anything else that drifts: “What the grey sends, the fire sends back, or it comes again.”

The whole time, the cities had it in writing. A stranger had come armed, and been put down, and his boat had been burned and set adrift. If any man swore afterward that he’d seen a burning boat on the water, that was the same boat, set adrift, and the man was drunk. And who argues with writing?

But the children had a rhyme about it, and children argue with anything. When I was seven, we skipped rope to it on the dock, two turning and one jumping. Jory of Eldhythe and I turned the rope, because we were the biggest kids and the slowest. To us, the strangest part of it was the greeting.

Then the children of the dock sang, as they still sing it today:

Grey boat, grey boat, out of the grey, knelt at the warden’s door one day. A stone for a present, a cut for a kiss. Who ever heard of a hello like this? Burn it and push it and give it to the tide. But the grey boat turned, and the grey boat cried.

There were two more lines. We didn’t sing those on the dock. If a ship-master heard them, he’d take the rope out of our hands and not say why:

Grey boat burning, and after it, the rain. And the one it comes for won’t come home again.

It was Merrick, the oldest of the ship-masters, who took the rope out of our hands one evening at the turn of the tide. He coiled it on his arm, like a man coiling a line he means to keep, and said, “Not that one.” That was the only reason he ever gave, even though he had to give it to every child on the dock. I didn’t understand it then. I think I do now.

His father was Daveth.

Merrick is sitting by the door tonight, and he hasn’t walked out.

I skipped to that rhyme at seven. I understood it at forty, on the beach under this wall, in the first holding of the war, the first time I heard one of their ships burn. It screamed, just as the outpost men had sworn. It kept screaming after the smoke had hidden it. All along the wall, the old sailors turned their faces away from the sea, Merrick first of all. I stood in the smoke with the rhyme turning inside me like the rope on the dock. I thought of the three grey stones over my grandmother’s fire, and the pipes knocking on the lowest one.

All that first winter, Jory whistled it on the east wall. Just the tune, never the words, the way he used to whistle it on the dock when we were boys. He planned to drink again at my grandmother’s fire when the havens came home. Every morning Merrick told him to stop. Every morning Jory laughed and said what he always said: “It’s just a rhyme.”

On the morning he died, in the last weeks of that winter, the tune went along the walkway ahead of him, the way it used to go along the dock ahead of both of us. Think of a mooring rope lying loose on the dock at low tide. The children pick it up to skip with, and the old men let them. Then the tide comes in under the boat and lifts it, and the rope is pulled out of the children’s hands. It rises out of the water, tight as an iron bar, and hums. That’s how the rhyme rose up tight inside me, and I heard the two lines we never sang. Merrick told him to stop.

I didn’t tell him to stop.

Before the tune was finished, a fire-ship came burning onto the rocks under the east wall, and the tune stopped.

His is the first grave on the east face.

Tonight, if the walls of my grandmother’s house are still standing on the dock at Eldhythe, the three grey stones are still hanging there over a cold fireplace, with no roof but the rain.

We forget. We’ve always been good at that. But Mystwood remembers.

I have laid this tale the way it was laid for me.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

And the Captain pushed his hood back.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

IV.6 · The Voyage of the AelvarenWood leaf

KNOWN IN THE THIRD SUMMER · OF THE AELVAREN’S VOYAGE HOME ON THE EVENING OF THE GUEST’S DEATH, SEVENTY-FIVE YEARS BEFORE THE BANNER, AND OF THE NIGHT IT CAME HOME.

Known from the heart of a Standard-Bearer of the Tide of Remembering, which Halvard took up in the vault while Rhyna stood on the wall; and in that same breath, say the soldiers who stood beside her, she thrust both her hands into the rain-butt, and would not say why. Every hull grown since holds this in some measure, the two of them say, yet none so clear as this heart; and it was quickly told, for grief is the one thing in the wood for which the shore has words enough. The wood says two. The ship-masters say twenty. I have kept both. One thing more the wood cannot say of the council that judged it, and so I say it, who heard the words of Brenn: They did not know it was ignorance. Told by Halyna, by turns, in two inks, and set down by me.

This is held in the grain.

And not in this grain only. The hull that brings it to you was not there; yet it knows, for it grew out of what we were.

We were the Aelvaren, the Bearer of the Binding, and proud of the name: a small boat of the old wood, grey and light. Through the thin breath we bore Aelrhen to your shore, and he went up from us to the door. All that morning we lay in the shallows, and waited to carry him home with the rite made.

Before evening Aelrhen came back to us broken; and we bore him home burning, and twice on that road we struck.

The men of that house dragged him down to us by the heels. They threw him into us, as men throw a net they mean to burn; and his blood ran into our boards. What a child of the long-lived knows, in his last hour, goes with his blood into the wood that drinks it.

So we had all of it.

Then the men of that house put fire to us. In its grief the old wood raged, and burned higher than their fire, and screamed, for wood can. With poles they shoved us off, and the current took us out toward the open sea, away from home.

Against the current we turned, burning, for no water holds a keel that knows its way; and we sailed ourselves home.

Even charred, even dying, the wood knows the way home.

Twice on that road, where the breath thins at its edge, hulls of the shore-men lay across our way. Heavy they rode, and low, with the iron in them, and the cut wood of our kin stacked in their holds.

We went into them burning, low, where they were heaviest. The iron had gone into us; now we went into the iron. Our kin in their holds took our fire, and both hulls broke and burned.

Of the men in them the grain holds nothing.

Under the thinnest of the breath we found the old channels, the hidden waters among the roots, where no keel of the shore-men has gone. All that way we burned, and the fire went out of us slowly under the closing breath. Of us there was left a keel and a few ribs, and in them the bones of Aelrhen.

In the night we came home; and the long-lived of the council judged what we carried, and laid our keel among the roots.

Black and small we came up out of the channels onto the pale sand, and under the silver elders we were still. The long-lived of the council came down to us, and knew us, for they had sent us.

They laid their hands on our keel, many hands together. They knew the gift set down, and the foot that put it in the dust. They knew the neck held gently, and the foreheads, and the blade. They knew the blows, the heels and the throwing, the fire and the poles. Wood can hide nothing.

They saw their holiest peace answered with murder and flame. They knew a murder, and it was one. But why the host’s arm drew back from the blade, they did not know, for fear leaves no mark on wood. Nor did they ask.

They saw only an act of war.

Our keel the council laid among the roots of the silver elders, with the bones of Aelrhen in its ribs. So the long-lived lay down their dead; and the roots drank.

From that grove the knowing went to every grove, root to root, to the far edge of the world. Every young tree that was to be a hull drank it with the rain. So every hull that has come against your wall has carried our grief in it; and the grain holds it still.

And no one answered.

KNOWN IN THE THIRD SUMMER · ABOUT THE AELVAREN’S VOYAGE HOME ON THE EVENING OF THE GUEST’S DEATH, SEVENTY-FIVE YEARS BEFORE THE BANNER, AND THE NIGHT IT CAME HOME.

Known from the heart of a Standard-Bearer from the Tide of Remembering. Halvard picked it up in the vault while Rhyna was standing on the wall. At that same moment, say the soldiers who were standing next to her, she shoved both her hands into the rain barrel, and wouldn’t say why. Every ship grown since then holds this in some amount, the two of them say, but none as clearly as this heart. It didn’t take long to tell, because grief is the one thing in the wood the shore has enough words for. The wood says two boats. The ship-masters say twenty. I have kept both. There’s one more thing the wood can’t say about the council that judged it, so I’ll say it, since I heard Brenn’s words: They did not know it was ignorance. Told by Halyna, taking turns, in two inks, and written down by me.

This is held in the grain.

And not only in this grain. The ship that brings it to you wasn’t there. But it knows, because it grew out of what we were.

We were the Aelvaren, the Bearer of the Binding, and we were proud of the name. We were a small boat of the old wood, grey and light. Through the thin breath, we carried Aelrhen to your shore, and he walked up from us to the door. All that morning we lay in the shallow water and waited to carry him home with the ceremony done.

Before evening, Aelrhen came back to us broken. We carried him home burning. Twice on that road, we struck.

The men of that house dragged him down to us by his heels. They threw him into us, the way men throw in a net they plan to burn. His blood ran into our boards. Whatever a child of the long-lived knows in his last hour goes with his blood into the wood that drinks it.

So we had all of it.

Then the men of that house set fire to us. In its grief, the old wood raged. It burned higher than their fire, and it screamed, because wood can. They pushed us off with poles, and the current took us out toward the open sea, away from home.

We turned against the current, burning. No water can hold a keel that knows its way. We sailed ourselves home.

Even burnt, even dying, the wood knows the way home.

Twice on that road, where the breath thins at its edge, ships of the shore-men lay across our path. They rode heavy and low, with the iron in them, and the cut wood of our family stacked in their holds.

We rammed into them burning, low, where they were heaviest. The iron had gone into us. Now we went into the iron. Our family in their holds caught our fire, and both ships broke apart and burned.

The grain holds nothing about the men in them.

Under the thinnest part of the breath, we found the old channels, the hidden waters among the roots, where no shore-man’s keel has ever gone. We burned the whole way. The fire went out of us slowly under the closing breath. All that was left of us was a keel and a few ribs, and inside them, the bones of Aelrhen.

We came home in the night. The long-lived of the council judged what we carried, and laid our keel among the roots.

We came up out of the channels onto the pale sand, black and small. Under the silver elders, we went still. The long-lived of the council came down to us, and knew us, because they had sent us.

They put their hands on our keel, many hands together. They knew about the gift set down, and the foot that kicked it into the dirt. They knew about the neck held gently, the foreheads, and the blade. They knew about the blows, the heels and the throwing, the fire and the poles. Wood can’t hide anything.

They saw their holiest peace answered with murder and fire. They knew it was a murder, and it was one. But they didn’t know why the host’s arm pulled back from the blade, because fear leaves no mark on wood. And they didn’t ask.

They saw only an act of war.

The council laid our keel among the roots of the silver elders, with Aelrhen’s bones in its ribs. That’s how the long-lived lay down their dead. And the roots drank.

From that grove, the knowing spread to every grove, root to root, to the far edge of the world. Every young tree that was going to become a ship drank it in with the rain. So every ship that has come against your wall has carried our grief inside it. And the grain still holds it.

And no one answered.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

BOOK FIVE · THE BOOK OF THE TIDES

From the vote under the thin sky, seventy-five years before the banner, to the sixth winter of the siege: a doom set in motion by the dead, crewed by the living, against a people who had never known they were the foe; a Tide each winter, and how each side learned the other at the wall.

V.1 · The First GlyphStone leaf

LAID AT THE END OF THE FIRST MONTH OF THE SIEGE · OF THE NIGHT THE FIRST ASSAULT WAS BROKEN, ABOUT THE TWENTY-FOURTH NIGHT AFTER THE BANNER.

Laid by Seren Two-Inks, who held the lamp, a few nights after the holding that it tells of, and on the third night that this hearth ever heard a tale. The word stands upon its leaf as I wrote it that night on the shingle, alone in the middle of the page, and the sign is drawn on the leaf that faces it, in my hand; and I have mended neither.

“Hold the stone, Seren. I tell myself so every time.”

Hear now how the first word came up out of the wood, and hold it, for it was the first.

On the shingle Halyna, the two of them, held the wood, and I held the lamp; and as it began, so it has gone on, with their four hands on the grain, and my one hand on the lamp.

It was the night the first assault was broken, in the first month of the siege, and in under this wall the tide was bringing the wreck-wood: grey planks and broken ribs, and a length of keel as long as a gate, rolling over and over in the shallows, as though the sea were turning each piece to look upon its underside.

The soldiers would have burned it all upon the tide-line, by the old way of the coast that every man of them had from his father; and as they dragged the first of it up the stones, they said the old words over it: “What the grey sends, the fire sends back, or it comes again.” So the fires went up along the tide-line one after another, as the lamps go up along a quay at dusk; and the Captain of the Torn Cloak let them burn the most of it, and stood among them, and was still.

While the first fires were catching, Hale the runner came up from the water’s edge, where the runners walk after every holding to see what the sea has brought; and he was seventeen, and had run his first holding that same morning. Past the fires he went, and the men about them, and past the Captain, to Halyna, to Halvard Stone-Warden and Rhyna of the Inner Gate, where the two of them stood at the foot of the wall; and he said what he had seen in three words, as a runner says it, and no more:

“Marks on the wood.”

At the turn of the tide Halvard and Rhyna went down the shingle together, slow, his hand beneath her elbow on the wet stones, and I came behind them with the lamp, for I was their third, and the light was mine to carry. The men hauled one plank up out of the surf for them and laid it on the stones, and I set the lamp down beside it, and we looked. Upon it, running with the grain, fine and shallow as the first line a mason scores across a dressed face, was a carving: one figure, and no more. And one long edge of the plank was raw, split down the length of the grain, as if the rest of it had been torn away.

Of things that might speak to us, we had three that night: the mute stones of the archive chest, which two old archivists of Eldhythe carried out of its burning, and I carried up the mountain after them, and which said nothing to anyone; a bundle in that same chest, sealed with the mark of those two, Wendhessa, which no pair of the guild would break; and now this plank. But the axemen of the felling had left a saying in the havens, that the black wood would sometimes put a thought into a man’s hand, if he rested it there long enough. Nothing else was left us to try.

So Rhyna sat down on the wet shingle beside the plank, as a mother sits down by a sick child in the night, and laid her two palms flat upon the grain; and Halvard, standing behind her, bent and laid his hands over hers. They did not speak. The tide came and went on the shingle. The lamp burned down a finger’s width.

Then came the knowing up into Rhyna’s palms; and it burned, she says, not as fire burns, but as cold iron burns in the deep of the winter, when the smith’s boy takes it up bare-handed and cannot let it go. The sign’s own shape was in the burning, one stroke curling forward, like a wave about to break; and in that same breath, with no word from her, Halvard lifted his head.

“Shore,” he said.

Rhyna said that he was right.

Halvard could not say, then or after, how he knew it; it was as though someone had put the word into his mouth, he said, and gone away. They knew more than the word, the two of them, but the rest would not go into words, for it was a forest’s thought, and they had only the shore’s; and though they held the plank till the lamp was low, one word was all that they could tell.

When the two of them had let go, and risen stiff from the stones, I sat down where Rhyna had sat, with the lamp at my knee and the plank cold and wet in the light of it, and laid my own palm upon the grain, alone. As a girl who carries water to the masons on a hot day, a cup at a time, stoops at the river’s edge to fill her cup, and the river is in flood, and takes the cup out of her hand, and very nearly the hand: so something came up into my palm out of the wood, too great for me to hold.

I let go.

Halvard put the lamp back in my hand.

After that, while the fires burned down along the tide-line, others came down the shingle to try the plank, for word had gone up the wall that the wood had spoken. Jory of Eldhythe came first, a soldier of the east wall, who is sitting at the back of this hall tonight beside Voss; and he laid his hand upon the wood, laughing, and said, “Wet planks,” and went back up the shingle whistling a tune the old ship-masters hate.

Last came Enno and Della, the two children of the guild who walked up the mountain hand in hand, and laid their four hands upon the plank together; and he said the wood was cold, and she said it was not, and both of them were right, and that was all it would give them.

Before we went up, I sat on the shingle by the plank with this Book upon my knees, and opened it at a clean leaf, though leaves were dear to me then and I had few. One word I set in the middle of the leaf, shore, and nothing else; but ere the ink touched the page I said it over three times, as I do with every word, to hear whether it would hold. It held, for it is short and square, and it sits in the mouth as a dressed stone sits in its bed of mortar. So one word had the whole of a leaf, and the whole of the leaf held one word. On the facing leaf I drew the sign as it ran on the plank, one stroke curling forward, with the lamp held close; and in the sea air the ink would not dry.

The word looked very small on its leaf.

When we came up from the shingle that night, with the fires burning low behind us on the tide-line, and the one plank going up ahead of us on the shoulders of two soldiers, Halvard and Rhyna walked a little before me; and ere we came to the gate, one of them said, and the other finished:

“The grey man of the old tale came to a door with three words, and no sentence.”

“Now it is we who have one word, and no sentence. The right sign, known truly; and a sign without a sentence is only a mark.”

Then the two of them went up into the hall together, and I came behind them with the lamp.

This I lay as it was laid for me.

And the hearth said: We remember.

The facing leaf holds the sign, drawn in Seren’s hand: one stroke, curling forward, like a wave about to break.

LAID AT THE END OF THE FIRST MONTH OF THE SIEGE · ABOUT THE NIGHT THE FIRST ATTACK WAS BEATEN, AROUND THE TWENTY-FOURTH NIGHT AFTER THE BANNER.

Told by Seren Two-Inks, who held the lamp, a few nights after the holding it describes. It was the third night this hearth ever heard a tale. The word sits on its page the way I wrote it that night on the beach, alone in the middle of the page. The sign is drawn on the page facing it, in my handwriting. I haven’t fixed either one.

“Hold the stone, Seren. I tell myself that every time.”

This is how the first word came up out of the wood. Hold on to it. It was the first.

On the beach, Halyna, the two of them, held the wood, and I held the lamp. That’s how it started, and that’s how it has gone ever since: their four hands on the grain, and my one hand on the lamp.

It was the night the first attack was beaten, in the first month of the siege. Down below this wall, the tide was bringing in wreck-wood: grey planks, broken ribs, and a piece of keel as long as a gate. It rolled over and over in the shallows, as if the sea were turning each piece over to look at its bottom.

The soldiers wanted to burn all of it on the beach, the old way of the coast, which every one of them had learned from his father. As they dragged the first pieces up the stones, they said the old words over it: “What the grey sends, the fire sends back, or it comes again.” So the fires went up along the waterline one after another, the way lamps get lit along a dock at dusk. The Captain of the Torn Cloak let them burn most of it. He stood among them, still.

While the first fires were catching, Hale the runner came up from the water’s edge, where the runners walk after every holding to see what the sea has brought. He was seventeen, and he had run his first holding that same morning. He walked past the fires and the men around them, and past the Captain. He went to Halyna, to Halvard Stone-Warden and Rhyna of the Inner Gate, where the two of them stood at the foot of the wall. He told them what he had seen in three words, the way a runner does, and nothing more:

“Marks on the wood.”

When the tide turned, Halvard and Rhyna went down the beach together, slowly, his hand under her elbow on the wet stones. I followed them with the lamp, because I was their third, and carrying the light was my job. The men pulled one plank out of the surf for them and laid it on the stones. I set the lamp down next to it, and we looked. On it, running along the grain, as fine and shallow as the first line a mason scratches across a smooth stone, was a carving. Just one shape. One long edge of the plank was raw, split down the length of the grain, as if the rest of it had been torn off.

We had three things that night that might speak to us. First, the silent stones in the archive chest, which two old archivists of Eldhythe carried out of the burning city, and which I carried up the mountain after them. They had never said anything to anyone. Second, a bundle in that same chest, sealed with the mark of those two, Wendhessa. No pair in the guild would break it. And now, third, this plank. The axemen of the Harvest had left a saying behind in the havens: the black wood would sometimes put a thought into a man’s hand if he rested it there long enough. We had nothing else left to try.

So Rhyna sat down on the wet beach next to the plank, the way a mother sits down next to a sick child at night, and laid her two hands flat on the grain. Halvard stood behind her, bent down, and laid his hands over hers. They didn’t speak. The tide came and went on the stones. The lamp burned down a finger’s width.

Then the knowing came up into Rhyna’s palms. She says it burned, but not the way fire burns. It burned the way cold iron burns in the middle of winter, when the blacksmith’s boy picks it up bare-handed and can’t let go. The shape of the sign was in the burning: one stroke curling forward, like a wave about to break. And at that same moment, without a word from her, Halvard raised his head.

“Shore,” he said.

Rhyna said he was right.

Halvard couldn’t say, then or later, how he knew it. He said it was as if someone had put the word in his mouth and walked away. The two of them knew more than the word, but the rest wouldn’t go into words. It was a forest’s thought, and they only had the shore’s words. They held the plank until the lamp was low, but one word was all they could tell.

When the two of them had let go and gotten up stiffly from the stones, I sat down where Rhyna had sat. I had the lamp by my knee, and the plank was cold and wet in its light. I put my own hand on the grain, alone. Think of a girl carrying water to the masons on a hot day, one cup at a time. She bends down at the river to fill her cup, but the river is flooding, and it pulls the cup right out of her hand, and almost takes the hand too. That’s what came up into my palm out of the wood: something far too big for me to hold.

I let go.

Halvard put the lamp back in my hand.

After that, while the fires burned down along the beach, other people came down to try the plank, because word had gone up the wall that the wood had spoken. Jory of Eldhythe came first, a soldier of the east wall. He’s sitting at the back of this hall tonight next to Voss. He put his hand on the wood, laughing, and said, “Wet planks.” Then he went back up the beach whistling a tune the old ship-masters hate.

Last came Enno and Della, the two children of the guild who walked up the mountain holding hands. They put their four hands on the plank together. He said the wood was cold. She said it wasn’t. Both of them were right, and that was all it would give them.

Before we went back up, I sat on the beach by the plank with this Book on my knees and opened it to a clean page. Pages were precious to me then, and I didn’t have many. I wrote one word in the middle of the page, shore, and nothing else. But before the ink touched the page, I said it three times, the way I do with every word, to hear if it would hold. It held. It’s short and square, and it sits in the mouth the way a cut stone sits in its bed of mortar. So one word had a whole page, and the whole page held one word. On the facing page I drew the sign the way it ran on the plank, one stroke curling forward, with the lamp held close. In the sea air, the ink wouldn’t dry.

The word looked very small on its page.

We came up from the beach that night with the fires burning low behind us. Two soldiers carried the one plank ahead of us on their shoulders. Halvard and Rhyna walked a little in front of me. Before we got to the gate, one of them said, and the other finished:

“The grey man in the old story came to a door with three words and no sentence.”

“Now we’re the ones with one word and no sentence. The right sign, truly known. And a sign without a sentence is just a mark.”

Then the two of them went up into the hall together, and I followed them with the lamp.

I have laid this tale the way it was laid for me.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

The facing page holds the sign, drawn in Seren’s hand: one stroke, curling forward, like a wave about to break.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

V.2 · The Night of the NamingStone leaf

LAID LATE IN THE SECOND SUMMER OF THE SIEGE, NEAR THE END OF THE SECOND TIDE · OF THE FIRST WINTER TO THAT NIGHT.

Laid by Seren Two-Inks, who held the lamp, at this hearth on the evening after that night. The planks I tell of lie in the lowest vault still, and anyone may go down and put a hand on them. The wood’s leaf faces it.

“Hold the stone, Seren. I tell myself so every time.”

Hear now how the planks that lie beneath this hall, under the floor where you sit, were named in a single night from the first of them to the last, and what the young wood had carried against our wall.

From the night of the first glyph we kept the wreck-wood, or as much of it as the soldiers’ fires and the tide would leave us; and the runners carried home every plank that bore a carving, two of them to a plank and the salt running down their arms, and we bore each one down, stair below stair, to the lowest vault under this hall, beside the foot of the shaft where the Rite had lain in the dark since the First Days, and laid it in its row upon the floor. The soldiers called them the tablets. They were only planks. By the end of the Hasty Tide fifty-three lay there, grey and salt-whitened, with the smell of the sea still in the grain; and every one save one had given Halyna, the two of them, a single sign, and each word I had written on a leaf of its own. The one had given nothing at all, though it was carved as finely as the rest.

Through all the Second Tide, when the hulls ran in pairs and their planks came in with two signs apiece, cut close together along the grain as names are cut close upon an old lintel, Halvard and Rhyna learned upon the shingle to hold the two signs together and to name them, the one hand of theirs or the other, and seldom both, for what the one of them knows, the other knows. I stood behind them with the lamp, while the cold of the stones came up through my shoes; and there were gaps in every telling, and I have not mended them with guesses. And all that while the fifty-three lay below us in the dark, each with its one word, and no one went down the stair.

On a night late in that Tide, at the end of the second summer, when the dark comes late to the ridge and goes early, Halvard and Rhyna went down to the lowest vault to try the old planks again, and I went behind them with the ink and the lamps. It is the oldest room beneath this hall, hewn out of the living ridge by the first builders, who went down from it afterward to raise the first of the havens, and its cold is the same in summer as in the deep of the winter; and that night, from the foot of the stair to the far wall, the rows lay upon its floor, grey upon grey, like a field the plough has turned up stony and left. The night was short and the rows were long, so they began at the two ends at once, Rhyna at the foot of the stair and Halvard at the far wall. It was the first time I had seen them work apart, and they were not far apart: the length of the vault.

Rhyna took up the first plank of all, the wave-sign that had said shore to her palms upon the shingle, with its one long edge raw and split along the grain, as if the rest of it had been torn away. At the far end, where it had been laid apart from the others, Halvard took up the one plank that had given nothing, not so much as a word, which the sea had brought in some weeks after the first. I stood between them in the middle of the vault with the lamp; and there is no wind so far beneath the hall, and the flame stood up straight in my hand.

As when a net-mender on the quay at evening, with the whole wet length of a net laid out along the stones, stoops and lifts one corner of it to look for the tear, and far off down the quay, beyond the reach of his arm, the other corner stirs upon the stones and rises with it, for it is all one net, and knot holds knot to the end of it: so Rhyna at the foot of the stair lifted her plank, and Halvard at the far wall lifted his.

Their two faces changed in the same moment.

Then Rhyna spoke, and it was not a word. It was a whole order, the first of all the orders I have written in this Book; and from the far end of the vault Halvard said it with her, word for word, in the one breath.

After that every plank gave up its whole order, whichever of the two held it, as if the wood, once it had come whole in them, had shown them how it is held. Halvard and Rhyna went along the rows toward each other all that night, plank after plank, and I went between them with the lamp and the ink and wrote each order under its word; and every order I said over three times ere I set it down, as I do with every word, to hear whether it would hold, and every one of them held. By the middle rows my hand ached to the wrist, and I did not stop. Here they are as I wrote them, for they were the first carvings of the grey that we ever truly knew:

To shore. As root to water, go. Wait on no bough. That was the wave-sign, the first glyph of all: the hulls of the first winter that ran straight at the coast, each by itself, and none of them waiting upon another.

Break wall. First stone, only stone. Strike till it opens or wood is ash. That was the closed square, which is how they cut the word for stone, the mute thing: the hulls that drew up before the first wall they touched, and would not leave it till they sank.

Go until struck. Where bark splits, turn aside. That was the lone hull that ran in and swerved from the first shell, while the rest held on and did not follow it.

Before bearer, thin wood. Thin wood falls; bearer keeps pace. Those were the cheap hulls at the head of the column. They were told to die first, and they died first.

Where it flashed, strike. Last flash, only flash. Those were the hulls that bent, each one of them, toward whichever gun of ours had last spoken, and toward nothing else upon the wall.

Wait in grey, as seed waits out frost. When stone is still, come. Those were the ones that hung at the edge of the grey and came in late, when we had done with looking; and it was one of these that hung back upon the first morning of the war, and Hale the runner ran it to the Captain.

Home when wounded. One bough falls; all turn, each by its own water. Those were the ones that turned at the first loss and scattered, every hull of them by its own road into the grey.

When Rhyna and Halvard met in the middle of the vault, the grey of the morning was coming down the stair, and the lamps had burned low. One plank more lay at the end of the last row, by itself, and it was not from our wall at all. A mason of Emberhythe, the forge on the burning shore, where the ground is warm underfoot in winter, had carried it up the mountain in his pack after the Fall, charred black at the one end, for he could not bear to bring nothing of his city away; and what it was, he did not know.

Halyna held it last, he and she, a hand each upon the grain, while the lamps went pale in the daylight.

It said Burn.

No one in the vault spoke, and for a long while I did not write. Then Halvard laid it back where it had lain, at the end of the last row, by itself.

That was all the young wood knew: one word to a hull, and in that word one order, and no other. Each hull had said its word at our wall, and said it again, and said it until it drowned.

All that first winter, at this fire, we had said that the grey sails had picked the wrong fortress; Kael the Counter said it first, and we all said it after him, and laughed, and I, who was yet a child, laughed with the rest. They had not picked anything.

The grey sails had one order apiece, and no other; and they had been sent.

When it was full light, or as full as it comes so far beneath the hall, I took up the first plank of all from the foot of the stair, and the silent plank from the far wall, and laid them side by side upon the floor, raw edge to raw edge; for all that night, between the orders, my eyes had gone back and back to those two raw edges. The daylight came down the stair as far as the third row, and no farther.

The broken grain met.

It met as the two halves of a split log meet. Halvard and Rhyna looked down at it with me, and neither of them spoke; and I have not asked them what it means, and they have not told me.

Then Halyna sat down on the floor of the vault among the planks, he and she, and I sat down with them; and for a long while none of us spoke, and the light lay upon the stair, and the lamps burned on beside the rows, and no one put them out. At last I said what I have written here, and the two of them let it stand:

“We had called the grey sails fools. I think they were the youngest of the wood, sent first, for the young grow quickest. The worst of the wood is not behind us. It is still growing.”

This I lay as it was laid for me.

And the hearth said: We remember.

LAID LATE IN THE SECOND SUMMER OF THE SIEGE, NEAR THE END OF THE SECOND TIDE · ABOUT THE TIME FROM THE FIRST WINTER UP TO THAT NIGHT.

Told by Seren Two-Inks, who held the lamp, at this hearth on the evening after that night. The planks I describe are still in the lowest vault, and anyone can go down and put a hand on them. The wood’s page faces this one.

“Hold the stone, Seren. I tell myself that every time.”

This is how the planks under this hall, under the floor you’re sitting on, were all named in a single night, from the first to the last. It’s also about what the young wood had carried against our wall.

From the night of the first glyph, we kept the wreck-wood, or as much of it as the soldiers’ fires and the tide left us. The runners carried home every plank that had a carving on it, two runners to a plank, with salt water running down their arms. We carried each one down, stair after stair, to the lowest vault under this hall. It’s next to the bottom of the shaft where the Rite had lain in the dark since the First Days. There we laid each plank in its row on the floor. The soldiers called them the tablets. They were just planks. By the end of the Hasty Tide, fifty-three of them lay there, grey and bleached by salt, still smelling of the sea. Every one except one had given Halyna, the two of them, a single sign, and I had written each word on its own page. The one plank had given nothing at all, even though it was carved as finely as the others.

All through the Second Tide, the ships came in pairs, and their planks came in with two signs each, cut close together along the grain, the way names are cut close together on an old doorway. On the beach, Halvard and Rhyna learned to hold the two signs together and name them. Usually one of them did it, not both, because whatever one knows, the other knows. I stood behind them with the lamp while the cold of the stones came up through my shoes. There were gaps in every telling, and I haven’t filled them with guesses. And all that time the fifty-three lay below us in the dark, each with its one word, and nobody went down the stairs.

One night late in that Tide, at the end of the second summer, Halvard and Rhyna went down to the lowest vault to try the old planks again. It was the time of year when the dark comes late to the ridge and leaves early. I followed them with the ink and the lamps. It’s the oldest room under this hall. The first builders cut it out of the solid ridge before they went down to build the first of the havens. It’s just as cold in summer as in the middle of winter. That night, from the bottom of the stairs to the far wall, the rows lay across the floor, grey on grey, like a field the plough has turned up full of stones and then left. The night was short and the rows were long, so they started at both ends at once: Rhyna at the bottom of the stairs, and Halvard at the far wall. It was the first time I’d seen them work apart. They weren’t far apart. Just the length of the vault.

Rhyna picked up the very first plank, the wave-sign that had said shore to her hands on the beach. Its one long edge was raw and split along the grain, as if the rest of it had been torn away. At the far end, where it had been laid apart from the others, Halvard picked up the one plank that had given nothing, not even a word. The sea had brought it in a few weeks after the first. I stood between them in the middle of the vault with the lamp. There’s no wind that far under the hall, and the flame stood straight up in my hand.

Think of a man mending nets on the dock in the evening. The whole wet net is spread out along the stones. He bends down and lifts one corner to look for the tear. And far down the dock, out of his reach, the other corner moves on the stones and lifts too, because it’s all one net, and every knot holds the next knot all the way to the end. That’s how Rhyna at the bottom of the stairs lifted her plank, and Halvard at the far wall lifted his.

Both their faces changed at the same moment.

Then Rhyna spoke, and it wasn’t a word. It was a whole order, the first of all the orders I’ve written in this Book. From the far end of the vault, Halvard said it with her, word for word, in the same breath.

After that, every plank gave up its whole order, whichever of the two of them held it. It was as if the wood, once it had come to them whole, had shown them how to hold it. Halvard and Rhyna worked along the rows toward each other all night long, plank after plank. I went back and forth between them with the lamp and the ink and wrote each order under its word. I said every order three times before I wrote it down, the way I do with every word, to hear if it would hold. Every one held. By the middle rows my hand ached up to the wrist, and I didn’t stop. Here they are as I wrote them. They were the first carvings from the grey we ever truly understood:

To shore. Go the way a root goes to water. Wait for no branch. That was the wave-sign, the very first glyph. It belonged to the ships of the first winter that ran straight at the coast, each one on its own, none of them waiting for another.

Break the wall. The first stone is the only stone. Strike until it opens or the wood is ash. That was the closed square, which is how they carve the word for stone, the silent thing. It belonged to the ships that pulled up in front of the first wall they touched and wouldn’t leave it until they sank.

Go until you are hit. Where the bark splits, turn away. That was the single ship that ran in and swerved away from the first shell, while the rest kept going and didn’t follow it.

Thin wood goes ahead of the bearer. The thin wood falls. The bearer keeps up. Those were the cheap ships at the front of the column. They were told to die first, and they died first.

Strike where it flashed. The last flash is the only flash. Those were the ships that each turned toward whichever of our guns had fired last, and toward nothing else on the wall.

Wait in the grey, the way a seed waits out the frost. When the stone is quiet, come. Those were the ones that waited at the edge of the grey and came in late, after we’d stopped looking. It was one of these that hung back on the first morning of the war, the one Hale the runner ran to the Captain.

Go home when wounded. When one branch falls, all turn, each by its own water. Those were the ones that turned and scattered at the first loss, every ship taking its own way back into the grey.

When Rhyna and Halvard met in the middle of the vault, the grey morning light was coming down the stairs, and the lamps had burned low. One more plank lay at the end of the last row, by itself. It wasn’t from our wall at all. A mason from Emberhythe, the forge on the burning shore, where the ground is warm even in winter, had carried it up the mountain in his pack after the Fall. It was burnt black at one end. He couldn’t stand to leave his city with nothing. He didn’t know what it was.

Halyna held it last, he and she, one hand each on the grain, while the lamps went pale in the daylight.

It said Burn.

Nobody in the vault spoke. For a long time I didn’t write. Then Halvard put it back where it had been, at the end of the last row, by itself.

That was all the young wood knew. One word to a ship, and in that word one order, and nothing else. Each ship had said its word at our wall, and said it again, and kept saying it until it sank.

All that first winter, at this fire, we had said the grey sails had picked the wrong fortress. Kael the Counter said it first, and we all said it after him, and laughed. I was still a child, and I laughed with everyone else. They hadn’t picked anything.

The grey sails had one order each, and nothing else. And someone had sent them.

When it was full daylight, or as full as it gets that far under the hall, I picked up the very first plank from the bottom of the stairs and the silent plank from the far wall. I laid them side by side on the floor, raw edge to raw edge. All night long, between the orders, my eyes kept going back to those two raw edges. The daylight came down the stairs as far as the third row, and no further.

The broken grain matched.

It fit together the way the two halves of a split log fit together. Halvard and Rhyna looked down at it with me, and neither of them spoke. I haven’t asked them what it means, and they haven’t told me.

Then Halyna sat down on the floor of the vault among the planks, he and she, and I sat down with them. For a long time none of us spoke. The light lay on the stairs, the lamps kept burning beside the rows, and nobody put them out. Finally I said what I’ve written here, and the two of them let it stand:

“We called the grey sails fools. I think they were the youngest of the wood, sent first, because the young grow fastest. The worst of the wood isn’t behind us. It’s still growing.”

I have laid this tale the way it was laid for me.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

V.3 · The Wreck That Went HomeWood leaf

KNOWN DEEP IN THE THIRD WINTER · OF TWO COMINGS OF ONE HULL, IN THE SECOND MONTH OF THE TIDE OF REMEMBERING.
