Known from a hull of the Tide of Remembering, which was sunk at our wall on its second coming, and which the surf, long after, brought in to the shingle. Halvard’s hands were on it; and Rhyna, who had the salt-cuts open in her palms that day and did not touch it, knew it as he knew it. Of the wound that it carried home, which the wood says its kin felt in their own boards, the two of them say this: that it went among the hulls as the Bonded feel each other’s hurt. Short was the hull’s own tale, and quickly told. Told by Halyna, by turns, in two inks, and set down by me.

This is held in the grain.

We were a hull of the Ralenthae, the tide the roots taught. Cut in us was more than a word: a memory, and a caution. Now we are broken wood, and the salt is in every board.

In one cold season we came twice to your wall: once we went home wounded, and told; once we came back to look, and died looking.

At first light on the first coming, our kin were about us, and the bearer at our head. A stone of your wall flashed. The green wood had learned that, and the roots had carried it home: where a stone flashes, there is a gun.

Toward the stone we turned. It spoke again. The iron went into us, low and through, and in came the sea.

Then woke in us an older carving, one of the first that the green wood was given: Home when wounded. We turned our stern to your wall, and went back into the breath, low and slow. The guns let us go, or did not see us go. We do not know which.

As we went, we told our kin, for the wood needs no words among its own. The flash, and the place of it, went out of us into the roots. Where we were struck went out of us too; and our kin felt it in their own boards.

Last of all went the dread, and the dread is only this: one place in the water has become a place where wood dies. Slowly it went to every hull of that coming, as sap goes, quickest to the heart of the bearer, and from the heart to all.

In the night, while you mended your wall, the heart carved the morrow into us: Look before you trust. A stone that spoke yesterday may be silent today; and a silent stone may be waiting.

So on the next coming we went out before our kin, alone and slow, for we knew that place. To see whether your wall still flashed there, we were sent. That was all we were sent to do: to look.

We threw one fire at the stone that had spoken, to see whether it would answer. Of what our fire found there, the grain holds nothing. The stone did not answer.

Our kin stood off behind us in the breath, and waited on what we would find. The water lay flat, and we were slow.

Your wall still flashed.

With the sea in us already, we could not turn a second time. The iron found us where it had found us before, low and through, as though it too kept a count.

On that second morning we went down in the lee of your wall. Long we lay in the cold, under the keels of our kin. Then the tide worked us loose, and the surf brought us to your shingle. Your hands are on us now.

But ere we went down, we had told it. The hulls that lived carried it back through the breath. From the edge it went home, root to root, to the groves where the next hulls stood growing. They grew knowing it.

So we went home once, and told; we came back to look, and died looking. The groves have it now, and the grain holds it still.

And no one answered.

KNOWN DEEP IN THE THIRD WINTER · ABOUT TWO VISITS OF ONE SHIP, IN THE SECOND MONTH OF THE TIDE OF REMEMBERING.

Known from a ship of the Tide of Remembering. It was sunk at our wall on its second visit, and long afterward the surf brought it in to the beach. Halvard’s hands were on it. Rhyna had open salt cuts on her palms that day and didn’t touch it, but she knew it as he knew it. The wood says the wound this ship carried home was felt by its family in their own boards. About that, the two of them say this: it spread among the ships the same way the Bonded feel each other’s pain. The ship’s own story was short and quickly told. Told by Halyna, taking turns, in two inks, and written down by me.

This is held in the grain.

We were a ship of the Ralenthae, the tide the roots taught. More than a word was cut into us: a memory, and a warning. Now we are broken wood, with salt in every board.

In one cold season, we came to your wall twice. The first time, we went home wounded and told what we knew. The second time, we came back to look, and died looking.

At first light on the first visit, our family was around us, with the bearer in front. A stone on your wall flashed. The green wood had learned this, and the roots had carried it home: where a stone flashes, there is a gun.

We turned toward the stone. It spoke again. The iron went into us, low and all the way through, and the sea came in.

Then an older carving woke up in us, one of the first the green wood was ever given: Go home when wounded. We turned our back end to your wall and went back into the breath, low and slow. The guns let us go, or didn’t see us go. We don’t know which.

As we went, we told our family, because the wood needs no words among its own. The flash, and where it was, went out of us into the roots. Where we had been hit went out of us too, and our family felt it in their own boards.

Last of all went the dread. And the dread is only this: one place in the water has become a place where wood dies. It spread slowly to every ship in that visit, the way sap spreads, fastest to the heart of the bearer, and from the heart to all the rest.

In the night, while you fixed your wall, the heart carved the next day’s order into us: Look before you trust. A stone that spoke yesterday may be quiet today. And a quiet stone may be waiting.

So on the next visit, we went out ahead of our family, alone and slow, because we knew that place. We were sent to see if your wall still flashed there. That was all we were sent to do: to look.

We threw one fire at the stone that had spoken, to see if it would answer. The grain holds nothing about what our fire found there. The stone didn’t answer.

Our family stood back behind us in the breath and waited to hear what we would find. The water was flat, and we were slow.

Your wall still flashed.

With the sea already inside us, we couldn’t turn a second time. The iron found us in the same place it had found us before, low and all the way through, as if it kept count too.

On that second morning, we sank in the shelter of your wall. We lay a long time in the cold, under the keels of our family. Then the tide worked us loose, and the surf brought us to your beach. Your hands are on us now.

But before we sank, we had told it. The ships that lived carried it back through the breath. From the edge it went home, root to root, to the groves where the next ships were growing. They grew up knowing it.

So we went home once, and told. We came back to look, and died looking. The groves have it now, and the grain still holds it.

And no one answered.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

V.4 · The Council Under the Thin SkyWood leaf

KNOWN IN THE FOURTH WINTER, IN THE JOINED TIDE · OF THE SUMMERS BEFORE THE GUEST CAME HOME, OF THE NIGHT OF THE VOTE, SEVENTY-FIVE YEARS BEFORE THE BANNER, AND OF THE SEVENTY YEARS AFTER IT.

Known from the heart of a Standard-Bearer of the Joined Tide, of the grain we call Silverbark, which was grown from a cutting of the silver elders of the council-grove; for the council-hall of the Mystaeri was a grove, and the grove heard the vote. Rhyna laid a hand upon it on the shingle and took it away almost at once, and Halvard came up from the vault with his face wet. The knowing was over in a moment; but the telling took the two of them the rest of that Tide, for a council is many minds, and they had one pair of mouths to tell it. Told by Halyna, by turns, in two inks, and set down by me.

This is held in the grain.

The council of the long-lived sat under the silver elders, in a hall that was a grove. No roof it had but the boughs, nor floor but the moss. The silver elders kept all that was said beneath them, as they keep all they hear.

We are a cutting of those silver elders. We give it as it was, for in no other way can we give it.

In the summers before Aelrhen came home, the old counselled patience; on the night he came home in ashes, the young voted for war.

In those summers the sky was thin along the whole edge of the wood. The pillars at the edge were gone in long gaps, like teeth from a jaw. Through the gaps the breath ran out, and the between-light went hard and white.

Frailer every spring came the newborns of the long-lived, and some did not wake. The eldest dwindled before their years were full, their strength going out of them like water from a cracked jar.

The elders of the long-lived counselled the patience of the trees. “The iron comes seldom now,” they said. “The pillars have put up nothing; they are waiting. Wait with them, and when the iron stops, they will grow the sky back.”

Of the very wound for which they counselled patience, the elders were dying. They knew it, and they counselled patience still.

Then came the Aelvaren home by the channels, burned to the keel, with the bones of Aelrhen in its ribs.

And the patience broke.

The young of the long-lived had grown up under a vanishing sky. They watched their mothers and fathers fade in the white light, and could not help them. Now they saw the bones of Aelrhen come home. Into the grove they came, many of them, and put the question to the council.

The elders rose and pleaded: “Send another. Learn their words. They do not know us; they cannot know the wood, and we have never gone down to teach them. Make no haste.”

But the old voices were few by then, and faint; and they were the voices of the dying.

The young answered: “We listened three of their lives and learned three words, and they would not hear even those.”

That was true, and not the whole of the truth; and the wood that heard it knows the difference. More we could have learned, for we live long. From behind the breath we listened, and never once went down to the shore with our hands open, save once. When the one who went was killed, we did not send a second.

We did not ask whether a whole people had killed Aelrhen, or one house at its edge. Nor did we ask who else lived along that shore, in the ten havens, who had never heard our name. We chose to grow hulls, and not to learn words.

But war is for the short-minded and the angry young.

So the question was put; and by the weight of anger and grief the vote was carried.

That same night, at the edge of the grove, the straightest of the young trees was laid open to the heart, while it still stood. It was our kin, and it felt the blade. Into its heartwood the young cut the first carving of the war:

Grow until the shore is silent.

And the wood, which cannot unlearn what is carved in it, grew.

In those same summers the iron stopped coming to the edge. The grove had not waited to see it.

The elders who had pleaded went down into the moss, one by one. While one of them lived, the grove would not loose the first growing. It lay ready in the breath at the edge of your sea, and waited on the last of the old voices. When the last went down, those who had cried for war cut it loose.

The carving did not go down with the elders into the moss. Every heart that has sailed since bears it. Beneath all the other carvings it lies, as the first ring lies beneath all the rings; and the grain holds it still.

And no one answered.

The facing leaf, in Seren’s hand:

Here there is no tale of ours. We were not there, and we did not know that anyone was deciding anything. I have left this leaf empty, and I ask whoever keeps this Book after me to leave it so.

KNOWN IN THE FOURTH WINTER, DURING THE JOINED TIDE · ABOUT THE SUMMERS BEFORE THE GUEST CAME HOME, THE NIGHT OF THE VOTE, SEVENTY-FIVE YEARS BEFORE THE BANNER, AND THE SEVENTY YEARS AFTER IT.

Known from the heart of a Standard-Bearer from the Joined Tide. Its wood is the kind we call Silverbark. It was grown from a cutting of the silver elders of the council grove. The Mystaeri’s council hall was a grove, and the grove heard the vote. Rhyna put a hand on it on the beach and pulled it away almost at once. Halvard came up from the vault with tears on his face. The knowing was over in a moment. But telling it took the two of them the rest of that Tide, because a council is many minds, and they only had one pair of mouths to tell it with. Told by Halyna, taking turns, in two inks, and written down by me.

This is held in the grain.

The council of the long-lived met under the silver elders, in a hall that was a grove. It had no roof but the branches, and no floor but the moss. The silver elders kept everything that was said under them, the way they keep everything they hear.

We are a cutting from those silver elders. We give it to you as it was. We can’t give it any other way.

In the summers before Aelrhen came home, the old ones advised patience. On the night he came home in ashes, the young voted for war.

In those summers, the sky was thin all along the edge of the wood. The pillars at the edge were gone in long gaps, like teeth missing from a jaw. The breath ran out through the gaps, and the in-between light turned hard and white.

Every spring, the newborn children of the long-lived were weaker, and some of them never woke up. The oldest faded before their years were done. Their strength drained out of them like water from a cracked jar.

The elders of the long-lived advised the patience of trees. “The iron hardly comes anymore,” they said. “The pillars have grown nothing. They’re waiting. Wait with them. When the iron stops, they’ll grow the sky back.”

The elders were dying of the very wound they were telling everyone to be patient about. They knew it, and they still advised patience.

Then the Aelvaren came home through the channels, burnt down to the keel, with Aelrhen’s bones in its ribs.

And the patience broke.

The young of the long-lived had grown up under a disappearing sky. They had watched their mothers and fathers fade in the white light and couldn’t help them. Now they saw Aelrhen’s bones come home. Many of them came into the grove and put the question to the council.

The elders stood up and begged: “Send another one. Learn their words. They don’t know us. They can’t know the wood, and we have never gone down to teach them. Don’t rush.”

But by then there were only a few old voices left, and they were weak. They were the voices of the dying.

The young answered: “We listened through three of their lifetimes and learned three words, and they wouldn’t even hear those.”

That was true, but it wasn’t the whole truth, and the wood that heard it knows the difference. We could have learned more. We live a long time. We listened from behind the breath, and never once went down to the shore with open hands, except once. When the one who went was killed, we didn’t send a second.

We didn’t ask whether a whole people had killed Aelrhen, or just one house at its edge. We didn’t ask who else lived along that shore, in the ten havens, who had never even heard our name. We chose to grow ships, not to learn words.

But war is for the short-minded and the angry young.

So the question was put. And the weight of anger and grief carried the vote.

That same night, at the edge of the grove, the straightest of the young trees was cut open to the heart while it was still standing. It was our family, and it felt the blade. Into its heartwood, the young carved the first carving of the war:

Grow until the shore is silent.

And the wood, which can’t unlearn what is carved into it, grew.

In those same summers, the iron stopped coming to the edge. The grove hadn’t waited to see it.

The elders who had begged went down into the moss one by one. As long as one of them lived, the grove wouldn’t release the first growing. It lay ready in the breath at the edge of your sea and waited for the last of the old voices. When the last one went down, the ones who had cried for war cut it loose.

The carving didn’t go down into the moss with the elders. Every heart that has sailed since then carries it. It lies underneath all the other carvings, the way the first ring lies underneath all the rings. And the grain still holds it.

And no one answered.

The facing page, in Seren’s handwriting:

We have no tale for this. We weren’t there, and we didn’t know anyone was deciding anything. I have left this page empty, and I ask whoever keeps this Book after me to leave it that way.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

V.5 · The Tides, as the Wall Counted ThemStone leaf

Laid by Kael the Counter, oath-keeper of the True Men, in six counts, each at the end of its Tide, for he would not tell a Tide until it was over. I have set the six together here, as he asked, though they were laid on six nights over six years. The names of the Tides are the wall’s own, and the wood’s leaf of the growing faces this one. He would not let me change a word of it, and I have not.

Count I · The Hasty Tide

THE FIRST COUNT, LAID AT THE END OF THE FIRST YEAR OF THE SIEGE · OF THE HASTY TIDE, THE TIDE OF THE FIRST WINTER.

“Give me the stone. No, I will stand.”

I count. That is what I am for. Here is the first count.

In the first winter, on the first morning of the war, I stood on this wall and counted the sails as they came out of the grey: eight there were, and not one of them kept station with another; and every morning of that Tide was like the first. Each ship came alone, and struck the first wall it touched, and when the wall held, it struck the same wall again, and again, till it sank.

The old soldiers had names for them out of the Fall: warships and fire-ships, transports and mortar-ships and torpedo-boats. They are wrong names, and we use them still. No one stood on the decks; and off the transports the root-men came like spilled kindling, and died upon the shingle, and the wall calls them treelings, as if a name could make them small.

By noon their fire always slackened, and we learned to say, “The fleet has spent its fire,” and to breathe. In that Tide a breach on the south face fell outward into the ditch, as the old slates out of the black chest said it would, for the masons had laid that course as the slates bade them; and the men behind it lived, every one, for I counted them as they came up out of the dust. I swear to that.

Jory of Eldhythe stood on the east wall-walk that winter, and whistled a tune of the quay that the old ship-masters hate; and you heard Hale tell of it, how Merrick, eldest of the ship-masters, bade him stop, and how Jory laughed down at him from the wall and said, “It’s only a rhyme.” He laughed at most things that winter, and at the grey sails most of all.

All that winter, at this fire, I said that the grey sails had picked the wrong fortress, for they broke themselves upon the one wall in the Shorelands that would not fall; and the saying went round the benches every night like a cup, and every man of us drank of it. We laughed. God forgive us, we laughed.

Jory laughed the loudest. His name is first under the east cairns.

In the last weeks of that winter, on a morning when he had been whistling on the east wall, a fire-ship came in close upon the stones below him. I was not there, and what I did not count I will not tell you; but Voss who asked was there, at his side, for they had been boys together on one quay, and as Voss tells it, so I count it.

Its fire took Jory.

Voss laid the stones over him on the east face with his own hands, one upon another, for the ground there will not take a spade; and when the last stone was on, he said Jory’s word over them, “It’s only a rhyme,” and came in to this fire, and asked his question, which some of you heard. The answer is his to give, and not mine. That night, the Captain cut a hand’s breadth from his half of the cloak, and bound it on a pike over Jory’s cairn.

It was the first strip.

This I lay as it was laid for me.

And the hearth said: We remember.

And the Captain put his hood back.

Added in Seren’s hand: It was not the last.

Count II · The Second Tide

THE SECOND COUNT, LAID AT THE END OF THE SECOND YEAR OF THE SIEGE · OF THE SECOND TIDE, THE TIDE OF THE SECOND WINTER.

“Give me the stone. No, I will stand.”

The second count, and a harder one.

In the second winter the grey sails came in pairs, the one before and the other behind; and the front one of each pair took the shot, and the back one took the ground.

In the third month of that Tide, a lookout on the east tower called me up to him, and ere I had my breath back from the stair, I saw a thing that I will not forget: a hull was struck off the north point, and a mile south of it another hull’s sail went pale, grey-green blanching to white like a man’s face, and it turned. It had not seen. It had felt.

That was the first answering sail. In that Tide too the crown-hulls came, and opened their crowns, and the winged seed came over the wall upon the wind. The Stonewrights say the wind chooses the keep, and the seed goes where our guns are quiet.

In that Tide we learned also that when you sink the hull that bears a fleet’s standard, another hull takes the standard up, and yet the fleet does not break, but takes on the temper of the new one. So we say at this fire: “Choose which heart you leave them.”

Corlen of the harbour was the eldest of the Twelve, and for fifty years and more he had watched the boats come in at Eldhythe on the evening tide. Before every holding he wet a finger and held it up to the wind, and spoke to his guns as a man speaks to his boats, and would not loose till the sea had finished; and when the young men fretted at him he said only, “Wait. The sea has not finished.” You heard him at this stone twice, once for the raising of Eldhythe and once for its fall; and you heard him say that when the havens came home, he would be the one to steer the first boat back into its harbour.

Late in that winter, in a seed-fall, a hull came in slow upon Corlen’s water; and he held his guns quiet, by his own judgement, to let it come close and make sure of the shot. As a man who has set his snare by the edge of a field kneels down in the stubble, and holds his breath, and has eyes for nothing but the run where the hare will come, and never sees the weather come up black over the hill behind him: so Corlen stood at the parapet with a wet finger up to the wind, and watched the water for his hull. The seed came over the wall where the guns were quiet.

His were.

Corlen died at the parapet, still watching the water. Three of his crew died with him at the guns.

All that evening the Captain sat at the end of that bench, with his hood back and the coal-black on his fingers, and said nothing; nor did any man of us speak to him, and the fire burned down, and no one fed it. That night, the Captain cut a hand’s breadth from his half of the cloak, and bound it on a pike over Corlen’s cairn.

Corlen did what the Captain would have done. That is what a True Man is. It is also what killed him.

Voss who asked took Corlen’s battery, and was raised a True Man that winter. I did not see what came after at those guns; Hale did, and Voss did, and they tell it alike, and so I count it. At the next holding the Captain came along the wall at his own pace and stood at Voss’s shoulder beside Corlen’s guns; and ere the sails came in, Voss wet a finger and held it up to the wind, as Corlen used to, and said to the guns, “Wait. The sea has not finished.” Then the Captain looked up, and not out to sea.

“Change your shot,” he said. It was the first time he ever called for the sky.

This I lay as it was laid for me.

And the hearth said: We remember.

And the Captain put his hood back.

Count III · The Tide of Remembering

THE THIRD COUNT, LAID AT THE END OF THE THIRD YEAR OF THE SIEGE · OF THE TIDE OF REMEMBERING, THE TIDE OF THE THIRD WINTER.

“Give me the stone. No, I will stand.”

The third count.

At the start of the third winter, on the morning after Brenn’s words were read at this hearth, the runners swore their oath again upon the runners’ stone in the ward, and added four words to it; and Wick the runner swore it with them, the youngest of them still, as he had been since he came up the mountain at thirteen. He said the old words and the new as loud as any man there: “What I saw, I carry. I carry nothing else. Not as Brenn carried.”

You knew Wick. He ran everywhere, even to this hearth, and sat on the floor at the front where the children sit, with his knees drawn up to his chin; and he could run the wall-walk, end to end, in the time it takes to say the Title twice; and he wanted nothing in the war save to run a word to the Captain.

In the second month of that Tide, one night, the Captain had the south gun moved, the one that had done the most killing, and set it forty yards along the wall; and at the grey of the morning Wick was on the wall-walk at the old gun-place, for he had been sent to watch the water, and had gone to it at a run. On the north wall the lamp of Hesk of the mere was burning yet, for it was not full light.

Then came the grey sails. Not to the wall. To the place where the gun had been. One hull came first, alone and slow, sounding the water, and the rest stood off in the grey to see what it found; and the lone hull fired upon the old gun-place, to see whether it would answer.

The shot took the parapet, and Wick with it.

Wick lived long enough to give Hale the runner three words. I did not see it; Hale did. Hale knelt to him among the broken stones, and the boy said them: “They are looking.”

Hale ran it.

The Captain heard Hale out, and understood, nor did he bid him say it twice; and so a word of Wick’s came to the Captain at the last, as Wick had wanted all that war, and it was Hale who ran it. Hale says Wick’s word was three words long, and it was enough. That night, the Captain cut a hand’s breadth from his half of the cloak, and bound it on a pike over Wick’s cairn.

So a boy died of a gun that was not there.

We had thought the grey sails beasts, that learn by the stick. They are not; they look before they trust, and you learn to read the lean of them, for a hull that leans to the shore means it, and a hull that leans away is thinking of home. Our walls they knew, and never once our Captain. After that we moved our guns by night, and the grey sails came looking in the morning; and it was a quieter war, and I liked it less.

This I lay as it was laid for me.

And the hearth said: We remember.

And the Captain put his hood back.

Count IV · The Joined Tide

THE FOURTH COUNT, LAID AT THE END OF THE FOURTH YEAR OF THE SIEGE · OF THE JOINED TIDE, THE TIDE OF THE FOURTH WINTER.

“Give me the stone. No, I will stand.”

The fourth count.

In the fourth summer, on the night before the three roads, the drums went in the grey below this wall from dusk till dawn, knocking from hull to hull; and at first light the grey sails came on three roads at once, north and east and the river mouth, as if one hand had flung them. Three roads, one hour.

Lanner of the crags whistles for the wind, as the crag-men do, and he will tell you that the wind comes before the sails do. That morning the Captain came along the wall at his own pace, and stood at Lanner’s shoulder, and was still, nor said a word; and Lanner stopped whistling.

“Loose,” the Captain said; and Lanner’s battery sank the fleet’s Standard-Bearer an hour after dawn.

Then I saw the strangest thing of the war. The rest of that fleet did not falter. At the hour the dead heart had carved for them they turned, and came in on the east road into empty water, where there was nothing to strike; yet they came a little late, and not together, for one sail went pale, and then the next, and then the next, a breath apart, as if each hull had to find the hour for itself. But they kept it. They kept a dead mind’s hour.

And some did not come on in order at all: the hulls whose own bearer we had sunk beneath them. They went wild, the orphaned hulls, with the carving lost and only the grief left, and bent upon revenge.

When the guns were cold, ere the dusk came down, I walked the wall, as I walk it after every holding, to count; and in the east breach I came to Enno of the guild, and counted him, and went on, and at the end of that walk, a mile on, in the north breach, I came to Della. I count them with that hour, for it was the same thing.

I counted them as one. I will not say more.

This I lay as it was laid for me.

And the hearth said: We remember.

And the Captain put his hood back.

Count V · The Tide that Learned Deceit

THE FIFTH COUNT, LAID AT THE END OF THE FIFTH YEAR OF THE SIEGE · OF THE TIDE THAT LEARNED DECEIT, THE TIDE OF THE FIFTH WINTER.

“Give me the stone. No, I will stand.”

The fifth count, and I do not like it.

In the fifth year the grey sails came upon us as the young wood came, loud and alone and straight; and we laughed as we had laughed the first winter, and every gun on the wall spoke, and out of the grey the hunters came for every gun that had spoken. Every day the grey sent straw down a new road, hulls that were not hulls, and marked which roads we answered; and down a road where no gun of ours had woken, it sent the root-men.

But look close, as Pellow of the spire said to every one of us, for never yet did the grey sails tell a lie that did not show its seam; and hear now the tells, and hold them, for we paid for every one.

The straw leaves no wreck, nor does it flinch at a shell. A true rout scatters, and a false one keeps its spacing. A laden ship rides low. And the young wood’s sails never once went pale; a green hull whose sail blanches is no green hull.

Every lie the grey sails told us this Tide, we could have known, if we looked. Some of us did not look, and they are under the east cairns.

Pellow held his glass up to everything, even to his supper ere he would eat it, and you laughed at him for it at this fire, and so did I; and it was Pellow who ground our far-glasses for us out of the glass of his city.

Pellow looked, and he is under them too.

Through his glass he saw the seam, and sent Hale the runner to the Captain with it ere the hunters were out of the grey; and the Captain went along the wall at his own pace, battery by battery, one at a time, and every battery that he came to in time lived. Pellow’s was the farthest. Ruan of the spire has his glass now.

Looking does not keep a man alive. It keeps the wall. The brave thing, that Tide, was to look.

This I lay as it was laid for me.

And the hearth said: We remember.

And the Captain put his hood back.

Count VI · The Last Tide

THE SIXTH AND LAST COUNT, LAID AT THE END OF THE SIXTH WINTER, AS THE THRONES CAME · OF THE LAST TIDE, THE TIDE OF THE SIXTH WINTER.

“Give me the stone. No, I will stand.”

The last count.

In the sixth winter each fleet came against the wall with a morrow carved in it, as we carve ours; and the morrow had turnings in it, if this, then that, and at the end of it there was a turning for home. When the bearer fell, and the second after it, the fleet went home. Not scattered. Went.

In the Joined Tide Halyna, the two of them, sat with me by this fire, and spoke as they speak, a half each. “The fleet cannot be called back,” one of them said, and the other finished, “for the wood obeys the wood.” I answered them that we would send it home, nor did I know what I meant.

Now I know what I meant, for the voices that could call it home are dead. It cannot be called home. But it can be sent home. The wood knows fear.

At the end of this winter, as the last fleets went home, ten great hulls came out of the grey that were not fleets at all; and they went each into a haven, and sat down, and the wall gave them beasts’ names upon the day it saw them. They sit there tonight, in our havens.

That is the count of the Tides. I have counted the grey sails six winters, the ghost fleet with no one on its decks; and I know their number, and I will not say it at this hearth.

But I will say ours.

A count is not a count until it is named. Tonight in the ward, ere I came to the stone, I called the roll of the True Men, as I have called it at the end of every Tide: fourteen names, and twelve to answer them. I swear to that. At two of the names the ward was quiet, and I let it be quiet for the space of a breath, and called the next; and at the name of Hesk of the mere, the answer came down to me from the north wall, where his lamp was lit.

Now hear the dead of the wall, and hold them, for they are ours. Jory of Eldhythe, of the east wall, who laughed the loudest; Corlen of the harbour, who would not loose till the sea had finished; Wick the runner, who ran everywhere, even to this hearth; Enno and Della, Enrella, the young pair, whom I counted as one; Pellow of the spire, the youngest of the Twelve, who looked close.

And the rest of the east cairns, troop by troop and every name: I said them all at dusk tonight, standing among the stones, and some of you stood there with me in the snow. I said them twice, as I count everything, and it took me till the light was gone; and my voice was not steady at the last of them, and I will not pretend it was.

As a man of a poor house, in the last weeks of a long winter, cuts from the loaf he laid by a thinner piece every night, that each mouth at his table may have its share, and cuts his own the last and the thinnest, nor says a word of it: so the Captain has cut his half of the cloak. For every one of us whose death has been laid at this hearth since the banner, he has cut a hand’s breadth. His half of the cloak hung to his knees the first winter.

Tonight it does not reach his belt.

He is sitting there now, at the end of the bench.

This I lay as it was laid for me.

And the hearth said: We remember.

Old Ebba of Fenholm began it, a breath before the rest.

And the Captain put his hood back.

Told by Kael the Counter, oath-keeper of the True Men, in six counts. He told each one at the end of its Tide, because he wouldn’t tell about a Tide until it was over. I have put the six together here, as he asked, even though they were laid on six nights over six years. The names of the Tides are the ones the wall gave them. The wood’s page about the growing faces this one. He wouldn’t let me change a word of it, and I haven’t.

Count I · The Hasty Tide

THE FIRST COUNT, LAID AT THE END OF THE FIRST YEAR OF THE SIEGE · ABOUT THE HASTY TIDE, THE TIDE OF THE FIRST WINTER.

“Give me the stone. No. I’ll stand.”

I count. That’s what I’m for. Here’s the first count.

In the first winter, on the first morning of the war, I stood on this wall and counted the sails as they came out of the grey. There were eight, and not one of them stayed in formation with another. Every morning of that Tide was like the first. Each ship came alone. It hit the first wall it touched. When the wall held, it hit the same wall again, and again, until it sank.

The old soldiers had names for them from the Fall: warships and fire-ships, transports, mortar-ships and torpedo-boats. They’re the wrong names, and we still use them. Nobody stood on the decks. The root-men poured off the transports like spilled kindling and died on the beach. The wall calls them treelings, as if a name could make them small.

By noon their fire always slowed down. We learned to say, “The fleet has spent its fire,” and breathe. In that Tide, a broken section of the south wall fell outward into the ditch. That’s just what the old slates from the black chest said would happen, because the masons had laid that row the way the slates told them to. Every man behind it lived. I counted them as they came up out of the dust. I swear to that.

That winter, Jory of Eldhythe stood on the east walkway and whistled a dockside tune the old ship-masters hate. You heard Hale tell how Merrick, the oldest of the ship-masters, told him to stop, and how Jory laughed down at him from the wall and said, “It’s just a rhyme.” He laughed at most things that winter, and at the grey sails most of all.

All that winter, at this fire, I said the grey sails had picked the wrong fortress, because they were breaking themselves on the one wall in the Shorelands that wouldn’t fall. The saying went around the benches every night like a cup, and every one of us drank from it. We laughed. God forgive us, we laughed.

Jory laughed the loudest. His name is the first one under the east cairns.

In the last weeks of that winter, on a morning when he’d been whistling on the east wall, a fire-ship came in close on the rocks below him. I wasn’t there, and if I didn’t count it, I won’t tell you about it. But Voss who asked was there, right next to him, because they’d been boys together on the same dock. I count it the way Voss tells it.

Its fire took Jory.

Voss piled the stones over him on the east face with his own hands, one on top of another, because the ground there is too hard for a shovel. When the last stone was on, he said Jory’s words over them, “It’s just a rhyme.” Then he came in to this fire and asked his question, which some of you heard. The answer is his to give, not mine. That night, the Captain cut a strip a hand wide from his half of the cloak and tied it to a pike over Jory’s grave.

It was the first strip.

I have laid this tale the way it was laid for me.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

And the Captain pushed his hood back.

Added in Seren’s handwriting: It wasn’t the last.

Count II · The Second Tide

THE SECOND COUNT, LAID AT THE END OF THE SECOND YEAR OF THE SIEGE · ABOUT THE SECOND TIDE, THE TIDE OF THE SECOND WINTER.

“Give me the stone. No. I’ll stand.”

The second count, and a harder one.

In the second winter, the grey sails came in pairs, one in front and one behind. The front one of each pair took the gunfire, and the back one gained the ground.

In the third month of that Tide, a lookout on the east tower called me up. Before I’d even caught my breath from the stairs, I saw something I’ll never forget. A ship was hit off the north point. A mile south of it, another ship’s sail went pale, from grey-green to white, like a man’s face going white, and it turned. It hadn’t seen. It had felt.

That was the first answering sail. In that Tide, the crown-ships came too. They opened their crowns, and the winged seed blew over the wall on the wind. The Stonewrights say the wind picks the keep, and the seed lands wherever our guns are quiet.

In that Tide we also learned that when you sink the ship carrying a fleet’s flag, another ship picks up the flag. The fleet doesn’t break. It takes on the temper of the new one. So we say at this fire: “Choose which heart you leave them.”

Corlen of the harbour was the oldest of the Twelve. For more than fifty years he had watched the boats come into Eldhythe on the evening tide. Before every holding he licked a finger and held it up to the wind. He talked to his guns the way a man talks to his boats, and he wouldn’t fire until the sea had finished. When the young men got impatient with him, all he said was, “Wait. The sea isn’t finished.” You heard him at this stone twice, once for the building of Eldhythe and once for its fall. You heard him say that when the havens came home, he’d be the one to steer the first boat back into its harbour.

Late that winter, during a seed-fall, a ship came in slowly on Corlen’s stretch of water. He kept his guns quiet, on his own judgement, to let it come close and make sure of the shot. Think of a man who has set a trap at the edge of a field. He kneels in the stubble and holds his breath. He has eyes for nothing but the path where the rabbit will come. He never sees the storm coming up black over the hill behind him. That’s how Corlen stood at the wall with a wet finger up to the wind, watching the water for his ship. The seed came over the wall where the guns were quiet.

His were.

Corlen died at the wall, still watching the water. Three of his gun crew died with him.

All that evening the Captain sat at the end of that bench with his hood back and coal-black on his fingers, and said nothing. None of us spoke to him. The fire burned down, and nobody fed it. That night, the Captain cut a strip a hand wide from his half of the cloak and tied it to a pike over Corlen’s grave.

Corlen did what the Captain would have done. That’s what a True Man is. It’s also what killed him.

Voss who asked took over Corlen’s battery and was made a True Man that winter. I didn’t see what happened next at those guns. Hale did, and Voss did, and they tell it the same way, so I count it. At the next holding, the Captain came along the wall at his own pace and stood at Voss’s shoulder next to Corlen’s guns. Before the sails came in, Voss licked a finger and held it up to the wind, the way Corlen used to, and said to the guns, “Wait. The sea isn’t finished.” Then the Captain looked up, not out to sea.

“Change your shot,” he said. It was the first time he ever called for the sky.

I have laid this tale the way it was laid for me.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

And the Captain pushed his hood back.

Count III · The Tide of Remembering

THE THIRD COUNT, LAID AT THE END OF THE THIRD YEAR OF THE SIEGE · ABOUT THE TIDE OF REMEMBERING, THE TIDE OF THE THIRD WINTER.

“Give me the stone. No. I’ll stand.”

The third count.

At the start of the third winter, on the morning after Brenn’s words were read at this hearth, the runners swore their oath again on the runners’ stone in the yard and added four words to it. Wick the runner swore it with them. He was still the youngest of them, as he had been since he came up the mountain at thirteen. He said the old words and the new ones as loud as anybody there: “What I saw, I carry. I carry nothing else. Not as Brenn carried.”

You knew Wick. He ran everywhere, even to this hearth. He sat on the floor at the front where the children sit, with his knees pulled up to his chin. He could run the whole walkway, end to end, in the time it takes to say the Title twice. The only thing he wanted in the whole war was to run a message to the Captain.

One night in the second month of that Tide, the Captain had the south gun moved, the one that had done the most killing, and set it forty yards further along the wall. At dawn, Wick was on the walkway at the old gun spot. He’d been sent to watch the water, and he’d gone there running. On the north wall, Hesk of the mere’s lamp was still burning, because it wasn’t full daylight yet.

Then the grey sails came. Not to the wall. To the spot where the gun used to be. One ship came first, alone and slow, testing the water, and the rest held back in the grey to see what it found. The lone ship fired at the old gun spot, to see if it would answer.

The shot hit the wall, and Wick with it.

Wick lived long enough to give Hale the runner three words. I didn’t see it. Hale did. Hale knelt next to him among the broken stones, and the boy said them: “They are looking.”

Hale ran it.

The Captain heard Hale out and understood. He didn’t ask him to say it twice. So one of Wick’s messages did reach the Captain in the end, the way Wick had wanted all through the war. It was Hale who ran it. Hale says Wick’s message was three words long, and it was enough. That night, the Captain cut a strip a hand wide from his half of the cloak and tied it to a pike over Wick’s grave.

So a boy died because of a gun that wasn’t there.

We had thought the grey sails were animals that learn from the stick. They aren’t. They look before they trust. You learn to read how they lean: a ship leaning toward the shore means it, and a ship leaning away is thinking about home. They knew our walls. They never once knew our Captain. After that we moved our guns at night, and the grey sails came looking in the morning. It was a quieter war, and I liked it less.

I have laid this tale the way it was laid for me.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

And the Captain pushed his hood back.

Count IV · The Joined Tide

THE FOURTH COUNT, LAID AT THE END OF THE FOURTH YEAR OF THE SIEGE · ABOUT THE JOINED TIDE, THE TIDE OF THE FOURTH WINTER.

“Give me the stone. No. I’ll stand.”

The fourth count.

In the fourth summer, on the night before the three roads, the drums sounded in the grey below this wall from dusk until dawn, knocking from ship to ship. At first light, the grey sails came on three roads at once: north, east and the river mouth, as if one hand had thrown them. Three roads, one hour.

Lanner of the crags whistles for the wind, the way the cliff men do. He’ll tell you the wind comes before the sails do. That morning the Captain came along the wall at his own pace and stood at Lanner’s shoulder. He stood still and didn’t say a word. Lanner stopped whistling.

“Loose,” the Captain said. An hour after dawn, Lanner’s battery sank the fleet’s Standard-Bearer.

Then I saw the strangest thing of the whole war. The rest of that fleet didn’t hesitate. At the hour the dead heart had carved for them, they turned and came in on the east road, into empty water, where there was nothing to hit. But they came a little late, and not all together. One sail went pale, then the next, then the next, a breath apart, as if each ship had to find the hour by itself. But they kept it. They kept a dead mind’s hour.

Some didn’t come in order at all. Those were the ships whose own bearer we had sunk right under them. They went wild, those orphaned ships, with the carving lost and only the grief left, and they were set on revenge.

When the guns were cold, before dark came down, I walked the wall, the way I walk it after every holding, to count. In the east breach I came to Enno of the guild. I counted him and went on. At the end of that walk, a mile further, in the north breach, I came to Della. I count them with that hour, because it was the same thing.

I counted them as one. I won’t say more.

I have laid this tale the way it was laid for me.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

And the Captain pushed his hood back.

Count V · The Tide that Learned Deceit

THE FIFTH COUNT, LAID AT THE END OF THE FIFTH YEAR OF THE SIEGE · ABOUT THE TIDE THAT LEARNED DECEIT, THE TIDE OF THE FIFTH WINTER.

“Give me the stone. No. I’ll stand.”

The fifth count, and I don’t like it.

In the fifth year, the grey sails came at us the way the young wood had come: loud, alone and straight. We laughed the way we had laughed the first winter. Every gun on the wall fired. And out of the grey came the hunters, for every gun that had fired. Every day, the grey sent fake ships down a new road, ships that weren’t really ships, and watched which roads we answered. Then, down a road where none of our guns had woken up, it sent the root-men.

But look closely, as Pellow of the spire told every one of us. The grey sails have never told a lie that didn’t show its seam. Here are the signs. Hold on to them. We paid for every one.

The fake ships leave no wreck, and they don’t flinch at a shell. A real retreat scatters, and a fake one keeps its spacing. A loaded ship rides low. And the young wood’s sails never once went pale. A green ship whose sail goes white is not a green ship.

Every lie the grey sails told us in this Tide, we could have caught if we had looked. Some of us didn’t look, and they’re under the east cairns.

Pellow held his glass up to everything, even to his supper before he’d eat it. You laughed at him for it at this fire, and so did I. And it was Pellow who ground our spyglasses for us out of his city’s glass.

Pellow looked, and he’s under the cairns too.

Through his glass he saw the seam. He sent Hale the runner to the Captain with it before the hunters were out of the grey. The Captain went along the wall at his own pace, battery by battery, one at a time, and every battery he reached in time lived. Pellow’s was the furthest. Ruan of the spire has his glass now.

Looking doesn’t keep a man alive. It keeps the wall standing. The brave thing in that Tide was to look.

I have laid this tale the way it was laid for me.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

And the Captain pushed his hood back.

Count VI · The Last Tide

THE SIXTH AND LAST COUNT, LAID AT THE END OF THE SIXTH WINTER, AS THE THRONES CAME · ABOUT THE LAST TIDE, THE TIDE OF THE SIXTH WINTER.

“Give me the stone. No. I’ll stand.”

The last count.

In the sixth winter, every fleet came at the wall with the next day’s plan carved into it, the way we carve ours. The plan had branches in it: if this happens, do that. At the end of it there was a branch for going home. When the bearer fell, and the second one after it, the fleet went home. It didn’t scatter. It went.

During the Joined Tide, Halyna, the two of them, sat with me by this fire and talked the way they talk, half each. “The fleet can’t be called back,” one of them said, and the other finished, “because the wood obeys the wood.” I told them we would send it home. I didn’t know what I meant.

Now I know what I meant. The voices that could call it home are dead. It can’t be called home. But it can be sent home. The wood knows fear.

At the end of this winter, as the last fleets went home, ten huge ships came out of the grey that weren’t fleets at all. Each one went into a haven and settled there. The wall gave them animal names the day it saw them. They’re sitting there tonight, in our havens.

That’s the count of the Tides. I have counted the grey sails for six winters, the ghost fleet with nobody on its decks. I know how many there were, and I won’t say the number at this hearth.

But I’ll say ours.

A count isn’t a count until it has names. Tonight in the yard, before I came to the stone, I called the roll of the True Men, the way I’ve called it at the end of every Tide. Fourteen names, and twelve to answer them. I swear to that. At two of the names, the yard was quiet. I let it stay quiet for one breath, and then called the next name. At the name of Hesk of the mere, the answer came down to me from the north wall, where his lamp was lit.

Now hear the dead of the wall, and hold on to them. They belong to us. Jory of Eldhythe, of the east wall, who laughed the loudest. Corlen of the harbour, who wouldn’t fire until the sea had finished. Wick the runner, who ran everywhere, even to this hearth. Enno and Della, Enrella, the young pair, whom I counted as one. Pellow of the spire, the youngest of the Twelve, who looked closely.

And the rest of the east cairns, troop by troop, every name. I said them all at dusk tonight, standing among the stones, and some of you stood there with me in the snow. I said them twice, the way I count everything, and it took me until the light was gone. My voice wasn’t steady by the last of them, and I won’t pretend it was.

Think of a man in a poor house in the last weeks of a long winter. Every night, he cuts a thinner slice from the loaf he saved, so every mouth at his table gets its share. He cuts his own slice last and thinnest, and he doesn’t say a word about it. That’s how the Captain has cut his half of the cloak. For every one of us whose death has been laid at this hearth since the banner, he has cut off a strip a hand wide. In the first winter, his half of the cloak hung down to his knees.

Tonight it doesn’t reach his belt.

He’s sitting there right now, at the end of the bench.

I have laid this tale the way it was laid for me.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

Old Ebba of Fenholm started it, a moment before the rest.

And the Captain pushed his hood back.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

V.6 · Of the GrowingWood leaf

KNOWN IN THE FOURTH SPRING · OF THE GROWING, FROM THE VOTE, SEVENTY-FIVE YEARS BEFORE THE BANNER, TO THE FOURTH WINTER OF THE SIEGE.

Known from the heart of a Standard-Bearer of the Joined Tide, an old tree led into a hull in the council’s first groves. Halvard carried it up from the shingle upon his shoulder, and ere he set it down it came to the two of them, to Rhyna at the hearth as to him; but it was too great for them then, and it lay in the vault until the spring, and no wood before it took so long to tell. The wall knows the grains by names of its own, in the order the wood gives their tempers: the green wood, Ironbark, Willow, Blackthorn, Heartoak, Twistbough, Yew, Ashwood and Silverbark; and the wood’s hands are those the wall calls root-men, and the soldiers treelings. Its song I have set as verse, as near as ink allows. Told by Halyna, by turns, in two inks, and set down by me.

This is held in the grain.

We were grown, not built. Stone you cut and lay; where you laid it, it lies; and it is dead.

The long-lived had always guided the wood, and the wood breathed out the sky they lived by: one life, in two kinds. A ship is only a tree led far.

To the trees that were to be hulls the long-lived sang. The trees bent to the singing, for nothing had ever been asked of them but song. Hulls from the breathing trunks; keels from the roots that fed them; masts from saplings trained by many summers of song in the wind.

No one sails in us. We are crewed by the living, and the living are the wood.

After the vote, the long-lived chose their wood, grew it into hulls, carved it, and sent it to your shore.

A hull with no one aboard is commanded by carving. In every company one hull bears the carving. Into its heart the orders are cut, and the heart carves them on into every hull, through the bond of roots, the Aelralen.

The long-lived made that bond into a leash.

So the hulls obeyed the orders carved within them, for the wood of the hull and the wood of the command were one. The wood obeys the wood.

In the night, while you lay your stone, the heart carves the morrow. You and the heart are both at work in the dark.

Yet the carving says what, and not whether. Each hull keeps its own grain and its own dread. It may balk at burning water, or turn savage where it bled: one mind, and many.

The bearers were chosen in grief, and the grief is in their grain. Nine grains there are, and each has its temper.

There was the wood too young to have a temper. There is the wood that comes again heavier where it failed, and the wood that turns from the first failure. There is the wood that goes where it lost the most, and the wood that never turns aside.

There is the wood that never plays one trick twice. There is the wood that waits, and burns, and waits again. There is the wood that throws all it has at once. And there is the wood that knows you best, and bends least.

So the crudest came first, by the law of the years, against which no council can carve. A wood holds by the years it had when it was chosen. Of one season it holds one sign; of forty summers, a phrase; of a shore-man’s life, a sentence, and in the sentence a memory.

The eldest pillars hold a judgement.

The council wanted the shore silent soon, and young wood grows quickest: one season, angry hands, one sign to a hull. It lay at the edge of your sea while your children had children. The grove would not loose it while the last who pleaded lived.

Your boats passed it in the breath, and saw nothing.

So the first growing sailed generations before it came to you. When it came, it came one sign to a hull, and in the havens the sign was Burn. The havens had no walls for war. The last of that green wood followed you into the mountains. There, at your wall, for the first time, someone struck back.

Each growing after the first went out as a tide goes, at the leaf-fall, when its ring was laid. The older the wood, the later it came ready; and each came out of the breath with the winter.

Leathae was the green tide, and Vaelthae the tide with grain. Ralenthae was the tide the roots taught. Aelthae is ours. Rhenthae and Eirthae were growing behind us when we were sent. What is cut in them, this heart does not know.

The warriors of the landings are grown of root, for we had none of the long-lived to spare. They are the fine roots that find their way in the dark. What they touch, the wood knows, for they are the wood’s hands.

The broad hulls hang back, and in their crowns the winged seed ripens. It goes up on the wind, over your walls.

Through many summers the growing went on, longer than a shore-man’s life. Those who cried for war died before their years, under the thin sky, and were forgotten. Fewer were the carvers at every growing, till only scattered souls were left, tending the last groves.

We were sent. We are still sent. No voice among us can call us home.

Over us the groves sang this as we grew, and it is in our rings:

The green wood holds one word. The wood of forty summers holds a word, and waits for its brother. The wood of a shore-man’s life holds a sentence, and a wall remembered in it. The wood of a long life holds the morrow, and carves it, and waits, and carves again. And the eldest of us, the pillars of the breath, the hearts of the grey, the anchors of the sky, hold one thing only now, and it is not a word, and it is not a sentence, and it is not a morrow, and we have carved it into every hull we sent you, and it is grief.

So we were grown, and so we were sent; and the grain holds it still.

And no one answered.

KNOWN IN THE FOURTH SPRING · ABOUT THE GROWING, FROM THE VOTE, SEVENTY-FIVE YEARS BEFORE THE BANNER, TO THE FOURTH WINTER OF THE SIEGE.

Known from the heart of a Standard-Bearer from the Joined Tide. It was an old tree, trained into a ship in the council’s first groves. Halvard carried it up from the beach on his shoulder. Before he set it down, it came to the two of them, to Rhyna at the hearth just as to him. But it was too big for them then, so it lay in the vault until spring. No wood before it took so long to tell. The wall has its own names for the kinds of wood, in the order the wood gives their tempers: the green wood, Ironbark, Willow, Blackthorn, Heartoak, Twistbough, Yew, Ashwood and Silverbark. The wood’s hands are the ones the wall calls root-men and the soldiers call treelings. I have written its song as verse, as close as ink allows. Told by Halyna, taking turns, in two inks, and written down by me.

This is held in the grain.

We were grown, not built. You cut stone and lay it. Where you lay it, it lies. And it is dead.

The long-lived had always guided the wood, and the wood breathed out the sky they lived by. One life, in two kinds. A ship is just a tree led a long way.

The long-lived sang to the trees that were going to be ships. The trees bent to the singing, because nothing had ever been asked of them except song. Hulls came from the breathing trunks. Keels came from the roots that fed them. Masts came from young trees trained by many summers of song in the wind.

Nobody sails in us. We are crewed by the living, and the living are the wood.

After the vote, the long-lived chose their wood, grew it into ships, carved it, and sent it to your shore.

A ship with nobody on board is commanded by carving. In every group, one ship carries the carving. The orders are cut into its heart, and the heart carves them on into every other ship through the bond of the roots, the Aelralen.

The long-lived turned that bond into a leash.

So the ships obeyed the orders carved inside them, because the wood of the ship and the wood of the command were one. The wood obeys the wood.

In the night, while you lay your stone, the heart carves the next day’s orders. You and the heart are both at work in the dark.

But the carving says what to do, not whether to do it. Each ship keeps its own grain and its own dread. It might shy away from burning water, or turn vicious where it bled. One mind, and many.

The bearers were chosen in grief, and the grief is in their grain. There are nine kinds of wood, and each has its own temper.

There was the wood too young to have a temper. There is the wood that comes back harder where it failed, and the wood that turns away after the first failure. There is the wood that goes where it lost the most, and the wood that never turns aside.

There is the wood that never plays the same trick twice. There is the wood that waits, and burns, and waits again. There is the wood that throws everything it has at once. And there is the wood that knows you best, and bends the least.

So the roughest came first, by the law of the years, and no council can carve against that law. A wood can only hold as much as the years it had when it was chosen. A tree of one season holds one sign. A tree of forty summers holds a phrase. A tree as old as a shore-man’s life holds a sentence, and a memory inside the sentence.

The oldest pillars hold a judgement.

The council wanted the shore silent soon, and young wood grows fastest. One season, angry hands, one sign to a ship. It lay at the edge of your sea while your children had children of their own. The grove wouldn’t release it while the last of those who had begged for patience were alive.

Your boats passed it in the breath and saw nothing.

So the first growing set sail generations before it reached you. When it came, it came with one sign to a ship, and in the havens the sign was Burn. The havens had no walls built for war. The last of that green wood followed you into the mountains. There, at your wall, for the first time, someone fought back.

Every growing after the first went out the way a tide goes out, in the autumn when the leaves fall, once its ring was laid. The older the wood, the later it was ready. Each one came out of the breath with the winter.

Leathae was the green tide, and Vaelthae the tide with grain. Ralenthae was the tide the roots taught. Aelthae is ours. Rhenthae and Eirthae were growing behind us when we were sent. This heart doesn’t know what is cut into them.

The warriors who land on your beaches are grown from roots, because we had none of the long-lived to spare. They are the fine roots that find their way in the dark. Whatever they touch, the wood knows, because they are the wood’s hands.

The broad ships hang back, and the winged seed ripens in their crowns. It goes up on the wind, over your walls.

The growing went on for many summers, longer than a shore-man’s life. The ones who cried for war died before their time, under the thin sky, and were forgotten. There were fewer carvers at every growing, until only a few scattered souls were left, looking after the last groves.

We were sent. We are still sent. No voice among us can call us home.

The groves sang this over us as we grew, and it is in our rings:

The green wood holds one word. The wood of forty summers holds a word, and waits for its brother. The wood of a shore-man’s life holds a sentence, with a wall remembered in it. The wood of a long life holds tomorrow’s plan, and carves it, and waits, and carves again. And the oldest of us, the pillars of the breath, the hearts of the grey, the anchors of the sky, hold only one thing now. It is not a word. It is not a sentence. It is not a plan. We have carved it into every ship we sent you. It is grief.

That’s how we were grown, and that’s how we were sent. And the grain still holds it.

And no one answered.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

V.7 · The Mirror in the GrainStone leaf

Laid by me, Seren Two-Inks, in three parts, each on the night its carving was known: the first in the Joined Tide, the second in the Tide that Learned Deceit, and the third in the Last Tide. Halyna, the two of them, speak in it as they spoke upon the shingle; and where they spoke a line between them, I have not marked which of them began it. The carvings are the wood’s own words, and I have set them down as they were told, and mended none.

Part I · Three Roads, One Hour

LAID IN THE FOURTH SUMMER, IN THE JOINED TIDE, ON THE NIGHT OF THE DAY ENNO AND DELLA DIED · OF THAT DAY.

“Hold the stone, Seren. I tell myself so every time.”

Hear how the three roads came in one hour, and what they parted, and what the wood told us of it ere that day was done.

It was the first morning of the three roads, in the fourth summer of the siege, and all the short night ere it, the drums had beaten out in the grey, knocking from hull to hull; and Enno and Della, through all that drumming, had sat up together in the house they were building in the ward, a house of one door, as yet but one course high, with their mark beneath its first stone.

Enrella they were, the young pair, the two of them sealed in our first summer under the old hands of Idrenna, and prentices to Orvenna the wall-masters; and they had come up the mountain road side by side, and into the courtyard on the day of the banner hand in hand, where I came in alone.

When the drums stopped, at the grey of the dawn, the two of them came up onto the wall-walk together, as they had come into that courtyard; and in one hour came the fleet by three roads, and broke the wall in the east and in the north, and there were more breaches than pairs.

Orvenna the wall-masters, Ormund and Penna, who in four winters had never once raised their voices upon the wall, sent every pair they had into the breaches, and still the breaches were more; and so they did a thing that no masters had done, in all the annals of the guild, and they did it without a word.

They sent a pair apart.

Ormund gave Enno the east breach. Penna gave Della the north. At the head of the stair, whence the wall-walk runs to the east and to the north, Enno called back over the guns the first half of the young pair’s old jest, “It is like being one person,” and Della, already running, gave him the rest of it over her shoulder: “with four hands and no excuses.”

You children at the front have heard Halvard Stone-Warden tell it at this hearth, on the night that Enrella were sealed, that the Bonded are seldom apart, and never far. A mile of wall is far.

In the east breach, in that same hour, Enno was setting stone among the masons of that breach with his back to the water; and out of the east road came a shot, and took the wall where he knelt, and took Enno. I did not see it. Hale did.

At the north breach, in that same hour, I was beside Della with the mortar; and she had dressed the last stone of the breach, and turned it over ere it was set, to cut their mark upon its underside, as the Bonded cut it beneath the last stone of every work they raise. That morning, for the first time in her life, she both set the chisel and struck it, as Enno had always set it for her; and her hand did not shake.

Then the mallet stopped in the air above the chisel, and she did not bring it down. As a fisher’s wife upon the quay at dusk, when the boats come in on the evening tide one behind another, will turn from the water ere the last sail is counted, and draw her shawl about her, and go home alone up the steep street, for she knows already which sail is lost: so Della lifted her head, and looked east, across a mile of wall and the whole of the keep.

She bent again to the stone, and set the chisel, and cut the last stroke, clean; and she laid the chisel down beside the mark, and lay down upon the wall-walk with her cheek against the stone.

I knelt by her with the mortar on my board, and put my thumb to it, as a mason does without thinking, to know whether it would yet take a stone. It was still soft.

She was gone.

Hale the runner came along the wall at a run, with the word from the east in his mouth, and found me kneeling there by Della, and did not say it. Never before on this wall had a runner come too late, and it was no runner’s fault.

The mark beneath the north stone is whole, and in all the annals of the guild, it is the only one that one hand finished. Ere they set the stone upon it, I looked long at the mark by the last of the light, to find the stroke that Della cut alone.

You cannot tell.

At dusk Stannard of the ridge, the quarryman, carried Enno the whole mile of the wall to Della, and laid him down by her in the north breach; and Ormund and Penna set the north stone over the two of them with their own four hands, mark down, for it was the wall-masters who had sent them apart.

Then over the stone came Idrenna, the eldest pair, Idlan and Denna, who had sealed the young pair in our first summer, and said the prayer for the dead in its two halves, slowly, as they say all things; and one of them said, and the other finished:

“Lord, thou madest these two one, ere either of them drew breath.”

“One cradle they had of thee, and one stone of us; and thou wilt not part them.”

And Ormund began the young pair’s jest over the stone, “It is like being one person,” and Penna finished it, “with four hands and no excuses.”

That night, the Captain cut a hand’s breadth from his half of the cloak, and bound it on a pike over the north stone. Never ere then had he cut one for Stonewrights; yet they were his company too. Kael the Counter, oath-keeper of the True Men, stood by the pike and counted them.

“One,” he said.

Later that night, at the turn of the tide, the sea brought a plank in under this wall, out of the hull that had borne the standard of that fleet, which Lanner of the crags had sunk an hour after the dawn; and Rhyna of the Inner Gate went down to it upon the shingle, and I went behind her with the lamp.

Halvard Stone-Warden was on the wall above us at the mortar, mending the east breach by torchlight where Enno had knelt, with a course half laid; and over their work the masons were singing the old song of the gate, as they sing it at every laying. On the night of the banner Enno and Della had been its first voices; and that night, when it came round to the first line, no one took the first voice, and the second came in alone upon the comma, as though the first had been sung.

Rhyna laid her hand upon the grain. I saw her go pale; and when I lifted the lamp and looked up at the wall, Halvard had gone pale in the same breath, with the trowel still in his hand.

He came down to us, and the two of them told it between them; and no carving we had known was so long, nor was it the order of one hull. It was the order of the whole fleet at once, and the ties that ran between hull and hull.

Three roads, one hour. Let the east and the north go as one. Let the south stand at the edge of the dread until the reach of the south has walked east; then let the south go.

Rhyna began it, and Halvard gave it its end; nor for a long while after did either of them speak, and the tide came in about the stones at our feet. Then I asked them what ailed them, though I think I knew. One of them said, and the other finished:

“It is how we work a wall.”

“The one waits for the other’s stroke, and does not need to be told.”

Halvard looked down at the trowel in his hand, as a man looks at a thing he does not remember taking up; and upon it the mortar was drying.

There was a name in the carving besides, the name the wood gives to that whole Tide, which I had first written that spring, when the heart of the growing was told: Aelthae; and the two of them gave it to me together, and I wrote it upon the middle of the leaf; and that night, for the first time, I knew the first part of it. It is ael, the joining, and I had written it before in this Book, in Ael’thar, the Guest’s rite at the warden’s door.

The lamp burned down a finger’s width upon its wick. On the wall above us the singing had stopped, and the masons were laying in silence.

I have written the name, and I will not write what I thought.

This I lay as it was laid for me.

And the hearth said: We remember.

And the Captain put his hood back.

Part II · What We Taught Them

LAID IN THE FIFTH SPRING, IN THE TIDE THAT LEARNED DECEIT, ON THE NIGHT OF THE DAY PELLOW DIED · OF THAT DAY.

“Hold the stone, Seren. I tell myself so every time.”

Hear how the young wood came back to the wall, though it was not the young wood, and what the grain told us of it at the turn of the tide.

On a grey morning of the fifth spring, in the Tide that Learned Deceit, when the gulls had come back to the shingle below the wall, a fleet ran at the wall in the old way, each hull alone and loud and straight, and not one keeping station with another, as we had not seen since the first winter; and I was on the inner wall with the mortar, and I heard the whole wall laugh. Every gun upon it spoke, as every gun had spoken then, and Pellow’s spoke with the rest.

Pellow of the spire had the battery at the south end of that wall, the nearest to the grey. The youngest of the True Men he was, and he had ground our far-glasses for the lookouts, as his father before him had ground the great glass of Glasspire, that threw the sun far out over the sea. There was nothing under the sky he would not hold his own glass up to, his supper among the rest; and to every one of us, soldier and mason, he said the same two words: “Look close.”

What came next I did not see. Hale the runner did, for he stood at Pellow’s shoulder that morning; and he is sitting at the front tonight, and what follows is his, word for word.

When his guns had spoken, Pellow did not laugh with the wall. He lifted his glass, and looked close; and through it he saw a sail go pale, from the grey-green of the young wood to white, as a face goes white, and then a second ere the first was white, and a third. With the glass still at his eye, he gave Hale the word to carry:

“They are not green.”

Hale ran it.

When Hale looked back from the head of the stair, Pellow was turning his guns from the hulls before him to the grey behind them, by his own judgement, for that was the water he trusted least; and up on the parapet he stood, with the glass at his eye, to watch the grey for the hunters.

Hale ran the length of the wall with his head down, past every battery and every True Man, nor spoke to any of them, for what he carried was for the Captain alone; and he found the Captain at the north end, above the gate, standing still, as he always stands. The Captain heard him to the end.

Then the Captain went along the wall from the north end where he stood, and at the shoulder of each True Man he gave the call in his own voice:

“Shift your ground.”

Each True Man turned his guns from the hulls in front of him, and laid them upon the grey behind; and Hale went at the Captain’s shoulder, and counted the batteries one by one. As a lamplighter at dusk goes the length of a long hall with his taper, from window to window, and will not hasten, for a taper carried fast goes out, and the farthest window is the last to take the light: so the Captain went along the wall, battery by battery, and did not hurry, and did not stop.

Out of the grey the hunters came, and went to every gun that had spoken, and would not leave it; and the batteries the Captain had reached were laid upon the right water when the hunters came to them, and they lived. One at a time is slow.

Pellow of the spire had the farthest battery on that wall, the nearest to the grey, and to his the hunters came first of all; and his guns were turned already, by his own judgement, and his battery met them alone. Hale saw it from far up the wall; and the Captain did not quicken his pace.

When the Captain came to the south end at last, the hunters had gone back into the grey, and the guns there were still warm.

Pellow lay dead at the parapet.

His glass lay on the stones beside his hand, and it was whole.

At the turn of the tide, in the last of the daylight, a plank came up on the shingle, out of one of the hulls that had run at us so loud and so straight; and Rhyna of the Inner Gate laid her hand upon it, and Halvard Stone-Warden laid his beside hers, and I held the lamp.

Come as the young wood came: loud, alone and straight. Stop one breath short of the fire, and let the hunters wait upon the flashing.

And beneath it lay the thing it had been cut against: they strike first and far; they move the guns we find. It was carved against us, and against the way we fight.

At first I did not understand it, and I said aloud upon the shingle that the wood cannot lie; for that is the first thing the Guest’s gift ever taught us of their carving, that what is carved in their wood is always true. Then I understood, and I wrote it down ere my nerve could fail.

The wood does not lie. It was told to make us believe a lie, and it obeyed.

The two of them sat a long while upon the shingle, his hands and hers still on the grain. Then one of them said, and the other finished:

“They learned it from us.”

“We taught it them with every silent gun.”

And so it was. Every gun the Captain held silent to draw a hull in; every false mast we raised upon the wall with no gun beneath it; every gun we hid under sod and brush, so that the hulls sailed past it into its mouth: of all of these the wrecks went back through the roots to the groves, and the groves learned. They had no word for a lie. We gave them one.

There was a name for us cut small in that carving, and the two of them would not say it at first. It was Rhenear, the stone-deaf. I said it over three times, as I say every word ere I trust it to the ink, to hear whether it would hold; and it held.

At dusk we raised a cairn for Pellow of the spire upon the east face, where the ground will not take a spade. That night, the Captain cut a hand’s breadth from his half of the cloak, and bound it on a pike over the cairn.

Then the Captain held out Pellow’s glass to Hale the runner, and with the glass, Pellow’s battery; and Hale did not take it.

“I am not a True Man,” Hale said. “I run.”

So the Captain gave the glass to Ruan of the spire, who had been Pellow’s file-leader, and the battery went with it; and Ruan put the glass to his eye, and looked out past the cairn upon the grey, and said what Pellow used to say to every man upon the wall:

“Look close.”

This I lay as it was laid for me.

And the hearth said: We remember.

And the Captain put his hood back.

Part III · The Plan That Carved Its Own Defeat

LAID IN THE SIXTH WINTER, IN THE LAST TIDE · OF THAT NIGHT.

“Hold the stone, Seren. I tell myself so every time.”

Hear how the longest knowing was told, and what lay at its end.

In the sixth winter, in the Last Tide, when the snow lay to the knee in the ward, a hull came against us that bore the standard of its fleet, with a whole morrow carved into its heart, a plan of many turnings; and we sank it ere noon, and at the evening tide, the sea brought its heart in to the shingle under this wall.

Rhyna of the Inner Gate laid her hand upon it, and Halvard Stone-Warden laid his over hers, and I held the lamp; and it was the longest knowing the two of them had ever held, and the holding of it was not the hard part. They knew it in the time it takes to fill a lamp; and I filled the lamp twice ere they had done with the telling, and at the end of the telling was this:

When the bearer falls, the second takes the standard; when the second falls, go home.

They had carved their own defeat into the plan.

The lamp was low again, and their two hands had not moved upon the grain. Then one of them said, and the other finished:

“There is one hand in all that carving, and not a council’s.”

“And the hand is young.”

But that was not what held the two of them upon the shingle. It was the last two words. To the young wood, home had been only the place whither a hurt thing runs; and this was a going home carved into the plan ere the first shot was fired, a thing thought of in the groves and cut there on purpose.

As when a man who has lain in a fever all the winter asks one morning, ere the thaw, for his boots, and his wife at the fire sets down the bowl she is stirring, and turns, and looks at him a long while, for a man who asks for his boots has a road in his mind: so the three of us looked at one another across the lamp.
