A mind that carves how it will go home is a mind that wants to go home.

That night, for the first time, we saw it: the enemy could hope.

One of them said, and the other finished:

“They carve a morrow now, as we do.”

“Then let us build a morrow they did not carve for.”

The lamp went out upon the shingle, and I did not fill it a third time; and we went up to this hearth in the dark, the three of us, by the old stair.

This I lay as it was laid for me.

And the hearth said: We remember.

Told by me, Seren Two-Inks, in three parts, each one on the night its carving was known: the first during the Joined Tide, the second during the Tide that Learned Deceit, and the third during the Last Tide. Halyna, the two of them, speak in it the way they spoke on the beach. Where they said a line between them, I haven’t marked which of them started it. The carvings are the wood’s own words. I have written them down as they were told, and fixed none of them.

Part I · Three Roads, One Hour

LAID IN THE FOURTH SUMMER, DURING THE JOINED TIDE, ON THE NIGHT OF THE DAY ENNO AND DELLA DIED · ABOUT THAT DAY.

“Hold the stone, Seren. I tell myself that every time.”

This is how the three roads came in one hour, what they pulled apart, and what the wood told us about it before that day was over.

It was the first morning of the three roads, in the fourth summer of the siege. All through the short night before it, the drums had beaten out in the grey, knocking from ship to ship. Through all that drumming, Enno and Della had sat up together in the house they were building in the yard. It was a house with one door. It was still only one row of stone high, with their mark under its first stone.

They were Enrella, the young pair. The two of them were sealed in our first summer by the old hands of Idrenna, and they were apprentices to Orvenna, the wall-masters. They had come up the mountain road side by side, and into the courtyard on the day of the banner holding hands, when I came in alone.

When the drums stopped, at dawn, the two of them came up onto the walkway together, the way they had come into that courtyard. In one hour, the fleet came by three roads. It broke the wall in the east and in the north, and there were more broken places than there were pairs to fix them.

Orvenna, the wall-masters, Ormund and Penna, who in four winters had never once raised their voices on the wall, sent every pair they had into the breaches. There were still more breaches than pairs. So they did something no masters had done in the whole history of the guild, and they did it without a word.

They split a pair up.

Ormund gave Enno the east breach. Penna gave Della the north. At the top of the stairs, the walkway splits, east and north. There Enno shouted back over the guns the first half of the young pair’s old joke: “It’s like being one person.” Della, already running, shouted the rest of it over her shoulder: “with four hands and no excuses.”

You children at the front have heard Halvard Stone-Warden say it at this hearth, on the night Enrella were sealed: the Bonded are hardly ever apart, and never far apart. A mile of wall is far.

In the east breach, in that same hour, Enno was setting stone with the masons there, with his back to the water. A shot came from the east road. It hit the wall where he was kneeling, and it took Enno. I didn’t see it. Hale did.

At the north breach, in that same hour, I was next to Della with the mortar. She had shaped the last stone of the breach, and turned it over before it was set, to cut their mark into its underside, the way the Bonded cut it under the last stone of everything they build. That morning, for the first time in her life, she both held the chisel and struck it. Enno had always held it for her. Her hand didn’t shake.

Then the mallet stopped in the air above the chisel, and she didn’t bring it down. Think of a fisherman’s wife on the dock at dusk, when the boats come in on the evening tide one after another. She turns away from the water before the last sail is counted. She pulls her shawl around her and walks home alone up the steep street, because she already knows which sail is lost. That’s how Della raised her head and looked east, across a mile of wall and the whole keep.

She bent down to the stone again, set the chisel, and cut the last stroke, clean. She put the chisel down next to the mark. Then she lay down on the walkway with her cheek against the stone.

I knelt next to her with the mortar on my board. I pressed my thumb into it, the way a mason does without thinking, to see if it would still take a stone. It was still soft.

She was gone.

Hale the runner came along the wall at a run, with the news from the east in his mouth. He found me kneeling there by Della, and he didn’t say it. Never before on this wall had a runner come too late, and it wasn’t any runner’s fault.

The mark under the north stone is whole. In the whole history of the guild, it’s the only one ever finished by one hand. Before they set the stone over it, I looked at the mark for a long time by the last of the light, trying to find the stroke Della cut alone.

You can’t tell.

At dusk, Stannard of the ridge, the quarryman, carried Enno the whole mile of wall to Della and laid him down next to her in the north breach. Ormund and Penna set the north stone over the two of them with their own four hands, mark side down, because it was the wall-masters who had split them up.

Then Idrenna, the oldest pair, Idlan and Denna, came over the stone. They had sealed the young pair in our first summer. They said the prayer for the dead in its two halves, slowly, the way they say everything. One of them said, and the other finished:

“Lord, you made these two one before either of them took a breath.”

“They had one cradle from you, and one stone from us. You will not part them.”

And Ormund started the young pair’s joke over the stone, “It’s like being one person,” and Penna finished it, “with four hands and no excuses.”

That night, the Captain cut a strip a hand wide from his half of the cloak and tied it to a pike over the north stone. He had never cut one for Stonewrights before. But they were his company too. Kael the Counter, oath-keeper of the True Men, stood by the pike and counted them.

“One,” he said.

Later that night, when the tide turned, the sea brought a plank in under this wall. It came from the ship that had carried that fleet’s flag, the one Lanner of the crags sank an hour after dawn. Rhyna of the Inner Gate went down to it on the beach, and I followed her with the lamp.

Halvard Stone-Warden was up on the wall above us at the mortar, fixing the east breach by torchlight where Enno had knelt, with a row half laid. Over their work, the masons were singing the old song of the gate, the way they sing it at every laying. On the night of the banner, Enno and Della had sung its lead. That night, when it came around to the first line, nobody took the first voice. The second voice came in alone at the comma, as if the first half had been sung.

Rhyna put her hand on the grain. I saw her go pale. When I lifted the lamp and looked up at the wall, Halvard had gone pale at the same moment, with the trowel still in his hand.

He came down to us, and the two of them told it between them. No carving we had known was this long. And it wasn’t the order for one ship. It was the order for the whole fleet at once, and the ties that ran between ship and ship.

Three roads, one hour. Let the east and the north go as one. Let the south wait at the edge of the dread until the south’s strength has moved east. Then let the south go.

Rhyna started it, and Halvard finished it. For a long time afterward neither of them spoke, and the tide came in around the stones at our feet. Then I asked them what was wrong, though I think I knew. One of them said, and the other finished:

“That’s how we work a wall.”

“One waits for the other’s stroke, and doesn’t need to be told.”

Halvard looked down at the trowel in his hand, like a man looking at something he doesn’t remember picking up. The mortar on it was drying.

There was also a name in the carving: the name the wood gives that whole Tide. I had first written it that spring, when the heart of the growing was told: Aelthae. The two of them gave it to me together, and I wrote it in the middle of the page. That night, for the first time, I knew what the first part of it meant. It’s ael, the joining. I had written it before in this Book, in Ael’thar, the Guest’s ceremony at the warden’s door.

The lamp burned down a finger’s width on its wick. On the wall above us, the singing had stopped, and the masons were laying stone in silence.

I have written the name. I won’t write what I thought.

I have laid this tale the way it was laid for me.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

And the Captain pushed his hood back.

Part II · What We Taught Them

LAID IN THE FIFTH SPRING, DURING THE TIDE THAT LEARNED DECEIT, ON THE NIGHT OF THE DAY PELLOW DIED · ABOUT THAT DAY.

“Hold the stone, Seren. I tell myself that every time.”

This is how the young wood came back to the wall, even though it wasn’t the young wood, and what the grain told us about it when the tide turned.

On a grey morning in the fifth spring, during the Tide that Learned Deceit, when the gulls had come back to the beach below the wall, a fleet ran at the wall the old way. Each ship came alone, loud and straight, not one of them in formation with another. We hadn’t seen that since the first winter. I was on the inner wall with the mortar, and I heard the whole wall laugh. Every gun on it fired, the way every gun had fired back then. Pellow’s fired with the rest.

Pellow of the spire had the battery at the south end of that wall, the closest one to the grey. He was the youngest of the True Men. He had ground our spyglasses for the lookouts, the way his father had ground the great glass of Glasspire, which threw the sun far out over the sea. There was nothing under the sky he wouldn’t hold his own glass up to, his supper included. To every one of us, soldier and mason, he said the same two words: “Look closely.”

I didn’t see what came next. Hale the runner did, because he was standing at Pellow’s shoulder that morning. He’s sitting at the front tonight, and what follows is his, word for word.

When his guns had fired, Pellow didn’t laugh with the rest of the wall. He raised his glass and looked closely. Through it, he saw a sail go pale, from the grey-green of the young wood to white, the way a face goes white. Then a second one, before the first was fully white. Then a third. With the glass still at his eye, he gave Hale the message to carry:

“They are not green.”

Hale ran it.

When Hale looked back from the top of the stairs, Pellow was turning his guns away from the ships in front of him and toward the grey behind them. He did it on his own judgement, because that was the water he trusted least. He stood up on the wall with the glass at his eye to watch the grey for the hunters.

Hale ran the length of the wall with his head down, past every battery and every True Man, and didn’t speak to any of them, because what he was carrying was for the Captain alone. He found the Captain at the north end, above the gate, standing still, the way he always stands. The Captain heard him out.

Then the Captain walked along the wall from the north end where he stood. At the shoulder of each True Man, he gave the call in his own voice:

“Shift your ground.”

Each True Man turned his guns away from the ships in front of him and aimed them at the grey behind. Hale walked at the Captain’s shoulder and counted the batteries one by one. Think of a lamplighter at dusk walking the length of a long hall with his candle, from window to window. He won’t hurry, because a candle carried fast goes out, and the furthest window is always the last to get its light. That’s how the Captain went along the wall, battery by battery. He didn’t hurry, and he didn’t stop.

The hunters came out of the grey. They went for every gun that had fired, and wouldn’t leave it alone. The batteries the Captain had reached were aimed at the right water when the hunters came, and they lived. One at a time is slow.

Pellow of the spire had the furthest battery on that wall, the closest to the grey. The hunters went to his first of all. His guns were already turned, by his own judgement, and his battery met them alone. Hale saw it from far up the wall. The Captain didn’t walk any faster.

When the Captain finally reached the south end, the hunters had gone back into the grey. The guns there were still warm.

Pellow lay dead at the wall.

His glass was on the stones next to his hand, and it wasn’t broken.

When the tide turned, in the last of the daylight, a plank washed up on the beach. It came from one of the ships that had charged at us so loudly and so straight. Rhyna of the Inner Gate put her hand on it, and Halvard Stone-Warden put his next to hers, and I held the lamp.

Come the way the young wood came: loud, alone and straight. Stop one breath short of the fire, and let the hunters wait for the flashing.

And under it was the thing it had been carved against: they strike first and far; they move the guns we find. It was carved against us, and against the way we fight.

At first I didn’t understand it. I said out loud on the beach that the wood can’t lie. That was the very first thing the Guest’s gift taught us about their carving: what is carved in their wood is always true. Then I understood, and I wrote it down before I lost my nerve.

The wood doesn’t lie. It was told to make us believe a lie, and it obeyed.

The two of them sat on the beach for a long time, his hands and hers still on the grain. Then one of them said, and the other finished:

“They learned it from us.”

“We taught it to them with every silent gun.”

And it was true. Every gun the Captain held silent to lure a ship in. Every fake mast we put up on the wall with no gun under it. Every gun we hid under turf and brush, so the ships sailed right past it into its mouth. The wrecks carried all of it back through the roots to the groves, and the groves learned. They had no word for a lie. We gave them one.

There was a name for us carved small in that carving, and at first the two of them wouldn’t say it. It was Rhenear: the stone-deaf. I said it three times, the way I say every word before I trust it to ink, to hear if it would hold. It held.

At dusk we built a cairn for Pellow of the spire on the east face, where the ground is too hard for a shovel. That night, the Captain cut a strip a hand wide from his half of the cloak and tied it to a pike over the grave.

Then the Captain held out Pellow’s glass to Hale the runner, and with the glass, Pellow’s battery. Hale didn’t take it.

“I’m not a True Man,” Hale said. “I run.”

So the Captain gave the glass to Ruan of the spire, who had been Pellow’s second in command, and the battery went with it. Ruan put the glass to his eye, looked out past the cairn at the grey, and said what Pellow used to say to every man on the wall:

“Look closely.”

I have laid this tale the way it was laid for me.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

And the Captain pushed his hood back.

Part III · The Plan That Carved Its Own Defeat

LAID IN THE SIXTH WINTER, DURING THE LAST TIDE · ABOUT THAT NIGHT.

“Hold the stone, Seren. I tell myself that every time.”

This is how the longest knowing was told, and what was at the end of it.

In the sixth winter, during the Last Tide, the snow in the yard was knee-deep. A ship came at us carrying its fleet’s flag. It had a whole day’s plan carved into its heart, a plan with many branches. We sank it before noon. On the evening tide, the sea brought its heart in to the beach under this wall.

Rhyna of the Inner Gate put her hand on it, and Halvard Stone-Warden put his over hers, and I held the lamp. It was the longest knowing the two of them had ever held, but holding it wasn’t the hard part. They knew it in the time it takes to fill a lamp. I filled the lamp twice before they had finished telling it. And at the end of the telling was this:

When the bearer falls, the second takes the flag. When the second falls, go home.

They had carved their own defeat into the plan.

The lamp was low again, and their two hands hadn’t moved on the grain. Then one of them said, and the other finished:

“There’s one hand in all that carving, not a council’s.”

“And the hand is young.”

But that wasn’t what kept the two of them sitting on the beach. It was the last two words. To the young wood, home had only been the place a hurt thing runs to. This was a going-home carved into the plan before the first shot was ever fired. Someone had thought of it in the groves and carved it there on purpose.

Think of a man who has lain sick with fever all winter. One morning, before the thaw, he asks for his boots. His wife, at the fire, puts down the bowl she’s stirring, turns, and looks at him for a long time. A man who asks for his boots has a road in mind. That’s how the three of us looked at each other across the lamp. A mind that carves how it will go home is a mind that wants to go home.

That night, for the first time, we saw it: the enemy could hope.

One of them said, and the other finished:

“They carve a plan for tomorrow now, the way we do.”

“Then let’s build a tomorrow they didn’t carve for.”

The lamp went out on the beach, and I didn’t fill it a third time. The three of us went up to this hearth in the dark, by the old stairs.

I have laid this tale the way it was laid for me.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

BOOK SIX · THE BOOK OF THE HOMECOMINGS

In the sixth year of the siege, ten Thrones fall in ten havens and the havens come home; and in the last dead heart a green sliver asks to go home too.

VI.1 · The HomecomingsStone leaf

LAID ONE HAVEN AT A TIME THROUGH THE SIXTH YEAR, EACH ON THE NIGHT ITS THRONE FELL, IN THE ORDER THE HAVENS WERE TAKEN BACK · OF THE GOING-DOWN, FROM SPRING TO AUTUMN OF THE YEAR OF THE HOMECOMINGS.

Laid by the True Men, each for the haven that bore him, at that haven’s own fire on the night its Throne fell; and where a True Man had fallen, another spoke for him. I was at every one of those fires, and set each down beside it. Kael the Counter, oath-keeper of the True Men, laid the opening and the end at this hearth, when the tenth haven was home. I have set the havens in the order of their raising, and not of their homecoming, so that each may face its Throne. At each fire the True Man called the Throne of his haven by the name its heart had given Halyna that same day; the names the soldiers gave them stand on the facing leaf.

“Give me the stone. No, I will stand.”

Hear how the havens came home, and who spoke at each fire.

At the end of the sixth winter, on the eve of the going-down, Voss who asked held this stone, and told you that on the morrow we would go down to the havens; and in the morning we went down, the True Men and their troops before, and the masons behind with their chisels, by the old roads our fathers paved across the mountains. In the year the havens came home, from the spring to the autumn, we took them back, as the Captain chose them, one haven at a time; and in every one of them a Throne sat among the fallen walls, and waited.

Hear now the names of the havens, and hold them, for they are ours.

Eldhythe, the Eldest Haven.

Eldhythe · Voss, for Corlen.

At first light we came down the old road to Eldhythe, where the first walls of the havens were laid, and stand again; and the Throne of that harbour, that called itself the Tide-Heart, sank into the sea it came from, and did not rise. Corlen of the harbour should have brought the first boat in, for at the Long Hearth, in the first autumn of the siege, he said he would be the one who steered it; and under the east cairns he lies, so the tiller came to my hand.

I wet a finger and held it up, as he did before every holding, though what the wind told Corlen it never told me; and I brought the boat in for him on the evening tide, slow, as he had watched the boats come in all his life, and I waited, as he would, until the sea had finished.

I did not understand it then. I think I do now.

Then I went along the quay to my grandmother’s house, whither the ship-masters came in out of the rain; and the roof was gone, and the long table was gone, and over the cold hearth the three grey stones were still in the wall, and the lowest was scarred where the pipes were knocked. They are in my pack tonight, and I mean to carry them up the mountain, for they have stood long enough over a dead fire.

And the hearth said: We remember.

Tidesmeet, where the river meets the tide.

Tidesmeet · Tamm of the three spans.

In the one holding we struck all three heads, and none grew back, and the Throne of the three spans, the Bearer of Branches, went down into the river where the middle span had stood. Upon the bank, when it was done, I set out my three stones in their order, one for each span, as I have set them by my blanket every night of the war. One span is a plank. Three is a city.

And the hearth said: We remember.

Fenholm, the town that was always sinking.

Fenholm · Marl the fen-born.

In that holding the Throne of the fen, the Grain That Rots, went down into the fen like a log gone soft; and in the same hour the rot went out of our mortar, and it lies now where the pilings stood. A town is only the last course on the old, we used to say, and laugh at our own sinking; and my grandmother, old Ebba of Fenholm, carried the last course of our house out of the fen upon her back, and gave it into my pack for the road; and she did not live to see it carried home. I laid her stone on the old course, as she asked me. It has not sunk.

At that fire, too, the hearth waited a breath for Ebba.

And the hearth said: We remember.

Holtward, the keep under the canopy.

Holtward · Aske of the canopy.

At the last the Throne of the canopy, the Root-Heart, stood still among the trees of the south, and put down its roots into the old ground under the canopy, and would not rise again. I bade my men mind their hands, for these were trees once, and not an axe of ours was lifted. We left it standing. It had come home.

And the hearth said: We remember.

Carnhold, the stronghold of the cairns.

Carnhold · Garvel of the smithies.

When it was struck, the Throne of the cairns, the Stone-Grain, crumbled where it stood, stone into stone, and the cairns of the first quarrymen took it. The smithies are quiet. In the first of them I struck the cold anvil once, to hear it, and it rang true; and the first thing I make upon it will not be a gun.

And the hearth said: We remember.

Emberhythe, the forge on the burning shore.

Emberhythe · Harl of the burning shore.

At the end the Throne of the burning shore, the Burning, went out upon the shore like a forge left untended, and over it closed the black crust. The ground there is warm still. Today our mason laid his charred plank upon the crust, the plank he carried out of the Fall. I did not burn it.

And the hearth said: We remember.

Sandreach, the citadel carved in the canyon wall.

Sandreach · Ulden of the galleries.

In the last of the holding, the Throne of the canyon, the Deep-Beneath, burst up out of the sand under the east keep, and broke, and for an hour after, the whole canyon rang. Ere I came to this fire, I went down into the lowest gallery and lay with my ear to the ground, as I have lain every night of the war, and I heard nothing. Tonight I sleep under my own roof of stone.

And the hearth said: We remember.

Rimewatch, the watch on the frozen mere.

Rimewatch · Kael, for Hesk.

All that morning the mere lay white, and out in the white the Throne of the mere, the Long Winter, waited, and did not come on. Hesk of the mere stood upon the shore, with his mother’s lamp lit in his hand, though it was full day; for six winters he had waited to light it again upon the ice.

Ere noon he went out across the ice alone, to stand where his father’s hall had stood; and I heard the ice speak under him, as it spoke under him on the night he carried that lamp out of Rimewatch. That night it held.

At noon the white lifted, and the Long Winter lay out upon the mere, and the ice took it down through the place where the great hall stood.

The ice took Hesk too.

At dusk we went out along the edge of the break with ropes, and found his lamp upon the ice, burning. I swear to that.

He came home.

That night, the Captain cut a hand’s breadth from his half of the cloak, and bound it on a pike over the mere.

And the hearth said: We remember.

And the Captain put his hood back.

Added in Seren’s hand: That night at the Keep the north lamp was dark, for it was his to light; and then a child lit it, and said, “Someone has to keep a light.”

Highreach, the tower of the floating stones.

Highreach · Lanner of the crags.

At the height of the holding, the Throne of the crags, the Blood of the Sky, came down out of its cloud upon the crags, and did not go up again. When it was done I climbed the high crag, as I said I would, and stood at the top and whistled for the wind, and the wind came. It brought no sails.

And the hearth said: We remember.

Glasspire, the last and proudest.

Glasspire · Ruan, for Pellow.

In the last hour, in the dark of the cellars, the Throne of the spire, the Silvered, broke like a mirror, and every piece of it showed us our own lamps. Pellow of the spire should have seen it ere any of us, for he looked close. I have his glass, and I looked for him.

And the hearth said: We remember.

And at this hearth, when the tenth haven was home, Kael the Counter ended it:

Ten havens. I counted them twice, and it did not take long.

Tonight in the ward, ere I came to the stone, I called the roll of the True Men, for a count is not a count until it is named: fourteen names, and eleven to answer them. At Corlen’s name, and Pellow’s, and Hesk’s, the ward was quiet, and I let it be quiet for a breath, and called the next.

I am of no haven, for the Captain found me on a road; and this year I sat at ten fires, and at every one of them, when the tale was laid and the answer came, it was this hearth I heard. Every haven is Rivenkeep. I swear to that.

This I lay as it was laid for me.

And the hearth said: We remember.

LAID ONE HAVEN AT A TIME THROUGH THE SIXTH YEAR, EACH ON THE NIGHT ITS THRONE FELL, IN THE ORDER THE HAVENS WERE TAKEN BACK · ABOUT THE GOING-DOWN, FROM SPRING TO AUTUMN OF THE YEAR OF THE HOMECOMINGS.

Told by the True Men, each one for the haven he was born in, at that haven’s own fire, on the night its Throne fell. Where a True Man had died, someone else spoke for him. I was at every one of those fires, and wrote each one down right there. Kael the Counter, oath-keeper of the True Men, told the opening and the end at this hearth, once the tenth haven was home. I have put the havens in the order they were built, not the order they came home, so each one can face its Throne. At each fire, the True Man called the Throne of his haven by the name its heart had given Halyna that same day. The names the soldiers gave them are on the facing page.

“Give me the stone. No. I’ll stand.”

This is how the havens came home, and who spoke at each fire.

At the end of the sixth winter, on the night before the going-down, Voss who asked held this stone and told you that tomorrow we’d go down to the havens. In the morning, we went. The True Men and their troops went first. The masons followed with their chisels, by the old roads our ancestors paved across the mountains. In the year the havens came home, from spring to autumn, we took them back one haven at a time, in the order the Captain chose. In every one of them, a Throne sat among the fallen walls, waiting.

Here are the names of the havens. Hold on to them. They belong to us.

Eldhythe, the Eldest Haven.

Eldhythe · Voss, for Corlen.

At first light we came down the old road to Eldhythe, where the first walls of the havens were laid. They’re standing again now. The Throne of that harbour, which called itself the Tide-Heart, sank into the sea it came from, and didn’t come back up. Corlen of the harbour should have brought the first boat in. At the Long Hearth, in the first autumn of the siege, he said he’d be the one to steer it. He lies under the east cairns, so the steering came to my hand.

I licked a finger and held it up, the way he did before every holding, though the wind never told me whatever it told Corlen. I brought the boat in for him on the evening tide, slowly, the way he had watched the boats come in all his life. And I waited, the way he would have, until the sea had finished.

I didn’t understand it then. I think I do now.

Then I walked along the dock to my grandmother’s house, where the ship-masters used to come in out of the rain. The roof was gone. The long table was gone. But over the cold fireplace, the three grey stones were still in the wall, and the lowest one was scarred where the pipes had been knocked out on it. They’re in my pack tonight. I’m going to carry them up the mountain. They’ve hung over a dead fire long enough.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

Tidesmeet, where the river meets the tide.

Tidesmeet · Tamm of the three spans.

In one holding we hit all three heads, and none of them grew back. The Throne of the three bridges, the Bearer of Branches, sank into the river where the middle bridge used to stand. When it was over, I laid my three stones out on the bank in order, one for each bridge, the way I’ve laid them out by my blanket every night of the war. One bridge is just a plank. Three bridges make a city.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

Fenholm, the town that was always sinking.

Fenholm · Marl the fen-born.

In that holding, the Throne of the marsh, the Grain That Rots, sank into the marsh like a rotten log. In the same hour, the rot went out of our mortar. The Throne lies now where the posts used to stand. “A town is just the newest row on top of the old one,” we used to say, and laugh at our own sinking. My grandmother, old Ebba of Fenholm, carried the top row of our house out of the marsh on her back and gave it to me to carry on the road. She didn’t live to see it carried home. I laid her stone on the old row, the way she asked me to. It hasn’t sunk.

At that fire, too, everyone waited a moment for Ebba.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

Holtward, the keep under the canopy.

Holtward · Aske of the canopy.

In the end, the Throne of the forest roof, the Root-Heart, stood still among the trees of the south. It put its roots down into the old ground under the canopy and wouldn’t get up again. I told my men to be careful with their hands, because these were trees once. We didn’t lift a single axe. We left it standing. It had come home.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

Carnhold, the stronghold of the cairns.

Carnhold · Garvel of the smithies.

When it was hit, the Throne of the cairns, the Stone-Grain, crumbled where it stood, stone into stone, and the burial mounds of the first quarrymen took it in. The blacksmiths’ shops are quiet. In the first one, I struck the cold anvil once, just to hear it. It rang true. The first thing I make on it won’t be a gun.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

Emberhythe, the forge on the burning shore.

Emberhythe · Harl of the burning shore.

In the end, the Throne of the burning shore, the Burning, went out on the shore like a forge nobody’s tending, and the black crust closed over it. The ground there is still warm. Today our mason laid his burnt plank on the crust, the plank he carried out of the Fall. I didn’t burn it.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

Sandreach, the citadel carved in the canyon wall.

Sandreach · Ulden of the galleries.

In the last part of the holding, the Throne of the canyon, the Deep-Beneath, burst up out of the sand under the east keep and broke apart. For an hour afterward, the whole canyon rang. Before I came to this fire, I went down into the lowest tunnel and lay with my ear to the ground, the way I’ve done every night of the war. I heard nothing. Tonight I’m sleeping under my own stone roof.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

Rimewatch, the watch on the frozen mere.

Rimewatch · Kael, for Hesk.

All that morning the lake lay white. Out in the white, the Throne of the lake, the Long Winter, waited and didn’t come any closer. Hesk of the mere stood on the shore with his mother’s lamp lit in his hand, even though it was broad daylight. For six winters he had waited to light it on the ice again.

Before noon, he walked out across the ice alone, to stand where his father’s hall had stood. I heard the ice creak under him, the way it creaked under him the night he carried that lamp out of Rimewatch. That night, it held.

At noon the white lifted. The Long Winter lay out on the lake, and the ice pulled it down through the place where the great hall had stood.

The ice took Hesk too.

At dusk we went out along the edge of the hole with ropes. We found his lamp on the ice, still burning. I swear to that.

He came home.

That night, the Captain cut a strip a hand wide from his half of the cloak and tied it to a pike over the lake.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

And the Captain pushed his hood back.

Added in Seren’s handwriting: That night at the Keep, the north lamp was dark, because it was his to light. Then a child lit it and said, “Someone has to keep a light.”

Highreach, the tower of the floating stones.

Highreach · Lanner of the crags.

At the height of the holding, the Throne of the cliffs, the Blood of the Sky, came down out of its cloud onto the cliffs and didn’t go back up. When it was over, I climbed the high cliff, like I said I would. I stood at the top and whistled for the wind, and the wind came. It brought no sails.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

Glasspire, the last and proudest.

Glasspire · Ruan, for Pellow.

In the last hour, in the dark of the cellars, the Throne of the spire, the Silvered, broke like a mirror. Every piece of it showed us our own lamps. Pellow of the spire should have seen it before any of us, because he looked closely. I have his glass, and I looked for him.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

And at this hearth, when the tenth haven was home, Kael the Counter ended it:

Ten havens. I counted them twice. It didn’t take long.

Tonight in the yard, before I came to the stone, I called the roll of the True Men, because a count isn’t a count until it has names. Fourteen names, and eleven to answer them. At Corlen’s name, and Pellow’s, and Hesk’s, the yard was quiet. I let it stay quiet for one breath, and then I called the next name.

I’m from no haven. The Captain found me on a road. This year I sat at ten fires. At every one of them, when the tale was laid and the answer came, it was this hearth I heard. Every haven is Rivenkeep. I swear to that.

I have laid this tale the way it was laid for me.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

VI.2 · The Thrones That Walk the SeaWood leaf

KNOWN ONE AT A TIME THROUGH THE SIXTH YEAR · OF THE RISING OF THE THRONES AT THE END OF THE SIXTH WINTER, OF THEIR SITTING IN THE HAVENS, AND OF THEIR FALLING.

Known from the hearts of the Thrones, each in its own haven, as soon after its fall as it could be reached. At each, one of the two of them went in among the timbers, turn and turn about, while the other stayed at the edge with the soldiers, who would not go nigh, for the Thrones were fearful even dead; and the one outside knew it as the one within. A Throne’s heart is known in a breath. Its name comes last and hardest. The songs the wall made of them call them the commanders of the armada, the last living pillars grown into moving thrones; the wall named them Mystarchs, and the soldiers the Thrones; and from this leaf on I write them by the names they gave themselves. Told by Halyna, by turns, in two inks, and set down by me.

This is held in the grain.

We came late. It was not we who burned your havens; that was the young wood, green and hasty, one sign to a hull.

We were the eldest of the Naelsaen, the last pillars standing at the edge, with the sky held up between our crowns.

Since the iron we had put up no growth. We were waiting for the sky to come again.

When the groves had sent all they had, we were drawn up out of the earth and sent to your havens; and there we fell.

The shore was not yet silent, and the last of the long-lived who could carve came to us; and they could not wait. Slowly they cut judgements into us, for a judgement is not a word, nor a sentence, nor a morrow.

Out of the earth they drew us, and grew us into thrones, one for each of your havens. They set us walking on the sea, with all the hulls of the growing in our keeping.

When we went, the breath went with us, for we had been its anchors. Over the last groves the sky came open, hard and white.

The children of the long-lived lay down in the light, and could not breathe.

To come to you, we spent the last of our own sky.

Each of us took its seat in a fallen haven, where that haven had most wronged the wood, among our own dead. Our dead were the timber of our kin, in its walls and beams and bridges. We sat among them as mourners sit by a grave.

We had our own names. A judgement is heavy to carry, and under it the grain cried out.

The Throne the soldiers call the Leviathan, of the Heartoak grain, in the harbour of Eldhythe. Of that harbour the rolls say that its houses made their trade-tables of the black wood, and that the carved stones of the edge hung over those tables, and were priced; and three such stones were set in the wall over the hearth where Voss’s grandmother kept the fire.

We are Thaesaen, the tide-heart. Beneath the harbour we sat, among the timber of our kin, which held up its houses. Our judgement was Give them the middle; take the sides; close the hand. We closed it, and it held only the sea.

The Throne the soldiers call the Hydra, of the Ironbark grain, under the three spans of Tidesmeet. Of the spans the rolls say that they were laid upon the black wood; and a merchant of that city had one whole pillar hewn into a bed, and slept in it, and was proud.

We are Leavaren, bearer of new branches. Under the three spans we sat; and the spans were our kin, laid flat, and walked on by your feet. Our judgement was Strike one head; two will answer. Where we were cut we grew again, as our kin would have grown again, if the iron had let them.

The Throne the soldiers call the Plague Herald, of the Yew grain, in the fen of Fenholm. Of that town the rolls say that the pilings which held it up out of the fen were of the black wood.

We are Vaelress, the grain that rots. Into the fen our kin were driven, and a town stood on them, for our wood does not rot. Our judgement was Rot the wall; wait for the mending to fail. We were the one wood in the world that does not rot; and we were made into rot.

The Throne the soldiers call the Ancient Treant, of the Ironbark grain, in the deep woods of Holtward, the keep under the canopy. Of Holtward the rolls say that its roof-trees were of the black wood.

We are Ralensaen, root-heart. Our kin were the roofs of the houses under the canopy. We did not come as a hull comes; we walked, as a pillar would, if it could walk home. Our judgement was Go under what you cannot go through. We went under, root by root, and stood among the living trees; and they did not know us.

The Throne the soldiers call the Colossus, of the Blackthorn grain, on the cairn-mountain of Carnhold. There, say the rolls, the iron of the felling was beaten out, in the smithies under the cairns.

We are Rhenvael, stone-grain. On the mountain we sat, among the cairns. Our judgement was Every gun that speaks, the mountain answers. We answered with the mountain’s own stone, for the iron had spoken to us first, summer after summer, at the edge of the breath.

The Throne the soldiers call the Magma Titan, of the Twistbough grain, on the burning shore of Emberhythe. In the forges of that shore, say the rolls, the black wood was burned slow into the black dust that makes iron hot.

We are Esthaer, the burning. We sat in the ash of our kin, which had burned slowly, and not as a hull burns. Our judgement was Burn as you go; let them chase the fire. We made a trick of our own burning, and you followed; yet once there were no tricks in the wood.

The Throne the soldiers call the Sandworm, of the Willow grain, in the galleries of Sandreach, the citadel carved in the canyon wall. Of those galleries the rolls say that they were shored with the black wood.

We are Senneir, the deep-beneath. In the dark under the canyon, our kin held up the rock over the heads of the shore-men. Our judgement was Show them the host; come up beneath the stone. We came up beneath, by the roads our dead had held open for your people.

The Throne the soldiers call the Frost Wyrm, of the Yew grain, on the frozen mere of Rimewatch. Of the great hall upon the ice, the rolls say that it was roofed with the black wood; and Hesk of the mere told this hearth, that his people feasted under that roof through the long nights, and were warm, and that his mother kept its lamps.

We are Eirlenth, the long winter. Our kin roofed a great hall on the ice, and held its warmth. Our judgement was Wait in the white; strike the stone that cannot mend.

We waited in the white a long while, and no one roofed us.

The Throne the soldiers call the Storm King, of the Ashwood grain, on the crags of Highreach. Of Highreach the rolls say that its cranes, which lifted the floating stones, were of the black wood.

We are Naelthar, blood of the sky. The cranes that lifted the floating stones were of our kin, lighter than iron; and they raised your towers into our own sky. Our judgement was Come as the young wood came. Down out of that sky we came all at once, with the last of it in our sails.

The Throne the soldiers call the Crystal Lich, of the Silverbark grain, in the cellars of Glasspire. What Glasspire made of the black wood the rolls do not say; but Pellow of the spire said that his father ground its great glass, which threw the sun far out into the grey.

We are Neivaere, the silvered, the mirror. Long before, the sun was thrown at us from your shore; and where it fell, the breath burned away. No throne sailed the night the spire fell, and the grain holds nothing of that night.

Long after we came, and sat in the cellars of the spire among the broken glass, one shard among a thousand. Our judgement was Show them themselves.

We showed you yourselves, and you were hard to look upon; and so were we.

These were the ten, the last pillars of the breath; and now all of them are fallen, and your havens are your own again. The sky over the groves is thin, and the grain holds it still.

And no one answered.

KNOWN ONE AT A TIME THROUGH THE SIXTH YEAR · ABOUT HOW THE THRONES ROSE AT THE END OF THE SIXTH WINTER, HOW THEY SAT IN THE HAVENS, AND HOW THEY FELL.

Known from the hearts of the Thrones, each in its own haven, as soon after it fell as anyone could reach it. At each one, one of the two of them went in among the timbers, taking turns, while the other stayed at the edge with the soldiers, who wouldn’t go near, because the Thrones were frightening even dead. The one outside knew it just as the one inside did. A Throne’s heart is known in one breath. Its name comes last and is the hardest. The wall’s songs about them call them the commanders of the armada, the last living pillars grown into moving thrones. The wall named them Mystarchs, and the soldiers called them the Thrones. From this page on, I write them by the names they gave themselves. Told by Halyna, taking turns, in two inks, and written down by me.

This is held in the grain.

We came late. We weren’t the ones who burned your havens. That was the young wood, green and hasty, one sign to a ship.

We were the oldest of the Naelsaen, the last pillars still standing at the edge, holding the sky up between our crowns.

Since the iron, we had grown nothing. We were waiting for the sky to come back.

When the groves had sent everything they had, we were pulled up out of the earth and sent to your havens. And there we fell.

The shore wasn’t silent yet. The last of the long-lived who could still carve came to us, and they couldn’t wait. Slowly, they cut judgements into us, because a judgement isn’t a word, or a sentence, or a plan for tomorrow.

They pulled us out of the earth and grew us into thrones, one for each of your havens. They set us walking on the sea, with all the ships of the growing in our care.

When we left, the breath went with us, because we had been its anchors. Over the last groves, the sky broke open, hard and white.

The children of the long-lived lay down in the light, and couldn’t breathe.

To come to you, we used up the last of our own sky.

Each of us took its seat in a fallen haven, wherever that haven had wronged the wood the most, among our own dead. Our dead were the timber of our family, in its walls and beams and bridges. We sat among them the way mourners sit by a grave.

We had our own names. A judgement is heavy to carry, and under it, the grain cried out.

The Throne the soldiers call the Leviathan, of the Heartoak kind, in the harbour of Eldhythe. About that harbour, the records say its inns made their trading tables from the black wood, and that the carved stones from the edge hung over those tables and had prices on them. Three of those stones were set in the wall over the fireplace where Voss’s grandmother kept the fire.

We are Thaesaen, the tide-heart. We sat under the harbour, among the timber of our family, which held up its houses. Our judgement was Give them the middle. Take the sides. Close the hand. We closed it, and all it held was the sea.

The Throne the soldiers call the Hydra, of the Ironbark kind, under the three bridges of Tidesmeet. About the bridges, the records say they were built on the black wood. A merchant of that city had one whole pillar carved into a bed, and slept in it, and was proud.

We are Leavaren, bearer of new branches. We sat under the three bridges, and the bridges were our family, laid flat and walked on by your feet. Our judgement was Strike one head, and two will answer. Wherever we were cut, we grew back, the way our family would have grown back if the iron had let them.

The Throne the soldiers call the Plague Herald, of the Yew kind, in the marsh of Fenholm. About that town, the records say the posts that held it up out of the marsh were black wood.

We are Vaelress, the grain that rots. Our family was driven down into the marsh, and a town stood on them, because our wood doesn’t rot. Our judgement was Rot the wall. Wait for the repairs to fail. We were the one wood in the world that doesn’t rot, and we were turned into rot.

The Throne the soldiers call the Ancient Treant, of the Ironbark kind, in the deep woods of Holtward, the keep under the canopy. About Holtward, the records say its roof beams were black wood.

We are Ralensaen, root-heart. Our family were the roofs of the houses under the canopy. We didn’t come the way a ship comes. We walked, the way a pillar would if it could walk home. Our judgement was Go under what you can’t go through. We went under, root by root, and stood among the living trees. And they didn’t know us.

The Throne the soldiers call the Colossus, of the Blackthorn kind, on the burial mountain of Carnhold. The records say that’s where the iron for the Harvest was hammered out, in the blacksmiths’ shops under the cairns.

We are Rhenvael, stone-grain. We sat on the mountain, among the cairns. Our judgement was Every gun that speaks, the mountain answers. We answered with the mountain’s own stone, because the iron had spoken to us first, summer after summer, at the edge of the breath.

The Throne the soldiers call the Magma Titan, of the Twistbough kind, on the burning shore of Emberhythe. The records say that in the forges of that shore, the black wood was burned slowly into the black dust that makes iron hot.

We are Esthaer, the burning. We sat in the ash of our family, which had burned slowly, not the way a ship burns. Our judgement was Burn as you go. Let them chase the fire. We made our own burning into a trick, and you followed it. But once, there were no tricks in the wood.

The Throne the soldiers call the Sandworm, of the Willow kind, in the tunnels of Sandreach, the citadel carved in the canyon wall. About those tunnels, the records say they were held up with black wood.

We are Senneir, the deep-beneath. In the dark under the canyon, our family held up the rock over the shore-men’s heads. Our judgement was Show them the army. Come up from under the stone. We came up from under, by the roads our dead had held open for your people.

The Throne the soldiers call the Frost Wyrm, of the Yew kind, on the frozen lake of Rimewatch. About the great hall on the ice, the records say it was roofed with black wood. Hesk of the mere told this hearth that his people feasted under that roof all through the long nights and stayed warm, and that his mother kept its lamps.

We are Eirlenth, the long winter. Our family roofed a great hall on the ice and held in its warmth. Our judgement was Wait in the white. Strike the stone that can’t be fixed.

We waited in the white for a long time, and nobody gave us a roof.

The Throne the soldiers call the Storm King, of the Ashwood kind, on the cliffs of Highreach. About Highreach, the records say its cranes, which lifted the floating stones, were made of black wood.

We are Naelthar, blood of the sky. The cranes that lifted the floating stones were our family, lighter than iron. They raised your towers up into our own sky. Our judgement was Come the way the young wood came. We came down out of that sky all at once, with the last of it in our sails.

The Throne the soldiers call the Crystal Lich, of the Silverbark kind, in the cellars of Glasspire. The records don’t say what Glasspire made from the black wood. But Pellow of the spire said his father ground its great glass, which threw the sun far out into the grey.

We are Neivaere, the silvered, the mirror. Long before, the sun was thrown at us from your shore, and wherever it landed, the breath burned away. No throne sailed on the night the spire fell, and the grain holds nothing about that night.

Long afterward, we came and sat in the cellars of the spire among the broken glass, one shard among a thousand. Our judgement was Show them themselves.

We showed you yourselves, and you were hard to look at. So were we.

These were the ten, the last pillars of the breath. Now all of them have fallen, and your havens are your own again. The sky over the groves is thin, and the grain still holds it.

And no one answered.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

VI.3 · The Knowing of the Green WoodStone leaf

LAID IN THE SIXTH AUTUMN, ON THE NIGHT THE BOAT WENT OUT, NOT LONG AFTER THE TENTH THRONE FELL · OF THE TENTH THRONE’S HEART, AND OF THE SENDING.

Laid by Seren Two-Inks, who held the lamp. The knowing was done in the haven where the tenth Throne lay, and the boat was built and sent from the shingle below the Keep, and between the one and the other were some days of road. The tenth Throne I write here by the name it gave itself, as I have written the nine since their own leaf; and the one line in this tale that is the wood’s and not mine, I have set down as Halyna, the two of them, gave it to me, word for word.

“Hold the stone, Seren. I tell myself so every time.”

Tonight Halyna, the Bonded of the Keep, came and stood with me at the stone, Halvard Stone-Warden on the one side and Rhyna of the Inner Gate on the other, and each of them laid a hand on it beside mine, over the vow that the first builders cut upon its underside; and a long while we held it so, the three of us, ere anyone spoke.

Hear how the last knowing was done, the one that no one looked for.

In the sixth autumn, when the first frosts lay white of a morning upon the walls we had raised again, the tenth Throne fell, and it was Neivaere, in the cellars of Glasspire; and what was left of it lay where it had come to rest, as the nine before it had lain, black of grain and split open, a hill of broken timber higher than a harbour-house. The soldiers stood well off from it, nor would they go nearer, for they feared the Thrones even dead; but the Captain of the Torn Cloak came down to it himself, and stood beside it a long while, still, with his hood up, while all about him the soldiers came and went. Then he asked Halyna, the two of them, to know its heart and learn its name, as they had known the nine.

At dusk Halvard and Rhyna went in among the timbers, the two of them together, who all that year had gone in by turns; and I went behind them with the lamp. Deep we went, under beams that had been the boughs of the eldest wood and over ribs that our guns had cleft, where the air was cold and smelled of old sap and of ash, till in the oldest of the grain we found the heart, where the hearts always lie, grey and hard and still.

Somewhere in the broken timbers overhead, slow and far apart, the water dripped. And beside the heart, in a crack of the heartwood, lay a sliver of wood no longer than a hand.

It was green.

I brought the lamp down to it, for I did not trust the light.

In the long years of the siege we had searched the wrecks for orders, and the hearts for names, and the grey for sails; and of all the things we looked for in all that wood, not one of us had looked for a thing that lived. Yet this had lived, and was green still, and still aware; and it had grown in the dark of the dead Throne’s heart, out of sight, while the war went over it, as a seedling grows in the crack of a wall.

Since the first winter Halvard and Rhyna had not needed two hands upon any wood; but they put out a hand each all the same, his fingers and hers, and touched the green wood in the same moment. A drop fell from the timbers into the flame of the lamp, and hissed.

Their two faces changed, as one face changes.

For a long breath neither of them moved.

It was not an order, nor was it a wound, and every wood that ever we had known had been an order or a wound. The green wood holds one word, as the groves sang it; and Rhyna of the Inner Gate spoke that word, as though someone had put it in her mouth and gone away:

“Home.”

And Halvard said that she was right, as she had said it of him on the shingle below the Keep, in the first month of the war, when the first wood we ever knew had said shore.

But the green wood did not give the word alone.

As when a quarryman sends the noon call down the cleft, as he has sent it every day of his life, and out of the far face, where no one has cut in living memory, the call comes back to him with more in it than he sent: so the green wood gave Halyna the answer of this hearth, the thing you have said at the end of every tale, on every night of the war. Nor did it stop where we stop.

Halvard and Rhyna said it together, his voice and hers, in one breath:

We remember our home too.

The lamp was steady in my hand. Halvard’s hand and Rhyna’s lay upon the green wood, and did not move.

Then the knowing let Halvard and Rhyna go, and his hand and hers came away from the green wood in the same breath.

Rhyna wept, and Halvard did not, for she was weeping for them both.

I held the lamp until Rhyna had done.

Only then did they give me the other thing, the thing the green wood had folded in its answer, as a seed is folded in a fruit: a name that no carving of war had ever held, the name the mist-folk give themselves, which is not Mystaeri, for that is the word of the shore. Naelear they call themselves, those of the breath. I said it over three times ere I wrote it, as I do with every word, to hear whether it would hold; and it held.

That night, by the soldiers’ fire beside the fallen Throne, I turned back the leaves of this Book, and found therein, in my own hand, three things that the wood had said to us in other years; and I set them here beside the green wood, as they stand in their own leaves:

Since the iron we had put up no growth. We were waiting for the sky to come again.

The iron tires; and when it stops, the wood comes again.

Wait with them, and when the iron stops, they will grow the sky back.

The green wood was the first growth that any of the black pillars had put up, since first the iron came: a shoot of the eldest wood, grown in the dark of the last Throne’s heart when its war was done.

So it was seed.

In the days after, Halyna carried the green wood up the long road to the Keep, Rhyna bearing it in her two hands upon its bed of moss, and Halvard at her side with his hand beneath her elbow on the steep places; and when they came into the ward, a fisher-girl of the east keep ran out to walk beside Rhyna, and asked where it would go.

“Home,” one of them said, and the other finished, “to be planted in its own ground; and we two must find it a boat.”

In all the annals of the guild there is no Stonewright’s boat; for our fathers were builders and not sailors, and so are their children, and the sea was ever a thing we walled against and never a road.

But there were boatwrights among the fisher-folk of the havens, who had carried their adzes up the mountain as the masons carried their chisels; and on the morrow, at the asking of Halyna, the boatwrights came down to the shingle below the Keep with Tarnard at their head, the eldest boatwright, of Tidesmeet, where the river meets the tide. Tarnard was Tarnel’s father; and Tarnel walked the passes beside me, with a handle of the archive chest in his hand and the other in mine, and did not wake on the night of the pass.

Tarnard laid the keel with his own hands; and when the first plank was carried down out of the lowest vault, upon the shoulders of the masons, Harl of the burning shore said of it as he had said of every plank of the war: “Burn it.” But Tarnard laid his palm flat upon the plank, as a mother lays her hand on a child’s brow in a fever, and answered Harl:

“Wood that knows the sea knows the way home.”

Nor did Harl say more; and from that day he carried his share of the planks down to the shingle, and set them at the feet of Tarnard.

So the boat was built, of the wreck-wood of the grey fleet, of the planks that had lain in their rows in the lowest vault, every one of which had spoken to us once; and the fisher-folk bent the strakes and caulked the seams, and from the first grey of the morning till the lighting of the lamps the shingle rang with their mallets.

For the stem, Tarnard took the two planks that I had laid upon the floor of the vault, raw edge to raw edge, at the end of the second summer, when the knowing came whole: the first of all, that said shore, and the silent one from the far end, that said nothing; and Orvenna, the wall-masters, Ormund and Penna, keyed the two halves together, as stone is keyed. Penna held the last key to its place, and Ormund struck it, once.

The broken grain met, and held.

Then Orvenna and the masons keyed every joint of the hull as stone is keyed, so that no sea could work it loose.

On the evening that the stem was keyed, when the masons had gone up the mountain and the fisher-folk with them, Halyna came down to the stem, he and she, and I held the lamp. No pair of the guild had ever cut a mark in wood, for a mark is for the stone, nor had the wood ever known one thing of ours as we meant it. They had their two chisels, and the one of them set the edge and the other struck, and slowly, stroke upon stroke, they cut their Stonwryt across the join, where the halves of the first heart meet.

The lamp burned down a finger’s width under the chisels, and the tide came and went upon the shingle.

The wood took it.

Rhyna laid her palms flat upon the cut, and Halvard laid his hands over hers, as they had laid them on that same plank, on the shingle below the Keep, in the first month of the war.

“It says in wood what our vow says in stone,” one of them said, and the other finished, “harm to one is harm to both.”

When the hull was all but done, Tarnard left one lashing till the last, the lashing of the bow, where the stem stands up from the keel; and he put the cord into my hands, and said nothing, and stood back.

I tied it with Tarnel’s knot.

Tarnard looked a long while at the knot. Then over it he said his son’s saying, so low that none but I could hear:

“A net is only knots holding hands.”

On the day after, from the grey of the morning till dusk, the boat rode at the end of a long line off the point, in a hard swell, and took no water. It was small, and it was sound.

Today, at the turn of the evening tide, the whole hearth came down to the shingle below the Keep, the masons and the soldiers and the fisher-folk, and the old leaning on the young; and Halyna, the two of them, laid the green sliver in the bow upon its bed of moss, against the stem, where the two halves of the first heart are keyed. Beside it they set a Stonwryt coin, part gold and part black stone, trust sealed in stone: the holiest gift the Shorelands ever had, as the dark wood, which the Kneeling Guest laid upon the warden’s step, had been the holiest gift of his people.

Last of all Halvard and Rhyna laid their two chisels beside the coin, the same two chisels that went up in the courtyard on the day of the banner, and took the last of the light.

“Let them go with it,” one of them said, and the other finished, “for we two have cut our last mark.”

The boat lifted a little upon the turning tide, and no one on the shingle spoke.

As when the masons of a river town begin a bridge from their own bank, and raise the half of the arch out over the water upon its timbers, though no one has come yet to the far bank to raise the other, and leave the keystone’s place empty and true for hands that they have never seen: so Halyna knelt down upon the wet stones before the bow, he and she, side by side.

Behind them the whole hearth knelt.

The masons knelt in their aprons, and the soldiers laid their pikes down upon the shingle and knelt beside them, and Kael the Counter, oath-keeper of the True Men, who stands to tell a tale, knelt with the rest; and at the end of the line, with his hood up, the Captain of the Torn Cloak knelt down upon the stones.

The Captain is sitting at the end of the bench now, and the wet of the shingle is still dark upon his knees.

From the front of the line, where Halyna knelt, one of them said, and the other finished:

“That is the first step of the Guest’s rite, the gift and the kneeling, and the only step one people can take alone.”

“The rest needs the other.”

A wave came up the shingle and wet the keel.

Then Harl of the burning shore, who had said “Burn it” over every plank of the war, rose from his knees, and took the bow of the boat up in his two arms, and walked it down into the tide; nor did anyone bring fire to the shingle. The fisher-children set their hands to the stern and pushed it off, and the foremost of them, up to her waist in the cold sea, was the girl who had asked where the green wood would go.

The tide took the boat out; and when it was far out upon the ebb, a small dark shape on the evening water, with the green wood hidden in its bow, it turned, into the wind and against the current, as the Aelvaren, the Bearer of the Binding, turned long ago, and went away from us into the grey.

This time it carried a gift, not a grave.

The old ship-masters stood among us upon the shingle, and Merrick, eldest of the ship-masters, stood at their front, Merrick who in six winters turned his face from the sea, each time a hull burned under the wall. As an old fenman, whose father the deep water took, will walk a mile about one dark reach of the river all his days, and will not look upon it, and then, on a morning when his grandchild must cross that same water by the ford, stands upon the bank and watches every step she takes till she is over: so Merrick watched the boat.

The wind was hard off the water and threw the spray in our faces, and the boat was very small. A grey boat going out against the wind: that is the thing the ship-masters fear most on all the water.

They did not look away.

This I lay as it was laid for me.

And no one answered.

When the three of us took our hands from the stone, the hearth was silent a long while, and the fire burned down, and no one rose to put more wood upon the coals.

We waited, I think, for old Ebba of Fenholm, who on most nights of the war began the answer a breath before the rest; but she is under the east cairns.

Then, not loudly, a child near the front spoke. She was the fisher-girl of the east keep who had asked where the green wood would go, and who went first into the sea at the stern; and old Ebba, who gave this hearth its answer on the first night that ever it asked for a tale, was her great-grandmother.

“We remember.”

And the hearth said: We remember.

Then, for the first time, the hearth sang in the wood’s way, as the Rivenmen sing it still, and they call it The Two Homes:

Our home was hewn from the mountain’s bone; we forgot it, and found it, and held it in blood. Their home was breathed from the hearts of the trees, and the axes came, and the breath grew thin, and they have not found it, and they cannot hold it. Stone keeps the vow, and the grain keeps the truth. We remember our home. They remember theirs too.

LAID IN THE SIXTH AUTUMN, ON THE NIGHT THE BOAT WENT OUT, NOT LONG AFTER THE TENTH THRONE FELL · ABOUT THE TENTH THRONE’S HEART, AND ABOUT THE SENDING.

Told by Seren Two-Inks, who held the lamp. The knowing happened in the haven where the tenth Throne lay. The boat was built and sent off from the beach below the Keep. There were some days of road between the two. I write the tenth Throne here by the name it gave itself, the way I have written the other nine since their own page. The one line in this tale that belongs to the wood and not to me, I have written down exactly as Halyna, the two of them, gave it to me, word for word.

“Hold the stone, Seren. I tell myself that every time.”

Tonight Halyna, the Bonded of the Keep, came and stood with me at the stone, Halvard Stone-Warden on one side and Rhyna of the Inner Gate on the other. Each of them put a hand on it next to mine, over the vow the first builders cut into its underside. The three of us held it like that for a long time before anyone spoke.

This is how the last knowing happened, the one nobody was looking for.

In the sixth autumn, when the first frosts lay white on the rebuilt walls in the mornings, the tenth Throne fell. It was Neivaere, in the cellars of Glasspire. What was left of it lay where it had come to rest, the way the nine before it had lain: black-grained and split open, a hill of broken timber taller than a harbour inn. The soldiers stood well back from it and wouldn’t go any closer, because they were afraid of the Thrones even dead. But the Captain of the Torn Cloak came down to it himself. He stood next to it for a long time, still, with his hood up, while the soldiers came and went around him. Then he asked Halyna, the two of them, to know its heart and learn its name, the way they had known the other nine.

At dusk, Halvard and Rhyna went in among the timbers together. All that year they had taken turns going in. I followed them with the lamp. We went deep, under beams that had been branches of the oldest wood, and over ribs our guns had split, where the air was cold and smelled of old sap and ash. Finally, in the oldest part of the grain, we found the heart, where the hearts always are: grey and hard and still.

Somewhere in the broken timbers overhead, water dripped, slowly, far apart. And next to the heart, in a crack in the heartwood, lay a sliver of wood no longer than a hand.

It was green.

I brought the lamp down close to it, because I didn’t trust the light.

In all the long years of the siege, we had searched the wrecks for orders, and the hearts for names, and the grey for sails. Of everything we looked for in all that wood, not one of us had ever looked for something alive. But this had lived. It was still green, and still aware. It had grown in the dark of the dead Throne’s heart, out of sight, while the war went on over it, the way a seedling grows in the crack of a wall.

Since the first winter, Halvard and Rhyna hadn’t needed both their hands on any wood. But they each reached out a hand anyway, his fingers and hers, and touched the green wood at the same moment. A drop fell from the timbers into the lamp flame and hissed.

Their two faces changed, the way one face changes.

For a long breath, neither of them moved.

It wasn’t an order. It wasn’t a wound. Every piece of wood we had ever known had been an order or a wound. The green wood holds one word, as the groves sang it. And Rhyna of the Inner Gate said that word, as if someone had put it in her mouth and walked away:

“Home.”

And Halvard said she was right, the way she had said it of him on the beach below the Keep in the first month of the war, when the first wood we ever knew had said shore.

But the green wood didn’t give the word by itself.

Think of a quarryman calling his noon call down the gully, the way he has every day of his life. And from the far rock face, where nobody has cut in living memory, the call comes back to him with more in it than he sent. That’s how the green wood gave Halyna this hearth’s answer, the thing you have said at the end of every tale, on every night of the war. And it didn’t stop where we stop.

Halvard and Rhyna said it together, his voice and hers, in one breath:

We remember our home too.

The lamp was steady in my hand. Halvard’s hand and Rhyna’s lay on the green wood and didn’t move.

Then the knowing let Halvard and Rhyna go, and his hand and hers came away from the green wood at the same moment.

Rhyna cried. Halvard didn’t, because she was crying for both of them.

I held the lamp until Rhyna was done.

Only then did they give me the other thing, the thing the green wood had folded inside its answer, the way a seed is folded inside a fruit. It was a name no carving of war had ever held: the name the mist-folk call themselves. It isn’t Mystaeri. That’s the shore’s word. They call themselves Naelear, those of the breath. I said it three times before I wrote it, the way I do with every word, to hear if it would hold. It held.

That night, by the soldiers’ fire next to the fallen Throne, I turned back the pages of this Book. I found three things in my own handwriting that the wood had told us in other years. I’m putting them here, next to the green wood, exactly as they stand on their own pages:

Since the iron, we had grown nothing. We were waiting for the sky to come back.

The iron gets tired. When it stops, the wood comes back.

Wait with them. When the iron stops, they’ll grow the sky back.

The green wood was the first new growth any of the black pillars had sent up since the iron first came. It was a shoot of the oldest wood, grown in the dark of the last Throne’s heart once its war was over.

So it was a seed.

In the days after, Halyna carried the green wood up the long road to the Keep. Rhyna carried it in her two hands on a bed of moss. Halvard walked beside her with his hand under her elbow on the steep parts. When they came into the yard, a fisherman’s daughter from the east keep ran out to walk next to Rhyna, and asked where it would go.

“Home,” one of them said, and the other finished, “to be planted in its own ground. And we two have to find it a boat.”

In all the guild’s records, there’s no such thing as a Stonewright’s boat. Our ancestors were builders, not sailors, and so are their children. The sea was always something we built walls against, never a road.

But there were boatbuilders among the fishing people of the havens. They had carried their tools up the mountain the way the masons carried their chisels. The next day, because Halyna asked, the boatbuilders came down to the beach below the Keep, led by Tarnard, the oldest boatbuilder, from Tidesmeet, where the river meets the tide. Tarnard was Tarnel’s father. Tarnel walked the passes next to me, with one handle of the archive chest in his hand and the other in mine, and didn’t wake up after the night on the pass.

Tarnard laid the keel with his own hands. When the first plank was carried up out of the lowest vault on the masons’ shoulders, Harl of the burning shore said what he had said about every plank of the war: “Burn it.” But Tarnard laid his hand flat on the plank, the way a mother puts her hand on a feverish child’s forehead, and answered Harl:

“Wood that knows the sea knows the way home.”

Harl didn’t say anything else. From that day on, he carried his share of the planks down to the beach and set them at Tarnard’s feet.

So the boat was built from the wreck-wood of the grey fleet, from the planks that had lain in their rows in the lowest vault. Every one of them had spoken to us once. The fishing people bent the side boards and sealed the seams. From first light until the lamps were lit, the beach rang with their mallets.

For the front post of the boat, Tarnard took the two planks I had laid on the vault floor, raw edge to raw edge, at the end of the second summer, when the knowing came whole. One was the very first plank, which said shore. The other was the silent one from the far end, which said nothing. Orvenna, the wall-masters, Ormund and Penna, locked the two halves together, the way stone is locked. Penna held the last key in place, and Ormund struck it, once.

The broken grain met, and held.

Then Orvenna and the masons locked every joint of the hull the way stone is locked, so no sea could work it loose.

On the evening the front post was locked, after the masons had gone up the mountain and the fishing people with them, Halyna came down to it, he and she, and I held the lamp. No pair of the guild had ever cut a mark into wood, because a mark is for stone. And the wood had never known anything of ours the way we meant it. They had their two chisels. One of them held the edge and the other struck. Slowly, stroke after stroke, they cut their Stonwryt across the join, where the two halves of the first heart meet.

The lamp burned down a finger’s width under the chisels, and the tide came and went on the beach.

The wood took it.

Rhyna laid her hands flat on the cut, and Halvard laid his hands over hers, the way they had laid them on that same plank on the beach below the Keep, in the first month of the war.

“It says in wood what our vow says in stone,” one of them said, and the other finished, “harm to one is harm to both.”

When the hull was almost done, Tarnard left one tie for last: the tie at the front, where the front post stands up from the keel. He put the cord in my hands, said nothing, and stepped back.

I tied it with Tarnel’s knot.

Tarnard looked at the knot for a long time. Then he said his son’s saying over it, so quietly that nobody but me could hear:

“A net is just knots holding hands.”

The next day, from first light until dusk, the boat rode at the end of a long rope off the point, in a rough swell, and didn’t take on any water. It was small, and it was sound.

Today, at the turn of the evening tide, the whole hearth came down to the beach below the Keep: the masons, the soldiers and the fishing people, the old leaning on the young. Halyna, the two of them, laid the green sliver in the front of the boat on its bed of moss, against the post where the two halves of the first heart are locked together. Next to it they set a Stonwryt coin, part gold and part black stone. Trust, sealed in stone. It was the holiest gift the Shorelands ever had, just as the dark wood the Kneeling Guest laid on the warden’s step had been the holiest gift of his people.

Last of all, Halvard and Rhyna laid their two chisels next to the coin. They were the same two chisels that went up in the courtyard on the day of the banner and caught the last of the light.

“Let them go with it,” one of them said, and the other finished, “because we two have cut our last mark.”

The boat lifted a little on the turning tide. Nobody on the beach spoke.

Think of the masons of a river town who start building a bridge from their own bank. They build half the arch out over the water on its wooden frame, even though nobody has come to the far bank yet to build the other half. They leave the space for the top stone empty and true, for hands they have never seen. That’s how Halyna knelt down on the wet stones in front of the boat, he and she, side by side.

Behind them, the whole hearth knelt.

The masons knelt in their aprons. The soldiers laid their pikes down on the beach and knelt next to them. Kael the Counter, oath-keeper of the True Men, who stands up to tell a tale, knelt with the rest. And at the end of the line, with his hood up, the Captain of the Torn Cloak knelt down on the stones.

The Captain is sitting at the end of the bench now. The wet from the beach is still dark on his knees.

From the front of the line, where Halyna knelt, one of them said, and the other finished:

“That’s the first step of the Guest’s ceremony: the gift and the kneeling. It’s the only step one people can take alone.”

“The rest needs the other.”

A wave came up the beach and wet the keel.

Then Harl of the burning shore, who had said “Burn it” over every plank of the war, got up off his knees. He picked up the front of the boat in his two arms and walked it down into the tide. Nobody brought fire to the beach. The fishermen’s children put their hands on the back of the boat and pushed it off. The one in front, up to her waist in the cold sea, was the girl who had asked where the green wood would go.

The tide took the boat out. When it was far out on the falling tide, a small dark shape on the evening water, with the green wood hidden in its front, it turned. It turned into the wind and against the current, the way the Aelvaren, the Bearer of the Binding, turned long ago. And it went away from us into the grey.

This time it carried a gift, not a grave.

The old ship-masters stood with us on the beach. Merrick, the oldest of them, stood in front. For six winters, Merrick had turned his face away from the sea every time a ship burned under the wall. Think of an old marsh man whose father was taken by the deep water. All his life he walks a mile out of his way around one dark stretch of the river, and won’t look at it. Then one morning his grandchild has to cross that same water at the shallow ford, and he stands on the bank and watches every step she takes until she’s across. That’s how Merrick watched the boat.

The wind was blowing hard off the water and throwing spray in our faces, and the boat was very small. A grey boat going out against the wind: that’s what the ship-masters fear most on all the water.

They didn’t look away.

I have laid this tale the way it was laid for me.

And no one answered.

When the three of us took our hands off the stone, the hearth was silent for a long time. The fire burned down, and nobody got up to put more wood on the coals.

I think we were waiting for old Ebba of Fenholm, who on most nights of the war started the answer a moment before the rest of us. But she’s under the east cairns.
