Then, not loudly, a child near the front spoke. She was the fisherman’s daughter from the east keep, the one who had asked where the green wood would go, and who was first into the sea at the back of the boat. Old Ebba, who gave this hearth its answer on the very first night it asked for a tale, was her great-grandmother.

“We remember.”

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

Then, for the first time, the hearth sang the way the wood sings, as the Rivenmen still sing it. They call it The Two Homes:

Our home was cut out of the mountain’s bone. We forgot it, and found it, and held it with blood. Their home was breathed out of the hearts of the trees, and the axes came, and the breath got thin, and they haven’t found it, and they can’t hold it. Stone keeps the promise, and the grain keeps the truth. We remember our home. They remember theirs too.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

EPILOGUE

The tellers dead, one scribe left, and a grey boat come back.

The Stone out of the GreyThe leaf facing I.1

WRITTEN IN THE FOURTH YEAR AFTER THE HOMECOMINGS, ON THE LEAF THAT FACES THE FIRST TALE · OF THE SEVENTH WINTER AND THE SEVENTH SPRING.

Written by Seren Two-Inks, alone, upon the leaf that is bound facing Kael’s tale of the torn cloak, and laid at this hearth on the night the writing of it was done. From the first night of the hearth that leaf stood empty, with the grain of the wood drawn upon it and one line beneath it in my hand: Untold. Halyna cannot yet hold this deep. In the end no knowing of Halyna’s filled it.

“Hold the stone, Seren. I tell myself so every time.”

I write this alone, and I write it plain, with one ink and no measure but my own, for there is no one now to know the wood for me.

Now, in the fourth year after the homecomings, the two inks that I kept for Halyna, one ink for each of them, stand dry upon my table, the one beside the other, as they stood through every night of the war while I set the wood down; and I have not moved them, nor have I filled them.

Halyna are gone.

In the seventh winter, the winter after the tenth Throne fell, the two of them went in one night, as the Bonded go: Rhyna of the Inner Gate first, in her sleep, and Halvard Stone-Warden before the lamp was out, for he knew.

The two of them lie under the new capstone of the inner gate, in one grave, with one stone, and one name on it.

At dusk on the day after, we laid them there, in the gate that Halvard’s fathers cut and Rhyna’s forebears raised, and the snow came down through the gateway upon all of us who stood in the ward. Above the two of them, face down upon the underside of the capstone, lay the mark and the vow that they had cut there on the night of the Rite, and beside the vow the Title in the Captain’s strokes, where no eye will see them again unless the wall falls.

At the head of the grave stood Orvenna, the wall-masters, he and she, who never raise their voices on the wall; and Ormund began the old song of the guild, as he begins it at every laying of a stone, and sang it to the comma, and Penna sang the rest.

Then the Rivenmen sang, as they sing it still:

Stone upon stone, and the stone keeps faith; hand over hand, and the hand holds true; two at the gate when the grey sails gather, two at the gate, and the gate stands through.

That night, the Captain cut a hand’s breadth from his half of the cloak, and bound it on a pike over the gate.

It was the last of his half.

The Captain of the Torn Cloak went into that winter in a plain grey cloak, such as any soldier of the six companies might wear upon a road.

All the next day Idrenna, the eldest pair, who had put the first chisels into the small hands of Halvard and Rhyna, sat by the gate upon one bench and would not be moved. Over and over, in two halves, slowly, as the old pray, Idlan and Denna said the prayer for the dead, and no one else spoke; and one of them said, and the other finished:

“Lord, thou madest these two one, ere either of them drew breath.”

“One cradle they had of thee, and one stone of us; and thou wilt not part them.”

Idrenna were old when Halyna were young, and they have outlived them both.

In the lintel of the western stair, where the old wardens cut their names, the last name is Halvard’s, which he cut there with his own hand in the year the havens came home, under the names of his fathers; and below it the stone is smooth, and no one of his line is left to cut it.

I did not go with Halyna. To go where they could not, alone, and carry the Book on after them: for this the unbonded are left behind, the two of them told me once; and I did not believe it till I woke on the morning after, and lived. I am the first scribe of the guild to outlive the tellers, and so the Book goes on.

What the Covenant of Completion is, the Theoliths would not tell the children. I know now. God does not take the one without leaving the other a work to do.

This is mine.

On the night I laid my first tale at this hearth, I was afraid to speak. “Hold the stone,” one of Halyna said to me, and the other finished, “Hold it, and it will hold you.” There is no one now to finish it, and I say both halves alone.

Hear now what came to us out of the grey, and hold it, for the grey has sent nothing since.

In the seventh spring, on a morning of thin rain, when the sea below the Keep lay the colour of slate and the gulls were crying over the weed, a small grey boat came in upon the flood and ran itself up the shingle, as the Aelvaren, the Bearer of the Binding, once beached before the council-hall of the Mystaeri. No one was in it, and no one had rowed it.

By the old way of the coast it should have been burned upon the tide-line, and the old ship-masters stood at the head of the shingle and would not go down to it, for a grey boat that comes in on the tide is the boat that comes for you. One of them said the custom, as every man on that coast would have said it:

“What the grey sends, the fire sends back, or it comes again.”

Up to the gate a soldier of the watch went running, for fire.

Ere the fire came, the children of the Keep went down the shingle in a crowd, as children go down to whatever the sea leaves them, and the first of them laid both her hands flat upon the bow of the boat.

On the night the boat went out, when this hearth sat silent a long while and the fire burned down, it was a child who answered the wood at last, for none of us could: a fisher-girl of the east keep, the great-granddaughter of old Ebba of Fenholm, who said We remember.

It was the same child.

No ship-master on all that coast, in all the years that men have fished the edge of the grey, had ever laid a hand upon a grey boat; and the ship-masters at the head of the shingle did not move, nor did they look at one another.

Then down the shingle behind the children went Merrick, eldest of the ship-masters, whose father Daveth came home out of the grey upon a spar, and who had turned his face from the sea every time a hull burned under this wall; and he came slowly, as old men come down shingle, and the wet stones turned under his boots.

Merrick laid his hand on its gunwale.

When the soldier came down from the gate with the fire, no one took it from him, and he stood a while in the rain with the brand in his hand, and then he quenched it in the sea.

Last of all I went down, and the children parted before me as water parts before a keel, and let me come to the bow.

I knew the boat by its stem.

Its stem was the two halves of the first heart, the first wood that ever spoke to us, keyed together as stone is keyed; and across the join, where the two halves meet, was cut the mark of Halyna, the first mark of ours that ever went into wood. It was the selfsame boat that we sent forth in the sixth autumn, with the green sliver in its bow, and the coin, and his chisel and hers.

“Wood that knows the sea knows the way home,” Tarnard said, when he laid its keel upon this shingle. It had gone home, and it had come home.

In its bow, where we had laid the green sliver upon its bed of moss, there lay a stone.

It was not stone. Or it was stone that had been wood, grey-black and heavy, with the grain still in it, like the Guest’s gift that lay so long in the chest I carried up the mountain. The Rivenmen call it Myststone now, and they say it remembers all.

Whoever sent it had chosen, out of all the stones his folk had, the one that would carry a meaning. It is the one stone that speaks. Stone does not translate, and that is still true; but this was never only stone.

And it had been cut with a chisel. I know chisel-work, and every chisel leaves its own bite in the stone, as the chisels of the quarry-wardens left theirs in the old capstone, where Halvard Stone-Warden set his thumb on the day of the banner. The hand that cut this had learned the chisel late, and learned it well: young by their reckoning, I think, and seasoned by ours.

The rain had stopped. About me the children held their breath, and down the shingle the tide drew back over the stones with a long sound.

I knew the bite.

On the night of the Rite I held the lamp while that chisel cut a mark beneath the new capstone, and in the sixth autumn I held it while the same chisel cut the same mark across this stem. In the courtyard, on the day of the banner, I had watched it go up beside its fellow and take the last of the light.

It was cut in our own letters, so that anyone might read it. I did not lay my palms on it. I read it with my eyes. It was cut so that one could read it alone.

There upon the shingle, with the children close about me, I read it out to them:

In the memory of our home, our families...

The cutting stops there, in the middle of our Title, with the chisel’s last stroke still sharp at the end of the line.

Many nights since I have sat with it, when the hall was empty, and thought of the one who cut it. He never saw our torn cloak, for he never sailed; but every hull that came against our wall saw it flying, and what the hulls saw went home through the roots, and he knew it there, as the wood knows a wall, and learned our letters from it, for no other writing of ours ever went home to the groves. I have called him, to myself, the Last Carver; for I think that when he cut this there was no other.

And there is one small word in it that our Title has never had, the word the, set before memory, one word too many; and it is like the Guest’s three words at the warden’s door, I think: the right thing, said truly, by one who did not yet know how we say it. I have wondered whether the cutting stops there because his hand stopped.

No one came with it, and perhaps that is wise. We have not yet earned a guest.

Yet since that spring the inner gate has stood open every night, for I asked it, that the door might be as a guest would find it.

Before that spring was out, the staff was gone from the corner of the gatehouse, where it had leaned since the night of the Rite, and no one saw the Captain take it; and where it stood there is a clean stroke upon the wall, in the dust of seven winters. If you doubt the tale, go and look at the dust.

The slates say that when the work is done, the staff is laid down in it, and I think I know where it lies. Mine is the lamp now, and I know the stair. Some nights, when the hall is empty, I hear the wind go down it into the dark.

I have not gone down to look.

Over this hearth, where you sit, hang the three grey stones that were set once in the wall above a cold fire, on the quay at Eldhythe; Voss who asked carried them up the mountain out of his grandmother’s house, and no one at this hearth knocks a pipe on them. At every dusk the north lamp is lit upon the wall, by the child who lit it on the night that Hesk of the mere came home; for someone has to keep a light.

The archive chest that Tarnel and I carried up the mountain between us, a handle each, until the night of the pass, and that I carried alone thereafter, holds the mute stones still; and beside them now lie this Book, and the gift that was kicked into the dust at the warden’s door, and the Stone out of the Grey.

Grief is an inheritance, and it is paid out to the children.

Below the wall the sea turns upon the shingle, as it turned there before ever a stone was laid upon this ridge, and the sea has not finished. Above the inner gate, over the one grave of the two who cried the guild to its feet, the torn cloak still flies; and at night the wind comes over the high ridges where Rivenkeep still stands, and the old ones say it speaks the Title as it goes.

What we will send back, I do not know. I know only that it must be sent kneeling.

This I lay as it was laid for me.

And the hearth said: We remember.

And the Captain put his hood back.

WRITTEN IN THE FOURTH YEAR AFTER THE HOMECOMINGS, ON THE PAGE THAT FACES THE FIRST TALE · ABOUT THE SEVENTH WINTER AND THE SEVENTH SPRING.

Written by Seren Two-Inks, alone, on the page bound facing Kael’s tale of the torn cloak, and laid at this hearth on the night I finished writing it. From the first night of the hearth, that page was empty, with wood grain drawn on it and one line underneath in my handwriting: Not told yet. Halyna can’t hold anything this deep yet. In the end, none of Halyna’s knowings ever filled it.

“Hold the stone, Seren. I tell myself that every time.”

I’m writing this alone. I’m writing it plainly, with one ink and no beat but my own, because there’s nobody left to know the wood for me.

Now, in the fourth year after the homecomings, the two inks I kept for Halyna, one for each of them, sit dry on my table, side by side. They sat that way every night of the war while I wrote down the wood. I haven’t moved them. I haven’t filled them.

Halyna are gone.

In the seventh winter, the winter after the tenth Throne fell, the two of them died on the same night, the way the Bonded die. Rhyna of the Inner Gate went first, in her sleep. Halvard Stone-Warden went before the lamp was out, because he knew.

The two of them lie under the new capstone of the inner gate, in one grave, with one stone and one name on it.

We buried them there at dusk the next day, in the gate that Halvard’s ancestors cut and Rhyna’s ancestors built. Snow fell through the gateway on all of us standing in the yard. Above the two of them, face down on the underside of the capstone, were the mark and the vow they had cut there on the night of the Rite. Next to the vow was the Title, in the Captain’s strokes. Nobody will see them again unless the wall falls.

At the head of the grave stood Orvenna, the wall-masters, he and she, who never raise their voices on the wall. Ormund started the old song of the guild, the way he starts it every time a stone is laid. He sang up to the comma, and Penna sang the rest.

Then the Rivenmen sang, as they still sing it today:

Stone on stone, and the stone stays true. Hand over hand, and the hand holds on. Two at the gate when the grey sails gather. Two at the gate, and the gate stands strong.

That night, the Captain cut a strip a hand wide from his half of the cloak and tied it to a pike over the gate.

It was the last of his half.

The Captain of the Torn Cloak went into that winter wearing a plain grey cloak, the kind any soldier of the six companies might wear on a road.

All the next day, Idrenna, the oldest pair, who had put the first chisels into Halvard and Rhyna’s small hands, sat by the gate on one bench and wouldn’t be moved. Over and over, in two halves, slowly, the way old people pray, Idlan and Denna said the prayer for the dead. Nobody else spoke. One of them said, and the other finished:

“Lord, you made these two one before either of them took a breath.”

“They had one cradle from you, and one stone from us. You will not part them.”

Idrenna were already old when Halyna were young. And they have outlived them both.

On the top stone of the western stair, where the old wardens cut their names, the last name is Halvard’s. He cut it there with his own hand in the year the havens came home, under the names of his ancestors. Below it the stone is smooth. There’s nobody left in his family to cut it.

I didn’t go with Halyna. The two of them once told me that this is what the unbonded are left behind for: to go where the Bonded can’t, alone, and carry the Book on after them. I didn’t believe it until I woke up the next morning and was still alive. I’m the first writer of the guild ever to outlive the tellers. So the Book goes on.

The Theoliths wouldn’t tell the children what the Covenant of Completion is. I know now. God doesn’t take one without leaving the other a job to do.

This is mine.

On the night I laid my first tale at this hearth, I was too scared to speak. “Hold the stone,” one of Halyna said to me, and the other finished, “Hold it, and it will hold you.” There’s nobody to finish it now. I say both halves alone.

Now here’s what came to us out of the grey. Hold on to it, because the grey hasn’t sent anything since.

It was the seventh spring, on a morning of light rain. The sea below the Keep was the colour of slate, and the gulls were crying over the seaweed. A small grey boat came in on the rising tide and ran itself up onto the beach. It was the same way the Aelvaren, the Bearer of the Binding, once landed in front of the Mystaeri’s council hall. Nobody was in it. Nobody had rowed it.

The old way of the coast said it should be burned on the beach. The old ship-masters stood at the top of the beach and wouldn’t go down to it, because a grey boat that comes in on the tide is the boat that comes for you. One of them said the custom, the way any man on that coast would have said it:

“What the grey sends, the fire sends back, or it comes again.”

A soldier of the watch ran up to the gate to get fire.

Before the fire came, the children of the Keep ran down the beach in a crowd, the way children run down to whatever the sea leaves them. The first of them put both her hands flat on the front of the boat.

On the night the boat went out, when this hearth sat silent for so long and the fire burned down, it was a child who finally answered the wood, because none of us could. She was a fisherman’s daughter from the east keep, old Ebba of Fenholm’s great-granddaughter. She was the one who said We remember.

It was the same child.

In all the years men have fished the edge of the grey, no ship-master on that whole coast had ever put a hand on a grey boat. The ship-masters at the top of the beach didn’t move. They didn’t even look at each other.

Then Merrick, the oldest of the ship-masters, walked down the beach behind the children. His father Daveth came home out of the grey hanging on a broken mast. Merrick had turned his face away from the sea every time a ship burned under this wall. He came slowly, the way old men come down a beach, and the wet stones rolled under his boots.

Merrick put his hand on the side of the boat.

When the soldier came down from the gate with the fire, nobody took it from him. He stood in the rain for a while with the burning stick in his hand. Then he put it out in the sea.

I went down last. The children moved aside for me, the way water moves aside for a boat, and let me through to the front.

I knew the boat by its front post.

Its front post was the two halves of the first heart, the first wood that ever spoke to us, locked together the way stone is locked. Across the join, where the two halves meet, was the mark of Halyna, the first mark of ours that ever went into wood. It was the very same boat we sent off in the sixth autumn, with the green sliver in its front, and the coin, and his chisel and hers.

“Wood that knows the sea knows the way home,” Tarnard said, when he laid its keel on this beach. It had gone home. And it had come home.

In the front, where we had laid the green sliver on its bed of moss, there was a stone.

It wasn’t really stone. Or it was stone that had once been wood, grey-black and heavy, with the grain still in it, like the Guest’s gift that lay so long in the chest I carried up the mountain. The Rivenmen call it Myststone now. They say it remembers everything.

Whoever sent it had picked, out of all the stones his people had, the one that could carry a meaning. It’s the one stone that speaks. Stone does not translate, and that’s still true. But this was never just stone.

And it had been cut with a chisel. I know chisel work. Every chisel leaves its own bite in the stone, the way the quarry wardens’ chisels left theirs in the old capstone, where Halvard Stone-Warden put his thumb on the day of the banner. The hand that cut this had learned the chisel late, and learned it well. Young by their count, I think, and experienced by ours.

The rain had stopped. All around me the children held their breath. Down the beach, the tide pulled back over the stones with a long sound.

I knew the bite.

On the night of the Rite, I held the lamp while that chisel cut a mark under the new capstone. In the sixth autumn, I held it while the same chisel cut the same mark across this front post. And in the courtyard, on the day of the banner, I had watched it go up next to its partner and catch the last of the light.

It was cut in our own letters, so anyone could read it. I didn’t put my hands on it. I read it with my eyes. It was cut so that one person could read it alone.

There on the beach, with the children close around me, I read it out to them:

In the memory of our home, our families...

The cutting stops there, in the middle of our Title, with the chisel’s last stroke still sharp at the end of the line.

Many nights since then, when the hall was empty, I’ve sat with it and thought about the one who cut it. He never saw our torn cloak, because he never sailed. But every ship that came against our wall saw it flying. What the ships saw went home through the roots, and he knew it there, the way the wood knows a wall. He learned our letters from it, because no other writing of ours ever went home to the groves. In my own head, I’ve called him the Last Carver. I think that when he cut this, there was nobody else left.

There’s one small word in it that our Title has never had: the word the, in front of memory. One word too many. I think it’s like the Guest’s three words at the warden’s door: the right thing, said truly, by someone who didn’t know yet how we say it. I’ve wondered if the cutting stops there because his hand stopped.

Nobody came with it. Maybe that’s wise. We haven’t earned a guest yet.

But since that spring, the inner gate has stood open every night. I asked for it, so the door would be the way a guest would want to find it.

Before that spring was over, the staff was gone from the corner of the gatehouse, where it had leaned since the night of the Rite. Nobody saw the Captain take it. Where it stood, there’s a clean line on the wall, in the dust of seven winters. If you don’t believe me, go and look at the dust.

The slates say that when the work is done, the staff is laid down in it. I think I know where it is. The lamp is mine now, and I know the stairs. Some nights, when the hall is empty, I hear the wind go down them into the dark.

I haven’t gone down to look.

Over this hearth, where you’re sitting, hang the three grey stones that used to be set in the wall above a cold fire on the dock at Eldhythe. Voss who asked carried them up the mountain from his grandmother’s house. Nobody at this hearth knocks a pipe on them. Every evening at dusk, the north lamp is lit on the wall by the child who lit it the night Hesk of the mere came home. Someone has to keep a light.

The archive chest that Tarnel and I carried up the mountain between us, one handle each, until the night on the pass, and that I carried alone after that, still holds the silent stones. Next to them now are this Book, the gift that was kicked into the dirt at the warden’s door, and the Stone out of the Grey.

Grief is something you inherit. It gets paid out to the children.

Below the wall, the sea rolls on the beach, the way it rolled there before anyone ever laid a stone on this ridge. The sea isn’t finished. Above the inner gate, over the one grave of the two who called the guild to its feet, the torn cloak still flies. At night, the wind comes over the high ridges where Rivenkeep still stands. The old people say it speaks the Title as it goes.

I don’t know what we’ll send back. I only know it has to be sent kneeling.

I have laid this tale the way it was laid for me.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

And the Captain pushed his hood back.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

APPENDIX

Every carving the wood gave us, under its root.

The Book of Knowings

WRITTEN ONE CARVING AT A TIME THROUGH THE SIX WINTERS OF THE SIEGE AND THE YEAR OF THE HOMECOMINGS, AND CLOSED IN THE FOURTH YEAR AFTER THEM · OF EVERY CARVING THE WOOD GAVE US, FROM THE HASTY TIDE OF THE FIRST WINTER TO THE THRONES OF THE SIXTH YEAR.

Set down by Seren Two-Inks at the back of this Book, one carving at a time, as Halyna, he and she, knew each one from the wreck-wood and from the hearts of the Thrones; and here the carvings are gathered under their roots. It is the fleet’s own half of the Book, for every carving in it was cut by the wood, and none by us. Its last note is my own, of the three grey stones over this hearth, and it was laid last of all the leaves.

Every carving that the wood has ever given us begins with one stroke, and that first stroke is its root, as the first course is the root of a wall; and of roots there are ten.

Their shapes I learned by eye, at the tide-line and in the vault, long ere I could make out anything else in the grain; and the names I give them are my own, for a shape that has no name slips out of the hand like a wet stone. I said each name over three times before I wrote it, as I do with every word, to hear whether it would hold. They held.

The young wood cut the root and nothing more. Each Tide after it cut the same root again, and added to it, as a tree lays a ring about the rings within; and so the eldest carvings are only the youngest grown old.

In the late Tides I could tell the root of a carving by eye, from the wall, in the time it takes a wreck to roll over in the surf; and so I knew the kin of a carving long ere Halyna, he and she, had told the whole. Yet never did I know more than the root. The rest the wood gives only to the Bonded, and it must be known, not read.

More than a carving’s head the wood gives, for it gives the parts as well: who waited upon whom, and what each hull was told, and when the heart meant to turn; and those I have written in their places in this Book. Here I set down only the heads, from the youngest wood to the eldest.

The seven of the young wood I set down by the names the soldiers gave them, for the wall has used those names since the Night of the Naming, and they are worn smooth with handling, like the haft of an old mallet: To the shore, Break the wall, Go until struck, Before the bearer, Where it flashed, Wait in the grey, and Home when wounded. Their whole orders stand where I wrote them, on the nights they were known.

The root, as I name itWhat it looks like in the grainThe carvings, youngest to eldestWhose it was at the last
The Waveone stroke curling forwardTo the shore. (Hasty) → Through, not against. (Second) → Three roads, one hour. (Joined) → Give them the middle; take the sides; close the hand. (Deceit)Thaesaen, the Tide-Heart, who carved the last of these whole as its Judgement
The Lone Strokea short cut running ahead of a long oneGo until struck. (Hasty) → One goes; the rest follow the quiet. (Second) → Hold; hear the sounding; go where it is quiet. (Remembering) → Ask the silent water; heed its answer. (Joined) → Come as the young wood came. (Deceit)Naelthar, the Blood of the Sky, who carved the last of these whole as its Judgement
The Bar Beforea bar laid across a strokeBefore the bearer. (Hasty) → Screen the bearer. (Second) → Two roads to one stone. (Remembering) → The great hull first; the kept hand last. (Joined)Leavaren, the Bearer of Branches: Strike one head; two will answer.
The Sparka cut that ends in a star-notch, the flashWhere it flashed. (Hasty) → Make them speak; mark who speaks. (Second) → The first to speak, silence. (Remembering) → Pass the quiet gun; know it; take it going home. (Joined) → Sail the prize past their reach, and let them hunger. (Deceit)Rhenvael, the Stone-Grain: Every gun that speaks, the mountain answers.
The Mute Squarea closed square, which is how they cut the word for stoneBreak the wall. (Hasty) → The same stone, each morrow. (Remembering) → When the stone gives, the ram. (Joined)Ralensaen, the Root-Heart: Go under what you cannot go through.
The Two Mouthstwo strokes side by side, one of them hollowLoud in one place; land in another. (Second) → Work the east wall as if it mattered; the west is the road. (Remembering) → Let them see the host where it is not, and keep them seeing. (Deceit)Senneir, the Deep-Beneath: Show them the host; come up beneath the stone.
The Turned Sterna stroke that hooks back on itselfHome when wounded. (Hasty) → Show your stern; turn at the stone. (Remembering) → Draw them along the shore; come home by the far road. (Joined)Esthaer, the Burning: Burn as you go; let them chase the fire.
The Breatha gap left open between two cutsSpend their fire on wood of no worth. (Second) → Between their breaths. (Remembering) → When they strike the water, fall from the sky. (Joined) → They let the first wood pass; let the first wood bear. (Deceit)Vaelress, the Grain That Rots: Rot the wall; wait for the mending to fail.
The Knota stroke that stops in a knot of the grainWait in the grey. (Hasty) → Let the fire work. (Second) → Is the stone spent, or waiting? Look, then go. (Remembering) → Do the same thing thrice; the fourth time, mean it. (Deceit)Eirlenth, the Long Winter: Wait in the white; strike the stone that cannot mend.
The Smoothed Cuta carving filled and smoothed over, so that it can be felt and not seenGrey before the landing. (Second) → Look heavy where you are light. (Joined) → Sail straw down every road; where no fire wakes, send the landing. (Deceit)Neivaere, the Silvered: Show them themselves.

Seven carvings came of the young wood, and eight of the Second Tide; eight of the Tide of Remembering, and eight of the Joined Tide; and of the Tide that Learned Deceit, seven. The Last Tide carved no new head, but whole morrows, with turnings in them, out of heads already cut, and at the end of each a turning for home; and those morrows stand where they were known.

The Thrones gave eight Judgements of their own; and two of the Thrones cut no new Judgement, but carried an older carving whole, for the Wave and the Lone Stroke are the two roots in which every other carving is written. Forty-six there are in all. I have set down every one of them, and not one of them did I know.

One sign more came up the mountain in a mason’s pack, out of Emberhythe, the forge on the burning shore, and it was known on the Night of the Naming: Burn. It was cut in the Fall, and it came against the havens and never once against our wall; and in the year of the homecomings the mason who had carried it laid it down upon the black crust at Emberhythe, where the Burning went out. I set it here apart from the rest, and have not counted it among the forty-six; so there are forty-seven carvings in this Book, and forty-six in its count.

WRITTEN ONE CARVING AT A TIME THROUGH THE SIX WINTERS OF THE SIEGE AND THE YEAR OF THE HOMECOMINGS, AND FINISHED IN THE FOURTH YEAR AFTER THEM · ABOUT EVERY CARVING THE WOOD GAVE US, FROM THE HASTY TIDE OF THE FIRST WINTER TO THE THRONES OF THE SIXTH YEAR.

Written by Seren Two-Inks at the back of this Book, one carving at a time, as Halyna, he and she, knew each one from the wreck-wood and from the hearts of the Thrones. Here the carvings are grouped by their roots. This is the fleet’s own half of the Book, because every carving in it was cut by the wood, and none by us. The last note is my own, about the three grey stones over this hearth. It was the last page laid.

Every carving the wood has ever given us starts with one stroke. That first stroke is its root, the way the first row of stone is the root of a wall. There are ten roots.

I learned their shapes by eye, at the waterline and in the vault, long before I could make out anything else in the grain. The names I give them are my own, because a shape with no name slips out of your hand like a wet stone. I said each name three times before I wrote it, the way I do with every word, to hear if it would hold. They held.

The young wood cut only the root and nothing else. Every Tide after that cut the same root again and added to it, the way a tree grows a new ring around the old ones. So the oldest carvings are just the youngest ones grown old.

In the late Tides, I could tell a carving’s root by eye, from the wall, in the time it takes a wreck to roll over in the surf. So I knew what family a carving belonged to long before Halyna, he and she, had told the whole thing. But I never knew more than the root. The wood only gives the rest to the Bonded, and it has to be known, not read.

The wood gives more than a carving’s head. It gives the parts too: who waited for whom, what each ship was told, and when the heart planned to turn. I’ve written those in their places in this Book. Here I’m only writing down the heads, from the youngest wood to the oldest.

I’ve written the seven carvings of the young wood by the names the soldiers gave them. The wall has used those names since the Night of the Naming, and they’re worn smooth from handling, like the handle of an old mallet: To the shore, Break the wall, Go until struck, Before the bearer, Where it flashed, Wait in the grey, and Home when wounded. Their full orders are where I wrote them, on the nights they were known.

Learn it. Take it on the way home. (Joined) → Sail the prize just out of their reach, and let them hunger for it. (Deceit)Rhenvael, the Stone-Grain: Every gun that speaks, the mountain answers.
The Mute Squarea closed square, which is how they carve the word for stoneBreak the wall. (Hasty) → The same stone, every day. (Remembering) → When the stone gives way, the ram. (Joined)Ralensaen, the Root-Heart: Go under what you can’t go through.
The Two Mouthstwo strokes side by side, one of them hollowLoud in one place. Land in another. (Second) → Work the east wall as if it mattered. The west is the road. (Remembering) → Let them see the army where it isn’t, and keep them seeing it. (Deceit)Senneir, the Deep-Beneath: Show them the army. Come up from under the stone.
The Turned Sterna stroke that hooks back on itselfHome when wounded. (Hasty) → Show them your back. Turn at the stone. (Remembering) → Lead them along the shore. Come home by the far road. (Joined)Esthaer, the Burning: Burn as you go. Let them chase the fire.
The Breatha gap left open between two cutsSpend their fire on worthless wood. (Second) → Between their breaths. (Remembering) → When they shoot at the water, drop from the sky. (Joined) → They let the first wood through. Let the first wood carry the load. (Deceit)Vaelress, the Grain That Rots: Rot the wall. Wait for the repairs to fail.
The Knota stroke that stops in a knot of the grainWait in the grey. (Hasty) → Let the fire do its work. (Second) → Is the stone used up, or waiting? Look, then go. (Remembering) → Do the same thing three times. The fourth time, mean it. (Deceit)Eirlenth, the Long Winter: Wait in the white. Strike the stone that can’t be fixed.
The Smoothed Cuta carving filled in and smoothed over, so you can feel it but not see itGrey before the landing. (Second) → Look heavy where you’re light. (Joined) → Send fake ships down every road. Where no fire wakes up, send the landing. (Deceit)Neivaere, the Silvered: Show them themselves.

Seven carvings came from the young wood, and eight from the Second Tide. Eight came from the Tide of Remembering, and eight from the Joined Tide. Seven came from the Tide that Learned Deceit. The Last Tide carved no new heads. Instead, it carved whole plans for the next day, with branches in them, made out of heads already cut, and at the end of each one a branch for going home. Those plans are where they were known.

The Thrones gave eight Judgements of their own. Two of the Thrones cut no new Judgement, but carried an older carving whole, because the Wave and the Lone Stroke are the two roots every other carving is written in. There are forty-six in all. I’ve written down every one of them, and I didn’t know a single one myself.

One more sign came up the mountain in a mason’s pack, from Emberhythe, the forge on the burning shore. It was known on the Night of the Naming: Burn. It was cut during the Fall. It came against the havens and never once against our wall. In the year of the homecomings, the mason who had carried it laid it down on the black crust at Emberhythe, where the Burning went out. I’ve put it here apart from the rest, and haven’t counted it among the forty-six. So there are forty-seven carvings in this Book, and forty-six in its count.

Being set down anew in its own hands.

The Last Note · Three Stones over the HearthStone leaf

LAID IN THE FOURTH YEAR AFTER THE HOMECOMINGS, THE LAST LEAF OF THIS BOOK · OF THE THREE GREY STONES, CUT AT THE FEET OF THE BLACK PILLARS IN THE HARVEST, FIVE GENERATIONS TO THREE BEFORE THE BANNER; AND OF THE NIGHT THEY WERE READ.

Laid by Seren Two-Inks, last of all, on the night after the reading.

“Hold the stone, Seren. I tell myself so every time.”

Hear now the last thing that I learned, and the last that this Book will hold.

The three grey stones that hang over this hearth, where you sit, were cut at the feet of the black pillars in the Harvest, and carried away with the timber, and sold; and they hung in the wall above a harbour-house fire, upon the quay at Eldhythe, until Voss who asked found them over his grandmother’s cold hearth and carried them up the mountain.

On the first night of this winter, a child at the front asked what the stones said. In the havens, when a child asked that, the answer was always that the stones said nothing; and I did not tell her so, but told her that I did not know.

Every night since, when the hall was empty and the fire was low, I have held the lamp to the lowest stone; and I sought no meaning in it, for stone does not translate, but only the sounds. Since the third winter we have known this: that the Mystaeri cut our own words among their letters, by the sound, as near as their letters would go; and the sound of three words of ours I had from Brenn of the outpost, when Halyna, he and she, read his confession at this hearth.

Last night the hall was empty, and the fire was low, and the lamp burned down a finger’s width while I looked.

Then I had the three words, together upon the lowest stone, the sounds close and the symbols wrong:

Stop… Sky… Dying…

They hung over a harbour-house fire for three generations, and the ship-masters knocked their pipes out on them, and no one read them. I think I have read them. I say I think.

Stone carries no meaning. It carried only the sounds, and the sounds were close.

This I lay as it was laid for me.

And the hearth said: We remember.

LAID IN THE FOURTH YEAR AFTER THE HOMECOMINGS, THE LAST PAGE OF THIS BOOK · ABOUT THE THREE GREY STONES, CUT AT THE FEET OF THE BLACK PILLARS DURING THE HARVEST, FROM FIVE TO THREE GENERATIONS BEFORE THE BANNER; AND ABOUT THE NIGHT THEY WERE READ.

Told by Seren Two-Inks, last of all, on the night after the reading.

“Hold the stone, Seren. I tell myself that every time.”

This is the last thing I learned, and the last thing this Book will hold.

The three grey stones hanging over this hearth, where you’re sitting, were carved at the feet of the black pillars during the Harvest. They were carried away with the timber and sold. They hung in the wall above a harbour inn fire on the dock at Eldhythe, until Voss who asked found them over his grandmother’s cold fireplace and carried them up the mountain.

On the first night of this winter, a child at the front asked what the stones said. In the havens, when a child asked that, the answer was always that the stones said nothing. I didn’t tell her that. I told her I didn’t know.

Every night since then, when the hall was empty and the fire was low, I have held the lamp up to the lowest stone. I wasn’t looking for a meaning in it, because stone doesn’t translate. I was only looking for the sounds. Since the third winter we’ve known this much: the Mystaeri carved some of our own words among their letters, spelled by sound, as close as their letters could get. And I knew the sound of three of our words from Brenn of the outpost, when Halyna, he and she, read his confession at this hearth.

Last night the hall was empty, the fire was low, and the lamp burned down a finger’s width while I looked.

Then I had the three words, together on the lowest stone. The sounds were close. The symbols were wrong:

Stop… Sky… Dying…

They hung over a harbour inn fire for three generations. The ship-masters knocked their pipes out on them, and nobody read them. I think I have read them. I say I think.

Stone carries no meaning. It only carried the sounds, and the sounds were close.

I have laid this tale the way it was laid for me.

And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.

Being set down anew in its own hands.
